Preface and version policy

This book assumes that you already know Git's object model, refs, detached HEAD, interactive rebase, and the practical difference between a patch and a name. Its subject is the different set of invariants that jj makes easy to preserve.

The baseline is jj 0.44.0, the release available in the author's NixOS environment. A 0.45.x comparison track is included because 0.45.0 was released shortly before this work and 0.45.1 is the separately captured newer CLI. Commands and options were inventoried from local help. The raw snapshots, source URLs, discrepancy notes, and validation report are in the companion project under research/.

Reading the version tags. both means verified in both tracks; 0.44 is the normative NixOS baseline; 0.45.x is a newer-release note. Current-main documentation is never silently treated as 0.44 behaviour. Re-run jj version, jj help, and the supplied inventory scripts after upgrading.
Print profile. The HTML is authored for PDF printing to a Kindle Scribe: portrait 7.5 × 10 in pages preserve the device's 3:4 aspect ratio, use a generous inner margin for handwriting, suppress screen navigation, and reduce table density only in print. Graph diagrams intentionally use a PDF-safe ASCII glyph set (-, |, +, \, and >) so the bundled Type 1 PDF fonts cannot substitute missing Unicode box characters. In a Chromium/Firefox print dialog choose Save to PDF, scale 100%, background graphics on, headers/footers off, and portrait orientation. The source contains no screen-only dependency or external asset.

Official material consulted includes the Jujutsu documentation, the Git comparison, revsets, filesets, templates, configuration, GitHub, Gerrit, conflicts, operation log, workspaces, and the release notes. These links may move; the URL/title/version ledger is authoritative for this edition.

Licensing is treated conservatively. The book is original prose and diagrams, uses short command examples, includes no third-party images or logos, and does not reproduce long documentation passages. See the companion research/licenses.md ledger for Apache 2.0, CC BY 4.0 image, GitHub, Gerrit, and distribution-attribution notes.

Part I — jj primer

This part supplies the jj knowledge that the rest of the book assumes. It assumes Git expertise, but it assumes nothing about your jj experience and nothing about the state of the directory in which you begin. You may be opening an existing jj repository, adding jj to an existing Git repository, cloning a remote repository, or simply reading the commands before trying them.

The goal is not to make you memorise a Git-to-jj translation table. It is to establish a usable first vocabulary: how jj identifies the current working copy, how filesystem edits become graph nodes, how a change is evolved or separated, how a name is created for publication, and how a local mistake is recovered. The later parts replace these working descriptions with precise graph, identity, language, and operation semantics.

1. Starting from any repository state

Begin by identifying what you actually have. Do not assume that the directory is empty, that Git’s HEAD is the work you want, or that a local bookmark represents the intended publication point:

jj version
jj root
jj status
jj log
jj workspace root
jj git root

If these commands work, you are already inside a jj workspace. The unqualified symbol @ selects its working-copy commit. jj status tells you whether the filesystem has edits or conflicts; jj log shows the visible commit graph and names; jj workspace root identifies the directory context; and jj git root identifies the backing Git repository when the repository uses the Git backend. These inspections are useful even when you do not intend to change anything.

If the directory is an ordinary Git repository, initialise jj in place with jj git init. In jj 0.44.0, colocation is the default unless git.colocate=false is configured; --no-colocate explicitly chooses a non-colocated layout. jj imports the existing Git history and refs into its view. If you want a new clone, use jj git clone URL DESTINATION, then enter the destination and inspect it. If you are genuinely starting with no repository, jj git init DIRECTORY is the setup path, but that is only one starting state, not the assumed one for this book.

In a colocated repository, native Git commands may also be useful for inspecting or transporting Git refs and objects:

git status
git log --all --decorate --oneline
jj git import
jj status

The Git view and the jj view are related but not identical. Git’s HEAD is not the definition of jj’s @, and a Git ref is not the definition of a jj change. Run native Git deliberately, then import Git-side ref changes before reasoning from jj’s graph. Part VII develops this boundary in detail.

Starting stateFirst useful actionWhat not to assume
Existing jj repositoryjj status, jj log, then inspect @.That the current change is clean, described, or published.
Existing Git repositoryjj git init, then inspect imported history.That Git HEAD and jj @ are the same pointer.
Remote repositoryjj git clone URL DESTINATION, then fetch/inspect.That a remote branch is a local bookmark or that remote state is current forever.
Empty directoryjj git init, then describe or create work.That every reader starts with an empty root.

2. The core jj cycle

Once the starting state is known, the daily cycle is small:

  1. Inspect the current working-copy commit and its parent.
  2. Edit files with normal Linux tools.
  3. Run a jj command that snapshots the workspace, or explicitly choose how snapshotting should work.
  4. Inspect the resulting patch.
  5. Describe the current change when its purpose is clear.
  6. Use jj new when the next piece of work should be a new child change.

For an existing graph, the transition can be pictured without pretending that the base is a newly created root:

before:

main@origin -> A -> B -> @
                         current unfinished change

edit src/parser.rs
jj status
jj diff
jj describe -m "Handle quoted parser input"

after snapshot and description:

main@origin -> A -> B -> @'
                         successor of the old @
                         same evolving change, new commit object

There is no git add stage in this cycle. The filesystem is a working input to jj’s snapshot mechanism. A normal command generally snapshots the current workspace at its beginning, so jj status, jj diff, or jj describe may first record edits that were made by the editor. The precise snapshot boundary, including --ignore-working-copy, is explained in Part II; the practical rule is to inspect after a command and do not confuse live files with the last recorded tree.

printf 'new parser case\n' >> tests/parser.txt
jj status
jj diff
jj describe -m "Handle quoted parser input"
jj show -r @
jj new -m "Document the parser case"
jj log

jj show -r @ displays the current commit and its patch. jj new creates a child and makes it the current workspace commit; the preceding change remains in the graph without needing a local branch name. If the current change is not ready to separate, continue editing it instead. If you need to work on an existing commit, use jj edit R; that moves the workspace’s current commit and updates the directory to its tree, rather than creating a new branch.

The two choices—evolve the current change or create a child—are the central everyday decision:

IntentCommand familyGraph consequence
Continue the current patchEdit files, snapshot, describeSuccessor version of the current logical change.
Start dependent worknewNew child change; current @ moves to it.
Return to an existing changeedit RWorkspace pointer moves; the selected commit is not rewritten merely by editing it.
Inspect without updating files--ignore-working-copyReads the recorded workspace state, which may be older than the filesystem.

3. The everyday jj vocabulary

The rest of this book uses these terms precisely. They are not interchangeable versions of “the current branch”:

TermWorking meaningImportant distinction
Commit IDIdentity of one stored commit object.Changes when tree, parents, or metadata change.
Change IDIdentity of an evolving logical change.Normally survives rewrites of that change; not a Git commit ID or Gerrit Change-Id.
Working-copy commitThe commit representing this workspace’s recorded files; selected by @.Not a Git index or an uncommitted overlay.
BookmarkA movable local name pointing at a commit.Usually a publication name, not the mandatory container for local work.
Remote bookmarkImported observation such as main@origin.Not a live server lookup and not a local bookmark that follows @.
WorkspaceThe association between a directory and its working-copy commit.Not a bookmark, clone, or branch; detailed use comes in Part XII.
OperationA repository state transition recorded in the operation log.Not a commit in the user-visible commit DAG.
RevsetExpression selecting commits.Different language from filesets and templates.
FilesetExpression selecting paths.Different language from commit selection.
TemplateExpression rendering repository values.Does not select or rewrite commits by itself.

Several states are independent. A change may be mutable or immutable; private or published; clean or conflicted; visible or hidden. A bookmark may point at an older commit while @ advances. A fetched remote bookmark may be immutable in the local rewrite policy while new local descendants remain mutable. These combinations are normal and are why the later chapters use separate names for separate state dimensions.

4. First graph operations

The basic editing operations are graph transformations. You do not need their full algebra yet, but you need to recognise their intent:

before:

root -> A -> B -> @
        API    tests

jj squash --into A

after:

root -> A' -> @
        API + tests

A' is an evolved successor of A

jj squash combines patch content into a destination. jj split divides one change into two according to selected paths or interactive editing. jj restore copies tree content; it is not a reset of a ref. jj revert creates a new change that undoes an earlier patch. jj duplicate copies a commit as a new logical change. jj abandon removes selected commits from the visible graph according to jj’s rewrite rules. jj rebase changes parent relationships or moves selected sources to selected destinations. In all of these, inspect the graph afterward because descendants can be rewritten automatically.

When a rewrite cannot apply a patch cleanly, jj records a conflict in the affected commit. Use jj status, jj log, and jj resolve --list to find it. Resolve the materialised conflict with your editor or a configured tool, then use jj resolve as appropriate. There is commonly no separate Git-style rebase --continue; resolving the conflict is another change to the stored commit, and descendants can continue to exist while the conflict is present.

For a first safe experiment, create a temporary bookmark before a broad rewrite:

jj bookmark create before-experiment -r @
jj log -r 'before-experiment::@'
jj squash
jj log
jj undo

The bookmark gives the graph a readable rescue name. The operation log records the transformation independently, and jj undo restores the prior repository operation when the mistake is local. A bookmark is not a substitute for understanding the operation log; it is simply a convenient additional reference.

5. Publication, conflicts, and recovery

Local jj work does not need a bookmark until another system needs a Git ref. The basic publication cycle is:

jj bookmark create parser-review -r @
jj git push --bookmark parser-review --remote origin
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

The local bookmark names the selected commit. The push exports that name and reachable Git objects. Later edits to @ do not automatically move parser-review; move it explicitly, inspect the proposed change, and push again. Fetching updates observations such as main@origin but does not automatically rebase unnamed work. jj bookmark track parser-review --remote origin records a relationship between local and remote names; it does not make the bookmark follow the workspace.

On GitHub, a pushed bookmark can be the branch behind a pull request. On Gerrit, the same local stack may be uploaded through a review ref and may also carry a Gerrit commit-message Change-Id. The jj change ID, Git commit ID, bookmark, remote bookmark, and hosting-service identity are separate. Parts VI–IX develop publication and hosting without collapsing those identities.

When something goes wrong, inspect before performing a second repair:

jj status
jj log
jj op log
jj op show OPERATION_ID
jj undo

jj op log is the beginning of recovery, not an analogue to a branch list. It records repository transformations, including snapshots, rewrites, ref movement, and imports. It cannot undo an external server side effect such as an already accepted push, but it can often recover the local graph or ref state. Part XI turns this short sequence into a recovery laboratory.

Checkpoint. Before continuing, you should be able to enter an existing or newly initialised jj repository, identify the workspace-local @, inspect and describe a change, create a child with jj new, return with jj edit, recognise a bookmark versus a remote bookmark, publish a selected commit, and locate the operation that preceded a harmless mistake. You do not need to know the full revset, fileset, template, or rebase languages yet; those are the subjects of the following parts.

Part II — The jj mental model for Git experts

6. DAGs, snapshots, and identities

In Git, a detached HEAD is a temporary refusal to give a name to the tip you are developing. In jj, unnamed mutable changes are the normal working state. That makes your existing detached-HEAD habit a useful starting point, but the analogy stops at the ref layer: jj maintains a working-copy commit, snapshots it at command boundaries, and regards rewriting mutable history as routine.

The primary object is a commit in a directed acyclic graph. A commit has a tree, parents, metadata, a commit ID, and a stable change ID. Rewriting a change generally creates a new commit ID while preserving the change ID. The change ID is the identity of the evolving patch; the commit ID is the identity of one particular version of its content, parents, and metadata.

main@origin
     |
     A  immutable imported commit
     |
     B  mutable change: qpv...  commit: 91ab...
     |
     C  mutable change: wmx...  commit: 42de...
     |
     @  working-copy commit; the filesystem is its next snapshot

Commit IDs therefore move after amend-like work. Change IDs normally do not. The evolution log records predecessors and successors, and a divergent change ID means two visible successors of the same logical change exist because independent operations rewrote it. A numeric offset such as xyz/0 and xyz/1 can select the current and prior visible evolution when the release supports that notation.

Mutable, immutable, hidden, and visible

Mutable commits may be rewritten. Immutable commits are protected by the configured immutable set; remote-tracked commits and other configured heads commonly form that boundary. --ignore-immutable bypasses the protection check, not the meaning of immutable_heads(). Hidden commits are not in ordinary visible searches but remain addressable by IDs, evolution, or explicit operation queries. Visible heads are the tips of the visible graph, not “all branches”.

The root commit has no parent. It is an ordinary graph anchor for expressions such as root()::; it is not an editable working change. Empty commits are still commits: “empty” describes the tree delta relative to parents, not absence from the graph.

Snapshot timing is a semantic boundary

Filesystem state and the last recorded tree are different states. By default jj snapshots the working copy at the beginning of every command, then updates the filesystem at the end if the working-copy commit was changed. Thus a command can observe edits that no previous command recorded. --ignore-working-copy deliberately reads a possibly stale commit and does not update it. --at-operation implies that mode because the historical repository view cannot safely snapshot today's filesystem.

Commands that inspect or mutate the repository normally trigger a snapshot. This includes familiar read commands such as jj status and jj log; use --ignore-working-copy for automation that needs a stable read or when another process owns snapshotting. The snapshot itself is an operation, and a mutating command may also create descendant rebases and working-copy updates within the same operation.

What the Git analogy gets right—and wrong

ConceptUseful Git analogyWhere it breaks
commit IDGit commit object IDMay be replaced routinely by a rewrite; do not use it as the long-lived patch identity.
change IDPartly like a patch identity or Gerrit review identityIt is jj identity; the Git backend may serialise it in a non-standard header, but it is not a standard Git object identity or a server-side review key.
working-copy commitA detached HEAD commitIt is continuously snapshotted and may be rewritten; @ is workspace-local.
bookmarkA local branch ref when publishingIt is a movable name, not the organising unit of local development.
remote bookmarkRemote-tracking branchTracking is an explicit relationship; it does not make a local bookmark follow @.
operation logPartly like a reflogIt records repository transformations as an operation DAG, with undo and operation restore.
conflictUnmerged index stateIt is a stored value in a commit and can propagate through descendants.

7. The working-copy commit and the default workspace

The default workspace, for now

For the mental model in this part, commands are assumed to run in jj's default workspace. A workspace is the association between a working directory and a working-copy commit; the unqualified symbol @ means the working-copy commit for the workspace from which the command is run. That is the only workspace fact needed to follow the snapshot and rewrite examples here.

Do not read “workspace” as a synonym for bookmark, branch, or clone. The full model—additional directories, workspace names, shared repository state, stale workspaces, and selective filesystem materialisation—is deferred to Part XII. Until then, treat the default workspace as the ordinary local context in which @ is resolved.

@ names the current workspace's working-copy commit. A filesystem edit changes the next snapshot of @; it does not directly mutate an arbitrary ancestor. jj new creates a new empty child and normally edits it, leaving the old working copy as a parent. jj edit R moves the workspace's working-copy pointer to one existing commit and updates files to that tree.

Each workspace has its own working-copy commit and workspace symbol, although all workspaces share the repository's commit store and operation history. other@ refers to another workspace's working-copy commit. A workspace is therefore a second working directory plus a local point of view, not a separate clone and not a bookmark.

shared commit DAG:       workspace A       workspace B
                         A@ --- child      B@ --- review
                               \               /
                                same immutable ancestors

Think in two dimensions: graph state (commits and edges) and materialised filesystem state (one checkout per workspace). Many confusing situations disappear when you ask which one a command is changing.

8. A repository is more than the commit graph

For difficult jj problems, model the repository as several coupled state machines. The commit DAG is the object users publish. The working-copy state is one local materialisation of one commit. Bookmarks and tags are names attached to commits. Remote bookmarks are imported observations and tracking relationships. The operation DAG records transitions among these states. A useful diagnostic asks which component changed instead of asking whether “the repository changed”.

State componentLocal scopeMoves whenWhat it cannot tell you
Commit store/DAGRepositoryCommit creation, rewrite, import, abandon, restore, operation recovery.Whether a file is currently materialised or published.
Working-copy commitWorkspaceFilesystem snapshot, edit/new/movement, rewrite affecting its target.Whether a bookmark points at it or a remote has it.
FilesystemWorkspace directoryEditor/build tools, checkout/update, and jj command completion.Whether content has been recorded in a commit.
Local bookmark/tagRepository ref stateExplicit create/set/move/delete/rename and imported ref changes.Whether its target is current @ or accepted by a server.
Remote bookmark/tagLocal observation of remoteFetch/import and local forget/prune operations.Current server state when the last fetch is old.
Operation DAGClone/repositoryAny integrated repository operation.Uncommitted external files or another clone's history.

These components are coupled without being identical. A filesystem edit is not a new commit until snapshotting. A commit rewrite can update the filesystem if a workspace points at the rewritten path. A bookmark move does not rewrite a tree. A push can change a server while leaving the local commit DAG untouched. A fetch can create remote bookmarks without changing local mutable commits. A workspace operation can make files appear or disappear while leaving every commit tree unchanged.

Snapshot boundary experiment

Run this in a disposable repository and record both the commit ID shown by jj log -r @ and the filesystem checksum:

printf 'one\n' > file.txt
sha256sum file.txt
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj status
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
printf 'two\n' > file.txt
jj log --ignore-working-copy -r @ -T 'description ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj status
jj log -r @ -T 'description ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n"'

The first log can cause the edit to be snapshotted depending on the repository's current state and command boundary. The explicitly ignored log must not record the second edit; status without the ignore option then observes/snapshots it. The exact operation count is not the conceptual point: filesystem state can be newer than the recorded tree, and normal commands reconcile that boundary.

Concurrency rule. Do not use --ignore-working-copy as a universal “read-only” flag while another process owns a workspace and then act on the result as if it were current. It gives a stable repository view at the cost of possibly stale filesystem information.

Commit ID, change ID, and evolution

A commit ID identifies one immutable description of tree, parents, metadata, and conflict contents. A change ID names the logical change whose successive commit versions are linked by evolution. If A is rewritten into A′, then:

before:             root -- A -- B -- @
                    change A = qqq...
after describe A:   root -- A′ -- B′ -- @′
                    change A = qqq...
                    commit A′ ≠ commit A

B and @ can acquire new commit IDs even if their file content appears unchanged, because their parent commit ID changed. The successor relation is therefore a graph/evolution fact, not a hash relationship a user should recreate manually. An author timestamp or signature rewrite has the same property: tree identity can remain equal while commit identity changes.

When two independent operations rewrite the same logical change, both successors may remain visible:

          A_1  (change qqq..., commit 111...)
         /
root --- A
         \
          A_2  (change qqq..., commit 222...)

This is divergence, not corruption. The change ID alone no longer selects one commit; an offset or explicit commit ID is needed. Inspect jj evolog before choosing which successor to retain, merge, duplicate, or abandon. A template can display divergent() and change_offset(); a revset can select divergent().

Visible heads and immutable heads

A head is a graph boundary calculated from a set. heads(S) removes selected ancestors that have a selected descendant. visible_heads() is a repository-level notion of visible tips, useful for understanding why a log's graph has a boundary that is not named by a bookmark. An immutable head is a safety boundary used to reject rewrites, commonly including imported trunk and other configured public points.

These are different questions:

jj log -r 'heads(all())'                 # all graph tips in the chosen domain
jj log -r 'visible_heads()'              # visible repository tips
jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'            # rewrite-protection boundaries
jj log -r 'heads(mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks())'

Do not equate a visible head with a branch, or an immutable head with a server branch. A bookmark can point to a non-head commit in the middle of a stack, and a remote bookmark can point to a visible head without making the whole ancestry immutable unless the configured immutable set says so.

Detached HEAD as a starting point, not the whole model

Your Git habit of working detached already separates “the commit under development” from “the name used for publication”. jj extends that habit in four directions: the working-copy commit is explicit and snapshotted; mutable ancestors can be edited directly; descendants are rebased as a consequence; and publication names are optional until a remote needs them. The analogy fails if you continue to think that the current commit is a disposable pointer outside normal ref movement. In jj, @ is a named workspace state and its evolution is part of the repository's normal operation history.

A practical consequence is that “where should I create a branch?” becomes “which graph boundary needs a stable publication name?” If the answer is “none yet”, keep the stack unnamed. If a GitHub PR or Gerrit review needs a ref, create a bookmark at that boundary and treat it as an interface object. Local graph reasoning remains independent of the publication naming convention.

Part II field manual: observing state without collapsing it

An advanced jj session becomes predictable when you stop asking for “the current state” as though there were one. Ask instead which projection you need: the filesystem projection, the working-copy commit, the visible commit graph, the named-ref projection, or the operation history. The commands overlap in output, but each is an instrument for one projection.

ProjectionPrimary questionInstrumentTypical stale source
FilesystemWhat bytes and modes are on disk?Shell tools, jj status, fileset-aware file commands.Unrecorded edits or generated files.
Working-copy commitWhat snapshot does jj currently associate with this workspace?jj show @, jj diff, templates.Disk changed after last snapshot.
Commit DAGWhich commits and parent edges exist?jj log, revsets, jj show.Hidden commits or a historical operation view.
Ref graphWhich names publish which commits?jj bookmark list, tags, remote-bookmark revsets.Remote state not fetched or native Git refs not imported.
Operation DAGWhich repository transformation produced this view?jj op log, op show, op diff.Unintegrated/concurrent operation or later recovery operation.

Experiment: an edit that is not yet a commit rewrite

Use a disposable repository and observe the sequence with the same full identifiers. First create a non-empty change:

mkdir -p /tmp/jj-observe/src
cd /tmp/jj-observe
jj git init --colocate
printf 'one\n' > src/value.txt
jj status
jj show @
jj op log -n 5

At this point a normal command may have snapshotted the file, depending on the command and configuration. The important observation is not the exact number of operations; it is that a filesystem edit and the stored working-copy commit are different moments. Make a second edit and use a repository-only query:

printf 'two\n' >> src/value.txt
jj --ignore-working-copy show @
jj show @
jj status

The first show deliberately asks the stored graph not to incorporate current disk changes. The second show may snapshot according to normal command behaviour. Compare its operation log entry and commit ID. This experiment is the antidote to the phrase “jj commits automatically”: jj snapshots at defined command boundaries, and the filesystem can be ahead of the stored snapshot.

Experiment: the same logical change through three commit IDs

jj describe -m 'Parser: add a limit'
first=$(jj log -r @ --no-graph -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"')
change=$(jj log -r @ --no-graph -T 'change_id ++ "\n"')
printf 'limit = 42\n' >> src/value.txt
jj describe -m 'Parser: add a bounded limit'
second=$(jj log -r @ --no-graph -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"')
printf '%s\n' "$first" "$second" "$change"
jj evolog -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id ++ "\n"'

The commit ID changes because the tree and description changed. The change ID is the handle for the evolving logical change. The evolution log can expose the predecessor even after the old commit becomes hidden from ordinary output. If a script stored only first, it now has an exact historical object; if it stored change, it can resolve the current evolution, subject to visibility and divergence.

Experiment: descendants are rewritten because parent identity is input

jj new -m 'Tests: exercise the limit'
printf 'test limit\n' > src/value-test.txt
jj status
jj describe -m 'Tests: exercise the limit'
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

Now edit the parent change explicitly. A normal workflow can locate it by its change ID rather than by an unstable commit prefix:

parent=$(jj log -r 'description(substring:"Parser: add")' --no-graph -T 'change_id ++ "\n"')
jj edit "$parent"
printf 'limit = 43\n' >> src/value.txt
jj describe -m 'Parser: add a bounded limit'
jj log -r '::@' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj evolog -r 'change_id("$parent")'

The child’s parent commit ID changed even when the child’s own patch text did not. jj must represent the child relative to the new parent object, so it creates an evolved successor or a rebased descendant. This is why a review stack can acquire new Git commit IDs without each change being independently edited.

Four meanings of “current”

PhrasePrecise interpretationUse a different phrase when you mean
Current working copyThe workspace-local commit resolved by @.A bookmark target or the integrated operation.
Current graphThe repository view at the integrated operation head.A graph at --at-operation.
Current trunkThe configured trunk revset, often a bookmark/remote bookmark.The server’s latest branch if no fetch has happened.
Current changeThe logical change selected by a change ID or workspace target.The exact commit object that was previously checked out.
Current remoteThe locally observed remote ref state.The server’s live state before a fetch.

Hidden commits, divergent changes, and visible heads

jj’s visible graph is a useful working projection, not a complete object inventory. A rewrite can create a successor while hiding the predecessor. An imported ref can make a previously hidden commit reachable again. A divergent change can have multiple visible evolutions sharing one change ID. These states are meaningful because they preserve the evidence needed to reason about history evolution.

predecessor P_0  (hidden after rewrite)
       | evolution
successor P_1-------+
                    |-- child C_1
divergent P_2--------+
same logical change ID; different visible commit IDs

visible_heads() is a topology query over the visible working graph. It is not “all bookmarks”, not “all remote tips”, and not “the commits a server considers heads”. A commit can be a visible head without a bookmark, and a bookmark can point to a non-head commit because another child is intentionally local. Use heads(X) over a carefully defined set when the question is about a particular graph region.

When diagnosing divergence, begin with:

jj log -r 'divergent()'
jj evolog -r 'divergent()'
jj log -r 'change_id("PREFIX")' --no-graph -T
  'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj op log -n 20

Compare tree diffs, descriptions, authorship, and parent edges. A convergence decision may choose one evolution, merge content, or preserve both as independent changes. Do not use jj abandon on one evolution merely to make the warning disappear; that is a semantic decision that may rewrite descendants and publication refs.

Immutable heads as a moving boundary

Immutable heads are a policy set derived from configuration and graph state. Their descendants may remain mutable. This lets a repository protect imported trunk while allowing a local stack above it to evolve. The boundary can move after fetch, tag import, or configuration changes, so “I edited this yesterday” is not a stable safety argument.

immutable head T
       |
       |-- mutable A
       |       \-- mutable B(@)
       \-- another imported line

Query the boundary before a broad rewrite:

jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@'
jj log -r 'mutable() ~ ::main@origin'

If a source set includes an immutable commit, the command should fail rather than silently change public history. --ignore-immutable is an escape hatch for deliberate maintenance, not a normal solution to a mistaken revset. Record the operation ID, the exact immutable set, and the reason for crossing it.

When the detached-HEAD analogy helps—and fails

The analogy helps because local development does not require a branch name. The workspace points at a working-copy commit, and the user can move it with edit or create children with new without maintaining a branch pointer. It also helps because commit IDs are content-addressed and the user can inspect a graph directly.

It fails in five important ways. First, jj’s @ is a workspace symbol, not a detached Git HEAD file. Second, jj’s working-copy commit is a first-class mutable graph node, not an uncommitted index/worktree overlay. Third, descendants are automatically rebased when an ancestor evolves. Fourth, conflicts are values stored in commits and can propagate without an interrupted rebase session. Fifth, operation history records repository transformations beyond ref movements. Use detached HEAD as an entry point, then replace the analogy with the jj state model.

Notation and proof habits

Throughout the book, a capital letter names a commit only within a diagram. A bookmark is written name; an imported remote bookmark is written name@remote; a logical change is written X(change); a workspace-qualified working copy is written workspace-name@ when disambiguation is needed; an operation is written O_n. A diagram edge is a parent relationship, not necessarily a bookmark movement.

For every graph-changing example, prove three things: the selected source set, the resulting parent edges, and the identity/evolution mapping. For every publication example, prove the local bookmark target and the observed remote target. For every recovery example, prove which operation created the restored view. These proof habits scale better than memorising command folklore.

Graph-first diagnosis: the repository-state tuple

When a jj result surprises you, do not collapse the explanation into “the branch moved”. At minimum, describe the state as a tuple:

R = (C, W, F, B, T, Q, O)

C  commit DAG and trees
W  workspace records and each workspace's @
F  materialised filesystem per workspace
B  local bookmarks and their targets
T  tags and remote observations/tracking relationships
Q  configuration, visibility, and immutability policy
O  operation DAG/current operation view

A command usually changes one component and may derive changes in others. Filesystem editing changes F; the next snapshot updates C and O. jj edit changes W and F, and may snapshot the old F first. jj rebase changes C and O, may update W/F when a workspace target is affected, and can move B when a bookmark points into the rewritten graph. jj git fetch changes C's imported objects and T's remote observations but does not change an unnamed local child. jj git push changes the external server and may update local tracking state, but it is not a commit rewrite.

ObservationPossible explanationsDisambiguating command
File exists on disk but is absent from jj diff.It is untracked/ignored, the snapshot is stale, or the diff commit is not @.jj status, then jj log -r @; compare with --ignore-working-copy.
Commit ID changed but description did not.Parent, tree, timestamp, author, signing, or export metadata changed.jj evolog -r CHANGE and jj op show -p.
Bookmark is behind the current working copy.Bookmarks do not follow @ automatically.jj bookmark list; move it explicitly if publication is intended.
Remote bookmark is present but push is rejected.Local remote observation is stale, server ref advanced, or ancestry is not acceptable.Fetch, compare both targets and the graph, then decide.
Another workspace shows a new commit.Shared graph rewrite or shared ref movement; its own @ is still local.jj workspace list and logs from both directories.
--at-operation shows a different tree than disk.Historical repository view deliberately ignores today's filesystem.Return to the current view and inspect the working-copy snapshot separately.

This tuple prevents a common category error: interpreting an operation-level change as a commit-level change, a ref observation as a server action, or a filesystem absence as a deletion from history. Write the tuple components in an incident note before trying a repair.

Snapshotting as a state machine

filesystem edit
      |
      ▼
F_new differs from recorded tree C(@)
      | normal jj command
      ▼
snapshot operation records C(@)' with F_new
      | command mutation
      ▼
rewrite/create descendants and update workspace filesystem
      |
      ▼
new operation view O_next

Snapshot timing is a semantic boundary because a command can both observe and cause the transition. A normal jj log may first record an editor's changes, then render the new working-copy commit. A log with --ignore-working-copy reads the recorded tree without reconciling disk. A command at --at-operation OP cannot safely snapshot current files into a historical operation, so it implies the same non-snapshot mode.

Use these paired experiments when debugging an editor or build process:

printf 'unrecorded\n' > note.txt
jj log --ignore-working-copy -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ \"\\n\"'
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ \" \" ++ description.first_line() ++ \"\\n\"'
jj op log -n 3
printf 'second\n' >> note.txt
jj status
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ \" \" ++ description.first_line() ++ \"\\n\"'

The exact number of operations depends on whether the first recorded tree already contained the file and on configuration. The invariant to test is not a fixed operation count; it is that ignored inspection cannot be used to claim current filesystem state, while normal snapshotting reconciles the working copy at the command boundary.

Identity through a rewrite

before: base--A--B--C(@)
                   A id a1 / ch A
                   B id b1 / ch B
                   C id c1 / ch C

edit A:  base--A'--B'--C'(@)
                A' id a2 / ch A
                B' id b2 / ch B
                C' id c2 / ch C
IdentityBeforeAfterInterpretation
Commit ID of Aa1a2New exact object because content/metadata changed.
jj change ID of Ach Ach ASame logical evolution under ordinary rewrite.
Commit ID of B/Cb1/c1b2/c2Parent identity changed, so descendants are successors.
Bookmark at Cpoints c1normally points c2Local ref follows the rewritten graph, subject to conflict/divergence rules.
Remote bookmarkobserves server objectstill observes old object until publication/fetchRemote observation is not local bookmark auto-follow.
OperationO_beforeO_afterRepository transition records the rewrite and collateral effects.

The table explains why a review workflow can retain a logical change identity while sending a new commit object to a Git host. It also explains why a descendant's commit ID is not evidence that its own files were edited: the parent ID alone is sufficient to change its object ID.

Visibility and divergence diagnosis

Visibility controls ordinary search, not object existence. An abandoned predecessor can remain in the store and in an evolution chain. A divergent change has more than one visible evolution for one jj change ID, usually because two operations independently rewrote the same logical change. A hidden predecessor and a divergent successor can coexist. Use all(), hidden(), divergent(), and evolog as distinct probes rather than one broad “show everything” command.

jj log -r 'divergent()'
jj log -r 'hidden()'
jj evolog -r 'change_id(CHANGE)'
jj op log
jj log -r 'heads(mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks())'

A visible head is a maximal node in a selected graph domain. It need not have a bookmark. A bookmark can point to an internal node with a child that is intentionally local. A remote bookmark can be a visible head without making all its ancestors immutable if the policy says otherwise. These relationships are reasons to state the revset domain whenever using a word such as “head”, “public”, or “unpublished”.

The detached-HEAD analogy boundary test

Git detached-HEAD intuitionjj resultWhere the intuition fails
There is one unnamed tip.@ names this workspace's working-copy commit.Other workspaces have other @ values and the graph can have many unnamed heads.
Editing a file dirties the checkout.Editing dirties the next snapshot of @.Normal jj commands may record that state; the boundary is not git commit.
Amend replaces HEAD.Rewriting makes a successor with a normally stable change ID.Descendants are automatically rebased and conflicts are values.
Branch is needed to publish.Bookmark is created at publication.Bookmarks are not the organising unit of local editing.
Reflog recovers ref movement.Operation DAG and evolution log recover different questions.Operation IDs are clone-local and not Git transport objects.

The analogy is therefore a bridge, not the destination. Use it to understand why unnamed work feels natural, then deliberately switch to the tuple, graph, identity, and operation vocabulary for anything involving rewriting, publication, or recovery.

Part III — Rewriting history as ordinary work

9. Patch-stack surgery

The default question is not “which branch do I rebase?” but “which commits should be rewritten, and where should their parent edges point?” Every rewrite produces successor commit IDs and usually preserves change IDs. Descendants are automatically rebased when a rewritten commit changes their parent or patch context.

Editing and metadata

CommandGraph/content effectUse
jj edit RMoves the workspace pointer; does not rewrite R.Inspect or continue work on an existing change.
jj describe RRewrites description and selected metadata.Rename a change while preserving its change ID.
jj metaedit RRewrites metadata without changing file content.Author, committer, timestamp, or signing metadata as supported.
jj diffedit RReplaces selected content using a diff editor.Touch up a patch in place.
jj squashMoves a patch into a destination, commonly its parent.Fold fixups or selected files.
jj splitReplaces one change with two successors.Separate logical concerns interactively or by fileset.
jj absorbMoves hunks from a change into suitable mutable ancestors.Distribute fixups across a stack.
jj duplicate RCreates a new change with R's content.Copy a patch to another location; identity is new.
jj abandon RRemoves selected commits from visible graph and reconnects descendants.Discard a change; recover through operation history if needed.
jj restoreRestores selected paths in a destination commit to content from a source tree; without explicit operands, restores @ from its parent.Content undo or replacement without moving parent edges or selecting a new commit location.
jj revert RCreates a new change applying the inverse of R.Publish an explicit reversal; it is not abandonment.

For a stack A—B—C—@, editing A may produce A′—B′—C′—@′. B, C, and @ have new commit IDs because their parent trees changed; their change IDs identify the evolving logical changes. A conflict in B′ is not a failed transaction to be continued: it is a value in B′, and later operations can rewrite or resolve it.

Content movement example

before:       A -- B -- C -- @
              fixup in C

jj absorb:
after:        A′ -- B′ -- C′ -- @′
              hunk is applied to the earliest compatible mutable change

jj squash --from C --into B:
after:        A′ -- B″ -- C″ -- @″
              C″ is generally empty or contains its remaining patch

Use jj restore --from R --into S -- fileset to undo or replace selected content in S: the selected paths in S are restored to the content they had in source commit R. In the common case, jj restore restores the working-copy commit from its parent, undoing the current commit’s content changes while preserving its description and other metadata. Use jj restore --changes-in R when the intent is specifically to undo the patch introduced by R. This is different from jj duplicate R, which creates a new patch with copied content, and from jj revert R, which creates a new inverse patch. These are three different intentions that Git users often collapse into “cherry-pick”.

10. Rebase selection and destination algebra

0.44 baseline. The installed help defines source selectors -s/--source, -b/--branch, and -r/--revisions, with -b @ as the default. Destinations are -o/--onto (aliases -d/--destination), -A/--insert-after (alias --after), and -B/--insert-before (alias --before). The long forms are preferable in durable documentation.

-s X selects X and all descendants; multiple source roots become direct children of the destination. -r X selects exactly X (or the set supplied), preserving dependencies inside the set and filling holes by rebasing descendants. -b X selects the branch relative to the destination: in the common single-root case, (destination..X)::. None of these selectors means “a Git branch”.

O                 O
|                 |
M                 M′
|        -s M -o O|
L        ------->  L′
|                 |
K                 K′
|                 |
J                 J

-o O moves selected commits onto O and leaves O's existing descendants alone. -A O inserts the selected commits after O and rebases O's existing descendants on top of them. -B O inserts before O by using O's parents, then rebases O and its descendants. Repeat -o to create a merge commit:

jj rebase -s L -o K -o M

That command gives L′ parents K and M. It is often clearer than manually constructing a merge. -A and -B can be combined for merge surgery.

Rebase is graph transformation plus patch preservation. If the destination contains incompatible content, jj records the conflict in the rebased successor. A subsequent jj resolve, jj diffedit, or ordinary edit changes that value; there is no Git-style “rebase session” state that requires --continue.

11. Rewrite experiments: preserve a patch, change its context

Every history rewrite is easier to reason about when the patch and its parent context are written separately. A commit's effective patch is the difference between its tree and the automatic merge of its parents. Rebase changes parent context and asks jj to preserve that patch. Squash changes which commit owns content. Split partitions one patch. Restore changes tree content directly. Metaedit changes metadata while leaving the tree alone.

OperationPrimary inputPrimary resultTypical identity result
describe/metaeditmetadatasame tree, new metadatanew commit ID; same change ID unless requested otherwise.
diffeditone commit's patchedited patchsuccessor of same change.
squashsource patch and destinationcontent ownership movessource/destination evolutions; descriptions need review.
splitone patch/fileset/hunkstwo sequential changestwo resulting change identities; inspect evolog.
absorbhunks plus compatible ancestorsinference-based distributionseveral changes can evolve.
restoresource/destination commits or changes-inRestores selected destination paths from a source tree, or undoes a commit’s patch.Destination successor; descendants may be rebased.
revertselected commit patchnew inverse patchnew change identity; original remains.
duplicateselected commit treenew copy at new parentsnew change identity.
abandonselected graph nodeshidden nodes and reconnecting childrensuccessors for affected descendants.

Amending an early change

before:
root -- A -- B -- C -- @
         |    |    |
       API   tests docs

jj edit A
# edit API
jj describe -m 'API: corrected quota invariant'

after:
root -- A′ -- B′ -- C′ -- @′
         |    |    |
       API   tests docs
A′ is the successor of A; B′, C′, and @′ preserve descendants over new context.

The operation is not “change A and leave B/C hash identities intact”. B's parent ID changed, so B's commit ID necessarily changes even if its patch is textually identical. This is why commit IDs should not be used as stable review identity in local automation. A bookmark pointing to C follows the rewritten successor chain, while an already-pushed remote bookmark remains a server state to be updated.

Partial squash and patch ownership

before:                  after jj squash --from C --into B -- src/**
root - A - B - C - @     root - A′ - B′ - C′ - @′
          B: API             B′: API + src/ fix
          C: docs + fix       C′: docs

The result is not necessarily an empty C: the selected files moved, unselected content remained. If the fileset selects only part of a file, the diff editor/hunk selection determines the actual partition. A child depending on the old tree is rebased automatically. Use jj diff -r C′ and jj show -r B′, not only descriptions, to verify ownership.

Splitting a change without losing descendants

before:             split A by tests
root -- A -- B      root -- A_1 -- A_2 -- B′
       API+tests           API     tests

B is not simply left pointing at A_1. Its patch must be reapplied over A_2, so B′ is a successor and can conflict if B depended on the combined original tree. A publication bookmark at B follows B′ locally. If A was already uploaded to a review system, review identity and commit identity must be considered separately; for Gerrit, the footer in each resulting description is a server concern.

Abandoning an ancestor

before:              abandon B
A -- B -- C -- D      A ----- C′ -- D′
     \                C′ preserves C's patch over A where possible.
      useful content  With --restore-descendants, content preservation
                      rather than patch preservation changes the trade-off.

Abandon is therefore not equivalent to deleting a node from a drawn graph. It changes the parent context of descendants. The default tries to preserve descendant patches; --restore-descendants instead preserves their content, which can change their effective diff. Use the option only when that distinction is the desired one.

Rebase source selection as a set transformation

For a non-linear graph, write out the source set before choosing a destination:

jj log -G -r 'roots(main@origin..@)'
jj log -G -r 'main@origin..@'
jj rebase -r 'roots(main@origin..@)' -o main@origin

-r operates on the explicitly selected commits and preserves dependencies inside that set. -s selects a commit and all descendants, which may include unrelated side branches. -b is a branch-oriented convenience selection relative to the destination and is useful for the usual stack but should be inspected in a fork. If the selected set has multiple roots, each root can become a child of the destination; this is a feature, not an implicit merge.

Insert-after and insert-before

before:       B   C                 after -A A:
               \ /                 B   C
                A                       \ /
                                        X
                                        |
                                        A

before:       A -- B -- C            after -B B with X:
                                      A -- X -- B′ -- C′

Insert-after creates a new intermediate change after selected commits and rebases their existing children on it. Insert-before places selected commits before the target and rebases the target and its descendants. These operations alter edges beyond the selected commit, so bookmark and descendant consequences are larger than a simple “move X”. Use the long option names in scripts and inspect the resulting graph.

Conflicts are results, not interrupted sessions

When a patch cannot be reapplied cleanly, jj stores a conflict in the successor commit. The rewrite operation can still finish, and descendants can be rebased over that conflicted value. This means the next command can be unrelated work, jj resolve, another rebase, or a deliberate inspection. The absence of a --continue flag is a consequence of storing the intermediate state, not a promise that conflicts are less real.

Rewrite safety protocol

  1. Save the current operation ID and log view for a complicated rewrite.
  2. Inspect the source set and destination set separately.
  3. Run a dry/read command showing the expected paths and commits.
  4. Perform the rewrite with an explicit long-form option.
  5. Inspect jj log -r '::@', changed IDs, bookmarks, and conflicts().
  6. Use jj evolog for change continuity and jj op show for repository-wide effects.
  7. Only then move or push publication bookmarks.

Worked model: one filesystem, three identities, two histories

It is worth slowing down over a state that Git users often describe with a single word—“the current commit”—because jj deliberately exposes several different objects. In the following laboratory, use O for the operation head, C for the commit currently checked out in the workspace, W for the workspace record, and R for a remote bookmark. These are not alternative spellings for one pointer.

operation view O_0:
  root--A--B--C(@)
            ▲
            \-- local workspace W/dev records C

Git publication view:
  main@origin -- B
  (no local bookmark)

working directory:
  files on disk = snapshot(C), plus an unrecorded edit to src/parser.rs

After editing src/parser.rs, the file has changed but the commit graph has not. A command that needs a current working-copy view normally snapshots the filesystem into a new working-copy commit. The result is not “the file is staged”; there is no index-shaped intermediate object in the jj model. The graph becomes:

root--A--B--C--D(@)
              ▲
              \-- D is the new working-copy commit

main@origin -- B
filesystem = snapshot(D)

The change ID normally remains stable as C evolves into D because the evolution is a rewrite of one logical change. The commit ID changes because the commit object contains a new tree and therefore has new content. If C had a descendant E, jj may rebase E automatically onto D. That rebase can produce a new commit ID for E even though no user explicitly selected E. The operation log records the whole transaction, including both the direct snapshot and descendant update.

Now create a local publication bookmark:

jj bookmark create parser-review -r @
jj git push --bookmark parser-review --remote origin

The state now has a local publication name and a remote observation. Editing @ again creates E, but parser-review still points to D until moved. This is the key separation:

root--A--B--C--D--E(@)
                  ▲
parser-review ----+?  no: it still points to D

main@origin -- B
parser-review@origin -- D

In a graph display the bookmark glyph may make the relationship look visually close to @, but it is a named ref with its own target. A publication operation is therefore explicit state transition: move the bookmark, then push it. This separation is what lets a detached-HEAD-style local workflow continue after publication without turning every local movement into a remote branch update.

Snapshot timing is part of the command contract

“The working copy is a commit” does not mean every shell write immediately creates a repository operation. The filesystem can be ahead of the last recorded snapshot. Commands that inspect or mutate repository state may first update the working-copy commit; global options can alter that behaviour. A reproducible script should choose deliberately between a live view and a repository-only view.

QuestionLive defaultStable/explicit alternative
Should disk edits be represented?Allow the command to snapshot the workspace.--ignore-working-copy for a stored-graph query.
Should the command use the current integrated operation?Use the repository’s normal operation head.--at-operation OP for historical inspection.
Should a rewrite cross a policy boundary?Respect immutable heads.--ignore-immutable only as a deliberate override.
Should an automation operation become the caller’s current operation?Integrate normally.--no-integrate-operation, followed by explicit inspection/integration.

These switches are not interchangeable. Ignoring the working copy changes what is read from disk; selecting an earlier operation changes the entire repository view; ignoring immutability changes which rewrite is permitted; suppressing operation integration changes the operation DAG’s current head. Treat them as four independent axes in a script’s design.

Commit identity under rewrite

A commit ID identifies one immutable commit object in the backend. A change ID identifies an evolving logical change and is carried through its sequence of rewritten commit objects. The distinction becomes visible in a three-step amendment:

logical change X:
  X_0 = commit id 1a…, change id k1…
  X_1 = commit id 4f…, change id k1…  (description amended)
  X_2 = commit id 92…, change id k1…  (parent rebased)

predecessor relation:
  X_0 → X_1 → X_2

When a rewrite is copied instead of evolved—jj duplicate, for example—the new commit receives a new change identity. A later operation that edits the duplicate evolves the duplicate’s change, not the source. This is why a review workflow should decide whether feedback means “new patch set for the same logical change” or “a separate change derived from this one”. The command choice encodes that decision.

Commit IDs are suitable for immutable evidence: a build record, a fetched object, a server-side patch set, or a signed object. Change IDs are suitable for local evolution and for identifying a logical patch while its commit ID changes. Neither should be used as a replacement for a publication bookmark: a bookmark names a movable graph position, while an ID names a commit or logical evolution.

Mutable and immutable are graph predicates

A useful mistake to avoid is treating mutable as a synonym for private. Mutability is a property of whether jj’s rewrite policy permits changing a commit; publication is a property of whether a ref has been sent to another repository. A mutable commit can be pushed, and a user may intentionally amend it with a force update. Conversely, a private commit can be made immutable by configuration. Use revsets to ask the precise question you need:

jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@'
jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj log -r 'hidden()'
jj log -r 'divergent()'

Visibility is another independent predicate. Hidden predecessors are omitted from ordinary output and selection, but they remain part of the commit store and can be reached through evolution-oriented commands or explicit historical views. Divergence means that multiple visible commits share a change identity; it is not merely “two commits with similar text”. A convergence operation must decide how to combine those evolutions, and that decision deserves an operation-log checkpoint.

Workspaces make @ plural without making the graph plural

Each workspace has a workspace-local working-copy commit. The symbol @ resolves relative to the workspace from which the command is run. The commit store and operation history are shared by workspaces in one repository, so a rewrite in one workspace can update or stale another workspace’s checkout.

shared commit DAG:
       trunk--A--B
                  |-- dev@  C
                  \-- review@ D

workspace records:
  dev    → C
  review → D

jj edit C in the review workspace changes review’s @; it does not move dev’s @. A graph rewrite can nevertheless affect both records if the commits they name are rewritten. The operational rule is to use explicit commits in scripts and use @ only where workspace-local intent is genuinely wanted. For dashboards, build jobs, and review commands, print both the workspace name and the resolved commit ID.

Rewrite laboratory: patch ownership, context, and descendants

History editing in jj is best understood as changing patch ownership in a graph. The following stack has a base, an API patch, a test patch, and a working copy:

main@origin
    |
    A  (base)
    |
    B  (API)
    |
    C  (tests)
    |
    @  (documentation edits)

Suppose a review comment says that one hunk in C belongs in B. The operation is not “stage that hunk, amend B, and hope C can be replayed”. First choose the logical operation: move content from a descendant into an ancestor while preserving the rest of both changes. One safe sequence is:

jj edit C
jj squash --into B --interactive
jj edit B
jj describe -m "API: expose parser diagnostics"
jj log -r 'stack(B)'

The interactive squash changes B’s tree and leaves only the residual patch in C. When B evolves, C and @ are descendants and are automatically rebased. If their patches apply cleanly, their logical changes continue with new commit IDs. If the context no longer exists, the rebased descendant records a conflict rather than forcing the user into an interrupted rebase session.

before:
A--B(API)--C(tests)--@ docs

after moving a hunk from C to B:
A--B′(API + hunk)--C′(remaining tests)--@′ docs

possible conflict:
A--B′--C′[conflict]--@′[conflict propagation]

The important invariant is that the command’s source and destination are explicit. “Squash” is not a universal synonym for “combine whatever is nearby”: it has a source commit, a destination commit, optional file selection, and rules for descriptions and empty commits. The same discipline applies to restore: restore content from a source tree into a destination commit; do not describe it as a reset unless you have specified which tree and which commit are changing.

Choosing a rewrite primitive

IntentPrimitiveIdentity consequenceGraph consequence
Make the current change’s description or metadata correctdescribe, metaeditEvolves the same change.Usually no parent/child topology change.
Change which files/hunks belong to a changediffedit, squash, split, absorbSource/destination changes evolve; a split creates new logical changes as appropriate.Patch ownership changes; descendants may rebase.
Move an existing logical change to another parentrebaseChange identity is intended to persist.Parent edge changes; descendants follow.
Create another logical copyduplicateNew change identity.New node and descendants only if selected later.
Remove a node’s contribution while retaining descendantsabandonSource disappears from visible graph; descendants are rebased.Topology is simplified, possibly with conflicts.
Apply an inverse patchrevertNew change; source remains.New child or selected destination receives inverse content.

Rebase source modes

The three source selectors answer different graph questions. --branch REV selects a branch-like region from the specified commit to its descendants under the command’s source rules. --source REVSET selects the commits in the set and their relevant descendants according to rebase semantics. --revisions REVSET selects exactly the commits to rewrite. In a linear stack these can appear equivalent; in a fork they are not.

        B--C
       /
A-----+
       \ D--E

--revisions 'C'       selects C
--source 'B'          selects the source region rooted at B
--branch 'B'          selects the branch beginning at B

Do not infer selection from the visual nearest node. Evaluate the revset first with jj log -r, then run the rebase with an explicit destination. A destination is a graph insertion instruction: --destination/--onto places the selected commits after a parent; --insert-after and --insert-before place them relative to a commit while preserving a different relationship. Repeating a destination can intentionally create a merge-shaped result. If the destination is a set, the result can have multiple parents; this is a graph operation, not a textual “move branch” shortcut.

When automatic descendant rebasing surprises you

Automatic rebasing is a consequence of maintaining a coherent graph after a rewrite. It is surprising when the user thinks only of the selected node and forgets that descendants refer to its old commit ID. Consider a child that touched the same lines as its rewritten parent:

old: A--B(line 10 = old)--C(line 10 = test)--D
new: A--B′(line 10 = new)--C′[conflict]--D′[conflict]

The conflict belongs to C′’s relationship to B′; D′ may display conflict state because its parent is conflicted or because its own patch cannot be applied. Resolve at the earliest commit whose conflict is conceptually understood, then inspect descendants. Resolving only the working copy can leave the earlier stack member conflicted and cause the next rewrite to re-materialise the problem.

Rewrite exercises with invariants

The safest way to learn a rewrite command is to write an invariant before executing it. An invariant is a statement about what must remain true; it is stronger than “the command should probably work”. Examples include “the API change remains one logical change”, “the test change remains a descendant of the API change”, “the release bookmark does not move”, and “files outside the selected fileset are byte-for-byte unchanged”. After the operation, test the invariant with a revset, diff, template, or external test.

Exercise: amend an ancestor without losing the stack

before:
main@origin--A--B--C--D--@
                 API tests docs current

invariant:
  B's change ID remains the API review
  C and D remain descendants
  main@origin is unchanged

jj bookmark create safety/ancestor-rewrite -r @
api=$(jj log -r 'subject(substring:"API")' --no-graph -T 'change_id ++ "\n"')
jj edit 'subject(substring:"API")'
# edit implementation
jj describe -m 'API: enforce the documented invariant'
jj log -r 'safety/ancestor-rewrite::@'
jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

Compare the old and new API commit IDs through the safety bookmark and evolog. The change ID should identify the evolution, while C and D may have successor commit IDs. If the safety bookmark is deleted before inspection, the graph can still be recovered through operations, but the user’s intended boundary is no longer named.

Exercise: split by path, then repair the boundary

X:
  src/api.rs
  src/cli.rs
  docs/api.md
  tests/api.rs

desired:
  X_1 = src/api.rs + tests/api.rs
  X_2 = src/cli.rs + docs/api.md

A path-only split cannot express the first grouping because it is not a directory boundary. Start with an interactive split or use a temporary split followed by squash. The key is to preserve a testable first commit:

jj split -r X --interactive
jj log -r 'X::@'
jj diff -r X
jj diff -r 'X+'
# If tests belong in the implementation change:
jj squash --from 'X+' --into X -- 'root-file:tests/api.rs'

The resulting descriptions matter. A commit containing src/api.rs but no test can be acceptable if the project’s test suite supports it; otherwise the split is structurally neat but semantically poor. The operation log can reverse the whole experiment, which is preferable to trying to reconstruct patch ownership by hand.

Exercise: duplicate versus revert

A--B(feature)--C
 \------------D(other line)

duplicate B onto D:
A--B--C
 \----D--B′

revert B after C:
A--B--C--R(inverse of B)

Use duplicate when the feature should be applied as a new logical change in another context. Use revert when the original feature remains part of history but its effect should be counteracted. The two can produce similar files and radically different review semantics. A duplicate should receive a new change ID; a revert is also a new change whose description should explain which prior effect it negates.

Exercise: abandon an ancestor with dependent descendants

jj bookmark create safety/abandon -r @
jj abandon B
jj log -r 'safety/abandon::@'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj op show

Abandoning B removes B’s contribution and reconnects descendants according to jj’s rules. If C depended on B’s files, C may become conflicted or change content. This is not an operation to use for “cleaning an ugly local history” without reviewing descendants. If B was a publication boundary, inspect every bookmark and remote bookmark that reached it.

Exercise: arrange a fork deliberately

A--B--C
   \
    D--E

With an arrangement operation, select only the intended graph region and retain a safety bookmark at the head. Afterward use parent output rather than visual indentation to prove whether the result is a fork, a linear reordering, or a merge. For a scripted result, express the same intent with new, rebase, or parallelize, which make the source and destination visible in a code review.

Patch-stack review as a graph invariant

A stack is useful when each change has a review-sized purpose and its parent relationship reflects a dependency. A stack is not automatically a linear sequence: a merge, fork, or independent sibling can be part of a larger review graph. Before publication, classify each edge:

Edge typeMeaningReview implication
Dependency edgeChild patch assumes parent’s API/content.Parent rewrite can rebase/conflict the child.
Ordering-only edgeChild happens later but could stand alone.May be parallelised or published separately.
Integration edgeMerge combines independent lines.Parent order and conflict resolution are semantic.
Publication edgeBookmark/ref names a tip.Moving it changes server-visible reachability, not logical dependency.

This classification guides rebase and publication. A GitHub stacked PR generally wants explicit linear dependency edges. Gerrit can represent a linear patch series and topics, but server policy still determines how it displays independent parents. A local graph can be richer than either host’s review UI; publication should select the projection that the host understands.

Metadata surgery and identity policy

Descriptions are part of commit identity and review protocol. A description may carry a human subject, body, trailers, Gerrit Change-Id, issue references, release notes, and automation markers. Changing it evolves the commit ID and can rebase descendants even if the tree is unchanged. Decide whether a trailer belongs to the logical change before using metaedit --update-change-id.

Author metadata describes who authored the change; committer metadata describes the operation that created the current commit object. An amended change can retain its author while changing its committer timestamp. Rewriting an imported commit can therefore be visible in both the object ID and metadata even when the patch content is identical. Queries using author and committer predicates answer different audit questions.

jj log -r 'author_email(exact:"author@example.org")'
jj log -r 'committer_email(exact:"rewriter@example.org")'
jj log -r 'author_date(after:"2026-01-01") & committer_date(after:"2026-09-01")'

Use explicit timestamps in reproducible release work. A “recent” revset based on the local clock is useful interactively but is not a stable build input. The operation log adds a local record of when a rewrite happened, while the commit’s author/committer fields are part of the Git object exported to a remote.

Rewrite recovery checklist

  1. Stop mutating the repository.
  2. Save the current operation ID and workspace name.
  3. Save a graph listing with full commit and change IDs.
  4. Identify whether the mistake is a tree edit, parent rewrite, ref movement, or operation-view problem.
  5. Use op diff and evolog to identify the intended predecessor/successor.
  6. Prefer one operation-level undo/restore over manual reconstruction.
  7. Re-run the invariant checks and tests.
  8. Move or push bookmarks only after the local graph is correct.

The checklist is intentionally conservative around public refs. An operation can be undone locally after a push, but the remote ref remains changed until a new push updates it. Treat local recovery and publication rollback as two separate incidents with separate evidence.

Rewrite laboratory: patch ownership and graph surgery

History rewriting is ordinary jj work, but “ordinary” does not mean consequence-free. A rewrite changes an exact commit object and can change the parent identity supplied to every descendant. The useful unit of planning is the transformation of a graph region: identify source nodes, choose new parent edges, predict successor relationships, and inspect the resulting patch stack.

Select the operation by intent

IntentPrimary commandWhy not the tempting equivalent?
Change only the message or metadata.describe/metaeditrestore changes tree content; revert adds inverse content.
Touch up selected hunks in one change.diffeditsquash moves content across a boundary.
Move selected content into another change.squashduplicate creates another logical change.
Divide one patch into reviewable pieces.splitnew creates a boundary but does not redistribute an existing patch.
Move a whole change/stack to a new base.rebaseedit changes the workspace pointer, not parent edges.
Copy a patch to another line.duplicaterebase follows the same logical change; copy needs a new identity.
Publish an inverse without rewriting the original.revertrestore changes a destination tree and can erase ownership evidence.
Declare a change no longer wanted.abandonDeleting a bookmark only removes a name.

The commands can be composed, but the order communicates ownership. Split before squash when one mixed patch should become two review changes; squash before rebase when the destination context should be the context in which content is reviewed; rebase before editing an early change when the conflict should be resolved against the new base.

Rebase source matrix

base--A--B--C(@)
          \
           X--Y

-r B       select exactly B
-s B       select B and every descendant in the visible graph
-b B       select B's branch relative to the destination
-r B|X     select two roots/regions; inspect whether they are connected
-r B|C     select a set with a hole; do not infer a stack from labels

-r is the precise choice when the selected set itself is the policy. -s is the stack choice when a change and all descendants should move together. -b is a branch-relative convenience and depends on the destination. A source set can contain several roots and a destination can be repeated; the resulting operation may create several children or a merge shape. Never replace a source selector with another because it is shorter without evaluating the set.

jj log -G -r 'B'
jj log -G -r 'B::'
jj log -G -r 'roots(B::)'
jj log -G -r 'heads(B::)'
jj rebase -r B -o X
jj rebase -s B -o X
jj rebase -b B -o X

Destination matrix

DestinationGraph operationFailure/edge case
-o Y/-d YPlace selected source on Y.Y inside the source closure would create a cycle and is rejected.
Repeated -o Y -o ZGive the selected change multiple parents.Creates a deliberate merge; review both parent diffs.
-A YInsert selected commits after Y.Y's existing descendants are rebased over the insertion.
-B YInsert selected commits before Y.Y and its descendants may receive successors.
before: base--A--B--C
                  \
                   X--Y

-r B -o X:
  base--A--B'--C'
             \
              X

-r B -A X:
  base--A--X--B'--C'

-r B -B X:
  base--A--B'--X'--Y'

The apparent similarity between -o, -A, and -B hides which existing edge is replaced. -o chooses parent destinations for the selected source. Insert-after and insert-before alter an existing line and consequently move a larger graph region. Use a temporary bookmark at the intended tip and compare jj op show -p when testing a new pattern.

Ancestor rewrites and descendants

Suppose C is a child of B and its commit object records B's commit ID as a parent. If B becomes B', C's old object cannot remain the current representation of the same logical change on that edge: its parent field would be wrong. jj creates a successor C' whose patch is applied to B'. If the patch applies cleanly, C' is clean. If it does not, C' stores a conflict. If C's description or tree was independently rewritten at the same time, its change evolution must still be interpreted separately.

before: main--B--C--D(@)
rewrite B:
after:  main--B'--C'--D'(@)
         B/B' same change IDs
         C/C' same change IDs
         D/D' same change IDs
         all commit IDs may differ

A bookmark targeting D normally follows the rewritten graph target, while a remote bookmark remains the last fetched server observation. If a local bookmark and remote bookmark now identify different descendants, that is exactly the state to inspect before a push. The operation log captures the bulk rewrite; each evolution log captures one logical change's continuity.

Partial squash and patch ownership

A--B(@)
B patch:
  src/parser.rs
  docs/parser.md
  tests/parser.rs

squash docs into A:
A'--B'(@)
A' owns docs/parser.md
B' owns src/parser.rs + tests/parser.rs

A fileset narrows the content transfer, but hunk boundaries can still matter within a file. Use an interactive diff editor when the policy is “some hunks of this file”. After a partial squash, inspect both source and destination diffs. An empty source may remain as an intentional message boundary or may be abandoned; do not infer the desired cleanup from its emptiness.

jj diff -r @
jj squash --into @- -- 'root-file:"docs/parser.md"'
jj diff -r @-
jj diff -r @
jj log -G -r '::@'

Split with a descendant

before: A--B--C(@)
             B = API + docs
             C = tests depending on API

split B:
after:  A--B1--B2--C'(@)
          API  docs   tests rebased

Splitting introduces a new parent edge between B1 and B2. C is automatically rebased over the resulting sequence. If C's patch was written against the combined B tree, the rebase can be clean or conflicted depending on context. Verify both diffs and the change IDs of B1/B2/C'. A split creates a new logical change for one side; it does not preserve one change ID across both output commits.

Duplicate, revert, abandon: three histories

duplicate: main--A--B
                    \
                     Bcopy     new change identity

revert:    main--A--B--R      R applies inverse of B

abandon:   main--A----C       B removed/hidden, C reconnected as policy permits

Duplicate copies a tree/patch to a new location and keeps the original. Revert adds an explicit inverse commit, preserving the original's history and review evidence. Abandon removes the original from ordinary work and asks jj to update dependants; it is appropriate only when the change itself is no longer part of the intended graph. If a remote review exists, remote cleanup remains separate.

Rewrite safety protocol

  1. Write the graph question in words and select a revset.
  2. Render the source set and destination set with no graph and a template.
  3. Check immutability and publication refs.
  4. Create a temporary bookmark at a useful tip.
  5. Run the smallest operation that expresses the intent.
  6. Inspect commit/change IDs, descendants, bookmarks, conflicts, and operation detail.
  7. Run tests against the new workspace tree.
