  8. Publish only after the local graph and remote comparison are understood.

This is not ceremony borrowed from Git. Mutable history makes small edits cheap, so the scarce resource becomes accurate graph intent. An operation checkpoint gives recovery; a change ID gives continuity; a bookmark gives a publication handle; and a revset gives a repeatable selection.

Part IV — Revsets: a query language

12. Revsets as values

A revset is a value denoting a set of commits. Commands impose cardinality constraints: jj edit, jj describe, and many destination arguments require exactly one; jj log -r, jj abandon, jj rebase -r, and jj diff can accept sets. A syntactically valid expression can therefore still be operationally invalid.

Symbols and resolution

@ is the current workspace commit; workspace@ selects another workspace. Full or unique-prefix commit IDs resolve to one commit. Full or unique-prefix change IDs resolve to a visible commit, except ambiguous/divergent IDs require an offset such as xyz/0. Tags, bookmarks, and remote bookmarks are symbols. In 0.44 the documented precedence is tag name, bookmark name, then commit/change ID; use commit_id(X), change_id(X), bookmark(X), or tag(X) when scripts must disambiguate.

Shell quoting is part of the language boundary. Quote expressions containing |, ~, parentheses, or spaces. A literal symbol that looks like an operator can require nested quoting, for example jj log -r '"x-"'.

Revset operators and precedence

BindingSyntaxMeaning
1f(x)Function call.
2x-, x+Parents or children of members of x.
3p:xString/date pattern or pattern alias.
4x::, ::x, x::yDescendants, ancestors, or descendants of x that are ancestors of y.
4x.., ..x, x..y, ..Difference-style reachability ranges, with root/visible-head rules.
5~xComplement of x within the revset search space.
6x & y, x ~ yIntersection; difference.
7x | yUnion.

Strongest binds first, equal-strength infix operators associate left-to-right. x..y is ::y ~ ::x; it is not necessarily one ancestry path. x::y is x:: & ::y. The left side of .. does not distribute over union: (A|B).. is equivalent to A.. & B.., not A.. | B...

D
|\
B C       ::D = {D,C,B,A,root()}
|/        B..D = {D,C}
A         B::D = {B,D}
|         B+ = {D}
root      B- = {A}

Patterns, dates, and aliases

String predicates accept pattern kinds such as exact:, substring:, glob:, and regex:, with quoting for spaces and punctuation. Date patterns accept date literals and ranges using the syntax documented by the installed help. Prefer explicit pattern kinds in scripts; defaults and the old “glob by default” configuration have changed across releases.

[revset-aliases]
mine = 'author(exact:"A. Engineer")'
stack(x) = 'x:: & mutable()'
reviewable(x) = 'x:: & ~immutable_heads() & ~empty()'

Aliases are parsed as revsets and parameterised aliases substitute expressions, not shell text. Keep aliases small, name them after the set they denote, and test them against a fixture graph. A repository alias can shadow a user alias; inspect effective configuration with jj config list.

13. Revset operators and public functions

The following reference groups the public 0.44 functions by the property they expose. Function availability and signatures are captured from jj help -k revsets; the raw installed text is the release-specific authority.

FamilyFunctions and signaturesNotes
Universe/topologyall(), none(), root(), heads(x), roots(x), ancestors(x,[depth]), descendants(x,[depth]), parents(x,[depth]), children(x,[depth]), first_parent(x,[depth]), first_ancestors(x,[depth]), reachable(srcs,domain), connected(x)Heads and roots are relative to the supplied set; traversal depth is optional.
Graph boundariesvisible_heads(), immutable_heads(), mutable(), immutable(), visible(), hidden(), conflicts(), empty(), merges(), forks()These are predicates/sets over repository state, not Git branch lists.
Graph selectionlatest(x,[count]), fork_point(x), merge_point(x), bisect(x), exactly(x,count), coalesce(revsets...)Useful for reducing a set to recent, boundary, balanced, or exact-cardinality results.
Identitycommit_id(prefix), change_id(prefix), description(pattern), subject(pattern), author(pattern), author_name(pattern), author_email(pattern), committer(pattern), committer_name(pattern), committer_email(pattern), mine()Pattern arguments are typed string patterns; mine uses the configured current user email.
Time/signingauthor_date(pattern), committer_date(pattern), signed()Use date patterns rather than relying on locale-formatted output.
Ref statebookmarks([pattern]), remote_bookmarks([name_pattern],[remote_pattern]), tracked_remote_bookmarks(...), untracked_remote_bookmarks(...), tags([pattern]), remote_tags(...), git_refs()0.45.x adds tracked/untracked remote-tag predicates; use explicit ref predicates when symbols may be ambiguous.
Remote stateremote_bookmarks(pattern, remote), tracked_remote_bookmarks(), present(x)Remote bookmarks are imported refs; tracking is a relationship.
Workspacesworkspaces(), working_copies()Workspace symbols let scripts reason about all local working-copy commits.
Files/contentfiles(expression), diff_lines(text,[files]), diff_lines_added(text,[files]), diff_lines_removed(text,[files])These query trees or diffs; the nested fileset expression is independent of revset syntax.
Evolution/operationsdivergent(), working_copies(), at_operation(op,x)Useful for recovering rewritten changes and inspecting historical repository views.

The detailed generated inventory and installed help are shipped beside this HTML. Function names have deliberately not been inferred from Git. If a script depends on a function, run jj log -r 'function(...)' in the target release during CI.

Composition and cardinality

immutable_heads().. & mutable() & descendants(@-)
bookmarks() ~ remote_bookmarks()
author(substring:"Engineer") & committer_date(after:"2026-01-01")
roots((main..@):: & conflicts())

First derive the set, then add a boundary, then add a predicate. Use roots() to turn a set of stack commits into its minimal roots before passing it to a command that should operate once per stack. Conversely, heads() is useful for publication candidates. Always inspect with jj log -r 'EXPR' before mutating.

14. Revset algebra cookbook

QuestionRevsetReasoning
My unpublished mutable workmutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()Mutable changes excluding anything reachable from an imported remote ref.
Ancestors of @ not on a remote::@ ~ ::remote_bookmarks()Reachable from @, subtract remote reachability.
Mutable descendants of RR:: & mutable()Descendant closure intersected with mutability.
Conflicted commitsconflicts()Stored conflict value, not merely filesystem markers.
Empty commitsempty()Tree equals parent merge result as defined by jj.
Mergesmerges()Multiple-parent commits.
Not reachable from publication bookmarksall() ~ ::bookmarks()Use a narrower visible set when hidden commits are not intended.
Heads without bookmarksheads(all()) ~ bookmarks()Graph tips that have no local bookmark name.
Recent authored workauthor(exact:"A. Engineer") & committer_date(after:"2026-08-01")Use explicit identity/date patterns.
Release to trunkv2.4.0..mainAncestors of main not already reachable from tag.
Divergent changesdivergent()Find concurrent successors of one change ID.
Gerrit stack rooted at Rroots(R:: & mutable()):: & mutable()Find mutable stack roots then its mutable descendants.
GitHub PR candidatesheads(mutable() ~ ::main@origin)Candidate tips not already reachable from imported trunk.

These are recipes, not universal policy. For example, “remote” may include an unrelated fork, and a publication bookmark may intentionally point to mutable work. The algebra tells you exactly what you asked; naming conventions and review policy decide whether that set is useful.

Revset laboratory: evaluate the set, then the command

A revset becomes reliable when it can be evaluated by hand. Use the following graph as a small algebraic universe. Letters are commits; names on the right are bookmarks or tags. Arrows point from parent to child.

                 H  (review-tip)
                /
root -- A -- B -- C -- D  (main@origin)
             \     \
              E --- F      (github/topic at F)
                   \
                    G      (tag v2.0 at G)

I is a second successor of B's change ID, so B/0 and B/1 are divergent.
J is a conflicted descendant of C.
K is an empty mutable child of D.

This graph is intentionally not a single stack. It contains an ancestry fork, a merge-like region, a publication bookmark, a tag, a divergent change, a conflict, an empty commit, and a remote boundary. The exact parent edges should be recorded in the fixture rather than inferred from the drawing; the drawing's purpose is to make the set calculations visible.

Primitive closures

ExpressionSet operationQuestion it answers
xResolve a symbol/set.Which exact commit(s) does this name denote?
x-Immediate parents.What are the direct inputs to x?
x+Immediate children.Which commits directly continue x?
::xAncestors including x.What can reach x?
x::Descendants including x.What can x reach?
x::yx:: & ::y.Which descendants of x also lie on ancestry to y?
x..y::y ~ ::x.What is reachable from y but not already reachable from x?
roots(S)Minimal members of S.Where do selected stack components begin?
heads(S)Maximal members of S.Which selected tips need publication or review?

Suppose A is an ancestor of D. Then A..D contains commits reachable from D after subtracting the ancestry of A; it is not a list of only the first-parent path in a graph with merges. A::D includes A and the descendants that are also ancestors of D. The distinction is inclusion versus difference, not two spellings for “between”.

jj log -G -r '::D'
jj log -G -r 'A..D'
jj log -G -r 'A::D'
jj log -G -r 'heads(A::D)'
jj log -G -r 'roots(A::D)'

Why range expressions surprise Git experts

Git's commit notation is commonly used in commands whose own semantics impose a first-parent or symmetric interpretation. jj revsets are values passed to many different consumers. The same set can be rendered by log, diffed, abandoned, rebased, or counted. Therefore start with membership, then ask whether the consumer accepts a set and whether its operation is meaningful for a disconnected set.

# These are different questions:
jj log -r 'A..D'                    # render selected commits
jj diff -r 'A..D'                   # total connected patch, if valid
jj rebase -r 'A..D' -o main@origin  # rewrite each selected commit
jj abandon 'A..D'                   # hide all selected commits

The last three commands do not have interchangeable consequences. A diff rejects gaps that log can display. Rebase preserves/rewrites patches and can produce conflicts. Abandon changes visibility and reconnects children. Revset syntax alone cannot tell you whether the operation is safe.

Precedence experiments

Operators bind in this order: function call; postfix parent/child; pattern prefix; ancestry/range; complement; intersection/difference; union. Write parentheses around policy. Compare these expressions in query-lab:

jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@ | bookmarks()'
jj log -r '(mutable() & ::@) | bookmarks()'
jj log -r 'mutable() & (::@ | bookmarks())'
jj log -r 'heads(mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks())'

The first two are equivalent under the documented precedence; the third is different because the union is inside the intersection. This matters for “unpublished work”: mutable() & (::@ ~ ::remote_bookmarks()) excludes remote ancestry before intersecting, while (mutable() & ::@) ~ ::remote_bookmarks() subtracts it afterward. They often coincide in a simple graph but diverge when the remote reaches a mutable commit outside ::@.

Visibility and the search domain

all(), visible(), and hidden() are not interchangeable views of one infinite universe. Ordinary symbol resolution and many commands operate on visible commits. Hidden commits remain in the store and can be found through explicit IDs, evolution, operation views, or a revset whose domain includes them. When a recipe says all(), explain whether it is intentionally forensic or merely using a broad visible set.

jj log -r 'visible_heads()'
jj log -r 'hidden()'
jj log -r 'all() & hidden()'
jj log -r 'present(commit_id("prefix"))'

A hidden commit can be a predecessor of a visible successor. It may be valuable recovery evidence. Do not treat hidden as equivalent to unreachable or garbage, and do not run GC while investigating a missing change.

Function families with operational reasoning

FamilyFunctionsHow to reason about them
Universeall(), none(), root()Start/end sets. none() is useful in aliases and conditional construction.
Traversalparents, children, ancestors, descendantsOptional depth limits immediate breadth; no depth means transitive closure.
First-parentfirst_parent, first_ancestorsUse only when merge parent ordering is a deliberate policy.
Reachabilityreachable(srcs, domain), connectedSeparate roots/sources from the domain to avoid accidentally traversing all refs.
Boundariesroots, heads, visible_headsReduce a set to minimal/maximal members; useful before rebase or publication.
Graph pointsfork_point, merge_point, latest, bisectThese choose graph-derived points; verify cardinality on forks/merges.
Refsbookmarks, remote_bookmarks, tracked/untracked forms, tags, git_refsNames are sets of target commits; tracking changes which refs are included.
Identitycommit_id, change_id, present, divergentCommit identity and change identity answer different questions.
Metadatadescription, subject, author/committer forms, mine, date forms, signedPattern kind and author/committer choice are part of the query.
Tree/conflictfiles, diff-line functions, empty, conflicts, merges, forksQuery stored tree/patch state; conflict markers alone are not the predicate.
State/evolutionmutable, immutable, hidden, visible, at_operation, working_copiesBind the query to local repository view, operation, or workspace state.

For each function, test the empty set, a singleton, a merge, a fork, a hidden commit, and a divergent change where the function's domain permits it. This is more valuable than testing only the happy-path linear stack.

Patterns and dates

String predicates accept explicit kinds including exact:, substring:, glob:, and regex:. A bare pattern's default is not a safe assumption for shared configuration. Use subject(exact:"API: quota") when one exact subject matters, description(substring:"quota") for a search, and description(regex:"^API:") for a policy prefix.

jj log -r 'subject(exact:"API: quota")'
jj log -r 'author_email(exact:"a@example.test")'
jj log -r 'subject(regex:"^(fix|revert):")'
jj log -r 'description(glob:"*security*")'

Date patterns are parsed dates, ranges, and relative forms according to the installed revset help. Compare author and committer dates deliberately; an imported commit can have a historical author date but a newer committer date after a rewrite. Use UTC when communicating a reproducible cutoff.

jj log -r 'author_date(after:"2026-01-01")'
jj log -r 'committer_date(before:"2026-09-01")'
jj log -r 'author_date("2026-08-01..2026-09-01")'

Symbol resolution and cardinality

A symbol can name a tag, local bookmark, remote bookmark, workspace, commit ID prefix, or change ID prefix. Ambiguity is not a cosmetic concern: jj edit may reject a set where jj log displays it. Use explicit functions or full IDs when a script crosses a publication boundary.

Consumer classTypical cardinalitySafe practice
Display: log, diff, abandon, signMany commits accepted, with command-specific connectedness.Inspect set; use --count or log before mutation.
Target: edit, describe, metaeditExactly one or an intentionally supported set.Use jj log --count -r 'EXPR' and require one.
Destination: rebase, restore, duplicateOften one; repeated options may intentionally mean multiple parents.Distinguish one set-valued option from repeated destination options.
Ref: bookmark/tag operationsName patterns may match many; target commit often one.Qualify remote and inspect tracking state.

Aliases as named algebra

[revset-aliases]
stack(x) = 'x:: & mutable()'
unpublished() = 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
reviewable(x) = 'x:: & ~immutable_heads() & ~empty()'
publication_heads() = 'heads(mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks())'
'author:x' = 'author(substring:x)'

Alias parameters substitute revset expressions, not shell strings. A parameterised alias should have a mathematical description in its documentation: stack(x) means the mutable descendant closure of x, not “the branch”. Test nested aliases, empty parameters if the grammar permits them, and names that might collide with builtin functions or user/repository layers.

Derivation exercises

  1. Start with “all my mutable changes”: mine() & mutable().
  2. Restrict to the current stack: intersect with ::@ or use a named stack alias.
  3. Remove anything already reachable from publication: subtract ::bookmarks() or a deliberately selected remote set.
  4. Reduce to candidate tips: apply heads().
  5. Require a reviewable change: subtract empty(), conflicts(), and immutable heads according to policy.
heads(mine() & mutable() & ::@ ~ ::remote_bookmarks())
  ~ empty()
  ~ conflicts()
  ~ immutable_heads()

Each line is a policy decision. A change can be empty but intentionally a merge marker; a conflicted commit may be useful in a WIP review; and a remote bookmark can be an unrelated fork. Treat the final revset as a policy expression and name it accordingly rather than hiding it behind a generic alias such as work().

Appendix C is the compact public-function and operator lookup. This chapter supplies the evaluation method and the counterexamples that a table cannot provide. Every expression used for a mutating example is run against the query fixture before being included.

Revset reasoning: a small algebra, not a bag of aliases

A revset expression denotes a set of commits in a particular repository view. The view matters: visibility, the selected operation, workspace state, and imported refs all affect symbol resolution and the universe against which predicates are evaluated. Read an expression in two stages. First determine the set denoted by each subexpression. Then determine how the consuming command interprets the set: as a source set, a destination set, a range, a singleton, or a set of independent targets.

Let G be the directed acyclic commit graph, with edges from a child to its parents. Let Anc(x) include x and every ancestor reachable by repeatedly following parent edges; let Desc(x) include x and every descendant reachable in the opposite direction. In the notation used by jj, ::x denotes Anc(x) and x:: denotes Desc(x). The operators are set operators layered over these graph traversals, so a complex query can be calculated on paper before it is given to the CLI.

       P--Q--R
      /       \
root-S         T--U
      \       /
       V-----W

Anc(U) = {U,T,R,Q,P,S,root}       Desc(S) = every node below S
Anc(U) & Desc(S) = {S,P,Q,R,T,U}
U- = {T}                           U+ = { } in this graph

The diagram is intentionally non-linear. A range expression that works by accident on a linear stack can be wrong here. For example, S..U is defined in terms of ancestry: it selects ancestors of U excluding ancestors of S. It is not “the commits printed between two lines”, and it does not mean “all nodes after S in a chosen first-parent walk”. If a command needs the first-parent corridor, express that corridor explicitly with first_ancestors or a corresponding supported function.

Precedence and parentheses

Parse from the inside out. Function calls and grouping bind most tightly; postfix parent/child operators bind more tightly than infix set operators. Intersection and difference bind more tightly than union. A useful safe style is to parenthesise every union used as an operand of a traversal or difference:

jj log -r '(heads(mutable()) & ::@) | main@origin'
jj log -r '::(@ | main@origin)'
jj log -r '(::@) ~ (::main@origin)'

The first query is the union of two already computed sets. The second computes the ancestor closure of a union. Those are not generally equal in a graph with two tips. The third is “ancestors of @ excluding ancestors of main@origin”; it is a precise unpublished-ancestry query only when the remote bookmark is the correct publication boundary.

ExpressionReadingCommon mistaken reading
A | BAll commits in either set.A path from A to B.
A & BCommits in both sets.A merge of A and B.
A ~ BA with commits in B removed.A minus only the tip named by B.
A::A and all descendants.Only the first child chain.
::AA and all ancestors.Only first-parent history.
A..BAncestors of B excluding ancestors of A.Every commit after A by date.
A::BDescendants of A that are ancestors of B.All commits reachable from either endpoint.

Symbols, identity, and ambiguity

Symbol resolution is a semantic operation, not shell variable expansion. A name can resolve to a local bookmark, a remote bookmark qualified by a remote, a tag, a workspace symbol, a change ID, or a commit ID prefix. If a short prefix is ambiguous, jj reports the ambiguity rather than silently selecting one. In scripts use a full enough prefix or disambiguating syntax, and prefer a bookmark or an alias for a stable human role.

jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj log -r 'review@origin'
jj log -r 'workspace("review")'
jj log -r 'change_id("qxy...")'
jj log -r 'commit_id("3f0...")'

Use change_id() when the intent is “the current visible commit for this evolving logical change”. Use commit_id() when the intent is “this exact immutable object”. This difference is crucial in scripts that run after a rewrite. A build verifier should normally pin a commit ID; a command that asks to edit a review change should normally resolve its change ID or bookmark, then verify the selected singleton.

Cardinality is a property of the consumer, not of the syntax alone. jj log accepts a set. jj rebase --revisions accepts a source set. jj edit requires one commit. Some commands accept zero or one commit and default to @. Before composing a script, test a query with an output that includes both IDs:

jj log -r 'my_query' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ "\n"'

If the command requires one result, make the cardinality visible and fail early in the shell:

count=$(jj log -r 'my_query' --no-graph -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"' | wc -l)
test "$count" -eq 1 || { printf 'expected one commit, got %s\n' "$count" >&2; exit 2; }
jj edit 'my_query'

This is more robust than adding latest() merely to silence an error. latest() is a selection policy: it chooses by committer timestamp from a set and can choose a different commit after a clock correction or import. Use it only when “latest” is actually the desired semantics.

Topology functions in detail

parents(X) and children(X) expose immediate graph relationships in their basic form, with documented optional traversal depth forms where supported. Postfix X- and X+ are convenient immediate-neighbour operations. ancestors(X) and descendants(X) perform transitive traversal; the prefix/suffix forms are compact equivalents for common cases. roots(X) and heads(X) reduce a set to nodes with no selected parent or no selected child respectively. These functions operate on the supplied set where their signatures say so, which is why heads(::@) differs from heads(all()).

jj log -r 'parents(@)'
jj log -r 'children(main@origin)'
jj log -r 'heads(mutable() & ::@)'
jj log -r 'roots(::@ ~ ::main@origin)'
jj log -r 'merges(::@)'
jj log -r 'forks(::@)'

merges(X) and forks(X) are structural predicates. A merge is a commit with multiple parents; a fork identifies commits whose selected topology branches. They do not identify “large” changes or review stacks. reachable(S, T) is useful when you need to ask whether the graph can reach one set from another, while connected(X) groups connected components where that is the function’s documented interpretation. When a query’s result seems too broad, replace it with a sequence of named subqueries and inspect each one.

Predicate composition

Most useful revsets are an intersection of a graph boundary with a semantic predicate. Start with the universe, narrow by reachability, then narrow by state:

(mutable() & ::@) ~ ::main@origin
(mutable() & descendants(main@origin)) & conflicts()
(::@ & description(regex:"(?i)security|auth")) ~ hidden()
heads(::@) & ~bookmarks()
(::@ & mine()) & ~immutable()

In the first expression, “mutable” alone would include unrelated mutable work and “ancestors of @” alone would include imported immutable history. The intersection establishes both. The difference removes the published ancestry only if main@origin is the relevant publication boundary; a topic bookmark or a second remote may require subtracting a union of several refs instead.

Metadata predicates such as author(), committer(), description(), subject(), and date predicates can be combined with topology. Conflict and emptiness predicates describe commit state, not filesystem state at this instant. A commit may be empty relative to its parent but still carry a meaningful description or review marker; a conflicted commit may have a materialised conflict in the working copy or may be inspected without materialising it.

Pattern and date arguments

Text predicates accept explicit pattern kinds. Treat patterns as data and quote the whole revset so the shell does not consume punctuation:

jj log -r 'subject(exact:"Fix parser")'
jj log -r 'description(substring:"parser")'
jj log -r 'description(glob:"Fix * parser")'
jj log -r 'description(regex:"(?i)CVE-[0-9]+")'
jj log -r 'author_email(exact:"dev@example.org")'

An exact pattern is appropriate for a machine-generated subject; a substring is appropriate for an exploratory query; a glob is useful for a controlled family; a regex is powerful but should be tested against descriptions containing punctuation and newlines. Pattern syntax belongs inside the revset grammar, so escaping has two layers: the shell quote and the pattern’s own quoting/escaping rules.

Date patterns should likewise be explicit. Use the installed help’s supported date forms rather than relying on a shell-generated locale string. A relative phrase such as “two weeks ago” is convenient interactively but can make an audit non-reproducible. For a reproducible report, use an absolute timestamp or a checked-in alias whose meaning is documented. Date predicates compare the relevant author or committer timestamp; do not assume they are interchangeable, especially after rebases and imports.

Algebraic derivations

Here are derivations rather than recipes. Let P be the union of publication tips and T be the local trunk tip. “Ancestors of @ that are not reachable from any publication tip” is:

(::@) ~ (P::)

where P itself may be bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks() or a deliberately restricted set such as main@origin | release@origin. “The patch stack above trunk” is not always trunk..@; for a stack with a merge or a topic base, use the ancestry corridor that reflects the publication boundary and then take heads or roots as needed:

stack = ::@ ~ ::main@origin
stack_heads = heads(stack)
stack_roots = roots(stack)

“Heads without bookmarks” is a two-part question. First compute graph heads, then remove commits carrying names:

heads(::@) ~ bookmarks()

This may still include a head with a remote bookmark if only local bookmarks are removed. If the policy is “not published anywhere”, subtract the union of local and remote bookmark targets, and include tags if tags count as publication in your environment. The correct expression depends on the policy; revset algebra exposes that policy instead of hiding it in a command alias.

Empirical evaluation protocol

For a non-trivial expression, record the fixture graph, the expression, the expected set by symbolic name, and the actual output containing commit and change IDs. Then test the expression after a rewrite. Queries based on a commit ID should intentionally stop matching the old object; queries based on a change ID or bookmark should follow the intended identity or ref. This protocol catches the most expensive class of revset bug: a query that succeeds while selecting the wrong commit.

# human-readable graph
jj log -r 'all()' --no-pager

# stable evidence
jj log -r 'my_query' --no-graph \
  -T 'commit_id ++ "\t" ++ change_id ++ "\t" ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# historical view when an operation is under investigation
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'my_query' --no-graph

Keep a fixture alias such as lab() in a disposable config rather than adding broad aliases to a user’s global configuration. The validation script in this project creates representative non-linear graphs and checks selected IDs; its purpose is to validate the book’s examples, not to replace the explanations above.

Revset workbook: calculate before executing

Use this workbook with a disposable repository. Give each node a description matching its letter, then replace letters with full IDs in the output. The expected sets below are mathematical results; the command’s presentation order is a separate concern.

          C--D--F
         /       \
root--A-B         G
         \       /
          E-----+

parents(F) = {D}
ancestors(F) = {F,D,C,B,A,root}
descendants(B) = {B,C,D,E,F,G}
merges(G) = {G}
forks(B) = {B}

Now evaluate a corridor:

C::F = descendants(C) & ancestors(F)
     = {C,D,F}

B..F = ancestors(F) ~ ancestors(B)
     = {C,D,F}  (assuming B is the boundary and E is not an ancestor of F)

heads(B::G) = {G}
roots(B::G) = {B}

The arithmetic is deliberately explicit. In a graph with a merge, a child can be reachable through more than one route, but set membership remains idempotent. If a query unexpectedly includes E, inspect the actual parent edges rather than relying on the diagram’s spacing.

Exercise: published versus unpublished

main@origin--A--B
                   \
                    C--D--@
release@origin------B
pr/api--------------C

Define the policy “ancestors of @ not reachable from either remote publication”:

(::@) ~ (remote_bookmarks()::)

But if remote_bookmarks() includes an unrelated remote head, it may remove too much. Narrow it:

(::@) ~ (remote_bookmarks(substring:"main")::)

Or use explicit names:

(::@) ~ (main@origin:: | release@origin:: | pr/api::)

These are not interchangeable when a remote contains multiple bookmarks. A cookbook recipe is correct only after its publication universe is specified.

Exercise: hidden and immutable state

jj log -r 'all()'
jj log -r 'hidden()'
jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@'

Compare the result of all() with the ordinary default log. The ordinary visible graph may omit hidden predecessors; all() is still evaluated in a view with its own visibility rules and explicit ancestors. Use present(X) when a symbol may not exist in the current view, and use at_operation(OP, X) when the question is explicitly historical.

Exercise: files and diff predicates

jj log -r 'files("root-glob:src/**/*.rs")'
jj log -r 'diff_lines_added("TODO", "root-glob:src/**")'
jj log -r 'diff_lines_removed("deprecated", "root-glob:src/**")'

These predicates select commits by their change/diff, not every commit whose resulting tree happens to contain a matching file. A rename, copy, merge, binary file, or conflict can make the intuitive reading wrong. Pair the query with jj diff -r on the resulting commits and verify the path scope.

Exercise: reachability with a domain

reachable(srcs, domain) is the function to reach through a deliberately bounded domain. Let E be a descendant of A and let A.. exclude A:

reachable(E, A..)

The domain prevents traversal outside the selected set. This is safer than using a broad descendant closure when the repository has multiple roots, but it also means a source not in the domain is ignored. Test the four cases: source in domain, source outside domain, path that exits domain, and disconnected source. A query that returns an empty set can be correct because the boundary excludes the source.

Exercise: dates and authors

jj log -r 'author_email(exact:"ada@example.org")'
jj log -r 'author_date(after:"2026-01-01") & committer_date(before:"2026-09-01")'
jj log -r 'mine() & mutable() & ::@'

Use exact patterns for an identity controlled by policy, substring patterns for interactive search, and regex only when the input convention warrants it. An imported commit can have an author date from months ago and a recent committer date; the intersection deliberately finds that shape. Do not call the result “recent work” without saying which timestamp was used.

Exercise: cardinality as a proof obligation

jj log -r 'exactly(subject(exact:"API: quota"), 1)'
jj edit 'exactly(subject(exact:"API: quota"), 1)'

The first command proves that a subject is unique in the current view. The second uses the same proof as a singleton command. If a later import adds another matching commit, the command fails closed instead of editing an arbitrary commit. When the desired policy is “the newest matching review”, use latest explicitly and document why that is safe.

Alias design exercises

Aliases should package a domain boundary, not hide an accidental query. Start with a parameterised alias that accepts a stack tip and a base:

[revset-aliases]
'patches(base, tip)' = 'roots(base..tip)::'
'unpublished(tip)' = '::tip ~ remote_bookmarks()::'
'conflicted-stack(tip)' = 'stack(tip) & conflicts()'

Before installing these globally, test the parameters with a fork and a merge. The alias patches can return multiple roots; a command that requires one root must apply roots or enforce cardinality. The alias unpublished encodes a broad publication policy and should be narrowed in a multi-remote repository. Alias design is policy design; review it like code.

Revset semantics laboratory: evaluate an expression by hand

A revset is not a textual shortcut for a branch name. It is an expression whose value is a set of commits in the repository view selected for the command. The same expression can produce a different set after a fetch, a rewrite, a visibility change, or a configuration change. That is a feature: a revset describes a question, while a commit prefix describes an object found at one moment.

The reliable way to learn an unfamiliar expression is to evaluate it in three passes. First resolve every symbol to a set. Next evaluate graph operators over those sets. Finally apply predicates and boundary functions. When an expression is used by a mutating command, add a fourth pass: determine whether the command accepts the resulting cardinality and whether its source/destination interpretation is what you intended.

A--B--C--D
   \
    E--F--G
        |--H

For the examples below, the important parent edges are:
  parents(B) = {A}; parents(C) = {B}; parents(D) = {C}
  parents(E) = {B}; parents(F) = {E}; parents(G) = {F}
  parents(H) = {F}

The drawing is intentionally explicit about parent edges. It is easy to read a diagonal line as decoration and accidentally reason about a different graph. Names in a diagram are not automatically bookmarks. In an actual fixture, obtain the edges instead of trusting the visual layout:

jj log -G -r 'all()' -T 'description.first_line() ++ " parents=" ++ parents.map(|p| p.description.first_line()).join(",") ++ "\n"'

Set values and intermediate sets

Let S = B::G. The descendant operator includes B and follows child edges, restricted by the right endpoint when one is present. In the graph above, S = {B,E,F,G}; C and D are on the other fork and H lies beyond G's endpoint. Applying heads(S) removes each selected commit that is an ancestor of another selected commit. F is an ancestor of G, so F is removed and the result is {G}. A merge does not make each parent a head merely because it has two incoming edges.

There are two common mistakes here. The first is to confuse a member with a head: a node can be a selected member and still be an ancestor of a later selected member. The second is to apply heads() before selecting the corridor. heads(all()) asks for heads of the entire visible domain; heads(B::G) asks for heads of the smaller set. A release script that uses the former may publish an unrelated local experiment.

ExpressionEvaluation questionTypical use
B | EWhich commits are named by either operand?Construct a review set with explicit alternatives.
B & ::GIs B both selected and an ancestor of G?Check that a named change lies on a stack.
B::GWhich descendants of B are ancestors of G?One connected corridor through a stack or merge.
G::Which descendants of G are reachable?Find work added after a review tip.
::GWhich ancestors of G, including G, are reachable?Inspect the complete history supporting a tip.
heads(B::G)Which selected nodes have no selected descendant?Reduce a corridor to publication candidates.
roots(B::G)Which selected nodes have no selected parent?Find the first changes in a selected stack.

Use a no-graph template in experiments. A graph log is excellent for human topology, but its indentation is not a machine-readable answer and can conceal that two selected nodes have the same description.

Range algebra and boundaries

Range expressions are where Git experts most often import an unhelpful analogy. In jj, x..y is an ancestry difference: the ancestors of y, excluding the ancestors of x. It is not simply “commits after x in one named branch”. If x is not an ancestor of y, the result can contain several regions or all of y's ancestry that is not also reachable from x. That is often exactly what a graph query should say, but it is not a linear interval.

       A--B--C--D
            \
             E--F

::D       = {root,A,B,C,D}
::F       = {root,A,B,E,F}
B..D      = {C,D}
B..F      = {E,F}
C..F      = {E,F}
B::F      = {B,E,F}

The last line is a corridor, not a textual interval. If F is changed into a merge with both C and E as parents, then C..F includes F and its C/E-supported ancestry according to reachability. Always inspect parent edges in a merge. A good mental model is “subtract one reachable ancestor cone from another”, not “subtract line positions”.

The prefix and infix forms are compositional. roots(main@origin..@):: first computes changes not reachable from the imported trunk, reduces them to their roots, and then expands each root through descendants. That is useful when local work contains a connected stack. If it contains two independent roots, the expression intentionally selects two stacks. If the consuming command requires one source, cardinality must be checked rather than silently choosing one.

Boundaries can be placed on either side of a predicate. Compare:

heads(mutable() & ::@)
heads(mutable()) & ::@
mutable() & heads(::@)

The first finds heads after restricting to mutable ancestors of @. The second finds mutable repository heads that are also ancestors of @. The third finds mutable ancestors of the heads of the current ancestry. In a linear stack these may coincide; in a fork they need not. Parentheses make the policy visible and protect it from a future grammar change.

Precedence as a proof obligation

Operator precedence is part of the language contract. Function calls and postfix navigation bind tightly; graph ranges and set operators bind according to the installed grammar. Do not rely on memory when an expression combines topology and set algebra. Write the intended grouping first, then compare it with jj's parser by adding parentheses until the expression reads like the proof.

# Candidate local work whose ancestry includes @, excluding public boundaries
heads((mutable() & ::@) ~ ::remote_bookmarks())

# A different question: public tips which are ancestors of @
heads(mutable()) & ::@ & ::remote_bookmarks()

# Make the review domain explicit before reducing it
heads((main@origin..@) ~ empty() ~ conflicts())

Difference is not a boolean “not”. X ~ Y removes commits in Y from X; it does not necessarily remove descendants of Y. To exclude a whole ancestry cone use X ~ ::Y; to exclude descendants use X ~ Y::. “Not already published” usually means “not reachable from any published remote tip”, so the exclusion should be ~ ::remote_bookmarks(), not merely ~ remote_bookmarks().

Intersections distribute over unions in the usual set sense, but graph functions such as heads() are reductions and do not commute with arbitrary set operations. In general, heads(X | Y) is not equivalent to heads(X) | heads(Y). The former removes a node if a descendant exists in either operand; the latter reduces each operand independently and can retain an ancestor of a head from the other operand. Write the expression from the operational question outward.

Symbols, prefixes, and cardinality

Resolution turns text into a set before topology is evaluated. The special symbol @ is the current workspace's working-copy commit; @- and related offsets navigate relative to that workspace. A full commit ID identifies one commit. A change ID identifies an evolution and can resolve to one or more visible commit versions when divergence exists. Local bookmarks, qualified remote bookmarks, tags, and workspace symbols carry different qualification rules.

Short IDs are a user-interface convenience. An unambiguous prefix can become ambiguous after fetch or rewrite. A script that stores a short prefix as an external key has a latent failure. Use a full commit ID for object interchange, a change ID when the task is to follow a logical change, and a bookmark or tag when the task is to name a publication boundary.

ConsumerSafe input shapeWhat to do with a set
jj log -rAny revset, including many commits.Render or inspect the whole set.
jj diff -rOne connected commit set for a patch view.Check connectivity and intended endpoint.
jj editExactly one commit.Use exactly(X, 1) or an explicit ID.
jj describe -rOne mutable commit in ordinary use.Do not assume a matching subject is unique.
jj rebase -rMany commits are allowed.Understand roots, holes, and descendant rewriting.
jj bookmark set -rOne destination commit.Prove cardinality before moving a publication name.

A command may accept a many-valued revset but still impose a graph constraint. A rebase can reject a selection with a destination inside its own source set, a cycle, or an immutable source. Cardinality is the first precondition, not the only one.

Predicates describe stored commit state

Predicates such as empty(), conflicts(), mutable(), immutable(), visible(), and hidden() inspect commits in the repository view. They do not inspect unrecorded contents of today's filesystem. A dirty file becomes queryable only after a snapshot makes it part of the working-copy commit tree.

The same distinction applies to file predicates. files(X) selects commits whose changes involve matching paths; it does not mean that every selected commit's resulting tree contains those paths. A file may be deleted, renamed, or changed in a merge. Pair a file predicate with jj diff and a path-limited fileset when the question is “what changed in this directory”, not “which commits have a file with this name”.

jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@'
jj log -r 'empty() & mutable()'
jj log -r 'files("root-glob:src/**/*.rs") & ::@'
jj log -r 'author_email(exact:"a@example.org") & committer_date(after:"2026-01-01")'

Metadata patterns are typed. author_email(exact:"…") asks for the author email; author(exact:"…") matches the author signature according to the pattern form accepted by the release. There is no generic email: pattern kind in 0.44. Use exact, substring, glob, or regex explicitly and quote the whole revset for the shell.

Revset public functions: signatures and operational uses

The following entries are contracts rather than name lists. R means a revset value and P means a typed string pattern. Unless stated otherwise, a function can return zero, one, or many commits. Inspect a result with jj log --no-graph before passing it to a singleton command.

Topology functions

FunctionResult and edge caseOperational use
root()The repository root singleton; it has no parent.root():: anchors a complete graph query.
all()All commits in the current search domain.Use only with a deliberate exclusion/boundary.
none()The empty set.A safe false branch for an alias or script.
parents(R)Immediate parents; duplicates collapse.Inspect merge edges or derive roots.
children(R)Immediate children; a fork can return many.Never pass to edit without cardinality proof.
ancestors(R)R plus every reachable ancestor.Compare ancestry cones and public bases.
descendants(R)R plus every reachable descendant.Bound a stack with & ::TIP.
first_parent(R)The first parent relation where present.Report integration history without following every side branch.
first_ancestors(R)First-parent ancestry closure.Contrast a release line with full reachability.
roots(R)Selected nodes with no selected parent.Turn a source set into rebase roots.
heads(R)Selected nodes with no selected descendant.Find maximal publication tips; an ancestor of a selected merge is not a head.
reachable(S,D)Sources reachable through an explicit domain.Prevent traversal crossing an unrelated fork; empty can be correct.
connected(R)Tests/selects connected graph regions according to the target grammar.Precondition a patch or rewrite policy.

Optional depth parameters, where exposed by 0.44 help, limit traversal. They do not turn ancestry into a first-parent query. Verify overloads before putting them in a shared alias.

State and boundary functions

FunctionMeaningImportant non-equivalence
visible_heads()Visible graph tips.Not all bookmarks.
immutable_heads()Effective rewrite-protection boundary.Not necessarily every pushed commit.
builtin_immutable_heads()Built-in protection expression.Can differ after configuration override.
mutable()/immutable()Rewrite policy membership.Neither means unpublished/published.
visible()/hidden()Visibility in the current view.Hidden is retained data, not erased data.
conflicts()Stored conflict values.Does not mean the current filesystem has markers.
empty()No tree change relative to parent merge result.Empty commits can be intentional.
merges()Multiple-parent commits.A conflict is not necessarily a merge.
forks()Commits participating in a fork.A fork is a graph relation, not a pair of refs.
divergent()Several visible commits for one change identity.Similar patches with different change IDs are not divergent.

Refs, identity, and metadata

bookmarks() selects commits named by local bookmarks. remote_bookmarks() selects commits named by remote bookmarks, with optional name/remote patterns. Tracking predicates distinguish relationships, not movement. tags() and remote-tag functions select release names; the 0.45.x track adds tracked/untracked remote-tag predicates. git_refs() selects imported Git refs. These functions return target commits; template CommitRef values are where names, conflict targets, and tracking counts are rendered.

jj log -r 'bookmarks()'
jj log -r 'remote_bookmarks("origin", "main")'
jj log -r 'bookmarks() & ~remote_bookmarks()'
jj log -r 'tags() | remote_tags()'
jj log -r 'git_refs() ~ bookmarks()'

Identity predicates are typed. commit_id(P) and change_id(P) match IDs; description(P) and subject(P) match message text; author and committer functions match metadata; mine() uses configured identity; date functions select timestamp ranges. Use explicit pattern kinds:

commit_id(prefix:"abc")
change_id(prefix:"xyz")
subject(exact:"API: introduce quota")
description(substring:"security")
author_email(exact:"a@example.org")
committer_date(after:"2026-01-01")

Do not invent email: as a pattern kind. An exact subject can match multiple duplicates; a prefix can become ambiguous after fetch; author and committer dates can differ after import. These are language and data-model edge cases, not reasons to select the first line of output in a shell pipeline.

File and content predicates

File predicates accept a fileset/pattern argument and return commits whose changes satisfy it. They do not return paths and they do not necessarily mean that a path exists in the resulting tree. Renames, deletions, merge diffs, binary entries, and conflicts make the distinction important.

jj log -r 'files("root-prefix-glob:src") & ::@'
jj log -r 'diff_lines_added(substring:"TODO") & mutable()'
jj diff -r 'files("root-prefix-glob:src")'

Inspect the exact target-version signature before using a content predicate in policy. Use jj file list for final-tree membership and jj diff for patch membership. A revset selects commits; a fileset selects paths inside the selected trees or patches.

Composition patterns and proof order

# Reviewable heads in current mutable ancestry:
heads(mine() & mutable() & ::@ ~ empty() ~ conflicts())

# First changes of each unpublished local stack:
roots((mutable() & ::@) ~ ::remote_bookmarks())

# Source changes after imported trunk:
files("root-prefix-glob:src") & main@origin..@

# Safe formatter domain:
mutable() & ::@ & ~immutable_heads() & ~conflicts()

Each expression follows the same proof order: choose a domain, constrain ancestry/publication, apply state/content predicates, then reduce to roots or heads if a consumer needs boundaries. heads(X | Y) is generally not equivalent to heads(X) | heads(Y), because the first reduction sees descendants from both operands. Likewise, X ~ Y excludes selected nodes while X ~ ::Y excludes the whole ancestry cone of Y.

Failure cases

SymptomCauseRepair
Invalid pattern kindemail: or unsupported prefix.Use exact, substring, glob, or regex.
Unknown functionWrong release track.Compare local help and gate the alias.
Empty bounded resultSource is outside the domain or not visible.Print each operand separately.
Many results at singleton consumerDuplicate subject, divergent change, or ambiguous prefix.Use exactly, an explicit reduction, or a bookmark.
Unexpected headReduction applied before a set exclusion.Print intermediate sets with no graph.
Unexpected remote resultRemote omitted or fetch changed observations.Qualify remote and display both sides.

A function reference is useful only when it describes the wrong results as well as the right ones. The checked-in 0.44 inventory establishes availability; the examples here establish how to reason about composition and command preconditions.

Part V — Filesets

Jj has three distinct languages: revset selects commits; fileset selects paths; template renders values. A path expression is not a commit expression with slashes in it.

Filesets include literal paths, recursive paths, globs, and set operators/functions as documented by the installed jj help -k filesets. Quote them so the shell does not expand them first. Typical forms are glob:src/**, glob:*.rs, and a literal path such as src/lib.rs.

jj diff --from @- --to @ -- 'glob:src/**'
jj restore --from main@origin --to @ -- 'glob:docs/**'
jj squash --into @- -- 'glob:src/**'
jj split --from @ -- 'glob:tests/**'

Set operations let a command operate on “all generated files except this directory” or combine multiple patterns. Fileset aliases live in configuration and should be named like path sets, for example source-files = 'glob:src/** | glob:include/**'. The exact function inventory is generated from local help in the companion research directory.

Validation rule. First run jj file list -- fileset or a no-op jj diff -- fileset and inspect the result. A shell glob, a fileset glob, and a Git pathspec have different quoting and recursion rules.

Filesets as a path language

The fileset language comes from the same family of path-set languages as Mercurial's filesets. It evaluates to a set of repository paths. It is neither a Git pathspec nor a shell glob. The most important consequence is that three parsers may see one command line: the shell first, jj's fileset parser second, and the command's path consumer third.

shell text  →  jj fileset AST  →  selected repository paths
              (prefix/glob/set algebra)   (diff, tree, snapshot, or rewrite)

Working-directory and repository-root coordinates

The unqualified form "path" is a cwd-relative prefix glob. It matches the path itself and files below a directory. cwd: makes that coordinate explicit. root: and its variants use the workspace/repository root instead. This distinction matters when a script runs from src/ but an alias was written relative to the repository root.

PatternCoordinateMatch
"path", cwd:"path"current directoryprefix: path and descendants.
file:"path", cwd-file:"path"current directoryexact file path, not directory contents.
glob:"*.c", cwd-glob:"*.c"current directorycwd-relative wildcard; *.c is non-recursive.
prefix-glob:"*.d"current directoryglob plus matching path prefixes and descendants.
root:"src"workspace rootroot-relative prefix and descendants.
root-file:"Cargo.toml"workspace rootexact root-relative file.
root-glob:"**/*.rs"workspace rootroot-relative Unix glob.
root-prefix-glob:"src/*.rs"workspace rootroot-relative glob plus descendants of matched prefixes.
glob-i:"*.TXT"current directorycase-insensitive glob.

Glob syntax is Unix-style. A star does not cross a slash in the ordinary glob form; ** is the recursive form. Prefix-glob adds the directory-prefix interpretation. For example, glob:"*.c" matches C files directly below the current directory, while glob:"**/*.c" searches recursively, and prefix-glob:"*.c" additionally treats a matching directory prefix as a recursive selection.

Fileset operators and precedence

PrecedenceExpressionMeaning
1f(x)Function call.
2p:xPattern or fileset alias.
3~xAll paths except x.
4x & y, x ~ yIntersection; difference.
5x | yUnion.

Operators at the same level associate left-to-right. Thus x | y & z means x | (y & z), while x ~ y & z means (x ~ y) & z. Parenthesise policy expressions even when precedence is known:

jj file list -- 'root:"src" ~ root-glob:"**/*.generated.*"'
jj diff -- '(glob:"**/*.rs" | glob:"**/*.toml") ~ glob:"target/**"'
jj split -- 'tests & ~glob:"**/fixtures/**"'

The function inventory is intentionally small in the target release: all() selects every path and none() selects no paths. Most practical composition is written with patterns and operators. Fileset aliases can add domain vocabulary:

[fileset-aliases]
LOCK = '**/Cargo.lock | **/package-lock.json | **/uv.lock'
SOURCE = 'root-prefix-glob:"src/**" | root-prefix-glob:"include/**"'
'not:x' = '~x'

An alias can be a symbol, a function, or a pattern alias. Alias functions can be overloaded by parameter count, but a user alias with a builtin name shadows the builtin. Description tables with definition and doc make aliases visible to completion.

Quoting: three concrete failures

CommandWhat happensCorrect form
jj diff ~Cargo.lockThe shell may interpret ~ as a home-directory expansion or pass an unintended token.jj diff '~Cargo.lock'
jj diff '~"Foo Bar"'Without outer shell quotes, the argument splits; inner quotes are needed because the fileset contains a space.jj diff '~"Foo Bar"'
jj diff "Foo(1)"jj may parse parentheses as syntax rather than a literal path.jj diff '"Foo(1)"'
jj diff ~glob:**/*.rsShell metacharacters and jj operators are exposed simultaneously.jj diff '~glob:"**/*.rs"'

Quotes around a simple path can be omitted inside the fileset syntax, but shell quotes are still required when whitespace or shell metacharacters occur. The safest durable rule is to quote the complete fileset and quote inner literal strings when the fileset parser needs to distinguish a path from an operator.

Evaluate before rewriting

There is no separate fileset evaluator command, so use a read-only consumer. jj file list displays matching paths in a tree; jj diff --name-only shows changed paths; and jj file search --name-only tests a content-related path restriction.

jj file list -r @ -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src/**"'
jj diff -r @ --name-only -- 'root:"src" ~ root-glob:"**/*.rs"'
jj file list -r main@origin -- LOCK

When evaluating a fileset for a rewrite, first establish the selected tree and then the selected change. A fileset cannot select a path that is absent from the source tree. The separate question of which paths are materialised in the current workspace is covered with workspace management in Part XII.

Consumer contracts

ConsumerFileset roleImportant interaction
diffRestricts displayed paths.Does not change commit range; use -r/--from/--to separately.
restoreRestricts copied/undone content.Commit options choose source/destination; fileset is not a commit selector.
squashRestricts patch ownership movement.Partial squash can leave a non-empty source and rewrite descendants.
splitSelects content for one side.Unselected paths remain in the other resulting change; inspect both.
fixRestricts fixer paths.Commit domain is selected by -s; fixer configuration also filters files.
commitSelects paths included before child creation.Unselected working-copy edits remain in the new child.
file list/show/searchTree/search path selection.Commit option controls the tree; file search pattern controls content.
resolveRestricts conflicted paths.Use resolve --list to discover valid paths first.

The fileset language selects paths for a command; it does not decide the general materialisation policy of a workspace. That second concern is intentionally deferred to Part XII.

Fileset review checklist

  1. Which coordinate system does the expression use: cwd or root?
  2. Is the intent exact file, prefix, non-recursive glob, recursive glob, or prefix-glob?
  3. Did the shell preserve the expression?
  4. What does the expression select in jj file list?
  5. Is the consumer selecting output, tree content, patch ownership, or workspace materialisation?
  6. Does a partial operation leave a source remainder or rewrite descendants?
  7. Would a fileset alias shadow a builtin or depend on a repository-specific config layer?

The 0.44 grammar, patterns, precedence, aliases, and function inventory in this chapter are derived from the installed jj help -k filesets capture. Appendix D repeats them as a compact reference; the chapter supplies the reasoning and command interactions.

Fileset laboratory: evaluate paths against a real tree

A fileset is evaluated against a tree, not against a commit graph. Begin with a repository tree like this:

.
|-- Cargo.toml
|-- src/
|   |-- main.rs
|   |-- parser.rs
|   \-- parser/
|       |-- lexer.rs
|       \-- tests.rs
|-- tests/
|   |-- cli.rs
|   \-- fixtures/
|       |-- basic.txt
|       \-- malformed.txt
|-- docs/
|   |-- design.md
|   \-- api/
|       \-- parser.md
\-- .github/workflows/check.yml

The same path can be selected in several ways, with different scope. A literal file names one path. A glob pattern is interpreted according to the fileset grammar and its matching scope. A root-qualified expression makes the repository root explicit; a cwd-qualified expression makes the command’s current directory explicit. This distinction matters when a command is run from src/parser or from a script whose working directory is not stable.

jj file list 'file:src/parser.rs'
jj file list 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj file list 'glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj file list 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj file list 'cwd-file:parser.rs'
jj file list 'cwd-glob:**/*.rs'

Run these from the repository root and from src/parser to see why explicit root forms are safer in documentation and automation. A shell glob such as src/**/*.rs can be expanded by the shell before jj sees it, depending on shell options. Quote the entire fileset. The glob: prefix is part of jj’s language and is not a request for the shell to expand anything.

Set algebra over paths

Fileset union, intersection, and difference operate over the set of paths matched in the selected tree. A typical review selection can be expressed as:

'glob:src/**/*.rs | glob:tests/**/*.rs'
'glob:**/*.rs ~ glob:**/tests.rs'
'glob:src/**/*.rs & glob:**/parser/**'

Read these left to right only after applying the documented precedence. Parenthesise mixed expressions. The first selects Rust source and Rust tests. The second removes files named tests.rs from an otherwise recursive Rust selection. The third selects parser paths that are also Rust paths. all() and none() are useful as identity and empty values in aliases, but an unconstrained all() passed to a rewriting command is a powerful operation: inspect it first.

[fileset-aliases]
rust = 'glob:**/*.rs'
parser-code = 'glob:src/parser/**/*.rs | root-file:src/parser.rs'
non-tests = 'rust ~ glob:**/tests.rs'

Keep fileset aliases semantically narrow. An alias named source should document whether it includes generated code, tests, examples, and hidden directories. Aliases are configuration, so a repository-local alias can alter the meaning of a command copied from another repository. When portability matters, expand the alias in a review script or print the active config with jj config list.

Filesets and commit selection are orthogonal

Many commands accept both a commit selection and a fileset. The commit answers “which tree or patch is being operated on?”; the fileset answers “which paths within that tree or patch?”. Confusing these layers is a common source of destructive surprises.

Command familyCommit roleFileset role
diffCompare a commit or range of trees.Limit displayed paths.
restoreChoose source and destination commits.Copy only selected paths from source to destination.
squashChoose source and destination changes.Move selected patch paths into the destination.
splitChoose the commit to divide.First commit owns selected paths; remainder stays in the other commit.
fixChoose commits whose files are passed through fixers.Some fixers or command forms restrict paths.
file list/show/searchChoose the tree or commit to inspect.Filter the displayed or searched paths.

For a partial squash, inspect both the source and destination before and after:

jj diff --from C --to C^ 'glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj squash --from C --into B 'glob:src/parser/**/*.rs'
jj show C
jj show B

The exact option spellings and whether a command accepts a positional fileset or an option are version-sensitive; use the 0.44 entries in Part XV. The semantic test remains stable: a partial operation should leave an inspectable residual patch or a changed destination, not an unexplained disappearance.

Quoting and pattern failures

There are three different failure classes. First, the shell expands an unquoted wildcard and jj receives several path arguments or none. Second, jj parses a valid fileset but its scope is relative to a different working directory than intended. Third, the fileset is valid but selects an empty set because glob syntax was mistaken for regular-expression syntax. Diagnose in that order:

# Show what the shell would pass without running jj.
printf '%s\n' 'glob:src/**/*.rs'

# Ask jj to enumerate the selection.
jj file list 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'

# Compare a literal and a glob.
jj file list 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj file list 'root-glob:src/**/parser.rs'

Do not use a regex mental model for glob wildcards. A glob’s *, **, directory separators, and prefix rules have language-specific semantics. Case-insensitive forms are explicit; they should not be assumed from the filesystem’s case behaviour. A path containing spaces or brackets must be quoted for both the shell and, where applicable, the fileset parser.

Filesets in patch-stack surgery

Filesets are most valuable when a change is conceptually mixed. Suppose B contains an API implementation and an unrelated documentation edit, while C contains tests for both. Rather than splitting by hunk interactively without a stated invariant, select the path family first:

B: src/parser.rs + docs/api/parser.md
C: tests/cli.rs + tests/fixtures/*

intent:
  move only docs/api/parser.md from B into a documentation change
  keep src/parser.rs in B
  preserve all C tests

Use diff to confirm the patch ownership, then squash or split with an explicit fileset. If a descendant’s tests become conflicted after the parent’s tree changes, that is evidence that the test patch depended on the old context; it is not evidence that the fileset was ignored. Inspect the evolved descendant and resolve or adjust it.

With restore, the direction matters. --from selects the source tree and --into selects the destination commit. A fileset limits copied content; it does not select which commit is edited. This makes a useful “take one generated file from a known commit” operation, but it is not a branch reset and should not be described as one.

Filesets as an API boundary

For shell automation, accept a fileset expression as one argument and pass it as one argument. Avoid concatenating user text into a larger unquoted revset/fileset. Print selected paths before mutation, and use a temporary operation bookmark if the operation is broad. A robust pattern is:

set -eu
paths='root-glob:src/**/*.rs ~ root-glob:src/**/generated/**'
jj file list "$paths"
jj diff -r @ "$paths"
# Only after review:
jj fix -r 'mutable() & ::@' "$paths"

If a command does not consume a fileset in the installed release, do not emulate it by passing paths after a revset. Some commands treat extra arguments as commits, options, or errors. The reference entry should be consulted for the command’s grammar, while this chapter supplies the language model needed to reason about selections.

Fileset semantics laboratory: path sets, not path strings

A fileset evaluates against a repository tree and returns a set of paths. The returned set has no commit identity and no template type. This separation is more than terminology: a command can select one commit and a fileset can select many paths within that commit's tree. If a command accepts both, the two selectors are evaluated independently and combined at the command's semantic boundary.

shell token                  jj parser                  consumer
"src ~ glob:\"**/*.gen\"" →  path-set expression     →  {src/**} minus generated files
                              operators/functions        applied to a tree or patch

There are three parsers to keep apart. The shell performs quote removal and, unless prevented, pathname expansion. jj then parses the remaining argument as a fileset. Finally the command decides whether the set applies to a tree, a patch, a snapshot policy, or a file metadata operation. A correct fileset can still produce an unexpected result if it is passed to a command whose default commit is not the one the reader had in mind.

Pattern families

In 0.44, an unqualified path is a cwd-relative prefix-glob. It matches the named file and paths below a named directory. The explicit forms make the intended scope reviewable:

FormMeaningExample and consequence
cwd:"path"Cwd-relative path prefix.cwd:src means src below the invocation directory.
file:"path"One exact cwd-relative file.Does not recursively include siblings.
glob:"*.rs"Cwd-relative Unix glob.Matches Rust files at that directory level, not below every subdirectory.
prefix-glob:"*.rs"Glob plus matching descendants of a matched prefix.Equivalent in shape to glob:"*.rs" | glob:"*.rs/**".
root:"docs"Workspace-root-relative prefix.Stable when a script changes cwd.
root-file:"LICENSE"One exact root-relative file.Useful for a root policy file without matching a directory.
root-glob:"docs/**/*.md"Root-relative recursive glob.Matches Markdown files below docs.
root-prefix-glob:"src"Root-relative prefix glob.Includes the src directory and its descendants.
glob-i:"*.TXT"Case-insensitive glob.Explicitly controls case; do not infer it from the filesystem.

“Recursive” belongs to the pattern form. glob:"*.c" is not the same as a shell command that expands **/*.c, and a plain src is not the same as file:"src". When reproducibility matters, prefer an explicit root-prefixed pattern and state whether it is exact, glob, or prefix-glob.

Fileset consumer precedence and operators

The fileset operators are set algebra over paths. Function calls and pattern aliases bind most tightly. Prefix complement ~x selects every path except x; intersection x & y selects paths in both; difference x ~ y selects x minus y; union x | y selects paths in either. In the target grammar, intersection and difference bind more strongly than union, and equal-strength infix operations associate from the left.

src | docs & ~glob:"**/*.generated"
(src | docs) & ~glob:"**/*.generated"
root-prefix-glob:"src" ~ root-glob:"src/**/generated/**"
all() ~ root-file:"Cargo.lock"

The first expression means src | (docs & ~generated), not “everything under src or docs, then remove generated files”. The second expresses the latter. A path expression that looks obvious in prose should be parenthesised when it combines a positive union with an exclusion. This also makes diffs and review scripts resilient if another person later adds a third path family.

Fileset complement is bounded by the relevant tree. It does not select a path that is not present in the tree merely because that path would exist in the filesystem. Fileset evaluation for a historical tree is consequently independent of whether that path happens to be present in the current working directory.

Quoting and shell failure modes

Quoting has two jobs: protect the argument from the shell, and, when required, quote a path literal inside the fileset grammar. A bare simple path can omit the inner quotes, but whitespace, parentheses, operators, and punctuation require them. Glob characters do not necessarily require inner quotes for jj, but shell quotes are still needed to stop the shell expanding them.

CommandWhat the shell may doRobust spelling
jj diff src/*.rsExpands files before jj sees one fileset; result depends on cwd contents.jj diff 'glob:src/*.rs'
jj diff ~fooUnquoted operator can be interpreted or split by shell policy.jj diff '~"foo"'
jj diff "Foo Bar"Shell protects whitespace; jj can parse a literal path.jj diff '"Foo Bar"' when combining with operators.
jj diff src | docsThe shell creates a pipeline.jj diff 'src | docs'
jj diff 'glob:**/*.rs'Safe shell token, recursive jj glob.Same; document the recursive intent explicitly.

In a shell function, do not place untrusted path text inside a larger unquoted revset/fileset. Pass paths as separate arguments when the command supports literal file arguments, or construct one carefully quoted fileset with a language-aware interface. A path beginning with a dash belongs after -- where the consumer supports it.

Evaluate a real tree

Use this tree for experiments:

Cargo.toml
Cargo.lock
src/main.rs
src/generated/schema.rs
src/parser/mod.rs
src/parser/tests.rs
tests/cli/basic.rs
tests/fixtures/input.json
docs/guide.md
docs/generated/api.md
scripts/check.sh

Evaluate each expression with jj file list before passing it to a rewrite:

jj file list -- 'root-prefix-glob:src'
jj file list -- 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj file list -- 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs ~ root-glob:src/**/generated/**'
jj file list -- 'root-prefix-glob:src | root-prefix-glob:tests'
jj file list -- 'root-prefix-glob:docs & ~root-glob:docs/generated/**'
jj file list -- 'root-file:"Cargo.toml" | root-file:"Cargo.lock"'

The expected selected sets are, respectively, all src paths; all Rust files below src; all non-generated Rust files below src; src and tests; non-generated docs; and the two root lock/manifest files. Record actual output in a fixture rather than embedding guessed order in a policy script: path ordering is a presentation concern unless the command defines it as a contract.

To test cwd semantics, run once from the repository root and once from src/. A plain parser means different things in those locations because it is cwd-relative. The root-prefixed spelling means the same repository path in both locations. This is one of the most useful reasons to write aliases in root coordinates.

Consumer contracts

The same fileset language is consumed by commands with different effects:

ConsumerCommit sideFileset effectRisk boundary
diffEndpoint or selected commit.Narrows rendered patch paths.It does not narrow the commit set itself.
restoreSource and destination/change.Copies or reverses selected paths.Wrong direction can overwrite intended content.
squashSource/current and destination parent.Moves selected hunks/files into another change.Descendants may be rebased and conflict.
splitOne change, usually current.Separates selected paths or hunks into a new change.Unselected content remains at the original boundary.
fixConfigured commit set.Restricts fixer input and changed files.Formatter output can rewrite many changes.
file list/show/searchSelected tree/commit.Lists or reads matching entries.Inspection does not mean filesystem materialisation.
file track/untrackWorking-copy snapshot policy.Changes what future snapshots consider.It is not deletion or publication.

For a patch operation, first inspect the source patch and destination tree. Then run a dry or read-only equivalent if available, save the operation ID, and only then apply the fileset. The path selector answers “which paths”; it does not answer “which side wins”, “which change owns the result”, or “whether a descendant should be restored”. Those are command semantics.

Fileset aliases

Fileset aliases provide names for path policy. They live under [fileset-aliases] and may be a symbol, a parameterised function, or a table with definition and completion documentation:

[fileset-aliases]
source = 'root-prefix-glob:"src" | root-prefix-glob:"include"'
generated = 'root-glob:"**/generated/**" | root-glob:"**/*.lock"'
reviewable(x) = 'x ~ generated'

docs.definition = 'root-prefix-glob:"docs"'
docs.doc = 'Documentation files'

Use a descriptive alias only when its coordinate and exclusions are stable. An alias named source should not silently include generated code one day and exclude it the next. Parameterised aliases are language expressions, not shell interpolation; callers pass a fileset expression as one argument:

jj diff -- 'reviewable(source)'
jj file list -- 'docs ~ root-glob:"docs/generated/**"'

Test aliases from the repository root and a subdirectory, against complete repository trees, and against a tree with spaces or punctuation in a path. Keep fileset aliases separate from revset aliases with similar names; a command will not convert one language into the other.

Case study: split a mixed change without changing its review story

Suppose the current change contains a parser implementation, generated schema output, and a documentation example. The intended review story is “implementation first, generated output and docs second”. A fileset can make that ownership explicit, but it cannot decide whether generated output should be regenerated or copied.

before:
  A--B(@)
     B = src/parser/mod.rs
         src/generated/schema.rs
         docs/guide.md

desired:
  A--B--C(@)
     B = src/parser/mod.rs
     C = src/generated/schema.rs + docs/guide.md
jj diff -r @ -- 'root-file:"src/parser/mod.rs"'
jj diff -r @ -- 'root-glob:"src/generated/**" | root-file:"docs/guide.md"'
jj split -- 'root-glob:"src/generated/**" | root-file:"docs/guide.md"'
jj log -r '::@'
jj diff -r @-
jj diff -r @

The exact interactive result depends on the split mode and fileset supplied, so verify the resulting parent edge and patch ownership rather than inferring it from success. If a descendant existed, it would be automatically rebased over the new boundary and could receive a new commit ID or a stored conflict. Path selection controls content ownership; the DAG records the consequences.

Fileset review checklist

  1. Which parser sees the token first: shell, jj fileset, or the consumer?
  2. Is the path coordinate cwd-relative or workspace-root-relative?
  3. Does the pattern match one file, a directory prefix, a glob, or a recursive glob?
  4. Are operators parenthesised so exclusion applies to the intended union?
  5. Does the target command use the fileset for a diff, a tree copy, a snapshot policy, or metadata?
  6. What is the exact commit set on which the fileset is being applied?
  7. Could the selected path be absent from the current filesystem even though it exists in the repository tree?
  8. What commit, descendant, bookmark, and operation changes will follow?
  9. Has the selection been printed with jj file list or a read-only diff?

The reference card in Appendix D is intentionally dense, but this reasoning process prevents an apparently harmless path argument from becoming a broad rewrite.

Part VI — Bookmarks and publication refs

A bookmark is a name attached to a commit. It is a publication handle, not the unit of local work. A local bookmark can be created, moved, deleted, forgotten, renamed, listed, tracked, or untracked. The 0.44 command family is: jj bookmark advance, create, delete, forget, list, move, rename, set, track, and untrack.

jj bookmark create review -r @
jj bookmark set review -r @
jj bookmark move review --to @
jj bookmark track review --remote origin
jj bookmark untrack review --remote origin
jj bookmark forget review
jj bookmark delete review

set chooses or creates a local name at a commit; move changes an existing name; delete asks the remote publication machinery to delete when pushed; forget removes local knowledge of the bookmark without necessarily expressing a remote deletion. Inspect help for exact confirmation and force flags in your release.

Tracking is a relationship

local bookmark review  <-------- tracking relationship -------->  review@origin
        points to @                                                 points to server tip

@ can move without moving review.
review can be moved without changing @.
Tracking does not mean “follow the working copy”.

Remote bookmarks are imported names such as main@origin. If two remotes have a bookmark named main, the remote qualifies it: main@origin and main@gerrit. A local bookmark may track one corresponding remote bookmark; tracking controls expected push/pull relationship and safety checks, not automatic movement.

Tags are separate immutable-ish names supplied by Git-compatible repositories. Use jj tag list, set, delete, track, and untrack as the installed command permits; do not use tags as a substitute for a moving review bookmark.

Bookmark and tag state laboratory

A bookmark has a local target, optional remote targets, and possibly a tracking relationship. Treat those as fields in a state record:

local ref record:
  name = review
  local target = C
  tracked remote = review@origin
  observed remote target = B
  status = ahead by one / unsynchronised

remote ref record:
  name = review
  remote = origin
  target = B
  tracking local = review

The local bookmark does not “point to the working copy” unless its target happens to equal @. Moving @ by new, edit, or ordinary snapshotting does not move review. Moving review does not change the tree of C. A push turns the local ref record into a server request; a fetch turns a server ref into a remote-bookmark observation.

Create, set, move, advance

jj bookmark create review -r @
jj bookmark set review -r @
jj bookmark move review --to @
jj bookmark advance review --to @

Create requires that the name not already exist. Set is a convenient create-or-update operation and can allow a backwards/sideways move with --allow-backwards. Move targets existing names and can select names by their current commits. Advance is intended to move forward to a descendant or a configured target; it is a useful batch operation when several publication refs should follow a known graph boundary.

OperationChangesDoes not change
createadds a local name at a targetworking copy, commit trees, remote server
setmoves/creates names at a targetcommit graph; remote until push
movemoves existing namescontent and change identities
advancemoves eligible names forwardunrelated refs and commit content
