Delete versus forget

Delete expresses that a local bookmark should cease to exist and, when tracked and pushed, can express remote deletion. Forget removes local knowledge. These are intentionally different because a user may have merged a GitHub PR and want to remove the local name without deleting a server branch, or may want to delete a stale server branch.

# local cleanup, preserve server state
jj bookmark forget review
jj bookmark forget --include-remotes review

# request server deletion
jj bookmark delete review
jj git push --remote origin --deleted

--include-remotes forgets remote observations too; a future fetch recreates a remote bookmark that still exists at the server. It is not a server deletion. Keep the distinction in cleanup scripts.

Tracking is a relationship, not movement

jj bookmark track review --remote origin
jj bookmark untrack review --remote origin
jj bookmark track review@origin

Tracking associates a local bookmark with a corresponding remote bookmark. It makes ahead/behind/synced state meaningful and gives push safety a known expected remote target. It does not move the local bookmark when @ moves, does not rebase a stack, and does not pull changes automatically.

With two remotes, the relationship is three-dimensional:

review (local) --tracks-- review@github
       |
       \--------------- review@gerrit is a different remote bookmark

main@github and main@gerrit can point to different commits.

Use --remote or an exact name@remote to avoid tracking the wrong remote. A bookmark may be tracked to one or more corresponding remote states according to the backend's model; the command's list output is authoritative for the actual repository.

Normal, deleted, and conflicted remote state

Fetching a remote that deleted or moved a ref changes the imported remote-bookmark state. A local bookmark can be ahead, behind, diverged, or absent. A conflicted bookmark/tag has multiple added or removed targets rather than one normal target. Before pushing a conflicted ref, inspect all targets and resolve the naming/ref policy; do not assume the last fetched value is the desired one.

jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked
jj log -r 'remote_bookmarks("review", "origin")'
jj log -r 'bookmarks("review")'

The exact string-pattern arguments to revset ref functions are release-sensitive; use the local help form when filtering names. The conceptual distinction remains stable: a local name is an explicit local ref, a remote name is an observation, and tracking is metadata linking them.

Tags and release policy

Tags are useful at release points because their meaning should not change as a mutable patch stack evolves. A local tag can be set to @, listed, tracked to a remote tag, and pushed. Moving an existing tag needs --allow-move in the target CLI and should require a release-policy review.

jj tag set v2.4.0 -r @
jj tag list --all-remotes
jj tag track v2.4.0@origin
jj git push --remote origin --tag v2.4.0

Do not use a tag as a review branch just because both are names. A moving review boundary wants a bookmark; a release identity wants a tag. 0.45.1 adds tracked/untracked remote-tag predicates; in 0.44, build release queries from the supported tag/ref functions recorded in the local inventory.

Publication invariant

Before publishing, be able to answer four questions:

  1. Which exact local bookmark is being pushed?
  2. Which remote and URL will receive it?
  3. Which remote bookmark was fetched as the expected old target?
  4. Which commit/change is the intended tip, and is it conflicted, empty, divergent, or immutable?
jj git remote list
jj bookmark list --remote origin --all-remotes
jj log -r 'review | review@origin'
jj diff --from review@origin --to review
jj git push --dry-run --remote origin --bookmark review

If any answer is unclear, stop at inspection. A publication ref is a small interface with potentially large external consequences; the local graph-first workflow does not remove that boundary.

Git backend: two object worlds and an import/export boundary

In a Git-backed repository, jj and Git share enough storage to interoperate, but they do not share the same semantic model. Git stores commit objects, trees, blobs, and refs. jj stores change metadata, operation history, workspace state, conflict values, and its view of bookmarks/tags. Some jj state is represented in Git-compatible objects; not all of it is meaningful to Git.

jj commit/change/operation/workspace model
        |
        |- exportable commit/tree/blob → Git object database
        |- local bookmark/tag → Git ref when exported
        \- change evolution/op/workspace/conflict metadata → jj semantics

Git refs/objects ← fetch/import → jj remote-bookmark/ref view

Colocated repository discipline

Colocation is valuable when GitHub/Gerrit tooling, hooks, Git inspection, and jj need one working directory. It also creates two writers and two sets of expectations. A Git command can update refs without jj seeing them until import. A Git command that updates the worktree can race with jj's snapshot. A jj rewrite can make Git's current branch/ref appear surprising until export.

jj git colocation status
jj git export
git -C "$(jj root)" show-ref
git -C "$(jj root)" log --all --oneline
git -C "$(jj root)" fetch origin
jj git import
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

Use one writer for the working directory. Git inspection commands that do not touch the worktree are usually safe; Git checkout/reset/restore commands require a snapshot boundary and explicit coordination. After Git-side ref changes, import before interpreting jj's remote state. After jj bookmark changes, export before asking a Git-native tool to inspect refs.

Clone and init choices

ChoiceWhere metadata livesUse when
jj git clone (0.44 default)jj plus a top-level Git checkout, unless git.colocate=falseNormal choice when Git/gh/Gerrit tools are part of daily work.
jj git clone --no-colocatejj repository with its backing Git data hidden under .jjUse when jj should own the workspace and Git is primarily transport.
jj git clone --colocateExplicitly request the normal colocated layoutUseful as documentary/script intent even though it is normally the default.
jj git init (0.44 default)Initialise a colocated Git-backed jj repository unless git.colocate=falseStart a repository that ordinary Git tools can also open.
jj git init --no-colocateInitialise with the backing Git repository hidden below .jjExplicit non-colocated layout.
jj git init --git-repo PATHUse an existing Git repository as the backing repository; mutually exclusive with --colocateFollow the installed help for the same-path special case.

After a clone, identify the trunk bookmark and remote name. The default remote is often origin, but a GitHub fork/upstream or Gerrit repository may use different names. Do not write scripts that assume the remote URL from a directory name.

Fetch, import, export, push

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git import
# graph work happens here
jj git export
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark review

Fetch is network object/ref transfer plus local remote-bookmark update. Import is local Git-ref observation. Export is local jj-to-Git-ref update. Push is network publication. A normal jj command can perform some import/snapshot work at its boundary, but explicit commands make automation and diagnosis clearer.

OperationNetwork?Primary directionChanges local commit DAG?
fetchyesremote Git → local objects/remote refsusually no mutable rewrite
importnolocal Git refs → jj viewno, but ref state changes
exportnojj bookmarks/tags → Git refsno
pushyeslocal Git refs/objects → remoteno, but remote state changes

Native Git commands that are safe, conditional, or hazardous

Command classAssessmentRequired boundary
git show, git log, git cat-fileUseful inspection of Git objects.Remember Git cannot show jj change/evolution semantics.
git show-ref, git for-each-refUseful ref inspection.Import after ref changes.
git fetchCan update Git refs.Run jj git import before relying on them.
git pushExternal publication.Explicit ref/remote; jj will not make a pushed mistake local-undoable.
git checkout, git reset, git restoreWorktree/ref writers.Do not race jj snapshot; import refs afterward.
gh pr checkoutGit-oriented hosting workflow.Inspect worktree and import refs before jj work.

Credentials, SSH, and signing

Jj's Git transport commonly uses Git's URL, credential helper, SSH agent, and known-host configuration. Gerrit and GitHub may have different hosts, keys, or permissions. Keep transport authentication separate from commit signing: an SSH key used to connect to a remote is not automatically a commit-signing key, and a good local signature does not grant push permission.

jj git remote list
ssh-add -l
git config --get-regexp 'credential|url|core.ssh'
jj sign -r @
jj log -r @ -T 'if(signature, signature.status(), "unsigned") ++ "\n"'

Do not put secrets in jj aliases or repository config. Test authentication with a harmless fetch, inspect the destination URL, and use a controlled key/agent environment in CI. Signing a stack rewrites IDs; publish the resulting bookmark only after checking the entire descendant chain.

Private commits and Git compatibility

Some jj/Git policies allow commits to be marked private so they are not pushed by ordinary publication. Private is a publication policy, not a different change identity or a hidden filesystem state. The exact private-commit configuration and push flags are release-sensitive; inspect the 0.44 help/schema before depending on them.

jj log -r 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
jj git push --remote origin --tracked
jj git push --remote origin --all

Use explicit bookmark selection for review work. Broad --all is appropriate only when all local names/tags are intended for that remote. A private mutable change can still be a descendant of a public bookmark and can still affect a pushed descendant if the graph boundary is not selected carefully.

Part VII — Git backend and interoperability

With the Git backend, jj stores its own metadata under .jj and uses Git objects/refs for interoperability. A colocated repository has both .git and jj metadata at one worktree; a non-colocated repository keeps jj's view separate. Colocation is convenient for an advanced Git user because Git-native tools, gh, hooks, and editors can inspect the same object database. It also means two programs can race to update refs or the worktree.

jj git init --colocate
jj git clone https://example.test/project.git project
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git import
jj git export
jj git push --bookmark review --remote origin
jj git remote list
jj git root

Jj notices Git-side ref changes during import/snapshot boundaries; after running native Git, use jj git import or a normal jj command that imports refs. Native Git does not understand change IDs, operation history, conflicts as jj values, or workspace pointers. It can inspect and transport Git objects, but its rewrites may make jj bookmarks/remote state stale until imported.

For colocated scripts, GIT_DIR=$(jj git root)/.git is sometimes useful, but prefer git -C "$(jj root)" ... and let jj report its root. Never use Git to reset the worktree while a jj process is snapshotting it. Git credentials, SSH agents, and signing configuration are generally consumed through the Git transport or configured jj signing/authentication facilities; test the exact credential helper in the target environment.

Push safety

Push bookmarks, not arbitrary working-copy commits, unless the release explicitly supports a change-based push mode and you understand its refspec. A tracked bookmark gives jj a lease-like expectation about the remote value. A force push is safe only when the changed remote tip is the one you intended to replace; review jj bookmark list, jj log -r 'review|review@origin', and the push preview first.

Git interoperability laboratory

The Git backend has two related but distinct storage views. jj stores its commit and operation metadata in the repository’s jj backend; the Git backend can also read and write Git objects and refs. A colocated repository adds a Git view to an existing checkout so that native Git commands and jj commands operate on nearby data. Colocation is convenient precisely because it removes a transport boundary; it is also risky because two tools can mutate related state without sharing jj’s operation semantics.

working tree
    |
    |-- jj view: commit DAG, change IDs, operation DAG, workspace
    |
    \-- Git view: commits/trees/blobs, refs, index, remote-tracking refs

A Git commit object is still a Git object, but jj gives it additional meaning. In the 0.44 Git backend, jj-created commits contain a non-standard change-id commit header when git.write-change-id-header is enabled (the default policy in the validation fixture). The value is a reverse-hex encoding of jj’s change identity, as described in the Git compatibility documentation. This header is object data, not a standard Git identity field: ordinary git log and git show do not normally print it, git cat-file -p can, and native Git operations are not required to preserve it. Independently, .jj records the change’s evolution, operations, and workspace view. Thus “the change ID is in the jj view” is the semantic statement; “the Git backend serialises a change-id header” is the 0.44 storage detail.

Laboratory: jj @, Git HEAD, and object reachability

0.44.0 verified boundary. Run this experiment in a disposable colocated repository. It is intentionally about pointers and reachability, not about converting a jj commit into Git at publication time.

Start with one ordinary Git commit, then let jj snapshot an edit without creating or moving a publication bookmark:

git -c init.defaultBranch=main init demo
cd demo
git config user.name "Example User"
git config user.email user@example.invalid
printf '%s\n' base > note
git add note && git commit -m base
jj git init --colocate
printf '%s\n' jj-snapshot >> note
jj status

Now ask both tools what they consider current. Use the full ID printed by the first command in the later commands:

jj log --no-graph -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
git status --short
git symbolic-ref HEAD
git log --oneline --decorate -3
git log --all --oneline --decorate -6
git show-ref
git cat-file -t JJ_COMMIT_ID
git cat-file -p JJ_COMMIT_ID | sed -n '1,14p'

In the 0.44 fixture, jj log reports a new working-copy commit and git cat-file -t reports commit. The object was created locally when jj made the snapshot; jj git push is not the conversion step. At the same time, git symbolic-ref HEAD remains refs/heads/main, and the target of that ref is still the earlier Git commit. Therefore git log, whose default starting point is Git’s HEAD, need not show the jj working-copy commit. git log --all may show more because it walks every ordinary and implementation ref that Git considers reachable, including a temporary refs/jj/keep/* retention ref in this release; do not treat that implementation ref as a publication interface. git show-ref makes the distinction visible. An object can exist in the object database without being reachable from HEAD, a local branch, or any other ref that a particular Git command walks.

The two status commands answer different questions. jj status describes the filesystem relative to jj’s working-copy commit and snapshots the filesystem at the command boundary. Git status compares the filesystem with the Git index and HEAD. In a colocated repository, a jj snapshot can therefore include note in the jj commit while Git still reports the path as modified relative to its index. The Git index is not jj’s staging area, and a Git status letter is not evidence that jj failed to record the edit.

Finally create an explicit publication boundary:

jj bookmark create review -r @
jj git export
git show-ref refs/heads/review
git symbolic-ref HEAD

The local review ref now reaches the same commit object, while Git HEAD can still name refs/heads/main. In a colocated workspace, creating or moving a bookmark may export its ref as part of the normal jj operation, so the explicit jj git export is a useful idempotent boundary for scripts. If the bookmark named by HEAD is deliberately moved or checked out through a coordinated Git operation, the target of HEAD can change; that is a different event from jj advancing its workspace-local @. A push sends the selected exported ref and reachable objects to a remote; it does not promise to move local HEAD.

Eventjj stateGit stateWhat may move
Filesystem editWorking tree differs from the recorded snapshot.Index/worktree comparison changes.No object or ref need exist yet.
jj snapshot or rewrite@, commit DAG, operation view, and possibly descendants change.Git-backend commit/tree/blob objects are created; implementation retention refs may appear.Not necessarily HEAD or refs/heads/*.
Bookmark create/set and exportA named jj bookmark points at a selected commit.Its corresponding local Git ref is updated by export/normal colocated synchronisation.The bookmark ref; HEAD only if it names that ref and it is moved.
jj git fetch then importRemote-bookmark observations enter jj’s view.Objects and remote-tracking refs are fetched, then read by jj.Remote-tracking refs; not local @ by itself.
jj git pushLocal graph is selected through a bookmark/tag/ref.Remote Git ref and reachable remote objects are updated.Remote state only, apart from local transport bookkeeping.
Native Git checkout/reset/commitMay leave jj’s view stale until import/snapshot.Git HEAD, refs, index, worktree, or objects can change.Whatever that Git command explicitly writes.

This model also explains the directory boundary. In a colocated repository, top-level .git remains the Git object database, refs, remotes, and native-tool layer; .jj remains the jj view, operation log, workspace metadata, and jj backend state. In a non-colocated repository, the backing Git repository is hidden below .jj; ordinary Git commands run in the worktree do not find a top-level .git. Use jj git root and explicit Git directory arguments only when you have deliberately chosen that integration.

Classify native Git commands before using them in a colocated workflow:

ClassExamplesBoundary
Read-only inspectiongit log, git show, git cat-file, git show-refSafe for state, but Git cannot display jj evolution or workspace semantics.
Git-ref mutationgit update-ref, git branch -f, git fetchImport before relying on the new ref view; record the exact ref.
Worktree/index mutationgit checkout, git reset, git restore, git add, git commitSerialise with jj; snapshot or import before interpreting the result. Git’s index is separate.
Network publicationgit push, gh pr createExplicitly select remote and ref; local jj operation recovery cannot undo server state.

Reference: colocated versus non-colocated layout

QuestionColocatedNon-colocated
Native Git commands in the project directory?Yes, subject to safe coordination.Use a separate Git checkout or explicit Git directory.
Git refs visible to jj?Imported/exported through the colocated backend.Managed through jj’s Git backend and transport.
Index meaningGit tools may update it; jj snapshots filesystem state, not Git staging intent.There is no native Git index in the normal working directory.
Concurrent writers?More ways to race; serialise Git/jj mutations.Still serialise jj operations, but fewer accidental ref mutations.
Best useGitHub/Gerrit tooling, hooks, IDEs, gradual adoption.Clean jj-only automation and isolated experiments.

In a colocated repository, a native git switch or git reset changes Git’s checkout/index/ref state. jj may not immediately incorporate all such changes into its integrated view. Run the appropriate jj Git import command or a normal jj command that refreshes the Git view before relying on a Git-side ref movement. Conversely, a jj bookmark push/export can update Git refs that a native Git process sees later. Treat the directory as a shared mutable resource.

Clone, init, fetch, import, export

jj git clone https://example.org/project.git project
jj git init --colocate
jj git remote list
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git import
jj git export
jj git root

jj git clone creates a jj repository backed by Git objects and configures a remote; in 0.44 it is colocated by default unless git.colocate=false or --no-colocate is used. jj git init has the same default, while --no-colocate explicitly hides the backing Git repository below .jj. --colocate is worth spelling in scripts when the layout is a deliberate precondition. fetch transfers remote objects and updates remote-bookmark observations; import refreshes jj’s view from Git refs where needed; export writes jj bookmark state into Git refs. The distinction between fetch and import matters when native Git has changed refs or when a script needs to separate network activity from local ref reconciliation.

Use jj git root to obtain the Git root when a tool expects GIT_DIR or a repository path:

GIT_DIR=$(jj git root) git -C "$(jj root)" show HEAD
git --git-dir="$(jj git root)" for-each-ref

Check the command’s output shape before embedding it in a path. A root command returns a directory; it is not automatically the value of GIT_DIR in every layout. Prefer git -C when operating on the working tree, and use --git-dir only when the command’s path semantics require it.

Fetch and remote bookmarks

Fetch updates the local observation of remote refs. It does not move the workspace’s @ and does not rebase local work automatically merely because the remote trunk advanced. After fetching, inspect:

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj log -r 'main@origin..@'
jj log -r 'remote_bookmarks()'

A remote bookmark is not a local bookmark with a decorative suffix. It is a name imported from a remote namespace. A local bookmark can track it, which gives jj a relationship for ahead/behind and push safety. Tracking does not make the local bookmark follow a working-copy commit; the local target still moves only through an explicit bookmark operation or a graph rewrite that preserves its target relationship.

Push safety and ref publication

Push sends selected local bookmarks or tags to a remote. The safest scripted form names the remote and bookmark, and performs a dry run or inspection first where supported:

jj log -r 'bookmark("pr/parser")' -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser --dry-run
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser

A non-fast-forward update is not automatically evidence that force is required. Compare the local bookmark target with the remote bookmark target and determine whether the local target descends from the remote target, whether another actor moved the remote, and whether server policy permits replacement. The tracking relationship supplies safety context; it is not a lease substitute for all server-side races. If the command offers a force/allow-rewrite option, use it only with an explicit intended old remote state and an audit note.

--tracked, --all, --deleted, and tag options widen the publication set. They are convenient interactively and dangerous in generic scripts. A script that publishes every tracked bookmark can unexpectedly update a second review or a stale local publication name. Prefer one explicit bookmark per publication action.

Credentials, SSH, and signing

jj Git transport normally uses Git’s configured remote URLs and credential mechanisms. SSH keys, agents, known-host verification, credential helpers, and HTTPS tokens remain deployment concerns; do not put secrets in jj config committed to a repository. Test authentication with a read-only fetch before a push. A remote URL can be inspected with jj git remote list or native Git configuration, but redact credentials from logs.

Signing is a property of commits or transport policy, not a synonym for a jj change ID. If the target version supports jj sign and jj unsign, configure the signing key and inspect the resulting signature fields through templates or Git verification. A rewrite after signing produces a new commit and generally requires signing the successor again. Gerrit and GitHub may apply additional server-side verification; local signing does not guarantee acceptance.

Native Git tooling: a safe contract

Use Git for tools that have no jj equivalent—gh, hooks, server-specific commands, object inspection, and specialised diff tools—but establish a boundary:

  1. Before a Git-side mutation, inspect jj’s current operation and workspace state.
  2. Make one class of mutation at a time: files, Git refs, or jj graph.
  3. After changing refs with Git, run the jj import/refresh path and inspect remote/local bookmarks.
  4. After changing graph state with jj, export/import as required and check what Git sees.
  5. Do not run two writers against the same working directory concurrently.

For GitHub, gh pr view and gh pr merge operate on server state and may fetch or inspect refs; they do not understand jj’s operation DAG. For Gerrit, server review commands and refs/for/... transport semantics are likewise outside jj’s local graph model. Keep the local bookmark that a host consumes explicit and verify it after every host-side action.

Part VIII — GitHub workflows

Branchless feature and pull request

main@origin
     |
     A
     |
     B
     |
     C
     |
     @

Fetch main, create or edit a child, and work without a local publication bookmark. Your stack is visible by graph and revset. When the change is ready, create a bookmark only for publication:

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin
# edit files; let jj snapshot
jj describe -m 'Add bounded cache'
jj bookmark create cache-pr --revision @
jj git push --bookmark cache-pr --remote origin

GitHub sees a branch ref because Git transport requires one; locally, the ref is disposable. After the pull request merges, fetch, move/edit onto updated main as appropriate, and jj bookmark forget cache-pr or delete it after confirming the server state.

Stacks and review

For one PR use one change. For stacked PRs, publish one bookmark per review boundary, each pointing at a stack tip:

jj bookmark create pr-base -r A
jj bookmark create pr-feature -r B
jj git push --bookmark pr-base --bookmark pr-feature --remote origin
jj rebase -b @ -o main@origin

Rewriting A automatically rebases B and its descendants. Move the publication bookmarks after the rewrite and push with the server's expected force-with-lease semantics. GitHub's UI merge operation is a server-side ref/graph action; jj learns its result on fetch.

gh pr create --head cache-pr, gh pr view, and gh pr checkout can coexist with jj. Treat gh pr checkout as a Git-oriented worktree/ref operation and inspect the result with jj status and jj git import. Fork remotes need explicit names: origin may be your fork while upstream is the source; qualify remote bookmarks in revsets.

GitHub PR lifecycle: local graph versus publication ref

GitHub consumes Git refs. jj lets the developer organise mutable work without local bookmarks and creates a bookmark only when a server-facing boundary is needed. The lifecycle has two independent timelines:

local jj timeline:  edit → rewrite → rebase → inspect → rewrite
GitHub timeline:              publish ref → review → update ref → merge

A local rewrite can happen many times between two GitHub ref updates.
The remote bookmark is an interface snapshot, not the local working state.

Single-change PR

main@origin -- base -- @
                         unnamed mutable change

publish:
main@origin -- base -- P
                         ▲
                       pr/cache
                       pr/cache@origin
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin -m 'Cache: bound memory use'
# edit files
jj status
jj diff
jj bookmark create pr/cache -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache
gh pr create --head pr/cache --title 'Bound cache memory'

The local bookmark is now a publication name. The working-copy commit can still be amended with jj describe, jj squash, or ordinary filesystem edits. After the next snapshot, the bookmark still points at the old commit until it is moved:

jj describe -m 'Cache: bound memory use and expose metrics'
jj bookmark set pr/cache -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache

Whether the push is a fast-forward depends on the server's previous ref and the rewrite. jj's push safety checks and remote tracking expose the expected old target; do not solve a rejection with an unreviewed force option. Inspect:

jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked
jj log -r 'pr/cache | pr/cache@origin'
jj diff --from 'pr/cache@origin' --to pr/cache

Stacked PRs

main@origin -- A -- B -- C
                   ▲    ▲
                 pr/api pr/metrics

Use one publication bookmark per server review boundary. The base PR's bookmark points to B and the dependent PR's bookmark points to C; the dependent PR should be created with the base PR as its target branch according to GitHub's UI policy.

jj bookmark create pr/api -r B
jj bookmark create pr/metrics -r C
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark 'pr/*'
gh pr create --head pr/api --base main
gh pr create --head pr/metrics --base pr/api

When A or B is rewritten, descendants are rebased locally and both bookmark targets may receive new commit IDs. Update bookmarks deliberately, then push both. A common mistake is moving only the top bookmark, leaving the base PR pointing to a predecessor that no longer represents the intended boundary.

jj edit B
# modify the early API change
jj describe -m 'API: correct quota invariant'
jj log -r 'pr/api | pr/metrics | ::@'
jj bookmark set pr/api -r B
jj bookmark set pr/metrics -r C
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/api --bookmark pr/metrics

Main moves during review

GitHub's main branch can move while local PR refs are under review. Fetch first, then rebase the mutable stack onto the new remote bookmark. In a stack, select the stack roots or use the branch selection that matches the actual graph:

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..pr/metrics)' -o main@origin
jj log -r 'stack(pr/metrics) & conflicts()'
jj resolve
jj bookmark set pr/api -r 'subject(exact:"API: correct quota")'
jj bookmark set pr/metrics -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark 'pr/*'

The exact stack-root expression should be verified in the repository; the important sequence is fetch, inspect, select, rebase, resolve, move publication refs, push. Do not rebase a remote bookmark itself. Remote bookmarks are observations of server refs and are normally immutable boundaries for local mutable work.

Merge, closure, replacement, and cleanup

After GitHub merges, the local repository does not learn the server graph until fetch. Fetch the updated main and decide whether local changes are now ancestors of it:

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'main@origin | pr/cache | ::pr/cache'
jj log -r 'pr/cache ~ ::main@origin'
jj bookmark forget pr/cache
jj bookmark forget --include-remotes 'pr/cache'

Use forget when the local publication name should disappear without asking GitHub to delete the branch. Use bookmark delete followed by an explicit push when the server branch should be removed. If a PR is closed and replaced, keep the old bookmark long enough to compare/refute server state, then forget it and create a new publication name or move the existing one intentionally.

Workflow: fork/upstream topology

jj git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote add upstream git@github.com:org/project.git
jj git fetch --remote upstream
jj new main@upstream
jj bookmark create me/cache -r @
jj git push --remote github --bookmark me/cache

Here main@upstream is the imported source boundary and the local bookmark is pushed to the fork. A local origin default can be unsafe if it changes meaning between clones. Use explicit remote arguments in aliases and qualify both remote bookmarks in revsets.

Native gh/Git interaction

gh pr view and gh pr create can operate against a colocated repository. Commands such as gh pr checkout are Git-oriented and may create/move refs or alter the worktree in ways jj must import. After any command that changes Git refs:

jj git import
jj status
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

Do not allow gh, Git, and jj to write the same worktree concurrently. The host/UI can merge or close a PR without local filesystem changes; fetch is the observation boundary.

Push rejection decision tree

  1. Fetch the named remote and inspect local/remote bookmark targets.
  2. Determine whether the server changed independently or the local bookmark was rewritten.
  3. Compare the intended tree and ancestry with jj diff and jj log.
  4. If the ref should advance normally, rebase or move it to the expected descendant.
  5. If replacement is intentional, use the explicit force/safety option required by local policy and record why.
  6. If the wrong ref was pushed, repair the server ref separately; jj undo only repairs local operation state.

GitHub field laboratory: one local graph, several PR views

A pull request is a server-side interpretation of a Git ref. jj has no obligation to organise local work around that ref. The local graph can therefore be optimised for editing while a publication bookmark exists only at the boundary GitHub needs to see. The cost is that the bookmark is an interface object whose target must be maintained explicitly.

A single PR over time

time 0:       main@origin--A--B(@)       no local bookmark
publish:      main@origin--A--B
                              ▲
                            pr/cache
                            pr/cache@origin
after review: main@origin--A--B'(@)
                              ▲
                            pr/cache      (old until moved)
                            pr/cache@origin (server's last copy)

The distinction between pr/cache and pr/cache@origin is the useful state. The local bookmark can move to B' before the push; the remote bookmark remains the last fetched observation. A log that prints only the local bookmark hides work waiting to be published, while one that prints only the remote bookmark hides the server's lag.

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin -m 'Cache: bound memory use'
# edit, test, and inspect the working-copy commit
jj diff -r @
jj bookmark create pr/cache -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache

GitHub-specific commands can now create the pull request. The server may choose a merge base, report checks, and expose a review URL; those are server facts, not jj graph facts. The local invariant is that the bookmark named in the Git push points to the tree and ancestry intended for review.

When review requests a change, edit the logical change rather than creating a fixup branch:

jj edit 'change_id(PR_CHANGE)'
# edit files
jj status
jj diff
jj describe -m 'Cache: bound memory use and expose metrics'
jj bookmark set pr/cache -r @
jj diff --from pr/cache@origin --to pr/cache
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache

A non-fast-forward refusal is a safety signal. It can mean that the remote advanced, the tracking observation is stale, the local target is not a descendant, or server policy/authentication failed. Fetch and compare before considering any force update. A force option, where supported, is a publication policy decision, not a repair for an unclear graph.

Stacked PRs and review boundaries

main@origin--A--B--C--D(@)
               ▲     ▲
             pr/api  pr/metrics

One bookmark at D can publish one PR containing the stack. Two bookmarks can publish dependent review boundaries: the base bookmark at B and the dependent bookmark at D. The dependent PR's base is a GitHub setting; jj's responsibility is to publish the two intended refs and preserve their ancestry.

IntentLocal publicationServer boundary
One review containing a stackOne bookmark at the tip; push the range it makes reachable.How GitHub displays individual commits and chooses its merge base.
Two dependent reviewsOne bookmark per boundary; update both after rewrites.Set the dependent PR's base to the first PR.
Independent reviewsSeparate roots or bookmarks from one base.Each PR targets trunk rather than another review ref.

When an early member changes, descendants may receive new commit IDs because their parent IDs changed. Their jj change IDs ordinarily remain stable because they are successors, not duplicates. Move every affected publication bookmark deliberately:

jj edit 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
# repair the early patch
jj describe -m 'API: reject negative quota'
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj diff --from pr/api@origin --to pr/api
jj diff --from pr/metrics@origin --to pr/metrics
jj bookmark set pr/api -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj bookmark set pr/metrics -r 'change_id(METRICS_CHANGE)'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/api --bookmark pr/metrics

Do not select a current review target by an old commit ID after a rewrite. Use the current bookmark or change ID when the intention is to follow the logical change, and use evolog to confirm the successor.

Trunk movement, rejection, and cleanup

Upstream main can advance while a PR is under review. A safe update is fetch, inspect, rebase only local mutable roots, resolve stored conflicts, move publication boundaries, and push:

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -G -r 'main@origin | pr/api | pr/metrics | ::pr/metrics'
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..pr/metrics)' -o main@origin
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::pr/metrics'
# resolve, then update each boundary and publish
jj bookmark set pr/api -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj bookmark set pr/metrics -r 'change_id(METRICS_CHANGE)'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/api --bookmark pr/metrics

If another machine published a successor of the same logical change, fetching may expose divergence rather than a simple rejection. Compare the evolutions before choosing a successor, creating a new bookmark, or using the 0.45.x converge command. A force push that merely suppresses the warning discards evidence and possibly another contributor's review.

Forks, gh, and the Git boundary

jj git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote add upstream git@github.com:org/project.git
jj git fetch --remote upstream
jj new main@upstream -m 'Fix parser'
jj bookmark create me/fix -r @
jj git push --remote github --bookmark me/fix

Here the fork is the publication destination and upstream is the source boundary. Qualify remote bookmarks and use explicit --remote in aliases. gh pr view, gh pr create, and gh pr merge operate on server state. gh pr checkout is designed around Git refs and worktrees; after any Git-side ref operation, serialise writers, run jj git import if necessary, and inspect status/bookmarks. Do not let gh and jj mutate one worktree concurrently.

GitHub lifecycle checklist

  1. Fetch the intended source remote.
  2. Develop unnamed mutable work until a review boundary exists.
  3. Use change IDs for local continuity and bookmarks for server interfaces.
  4. Print local and remote bookmark targets before every update.
  5. Update every stack boundary after an early rewrite.
  6. Fetch and compare before force-updating a rejected ref.
  7. After merge or closure, fetch, compare, and clean names deliberately.

Part IX — Gerrit

Gerrit reviews commits as patch sets and uses a message footer such as Change-Id: I... to associate new commit IDs with an existing review. A jj change ID is different: it identifies a jj evolution and is not a Gerrit Change-Id. Preserve Gerrit's footer in descriptions when the server/plugin requires it, and do not infer review identity from the jj change ID.

Jj 0.44 includes the gerrit command family and jj gerrit upload. Gerrit upload support is specialised and should be checked against the installed help and server setup. Git transport still matters: a traditional upload ref is commonly refs/for/main, with topics and push options supplied according to Gerrit policy. The server-specific protocol and UI cannot be fully validated without a Gerrit server; local fixture tests cover only jj's transport plumbing.

Realistic mutable-stack workflow

jj git clone ssh://gerrit.example/project repo
cd repo
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin -m 'API: introduce quota'
# edit; snapshot
jj new -m 'API: wire quota'
# edit; snapshot
jj log -r '::@ & mutable()'
jj gerrit upload --remote origin --remote-branch main

Use the Gerrit command's exact options in your release; the example expresses the workflow, not a universal server invocation. If using Git push directly, the equivalent transport is typically:

git push origin HEAD:refs/for/main

When review requests changes to the first patch, jj edit A, edit or jj squash, then allow automatic descendant rebasing to update B. Re-upload the series. Gerrit associates each patch set by its Change-Id footer, while jj retains change IDs through rewrites. When a review lands, fetch and rebase the remaining mutable stack onto main@origin; abandon obsolete local changes only after inspecting the operation log.

GitHub and Gerrit in one repository

jj git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote add gerrit ssh://gerrit.example/project
jj git fetch --remote github
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@github | main@gerrit'

Use separate publication bookmarks such as github/topic and gerrit/topic where your naming policy permits, or at least use clear prefixes. Conditional configuration can select different push defaults by repository path. Never assume a remote named origin is safe: inspect the full remote URL before pushing.

Gerrit patch-stack lifecycle

Gerrit reviews a sequence of Git commits as patch sets. A new Git commit ID can still update an existing Gerrit review when its message contains the same Gerrit Change-Id footer and the server accepts it. jj's change ID is generated and stored by jj for local evolution; it is not the Gerrit footer, is not a server lookup key, and must not be substituted for one.

jj identity:       change qqq... → qqq... → qqq...
Git identity:      commit 111... → 222... → 333...
Gerrit identity:   Change-Id Iabc... (same review, new patch sets)

A stack:
main@origin -- A -- B -- C
               |    |    |
             I-A  I-B  I-C

Prepare descriptions

Use descriptions that retain Gerrit trailers. A jj description is a complete commit message and may contain multiple trailers. Do not assume jj change_id output is a trailer. If a Gerrit hook or upload command adds a footer, capture and inspect the resulting description before building a stack policy.

jj new main@origin
jj describe -m 'API: introduce quota

The first patch establishes the invariant.

Change-Id: I1111111111111111111111111111111111111111'
jj new -m 'API: wire quota

Change-Id: I2222222222222222222222222222222222222222'
jj new -m 'API: expose metrics

Change-Id: I3333333333333333333333333333333333333333'
jj log -r '::@' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description ++ "\n"'

The footer values above are examples. A real server may reject malformed, duplicated, or policy-forbidden trailers. Whether jj itself inserts a footer, and how it interacts with a Gerrit plugin, must be checked against the installed gerrit upload help and server configuration.

Upload the stack

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The exact commit expression should select the intended stack and no unrelated mutable work. Use --dry-run first. In 0.44, important upload dimensions include remote, remote branch, commit, reviewers, CC, labels, topic, hashtags, message, WIP/ready/private state, notifications, validation, merged state, attention set, deadline, custom options, and repeated --option. Treat options that alter review metadata as server-facing side effects, not ordinary local graph operations.

A direct Git transport alternative is commonly git push gerrit HEAD:refs/for/main, with server-specific push options in the ref suffix or options. That form bypasses jj's Gerrit-aware selection and should be used only when the repository's Gerrit policy is understood.

Review changes an early patch

uploaded:
main -- A_1(I-A) -- B_1(I-B) -- C_1(I-C)

edit A locally:
main -- A_2(I-A) -- B_2(I-B) -- C_2(I-C)
       new Git IDs; same jj changes and Gerrit footers

jj edit 'subject(exact:"API: introduce quota")'
# edit files
jj describe -m 'API: introduce quota

Correct the invariant for zero-budget callers.

Change-Id: I1111111111111111111111111111111111111111'
jj log -r '::@'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The local descendants are automatically rebased over the new A. Their Git commit IDs change because their parent changed, even if their patches are unchanged. Gerrit can recognise each new commit as a new patch set only if the footer/server rules match. jj's change ID is useful for selecting and following the local change; Gerrit's footer is the review association.

Rebase after trunk movement

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@gerrit'
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@gerrit..@)' -o main@gerrit
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj resolve --list
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

If a patch has already landed, the fetched trunk may make one stack member an ancestor of main@gerrit. Remove that landed change from the mutable stack by rebasing the remaining descendants onto the new trunk, not by blindly uploading the old series again. Inspect the graph and review IDs first.

Partial landing and remaining stack

before server submit:  main -- A -- B -- C
after A lands/fetch:   main′-- A(remote)
                                  \
                                   B′ -- C′

The precise local shape depends on the server's submitted commit and fetch mapping. The invariant is that the remaining changes must be rebased onto the fetched trunk that contains the landed work. Their jj change IDs may persist through the local rebase; their Git commit IDs will change; Gerrit Change-Ids remain the server association if preserved.

Conflict in a reviewed stack

A trunk rebase can produce a stored conflict in B′ while C′ remains a descendant. Resolve the conflict in the local change, inspect every descendant, and re-upload the series. If the server rejects a patch set, separate the local graph repair from Gerrit-side review cleanup; changing a local description cannot delete a server review.

jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj resolve --tool meld
jj diff -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main

Topics, reviewers, and options

Review metadata can be attached on upload with reviewer, CC, label, topic, hashtag, WIP/ready, notification, and custom option flags in the target CLI. Make these choices explicit in a publication alias or command; do not assume a topic is encoded by a local bookmark name. A local bookmark is optional for jj's stack, while Gerrit upload needs a selected commit set and remote branch.

Gerrit and GitHub from one repository

jj git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote add gerrit ssh://gerrit.example/project
jj git fetch --remote github
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@github | main@gerrit'
jj bookmark create github/pr-cache -r @
jj git push --remote github --bookmark github/pr-cache
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The same local mutable stack can be published through two distinct mechanisms, but publication boundaries should be explicit. A bookmark intended for GitHub does not control Gerrit upload, and a Gerrit Change-Id footer does not make a GitHub branch a review. Use remote-qualified revsets and explicit remote options.

What is verified and what is not

BehaviourValidation status
jj change IDs survive ordinary local rewritesVerified in local jj fixtures where the rewrite is supported.
Commit IDs change when parents/content/metadata changeVerified with local log/evolution output.
Git remote/ref fetch and push mechanicsVerified with local bare Git remotes.
Gerrit footer remains in a rewritten description if user preserves itLocal message inspection; footer acceptance is server policy.
Gerrit review association, patch-set UI, permissions, submit, topicsServer-dependent; use Gerrit documentation and project policy.

This boundary is important: a technically rigorous local workflow must not pretend that a bare Git remote implements Gerrit's review database.

Gerrit protocol map: local commits and review refs

Gerrit workflows combine three identities: the Git commit ID uploaded over Git transport, jj’s change ID used for local evolution, and Gerrit’s Change-Id footer used by the server to associate patch sets. A fourth name may be involved: the local bookmark or remote bookmark used to reach the commit. Keep all four visible when diagnosing a review.

IdentityWhere it livesWhat survives a normal rewrite?What it is for
Commit IDGit object graph and server patch setNo; new tree/parent/metadata gives a new ID.Exact object, build, fetch, server evidence.
jj change IDjj change evolution metadata/renderingNormally yes, as a successor relation.Local logical change across amendments.
Gerrit Change-IdCommit message footerYes if description is preserved.Server review identity and patch-set association.
Bookmark/refLocal/remote Git ref namespacesOnly if explicitly moved/exported.Reachability and publication.

A review command should print all relevant fields before upload:

jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T '
change_id.short() ++ "\t" ++ commit_id ++ "\t" ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"
'
git log -n 1 --format='%H%n%B'  # inspect Gerrit footer through Git when needed

Use the native Git command only as a cross-check of the exported commit object. The jj description is the source of the commit message in the jj model; a colocated Git view may lag until export/import or normal backend synchronisation occurs.

Upload a stack deliberately

A stack upload should define its base and its upper boundary. A query such as stack(@) is convenient for the current workspace but can include a temporary child or omit a sibling review. For a repeatable review, select the exact stack by bookmark, remote trunk, or a named revset alias and print the result.

jj log -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::' --no-graph \
  -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit \
  --remote-branch main -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

Do not assume that a range’s root is the review base in a graph containing merges. If the stack forks, take the roots and heads explicitly and decide whether Gerrit should receive one linear series or several independent changes. Gerrit’s server interpretation of multiple commits and dependencies is authoritative; jj can prepare the graph but cannot change server review policy.

Review feedback on an ancestor

When an early change is amended, all descendants are candidates for automatic rebasing. This is precisely the operation that makes mutable stacks attractive, but it also means every later review commit may receive a new Git ID. Preserve each descendant’s description and Gerrit footer, inspect conflicts, run tests on the final head, and re-upload the series. If only one review is intended to change, do not accidentally upload an unrelated sibling.

jj edit 'change_id(qxy...)'
# edit and test
jj describe -m 'Core: reject invalid limits

Change-Id: I1234567890abcdef'
jj log -r 'change_id(qxy...)::'
jj log -r 'change_id(qxy...):: & conflicts()'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'change_id(qxy...)::'

If a descendant conflict is not yet understood, stop before upload. The conflict is stored and inspectable, but server-side review cannot generally represent an unresolved jj conflict as a meaningful patch. Resolve at the lowest commit that establishes the intended API, then retest descendants.

Topics, options, and server state

Gerrit topics and upload options are server metadata. A topic can group a series without changing jj graph topology. Reviewers, CCs, labels, hashtags, notification policy, WIP/ready/private state, and custom push options should be treated as part of the upload request, not as commit identity. Record them in a script or checked-in review note if the review must be reproducible.

In 0.45.x, gerrit.default-remote, gerrit.default-remote-branch, and gerrit.review-url can provide repository-specific defaults. The 0.44-compatible form passes remote and branch explicitly. This book does not claim that a particular Gerrit server enables every upload option; server plugins, permissions, and version determine acceptance.

After a change lands

Landing changes on Gerrit changes the remote branch, not the local operation DAG. Fetch, inspect the new remote bookmark, identify the corresponding local logical change, and rebase remaining changes. If the landed commit is not equivalent to the local one—for example, the server applied a submit strategy that changed the resulting commit—use patch/content and message evidence rather than assuming commit IDs match.

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@gerrit'
jj log -r 'remote_bookmarks() & description(substring:"Core")'
jj rebase -s 'roots(remaining_stack)' -d main@gerrit

Once a review is abandoned or replaced, keep a temporary bookmark until the replacement is verified. Then delete local publication names that no longer represent active server refs and use jj git push --deleted only when the deletion is intentional and permitted.

Gerrit stack lifecycle: three identities and two review iterations

Gerrit is unusually well matched to jj's mutable history, but only if the identities are kept separate. A local change ID says which jj evolution is being edited. A commit ID says which exact tree, parents, and metadata are being uploaded. A Gerrit Change-Id: I... footer says which server review should receive a new patch set. The local bookmark or remote bookmark answers how the object is reached. None of these four identifiers is a substitute for another.

jj graph                 exported commit             Gerrit review
A (base)                 1a2...                      -
B (API)                  2b3... + Change-Id: Iapi    API, patch set 1
C (tests)                3c4... + Change-Id: Itest   tests, patch set 1
D (metrics)              4d5... + Change-Id: Imet     metrics, patch set 1

jj change IDs remain stable through ordinary rewrites;
commit IDs change; Gerrit associates the new object by footer.

The footer must remain in the commit description for the association to work. jj does not manufacture a Gerrit review identity from its own change ID. If a description editor removes the footer, the next upload may be interpreted by Gerrit as a new review or be rejected by server policy. This is a protocol boundary, not a jj invariant. Preserve it deliberately, inspect it with Git or a template, and treat server acceptance as documentation- and policy-dependent.

Create and prepare the stack

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj new main@gerrit -m 'API: introduce quota'
# edit implementation
jj new -m 'API: validate quota'
# edit tests
jj new -m 'Metrics: expose quota counters'
# edit metrics
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj diff -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The current working-copy commit is an ordinary mutable stack tip. There need not be a local bookmark. Before upload, place a Gerrit footer in each review description according to the server's configured hook/plugin policy. A server may insert it automatically, but relying on a hook that is absent in a jj-created commit is a source of avoidable uncertainty. The following is illustrative message content; the exact footer value belongs to Gerrit:

API: introduce quota

Change-Id: I0000000000000000000000000000000000000001

Use jj describe or an editor while editing one change at a time. Keep the footer in the body rather than trying to encode it in a bookmark. Confirm every selected commit:

jj log -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::' -T '
change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n" ++
description ++ "\n"

A stack may be uploaded through the specialised command:

jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit \
  --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit \
  --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The dry-run boundary is valuable even when the server does not provide a full preview. It confirms the selected local commits and exposes command-level assumptions before an external side effect. The server decides whether the ref is refs/for/main, which commit messages are accepted, and how reviewers, topics, labels, WIP/ready, notifications, and validation options are interpreted. jj's local responsibility is selecting and exporting the commit set.

Review changes an early patch

before review: main--B--C--D(@)
                         B, C, D uploaded

review says:   B needs a stronger invariant
local edit:    main--B'--C'--D'(@)
               same jj changes, new commit IDs,
               same Gerrit footers if preserved

Edit the logical change by change ID, not by an old commit prefix:

jj edit 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
# edit implementation and tests relevant to B
jj status
jj diff
jj describe -m 'API: reject negative quota

Change-Id: I0000000000000000000000000000000000000001'
jj evolog -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

Because C and D have B as an ancestor, automatic descendant rebasing uses B' as their new parent. Their patches are replayed in the new context. If the replay is clean, C' and D' have new commit IDs but keep their jj change IDs; if context no longer applies, one or more descendants become conflicted commits. The correct next action is not a Git-style rebase continuation. Inspect the stored conflict set, work on the affected change, resolve it, and then inspect the whole stack.

jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj diff -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj resolve
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

After the local stack is coherent, re-upload the same commit set. Gerrit should associate each commit with its footer when the server's rules permit it, generating new patch sets rather than unrelated reviews. That association is a Gerrit server fact; local validation can only prove that the footer survived and that the exported commit message contains the expected bytes.

A lower change lands while the upper stack remains

Suppose B lands first, perhaps after review and submit, while C and D remain under review:

server main:  main--B
local before: main@gerrit--B--C--D(@)
after fetch:  main@gerrit--B'   # server's landed object may differ
local work:   C--D still based on old imported boundary

Fetch to import the server's new main and determine whether the landed change is identical, an amended equivalent, or a different object:

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -G -r 'main@gerrit | roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj diff --from main@gerrit --to 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

Rebase only the remaining mutable changes onto the new remote boundary. Do not rebase the remote bookmark itself and do not assume a commit ID equality is required for semantic equivalence:

jj rebase -b 'roots(main@gerrit..@)' -o main@gerrit
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main \
  --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

The remaining commits may receive new IDs again. Their Gerrit footers should remain constant if descriptions are preserved, so the server can update the existing reviews. If the server's submit strategy created a merge or changed the effective base, inspect the resulting ancestry and choose the new remote bookmark as the authoritative base.

Topics, reviewers, and upload options

Gerrit upload options are a second channel of review metadata. A topic is not a bookmark name. A reviewer flag is not a commit author. A WIP/ready flag is not a jj mutability predicate. Keep these channels explicit:

ChannelLocal jj meaningGerrit meaning
jj change IDEvolution identity used to find a mutable change.No direct server interpretation.
Commit message footerText in the commit description.Review association when server policy recognises Change-Id.
BookmarkOptional local publication name.Git ref/name used to transfer a commit set.
--topic or equivalentUpload option.Server-side topic grouping, if permitted.
--reviewer/--ccUpload option.Server review metadata, permissions permitting.
--label/--hashtagUpload option.Server label/hashtag request, subject to validation.

Pass explicit remote and branch arguments in portable scripts. The 0.45.x configuration surface documents Gerrit defaults such as gerrit.default-remote, gerrit.default-remote-branch, and gerrit.review-url; the 0.44 baseline should use explicit flags when the command supports them. A default reduces typing but increases the importance of inspecting effective configuration before a public upload.

Conflict, abandonment, and replacement

If a conflict appears after trunk movement, resolve the local graph first and re-upload only after the stack's patches are understood. If a review is abandoned on the server, that does not abandon the local jj change. The local change may be retained, renamed, duplicated into a new review with a new footer, or abandoned locally after an operation checkpoint. Choosing among these is a review-lifecycle decision.

# Preserve evidence before replacing a review
jj op log -n 10
jj evolog -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj bookmark create rescue-gerrit -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
# either amend the same footer and re-upload, or intentionally create a
# new change/message and new publication identity
jj duplicate -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)' -o main@gerrit

Do not use jj undo to claim that an already uploaded patch set disappeared. Undo changes local repository state; it cannot retract a Gerrit review. Use Gerrit's abandon/delete/restore controls according to server policy and then fetch to observe the resulting refs. Local and server cleanup are separate operations.

Evidence boundary for Gerrit

ClaimEvidence available in this edition
jj change ID and commit ID have distinct roles.Verified with local rewrite/evolution fixtures.
Automatic descendant rebasing changes descendant commit IDs.Verified with local jj graph experiments.
Commit message footer survives if the user preserves it.Verified by local message inspection.
Git refs and fetch/push mechanics.Verified with local bare Git remotes.
refs/for/main review creation and patch-set association.Documented from Gerrit authoritative documentation; no local Gerrit server.
Reviewer/label/topic permissions and submit strategy.Server-dependent; consult the target Gerrit installation.

Part X — Conflicts and merges

A jj conflict is a first-class, serialisable value in a commit. A rewrite can produce a conflicted successor; descendants can carry that conflict forward. The conflict may be materialised as marker files in the working copy, but the markers are a rendering of stored sides, not the authoritative state.

base -- A -- C
  \       \
   \- B -- merge/rebase result with sides A and B

jj log -r 'conflicts()'
jj resolve --tool meld
jj diff
jj describe -m 'resolved: quota conflict'

Use jj resolve with the configured merge editor/tool, or edit the materialised markers and let the next snapshot record the result. Multi-sided conflicts can have more than two input terms; do not assume a two-column Git index. Configure marker style and merge tools explicitly, and check the result with jj diff plus jj log -r 'conflicts()'.

Because the conflict is stored in the commit, you can continue unrelated work, resolve later, rebase a conflicted stack, split a conflict, or restore selected paths. A merge commit is an intentional multiple-parent commit; it is not the same as a conflict value. Use repeated rebase destinations or jj new A B where the command's syntax creates a merge, then resolve its content.

Conflict lifecycle laboratory

A conflict is a structured value in a commit tree. It has a base and one or more sides for a path; the working copy renders that value as conflict markers so a human or tool can produce a resolved file. The marker file is not the complete representation: a multi-sided conflict can encode more alternatives than a two-way marker layout suggests.

Creation by rebase

base -- A: change line 10 to "red"
  \
   B: change line 10 to "blue"

before moving B onto A:
base -- A
  \
   B

after jj rebase -r B -o A:
base -- A -- B′(conflict)
                  |
                  \- B's change is preserved as a stored conflict value

The command has completed: B′ exists and can be selected by its successor/evolution relationship. The conflict is visible to conflicts(), and a current workspace at B′ materialises markers. A descendant C is rebased onto B′ and may itself be conflicted or may carry the conflict value through untouched paths.

jj rebase -r B -o A
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj resolve --list
jj diff
jj resolve --tool meld
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

Resolution rewrites B′ into B″. C receives a successor C′ because its parent changed from B′ to B″, even if C's own file edits were not involved. The operation log contains both the conflict-producing rewrite and the resolving rewrite. If resolution was wrong, undoing the resolution returns to the stored conflict; restoring the earlier operation returns to the prior graph.

Manual markers versus a merge editor

There are two common resolution paths. jj resolve --tool NAME invokes the configured merge tool with the stored sides. Alternatively, edit the materialised file, remove markers, and let jj snapshot the working-copy commit. The latter is convenient for a simple file; the former is preferable when the conflict has binary files, directory/file changes, or a tool-specific merge policy.

# Discover paths before selecting a subset
jj resolve --list -r @
jj resolve --tool :ours -r @ -- 'root-file:"src/quota.rs"'
jj resolve --tool :theirs -r @ -- 'root-file:"docs/design.md"'

The built-in :ours and :theirs choices refer to applicable conflict sides, not universally to “my branch” and “their branch” in a Git branch sense. In a multi-sided conflict, inspect the materialisation and command help before choosing a side. A side choice is a semantic content decision, not a conflict acknowledgement.

Conflict propagation through a stack

A -- B -- C -- D
     ▲
     B′ conflicted after B is rebased

A -- B′(conflict) -- C′(conflict?) -- D′
                         ▲
                         descendants preserve/reapply patches over B′

Propagation is path-sensitive. C′ may not be marked conflicted if its patch does not overlap the unresolved path, but its parent tree still contains a conflict value. A stack can therefore contain a conflicted ancestor even when a descendant's own conflict() is false. Query the ancestry as well as the individual commit:

jj log -r 'conflicts()'
jj log -r 'conflicts()::'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj diff -r 'roots(::@ & conflicts())..@'

Do not use the presence of a marker file as the repository-level conflict test. The filesystem may be incomplete, the current workspace may point elsewhere, or markers may have been edited without a snapshot. The stored commit and conflicts() are the authoritative query.

Merge commits and conflict values

A merge commit has multiple parents; a conflict value is a tree entry whose content has multiple terms. A merge can be conflict-free, and a single-parent rebase can produce a conflicted commit. Conversely, resolving a conflict does not remove a merge commit's multiple-parent topology. Use merges() and conflicts() as separate predicates.

jj new A B -m 'Merge API and documentation'
jj log -r 'merges()'
jj log -r 'merges() & conflicts()'

The merge's diff is calculated against the automatic merge of its parents. If the parents disagree, resolving the tree content does not make the graph linear. Preserve the merge when it expresses integration history; use rebase/parallelize when the graph should express a stack instead.

Rewriting while conflicted

Because the conflict is stored, ordinary graph operations remain available. You can rebase a conflicted stack onto a new trunk, split the conflicted commit, restore one path from a known-good commit, or continue unrelated work in another workspace. Each operation may create a new conflict state; inspect the path-level result.

jj edit 'conflicts() & heads(::@)'
jj restore --from main@origin --into @ -- 'root-file:"Cargo.toml"'
jj split --interactive -r @
jj rebase -s @ -o main@origin
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

There is no mandatory conflict queue. The useful discipline is to make conflict state visible in the daily log, keep the operation that produced it, and resolve at a deliberate graph boundary rather than accidentally committing marker text as ordinary content.

Conflict checklist

  1. Identify the commit and paths with resolve --list and conflicts().
  2. Determine whether the conflict is in an ancestor and therefore affects the whole stack.
  3. Inspect the graph and the automatic merge base; do not infer sides from bookmark names.
  4. Choose tool, side, or manual edit; record the policy when a review is involved.
  5. Snapshot/resolve, then inspect diff, conflict predicate, descendants, and operation.
  6. Only publish after the selected publication bookmark points to the resolved successor.

Conflict lifecycle laboratory: from graph result to resolved tree

A jj conflict is a value in a commit, not a paused rebase session. That difference changes the work sequence. A rewrite can finish by producing a conflicted successor; descendants can be rebased over that value; unrelated changes can be inspected or even edited; and resolution can happen later. There is no hidden index state that must be cleared before the next jj command can make progress.

base--A--B--C(@)              clean stack
       \     B changes same line
rewrite: base--A'--B'--C'(@)  B' carries a conflict
                              C' may carry propagated conflict

The conflict has graph consequences and tree consequences. The graph stores the conflict as part of the commit's tree value. A working copy materialises conflict sides using the configured marker style so an editor can repair files. The materialised files are only the workspace view; the resolved content becomes a new snapshot and therefore a new successor commit. A conflict marker in a file is not, by itself, proof that the commit has a conflict object, and a conflicted commit need not currently be checked out.

Creation and propagation

Conflicts arise when a patch cannot be applied cleanly to its new parent or when multiple parent trees cannot be merged without choosing content. Common producers are rebase, squash, restore, split, and an explicit merge. The producer operation can complete even when the result is conflicted. Automatic descendant rebasing then attempts to apply each descendant patch to the changed context. A descendant that does not touch the affected region may remain clean; one that does may carry a conflict of its own.

jj log -G -r '::@'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & stack(@)'
jj show -r 'conflicts() & ::@' --summary

Query the graph before opening an editor. If B' is conflicted and C' is clean, editing C' first can still be useful for reviewing its independent patch, but a later repair to B' may rebase C' again. If the conflict is at an ancestor, resolving it can change every descendant commit ID even when their content remains semantically identical. Use change IDs to follow the logical changes and operation/evolution logs to account for object churn.

Materialisation and sides

For a two-sided conflict, the workspace contains a marker representation with a base and the competing sides according to the configured conflict marker style. A multi-sided conflict can contain more than two alternatives because repeated rebases and merges preserve more than one side. Treat markers as an input format to a merge editor, not a syntax to resolve by deleting lines at random.

jj log -r @ -T 'conflict ++ \" sides=\" ++ conflicted_files.map(|f| f.conflict_side_count()).join(\",\") ++ \"\\n\"'
jj resolve --list
jj resolve --tool :builtin

The exact resolution command and tool options are release-sensitive; use the installed help for the complete interactive surface. The stable invariant is that a resolver reads the stored conflict and writes a chosen tree. If the resolver leaves a conflict, the resulting commit remains conflicted. Check with jj log -r @ -T 'conflict' and inspect the files rather than assuming the tool's exit status means all conflict objects disappeared.

Manual resolution is a snapshot boundary

A safe manual repair has four checkpoints:

  1. Save the current operation ID and the conflicted change's change ID.
  2. Inspect the conflict sides and the intended semantic result.
  3. Edit files, run focused tests, and snapshot deliberately.
  4. Verify that the change is no longer in conflicts() and review every descendant.
jj op log -n 5
jj log -r '@' -T 'change_id ++ \" \" ++ commit_id ++ \" \" ++ conflict ++ \"\\n\"'
# edit files and test
jj status
jj log -r '@' -T 'change_id ++ \" \" ++ commit_id ++ \" \" ++ conflict ++ \"\\n\"'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj diff -r @

If a test or editor changes files after the initial snapshot, the next jj command may record them. If reproducibility matters, use an explicit snapshot command or run the command that owns the snapshot boundary, then record the operation. Avoid --ignore-working-copy for the verification that is supposed to observe the repair unless you first understand which recorded tree it will read.

Rewrite, squash, split, restore

When squashing into a conflicted or conflict-adjacent change, ask whether the incoming patch is meant to resolve the conflict or merely to add more content. A fileset can restrict the paths moved, but it cannot choose among conflict sides. When splitting a conflicted commit, the conflict may be divided between the resulting changes or remain in both if each patch depends on the unresolved context. Afterward inspect each commit's conflict predicate.

Restore can be used to replace a conflicted path with a known tree version, but it is not a conflict resolver in the semantic sense. It copies content from a source or reverses a selected change. A subsequent snapshot may clear the conflict if all conflict objects are represented by the edited tree; otherwise the conflict remains. Keep a temporary bookmark before a broad restore and use jj diff --from/--to to confirm the chosen side.

jj bookmark create before-resolve -r @
jj restore --from main@origin --into @ -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src"'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj diff --from before-resolve --to @ -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src"'

Merge commits versus conflict results

A merge commit has multiple parents. It can be clean or conflicted. A conflicted single-parent successor created by a rebase is not automatically a merge commit; it records an unresolved patch application against one new parent. Conversely, an explicit jj new P Q creates a merge shape even if the trees happen to agree. Use parent inspection, not conflict status, to distinguish these cases.

jj new A B -m 'Merge independent lines'
jj log -r @ -T 'parents.map(|p| p.commit_id()).join(\" \") ++ \"\\n\"'
jj log -r 'merges()'
jj log -r 'conflicts()'

A merge can be a deliberate review or integration boundary. Keep it only when the project wants that ancestry; otherwise rebase or simplify parents may produce a more linear patch story. A conflict may be resolved in a merge commit by editing the working copy, but the resulting tree must be reviewed as a merge result against both parents.

Validation matrix

StateGraph checkFilesystem checkNext safe action
Stored conflict, not checked outconflicts() & REV selects it.No marker is necessarily in current files.jj edit REV or inspect with show.
Stored conflict, checked outconflict template is true.Markers/materialised sides are present.Resolve or create a new child and leave it for later.
Clean descendant over conflicted ancestorDescendant absent from conflicts().Depends on current workspace.Review whether its patch is still semantically valid.
Resolution snapshotTarget removed from conflict set.Resolved file is materialised.Run focused tests and inspect descendants/evolution.
Unresolved multi-sided valueConflict side count remains greater than one.Marker style may show several blocks.Use a merge tool or resolve sides explicitly.

The most dangerous conflict mistake is treating a clean status as a semantic proof. A descendant can be syntactically clean while its assumptions were invalidated by the ancestor rewrite. Review the patch and tests, not just the marker predicate.

Part XI — Operation log, undo, and recovery

Jj has two graphs:

commit DAG:     root - A - B - C
                         \ rewrite
operation DAG:  op0 - op1 - op2 - op3
                           \ concurrent op

The commit DAG describes source history. The operation DAG describes repository transformations: snapshots, rewrites, imports, ref moves, and recovery. This is richer than a Git reflog, which is primarily a per-ref movement record.

jj op log
jj op show OPERATION
jj op diff --from OP1 --to OP2
jj op restore OPERATION
jj op abandon OPERATION
jj op revert OPERATION
jj undo
jj redo

jj undo reverses the latest operation; redo reapplies the most recently undone operation when available. op restore loads a repository state from the operation DAG. --at-operation OP inspects an old state without snapshotting the current filesystem. Operations created with --no-integrate-operation are not merged into the current operation head until explicitly integrated.

Recovery laboratory

jj log -r 'all()'
jj abandon 'stack-root::'
jj op log
jj undo
# or, after finding OP:
jj op restore OP

After a destructive-looking rewrite, stop running mutating commands, save the operation ID, inspect with jj --at-op OP log -r 'all()', and restore or undo. Operations from independent processes can diverge; jj may load their merge or let you inspect each head. Operations do not automatically synchronise across clones. Commit objects and bookmarks move via Git; operation histories remain local.

Recovery as an experiment, not a panic command

Recovery has two different targets. If the mistake is “the logical change is no longer where I intended”, use the commit graph, bookmarks, and evolution log. If the mistake is “the repository as a whole was transformed incorrectly”, use the operation DAG. A commit ID remembered from before a rewrite can locate a predecessor, but it does not by itself tell you which operation should be restored.

QuestionFirst toolWhy
What did the last command do?jj op showShows repository changes made by one operation.
Where did my change go?jj evolog -r CHANGEFollows one change's predecessors/successors.
What did the repository look like earlier?jj --at-op OP log -r all()Loads a historical repository view without snapshotting current files.
Undo the immediate mistake?jj undoCreates a reverse operation for the latest integrated operation.
Restore a named state?jj op restore OPMoves local repository state to an operation, with selectable portions.
