Compare two candidate states?jj op diff --from A --to BShows repository changes without guessing from current IDs.
Recover after a server push?Server/ref inspection plus local operation toolsLocal undo cannot undo an external remote mutation.

Exercise 1: rewrite the wrong change

jj log -r 'stack(@)'
jj op log -n 3
# mistakenly select an ancestor:
jj edit A
jj describe -m 'WRONG: temporary experiment'
jj op log -n 3

Do not immediately perform another edit to “put things back”. Record the newest operation ID and inspect it:

jj op show
jj evolog -r A
jj --at-op OP_BEFORE log -r '::@'
jj undo

undo reverses the operation that integrated the mistaken description/edit state. The working-copy filesystem may need a deliberate update, and any external files changed by an editor are outside the operation's protection. Verify the current @, the target change's description, and descendants before continuing.

Exercise 2: abandon useful work, then recover it

jj bookmark create rescue -r @
jj abandon -r 'change_id(important...)'
jj log -r 'visible()'
jj op log
jj evolog -r 'change_id(important...)'

The temporary bookmark is a publication/name safety net, but evolution and operation state are still useful. If the bookmark was deleted by the command, the commit may remain as a predecessor/hidden commit. Inspect the operation immediately and restore or undo before GC. A recovery bookmark can be created at a predecessor only after the exact commit has been selected.

Exercise 3: a bookmark moved to the wrong tip

jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark set review -r wrong-tip
jj op show
jj log -r 'review | review@origin'
jj undo

Undoing the local bookmark operation does not undo a push that already happened. If the wrong tip was pushed, inspect the server's current ref and use an explicit, policy-approved update. If the remote changed independently, tracking state may make the expected old target visible; do not force simply because a local undo makes the local name look right.

Exercise 4: several operations happened before discovery

Undo is intentionally convenient but may be the wrong granularity after later work. Use the operation graph:

jj op log
jj op show OP_BAD
jj op diff --from OP_BEFORE --to OP_BAD
jj --at-op OP_BEFORE log -r 'all()'
jj op restore --what repo OP_BEFORE
jj op log

The restore creates a new operation; it does not delete the old operation node. This gives the repository an auditable “restored to earlier view” event. If remote-tracking state is restored along with repository state, later pushes may compare against old observations; the experimental --what repo form can be preferable when the goal is to repair local graph/bookmarks while retaining current remote observations.

Exercise 5: evolution divergence

jj evolog -r 'change_id(abc...)'
jj log -r 'divergent() & change_id(abc...)'
jj show -r 'change_id(abc...)/0'
jj show -r 'change_id(abc...)/1'

Choose the successor by content and graph context, not by offset number. One successor may contain the reviewed change and another a local experiment. You can edit one, rebase it, duplicate it to a new change, or abandon the unwanted successor. Then inspect descendants and bookmarks: a publication name can keep a commit visible even when the logical change has divergent forms.

Exercise 6: operation views are local

Clone A and clone B share commits only after fetch/push. Their operation IDs may both begin with a root operation but are not a shared timeline. A commit recovered through jj op restore on A does not become a recoverable operation on B. To transfer a useful state, create a temporary bookmark, push it, fetch it, and create a new local working-copy commit on B.

# A
jj bookmark create handoff -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff

# B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'handoff@origin'
jj new 'handoff@origin'
jj bookmark forget 'handoff'

Delete the temporary remote bookmark only after verifying the receiving clone. The filesystem's uncommitted edits and local operation history are not transferred by this procedure; commit them deliberately or copy the files through a separate channel.

Recovery stopping rules

  1. Stop mutating the repository when the mistake is noticed.
  2. Record operation ID, current workspace, bookmarks, and relevant commit/change IDs.
  3. Use --at-op for inspection and avoid snapshotting into historical views.
  4. Choose undo for the latest operation; choose restore/diff for a named state.
  5. Do not run GC, abandon recovery evidence, or push until the local graph is understood.
  6. Separate local repair from server repair and record both.

Conflict laboratory: a conflict is stored in the graph

Construct two changes from the same base that edit the same lines, then merge or rebase them. jj can represent the unresolved result as a commit with a conflict value. The commit is still a graph node: it has an ID, a change ID, parents, descendants, and an operation that created it. “Unresolved” describes one value in its tree, not an interrupted global transaction.

        B: timeout = 30
       /
A-----+
       \ C: timeout = 60

merge M has parents B and C:
A--B----+
   \    |--M[conflict]
    C---+

When a conflict is materialised in a working copy, jj writes conflict markers or the configured marker representation into files. Materialisation is a projection of the stored conflict into the filesystem; editing the markers and running jj resolve records a resolved tree in the relevant commit. The conflict value itself can have multiple sides, including nested or repeated conflict terms after successive rewrites. Do not assume every conflict is a two-way “ours/theirs” merge.

jj status
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@'
jj resolve --list
jj resolve --tool=:ours
jj resolve --tool=:theirs
jj resolve --tool=meld

:ours and :theirs are convenient only where their interpretation is unambiguous. With a merge commit, “ours” is associated with the destination/first side and “theirs” with the other side according to the command’s merge model; in a multi-sided conflict, choosing one side may discard content from more than one parent. A real merge tool should expose every side, and the result must be tested rather than judged by marker absence alone.

Conflict propagation through descendants

before rewrite:
A--B--C--D
   B changes config key
   C changes same key as a dependent test
   D changes docs about C

rewrite B → B′:
A--B′--C′[conflict]--D′[conflict or adjusted]

Conflict propagation is why jj can let work continue on a stack while a lower change is unresolved. C′ is a valid commit to inspect, describe, or even further rewrite. Its descendant D′ is likewise present, though its patch may be represented relative to a conflicted parent. Resolve from the lowest semantically understood conflict upward. After resolving C′, inspect D′ because the stored conflict value may have changed even if D′’s own files were untouched.

There are two useful strategies. Resolve immediately when the conflict is small and the intended invariant is clear. Or carry the conflict while investigating the stack, then resolve after related changes reveal the intended final interface. The second strategy is safe only if your tests and publication tooling accept conflicted commits; a push or review upload may reject them, and a build workspace may not be able to materialise a useful tree.

Conflict-aware commands

OperationWhat conflict meansPractical check
RebaseThe patch could not be replayed cleanly onto a new parent.Find the earliest conflicted commit, then inspect its descendants.
SquashCombining trees or patches yields a conflict value.Verify source remainder and destination state before resolving.
SplitA selected portion may carry one side while the residual commit carries another.Inspect both resulting changes and their conflict predicates.
RestoreCopying content from a conflicted source can preserve conflict structure.Use show/diff on the source before materialising.
ResolveReplaces the conflict value with a concrete tree.Run tests and verify conflicts() no longer selects it.
Push/uploadServer transport may reject conflicted commits or represent them poorly.Resolve before publication unless the server workflow explicitly supports them.

Operation laboratory: undo is a graph operation

Every mutation creates an operation record describing a repository transformation. The operation log is a history of repository views, not a file-level reflog. A single operation may rewrite many commit objects, move bookmarks, update workspace records, and create conflicts. Consequently, recovery should begin by identifying the operation, not by guessing which commit ID “used to be there”.

jj op log
jj op show OP
jj op diff OP
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'all()'
jj --at-operation OP status

jj undo normally reverses the most recent integrated operation. It is convenient when the mistake is recent and unambiguous. jj op restore moves the repository view to a selected historical operation and is more surgical, but it can discard later integrated state from the current view. jj op revert, where available, creates an operation that reverses a selected historical operation while retaining a new place in operation history. jj op abandon removes operation records according to its own semantics; use it only after checking that the records are no longer required for recovery.

Exercise 1: the wrong ancestor rewrite

main@origin--A--B--C--D(@)
                  B = reviewed API
                  C = tests
                  D = docs
  1. Record the operation head and create a temporary safety bookmark at D.
  2. Edit B and make an intentionally wrong change to its description or tree.
  3. Observe the new IDs of B, C, and D, and inspect the operation entry.
  4. Use jj undo if this is the latest operation.
  5. Compare the restored graph with the safety bookmark and the earlier operation view.
jj bookmark create recovery-before -r @
before=$(jj op log --no-graph -T 'id.short() ++ "\n"' | head -n 1)
jj edit B
jj describe -m "WRONG"
jj op show
jj undo
jj log -r 'stack(@)'
jj bookmark delete recovery-before

The temporary bookmark is not needed for undo, but it gives a graph-level assertion. If the command sequence included an editor failure, an automatic snapshot, or an unrelated operation, the latest operation may not be the one you intended. In that case inspect operation IDs and use --at-operation before selecting a restore/revert strategy.

Exercise 2: abandon useful work, then recover it

jj bookmark create rescue -r @
jj abandon @
jj op log
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'rescue'
jj op restore OP

The exact placeholder OP must be replaced with an ID from the operation log. The example demonstrates a discipline rather than a memorised recovery command: make the lost object nameable, identify the operation that removed it, inspect the historical view, and only then restore or recreate the desired state. In real work, a change’s evolution log may be more informative than a commit prefix because the current commit is a successor of the abandoned predecessor.

Operation views and concurrency

Two processes can start from one integrated operation and produce independent operations. A process using --no-integrate-operation may leave its result outside the caller’s current operation head. An operation integration command can combine compatible histories, but integration is not an assertion that two semantic changes are conflict-free. Always inspect the resulting commit DAG and operation diff.

          O_1--O_2  (terminal A)
         /
O_0------+
         \
          O_3       (terminal B)

commit store may contain both results.
The current integrated view must be chosen/merged explicitly.

This differs from a Git reflog, where the primary object is usually the movement of a ref. jj’s operation records can include changes that never corresponded to a named ref and can restore an entire repository view. It also differs from a commit DAG: an operation can rewrite a commit into a successor without adding a child in the logical development graph. Use both logs when debugging.

Operation laboratory: repository views, not a reflog list

The commit DAG answers what commits exist and how they are related. The operation DAG answers how this repository view came to be. An operation can snapshot the working copy, create a commit, rewrite an ancestor and all its descendants, move bookmarks, import Git refs, update workspace metadata, or perform several of those actions together. Treating it as a second DAG is the key to reliable recovery.

commit DAG:       A--B--C(@)
                      \
                       D

operation DAG:   O0--O1--O2--O3
                         \   \
                          O4  O5

O2 may have rewritten B→B', C→C' while O4 is an
independent operation started from O2. Commit identity and
operation ancestry are related but not interchangeable.

Views and IDs

An operation ID identifies a repository transformation in this clone. Loading jj --at-operation OP log asks jj to evaluate the commit graph and refs as they were at that operation, while ignoring today's working-copy snapshot. It does not roll back the filesystem. A historical view is therefore an observation tool, not a checkout command.

jj op log -n 20
jj op show OP
jj op diff --from OP1 --to OP2
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'all()'
jj --at-operation OP status

Record operation IDs before a risky rewrite, not only after it. The ID printed by op log may be a short unique prefix; retain the full ID in an incident note when the repository is important. A later operation does not necessarily erase the earlier record, but garbage collection or operation abandonment can reduce recoverability.

Undo, restore, revert, and abandon are different

OperationTargetResultUse
jj undoMost recent integrated operation.Creates a reverse operation.Immediate, unambiguous mistake.
jj redoAvailable undone operation.Reapplies it when redo state exists.Recover from an over-hasty undo.
jj op restore OPNamed historical repository state.Creates a new state matching selected operation data.Return to a known graph/ref state.
jj op revert OPEffect of a selected operation.Creates a reversing operation while remaining in operation history.Reverse a named operation after later history exists.
jj op abandon OPOperation record.Removes/abandons an operation according to local semantics.Maintenance after recovery, not commit deletion.
jj abandon REVSelected commit.Hides/abandons a change and rebases or updates dependants.Declare graph work no longer wanted.

In particular, jj op revert is not jj revert. The former reverses repository-state effects; the latter creates an inverse content change. Likewise, jj op restore does not promise to restore an external push or today's files. If the mistake crossed a network boundary, repair that server separately and then fetch to make the local observation agree.

Recovery exercise: rewrite the wrong ancestor

before: main@origin--A--B--C--D(@)
                      B = reviewed API
                      C = tests
                      D = docs
  1. Create a temporary bookmark at D and record the operation head.
  2. Edit B and make an intentionally wrong metadata or content change.
  3. Observe successors B', C', D' and inspect the operation patch.
  4. Use undo only if the wrong rewrite is the latest integrated operation.
  5. Compare the restored graph with the bookmark and historical operation view.
jj bookmark create recovery-before -r @
jj op log -n 1
jj edit 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj describe -m 'WRONG'
jj op show
jj undo
jj log -G -r 'recovery-before | ::recovery-before'
jj op log -n 5

The temporary bookmark is not needed by undo; it is an independent assertion of the intended tip. If the editor or a status command caused an intervening snapshot, the latest operation may not be the rewrite. Find the operation that changed the target, use --at-operation to inspect it, and decide whether a named restore or an evolution-level repair is safer.

Recovery exercise: abandon useful work, then notice later

jj bookmark create rescue -r @
jj abandon @
jj op log -n 10
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'rescue'
jj evolog -r 'rescue'
jj op show OP

Replace OP with an operation selected from the log. The bookmark makes the commit nameable even if the default visible query no longer shows it. If the abandoned commit has successors, follow the change ID and evolution entries rather than restoring an arbitrary predecessor. The safe order is identify, inspect, name, compare, then restore or recreate.

Concurrency and operation integration

Two processes can start at one operation and produce independent operations. --no-integrate-operation deliberately leaves a successful operation outside the current integrated head and prints an operation ID. It does not make external side effects transactional and does not protect the filesystem from another writer. jj op integrate OP brings that operation into the current operation history; integration may expose divergent repository state or conflicts that were not visible from either isolated view.

jj status --no-integrate-operation
# save the printed operation ID
jj op log
jj op integrate OP
jj op diff --operation OP
jj log -G -r 'all()'

Use this mode when a supervisor wants to control integration or when testing concurrency. It is not a replacement for a filesystem lock. Editors, build tools, Git, jj, and workspaces can still race over files and refs. Serialise mutation of one worktree and use separate workspaces or clones for concurrent tasks.

Correlating evolution and operations

Evolution is change-centric; operations are repository-centric. If a commit has a surprising successor, jj evolog tells you which predecessor and successor belong to that logical change. The operation record tells you what else changed at the same time: descendants, bookmarks, remote-tracking refs, and workspace pointers. A recovery note should record both:

jj evolog -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)' -p
jj op log -n 10
jj op show OP -p
jj op diff --from BEFORE --to AFTER

A change may have several visible successors because two operations independently rewrote it. That is divergence, not an operation-log fork alone. Conversely, an operation can rewrite many changes while every change retains one logical evolution. Do not use the number of operation entries as a count of review changes.

Retention and garbage collection

Recovery depends on data that has not been pruned. Abandoning a commit hides it from ordinary views but may leave it addressable; abandoning an operation removes recovery metadata according to the command's semantics; repository GC can reclaim objects no longer considered reachable. Before maintenance, inspect the operation and evolution history, confirm that published refs and temporary rescue bookmarks are no longer needed, and make an independent backup if policy requires one.

jj op log
jj evolog -r 'all()'
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj util gc

The command's successful exit status is not a guarantee that every recovery path remains available. The operation log is local to a clone and is not transferred by Git push/fetch. A clone on another machine can recover commits that were published or fetched there, but it cannot use this clone's operation ID unless the repository state and operation store are shared through an explicitly supported mechanism.

Recovery decision record

RecordReason
Current operation ID and relevant earlier ID.Defines the repository views being compared.
Current commit/change IDs and bookmark targets.Separates exact objects from logical changes and names.
Whether a remote push/fetch occurred.Local operation tools cannot reverse server side effects.
Whether the filesystem has unrecorded edits.Historical operation views ignore today's working-copy snapshot.
Chosen recovery operation and why.Prevents a second recovery action from erasing the first intention.
Post-recovery log/evolution/ref evidence.Confirms the graph, not only the command's success message.

Part XII — Workspaces and multiple machines

Workspace model in detail

A workspace is jj's persistent association between one directory on disk and one working-copy commit in a repository. It is the unit that answers two questions which Git often answers through a worktree and HEAD: “which files are materialised here?” and “which commit does this directory currently represent?” Every jj repository has a default workspace, and it may have additional named workspaces. The name is a repository-level identity; the directory is its filesystem location; its working-copy commit is the mutable commit that @ resolves to when a command runs from that directory.

A workspace is not a bookmark: it is not a publication name and it does not have to be visible to a remote. It is not a clone: workspaces share the same repository commit store, bookmarks, tags, and operation history. It is also not merely another spelling for a Git branch or detached HEAD. The closest Git comparison is a Git worktree with an explicitly recorded, independently mutable checkout commit, but the comparison breaks because jj workspaces participate in the same mutable DAG and operation system rather than being separate ref-oriented checkouts.

one jj repository

shared commit store, refs, and operation DAG
        |
        +-- workspace "default"  /home/me/project
        |       default@ -> C        files for C
        |
        +-- workspace "review"   /home/me/project-review
                review@  -> R        files for R

@ means the workspace-local working-copy commit;
C and R may be unrelated commits.

Workspace state is therefore local in one sense and shared in another. The directory, its filesystem contents, its current working-copy commit, and its sparse patterns belong to that workspace. The commit objects those commits point to, the repository's bookmarks and tags, and the operation log are shared. A rewrite performed from review can change a commit that default is based on; jj may then need to rebase or mark default's working-copy commit stale. Conversely, moving review's @ does not move default's @.

Additional workspaces are created with jj workspace add DESTINATION. By default the new working-copy commit is created on the same parent set as the current workspace's working-copy commit. --revision R instead makes it a child of the supplied parent commits, with the same graph effect as jj new R; --name NAME controls the workspace symbol. The command creates another materialisation of the shared graph, not another repository. The remaining workspace lifecycle and sparse-pattern options are covered below after the basic state transition is visible.

before: one workspace

        A---B---C
                \
                 default@

jj workspace add --name review ../project-review -r B

after: two workspace-local tips in one graph

        A---B---C
            |   \
            |    default@
            \
             review@

The two directories can now be edited and snapshotted independently. A workspace is not a promise that its filesystem is current at every instant: an editor, build tool, or another jj process can change the directory after the last snapshot. Normal jj commands reconcile the live directory with that workspace's recorded working-copy commit; --ignore-working-copy deliberately reads the recorded state without doing so. Keep the shared graph, workspace record, last recorded tree, and live filesystem distinct.

The 0.44 workspace subcommands are add, forget, list, rename, root, and update-stale. A build workspace can edit a stack while a review workspace remains at a stable commit:

jj workspace add ../project-build -r @
jj workspace add ../project-review -r main@origin
jj workspace list
jj -R ../project-build status
cd ../project-build
jj workspace rename ci
jj workspace update-stale
jj workspace forget review

Each workspace has its own @; commit edits and graph rewrites are shared, so a rewrite can move the commit a second workspace points at. Update stale workspaces deliberately and inspect filesystem state before deleting one. Sparse patterns are workspace working-copy materialisation rules, configured with jj sparse list, set, edit, and reset; they do not change the commit tree.

Machines

Clones have independent operation DAGs and workspace pointers. Fetch/push commits and bookmarks through Git remotes; do not expect @ or jj undo on machine A to exist on machine B. To move unfinished work, publish a temporary bookmark, fetch it on the other clone, edit it there, and delete/forget the temporary publication after the handoff. This is safer and more observable than inventing operation-log synchronisation.

Workspace laboratory: one store, several materialisations

A workspace is a named view over a shared repository, with its own working-copy commit and filesystem. It is not a lightweight branch and it is not an independent clone. The commit store and operation history are shared; the workspace pointer, sparse patterns, and files on disk are local to that workspace. This division makes build/review separation powerful and makes concurrent filesystem writes dangerous.

shared commit store:  main--A--B--C
workspace dev:                  dev@ = C, full tree
workspace build:                build@ = B, sparse src/
workspace review:               review@ = A, full tree

A rewrite of A or B is visible to all workspaces because it changes the shared graph. Each workspace then needs its own update policy. A workspace whose recorded working-copy commit is rewritten may need its checkout record updated; a workspace with ordinary unrecorded filesystem edits is not thereby stale. Ask which workspace owns a file before running a formatter, test generator, or jj command.

Development, build, and review scenario

jj workspace add ../project-build -r @
jj workspace add ../project-review -r main@origin
jj workspace list
cd ../project-build
jj workspace rename ci
jj status
cd ../project-review
jj workspace root
jj log -r @

The rename command takes one new name for the current workspace in 0.44. It does not take an old and new name pair. Use the workspace's own directory or jj -R to select the operating location, then verify the new workspace symbol with jj workspace list. Workspace symbols can be used in revsets, but the filesystem directory remains the authority for which process is writing files.

The build workspace can edit or test a particular change without moving the development workspace's @. It still shares commits, so a rewrite performed from the build workspace can change what the development workspace sees. A review workspace can remain at an immutable remote boundary while the development workspace advances. This is a graph coordination tool, not a promise that test results remain valid after a later rewrite.

Build and review workspace invariants

A build workspace should normally be boring: pin it to an explicit bookmark or immutable commit, use sparse materialisation only when the build system supports it, and avoid making source edits there. If a build tool generates files, decide whether they are tracked inputs, ignored outputs, or disposable files before allowing a snapshot. A review workspace can remain at an imported remote bookmark while development continues elsewhere; its value is a stable observation point, not a publication name.

cd ../project-build
jj edit main@origin
jj status
# run the build; inspect generated files before any snapshot
cd ../project-review
jj log -r '@ | @-'

Do not assume that a workspace at main@origin follows later fetches. The remote bookmark may advance while the workspace's working-copy commit remains the previously selected commit. Update it explicitly, and verify the resulting commit ID before trusting a build result.

Stale workspaces and repair

In jj 0.44, a workspace is stale when its recorded working-copy commit is no longer the current commit for that workspace because another repository operation changed the recorded checkout state. Ordinary edits made by an editor, build tool, or native filesystem command are unrecorded working-copy changes; they do not by themselves make the workspace record stale. A moved directory, missing metadata, or a native Git ref change is a separate location/backend diagnosis, not a synonym for stale state.

Inspect all workspaces before attempting repair:

jj workspace list
jj workspace root
jj -R ../project-build status
jj -R ../project-review status
jj -R ../project-build workspace update-stale

update-stale repairs the stale workspace record/checkout relationship; it is not a general graph repair and it is not a command for incorporating arbitrary filesystem edits. If it reports that the workspace is not stale, leave the files alone and diagnose the live-versus-recorded snapshot instead. If files contain valuable unrecorded edits, snapshot them with an appropriate jj command or copy them aside before an operation that will replace the checkout. If the graph target is wrong, choose an explicit commit with jj edit or jj new in that workspace. If the directory was moved or damaged, repair the filesystem/location problem separately.

Sparse build workspace

Sparse state is per workspace. Use sparse set to specify patterns; sparse edit opens the external editor for the pattern configuration and takes no fileset argument in 0.44.

cd ../project-build
jj sparse set --clear --add src --add Cargo.toml
jj sparse list
test -f src/main.rs
test ! -e docs/guide.md
jj file list -- 'root-prefix-glob:docs'
jj sparse reset

The commit tree still contains docs and tests while the sparse filesystem does not materialise them. A build that runs successfully in a sparse workspace may therefore omit an input. Make the sparse policy part of the build command's evidence and run a full-tree validation before publication. Changing sparse patterns may add/remove files in the filesystem but does not create a content commit in the same sense as editing a tracked file.

Forget and cleanup

Forgetting a workspace removes its workspace metadata; it does not abandon the commits that workspace's @ pointed at. Check for unrecorded files and useful working-copy changes first. A forgotten workspace's commit may still be reachable from bookmarks or the graph, and its operation history remains part of the shared repository until retention removes it.

jj -R ../project-build status
jj -R ../project-build diff
jj workspace forget ci
jj workspace list
jj log -r 'working_copies()'

Clean the filesystem directory only according to the repository's workspace management rules. Do not confuse removing a directory with workspace forget; one is a filesystem action and the other changes repository metadata. If a stale directory is later reused, inspect its workspace identity and root before assuming it represents a fresh checkout.

Two machines and unfinished work

Independent clones have independent operation DAGs and independent workspace pointers. Git transport moves commit objects and refs; it does not move @, sparse patterns, workspace names, or operation IDs. The portable handoff for unfinished work is an explicit temporary bookmark:

# machine A
jj bookmark set handoff/alice -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff/alice

# machine B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'handoff/alice@origin'
jj new 'handoff/alice@origin'
# continue work, then publish the next handoff explicitly

Creating a child on machine B gives that machine its own working-copy commit. The original machine can continue to inspect the remote bookmark, but it must not assume the remote target follows B's new child. After handoff, delete or forget the temporary publication according to whether the server ref should remain available. If both machines edit the same logical change independently, fetching can expose divergent successors; compare evolutions before choosing a winner or converging.

Concurrency rules

SharedWorkspace-localConcurrency implication
Commit store and graph.Working-copy commit pointer.A graph rewrite can affect every workspace's target.
Operation history.Filesystem and sparse patterns.Operation inspection is shared; file writes still need serialisation.
Bookmarks/tags.Current directory and local tools.Ref moves are repository mutations visible to all workspaces.
Remote observations.Editor/build process state.Fetch/import and builds should not race a writer.

A robust multi-workspace routine gives each process one directory, one workspace, and one writer role. Use --ignore-working-copy only for a deliberate repository-view read. It prevents the command from reconciling the directory's files, but it does not make a concurrent filesystem process safe.

Part XIII — Configuration

Configuration is TOML and is layered. Built-in defaults are overridden by user config, repository config, workspace config, command-line --config-file, then command-line --config NAME=VALUE according to the installed precedence rules. On Linux, use XDG locations such as ~/.config/jj/config.toml and supported conf.d directories. Confirm actual paths with jj config path; do not assume a distribution's packaging layout.

jj config list
jj config get user.name
jj config set --user user.name 'A. Engineer'
jj config set --repo ui.diff-formatter ':git'
jj config set --workspace ui.pager 'less -FRX'
jj config unset --user ui.pager
jj config edit --user
jj config gc

The available 0.44 config commands are edit, gc, get, list, path, set, and unset. The exact scope flags should be taken from local help. In both captured releases, JJ_CONFIG selects the user/system TOML file or directory set; it is not an inline TOML string. Use --config for a one-command reproducible override and --config-file to load an additional file.

A maintainable Linux layout

~/.config/jj/
|-- config.toml
\-- conf.d/
    |-- 10-ui.toml
    |-- 20-revsets.toml
    |-- 30-templates.toml
    |-- 40-git.toml
    |-- 50-gerrit.toml
    \-- 60-aliases.toml

Organise by intent, not alphabetically. Important namespaces include user.*; ui.* (editor, pager, colour, timestamps, diff and merge presentation); git.* (backend, remotes, push/fetch, private commits); gerrit.* (including default-remote and default-remote-branch where supported); revsets.*; templates.*; merge-tools.*; fix.tools.*; signing.*; immutable-head settings; aliases; and snapshot/workspace behaviour.

[ui]
default-command = ["log", "-r", "stack(@)"]
paginate = "auto"
diff-editor = "meld"

[revsets]
log = "stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()"

[revset-aliases]
stack(x) = "x:: & mutable()"

[templates]
log = 'builtin_log_compact'

[gerrit]
default-remote = "gerrit"
default-remote-branch = "main"

Use jj util config-schema and the generated config inventory for the exhaustive target-release key surface. Some configuration is security-sensitive: repo/workspace config can be untrusted input in a checked-out repository, so 0.38+ layouts moved it outside the repository. Review effective config before using aliases or external tools.

Conditional configuration is best achieved by separate included files or repository-local policy selected by path, if the target release supports conditional includes. A robust fallback is explicit command aliases: jj git push --remote github --bookmark gh/topic and jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit. Never let a convenient default choose a publication remote silently.

Configuration design: build policy in layers

Configuration is executable policy. A useful configuration says what the repository should do when a human invokes a command, what a script may assume, and which decisions must remain explicit at a publication boundary. Keep the user layer ergonomic, the repository layer reproducible, the workspace layer local, and the command line layer temporary.

LayerPut hereDo not put here
Built-in defaultsFallback revsets, templates, pager, formatter behaviour.Organisation policy; defaults vary by release.
UserName/email, editor, pager, preferred display, personal aliases.Repository-specific remote or review policy.
RepositoryProject revsets, template aliases, fixer/merge-tool definitions, immutable heads.Secrets; assumptions that should apply to every checkout.
WorkspaceLocal default command, sparse/workspace-specific display or tool choice.Policy other contributors must discover implicitly.
Command lineOne-run overrides, CI pinning, explicit remote and output controls.Long-lived aliases; shell secrets embedded in process listings.

On Linux, verify the actual files with jj config path --user, --repo, and --workspace. XDG conventions are useful defaults but packaging can alter paths. JJ_CONFIG selects the user/system TOML file or directory set in the target release; it is not an inline TOML string. --config-file loads additional input, while --config NAME=VALUE applies an inline override.

Progressive setup

# identity first
jj config set --user user.name 'A. Engineer'
jj config set --user user.email 'a@example.test'

# safe display and explicit daily scope
jj config set --user ui.pager 'less -FRX'
jj config set --user revsets.log 'stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()'

# personal aliases
jj config set --user 'revset-aliases.unpublished()' 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
jj config set --user 'command-aliases.stack' 'log -r "stack(@)"'

Use config edit for nested TOML and multi-line values; command-line setting must satisfy both the shell and the TOML parser. The last line is illustrative of a command alias and should be checked against the exact alias syntax in the installed schema/help before adoption. A config test should inspect the effective value rather than assuming the write landed in the desired layer.

UI and output policy

ui.* settings govern editor, pager, colour, timestamps, diff formatter/editor, merge editor, default command, movement policy, and related presentation. Separate human output from machine output: a human default may use a pager, graph, colour, relative dates, and compact descriptions; a script should specify --no-pager --color=never, a bounded revset, and an explicit template.

[ui]
pager = "less -FRX"
paginate = "auto"
color = "auto"
default-command = ["log", "-r", "stack(@)"]
movement.edit = true
diff-editor = "meld"
merge-editor = "meld"

Do not put a default command that mutates state behind a shell prompt. A default log that snapshots is still a mutation in the operation log; if prompt latency and side effects matter, use a prompt-specific command with --ignore-working-copy.

Revset and template policy

[revsets]
log = 'stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()'
status = 'mutable() & ::@'
fix = 'reachable(@, mutable())'
sign = 'bookmarks()'

[revset-aliases]
stack(x) = 'x:: & mutable()'
unpublished() = 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
review-candidates() = 'heads(unpublished() ~ empty() ~ conflicts())'

[templates]
log = 'builtin_log_compact'

[template-aliases]
short-id = 'change_id.shortest(10)'
subject = 'description.first_line()'
state = 'separate(" ", if(conflict, "CONFLICT", ""), if(empty, "EMPTY", ""), bookmarks)'

Aliases are language code and should be reviewed as such. A revset alias affects selection; a template alias affects rendering. Keep names semantically precise. Document whether an alias includes hidden commits, remote refs, empty changes, or conflicts, because those choices determine whether a publication script is safe.

GitHub and Gerrit policy

[git]
push = "github"

[gerrit]
default-remote = "gerrit"
default-remote-branch = "main"
review-url = "https://gerrit.example/review"

[command-aliases]
publish-gh = ["git", "push", "--remote", "github", "--bookmark"]
publish-gerrit = ["gerrit", "upload", "--remote", "gerrit", "--remote-branch", "main"]

Use explicit remote arguments in publication aliases. A default push remote is convenient but dangerous in a repository used for both GitHub and Gerrit. The 0.45.1 documentation includes the dedicated Gerrit keys shown above; for 0.44, use explicit upload options when a key is absent or undocumented. Confirm exact command-alias value shape against jj help config and the schema for the target release.

Immutable heads and repository policy

The immutable-head configuration defines the commits jj protects from rewrite. A sensible project policy commonly includes imported trunk and tags/release boundaries, but the right set depends on whether the repository permits local rebasing over remote bookmarks. Explain the policy as a revset, test it against a fixture graph, and keep an emergency --ignore-immutable procedure separate from normal aliases.

[revset-aliases]
"immutable_heads()" = "main@origin | tags() | root()"

In 0.44 this is the alias that jj itself evaluates when it checks rewrite protection. It is deliberately under [revset-aliases], not a made-up [revsets] key. Verify the effective policy before relying on it:

jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj config get 'revset-aliases."immutable_heads()"'
jj rebase -s @ -d main@origin

The exact key/value syntax is release-sensitive, but the policy has an observable contract: protected commits appear in the query and an ordinary rewrite that would alter them is rejected. An immutable head is not automatically every server ref, and changing the config does not retroactively make a rewrite safe. Always inspect which commits are protected before using --ignore-immutable.

External tools and security

Merge tools, diff editors, fixers, signers, pagers, and command aliases can execute programs. Repository/workspace configuration can therefore alter what a command runs. Review config list and the file paths before operating in an unfamiliar repository. Prefer absolute executable paths or trusted PATH policy in CI, and do not let a checked-out repository silently replace a user's merge tool.

FamilyQuestions before enabling
merge/diff toolsWhat files are passed? Does the tool modify them? What exit status means success?
fix toolsDoes it support modified-line ranges? Is it deterministic? Can it rewrite every selected commit?
signingWhich key/agent is used? Does signing rewrite descendants? Is verification available on CI?
aliasesDoes the alias mutate, publish, or choose a remote implicitly?
pager/editorCan a non-interactive environment hang? Are colour and terminal escapes safe?

Configuration diagnosis

jj config path --user
jj config path --repo
jj config path --workspace
jj config list ui
jj config list git
jj config list gerrit
jj config get revsets.log
jj util config-schema

When behaviour differs between two shells, compare effective config, environment variables, current workspace root, and jj version. When behaviour differs between two repositories, compare repository/workspace config and remote URLs. When behaviour differs between 0.44 and 0.45, diff the captured schemas and command help rather than guessing from a release note.

Configuration reference conventions

Appendix F groups significant keys by user-facing purpose. Each reference row records the key, TOML type, usual scope, effect, default/absence behaviour, security or performance concern, and version note. The machine-generated schema remains useful for detecting newly added keys, but a schema cannot explain that git.push chooses a remote while bookmark tracking chooses a relationship, or that a template alias can shadow a builtin. Those interactions belong in the book.

Configuration laboratory: build a safe Linux configuration

Configuration is executable policy. Build it in layers so that a change in one layer has a visible reason and scope. Start with identity and UI, then add revset/template aliases, then repository transport, then hosting-specific policy.

[user]
name = "Ada Example"
email = "ada@example.org"

[ui]
pager = ["less", "-FRX"]
diff-editor = ":builtin"
merge-editor = "diffconflicts"
default-command = ["log"]

[revsets]
log = "builtin_log()"

[templates]
log = "builtin_log_compact"

Check the actual accepted value shapes with jj util config-schema for the target release; configuration keys and types are version-sensitive. The example illustrates design intent, not a universal drop-in file. Keep the base file free of credentials and repository-specific bookmarks.

Layered files and precedence

On Linux, user configuration normally lives under the XDG configuration directory, with jj’s own directory and optional fragments where supported. Repository and workspace configuration can narrow policy. Command-line --config values are useful for one invocation and for tests; --config-file loads a file without pretending it is the user’s permanent configuration. In 0.45.x, configuration commands also expose a direct --file target for editing a particular file; keep that out of 0.44 scripts.

LayerGood useRisk
Built-in defaultsPortable baseline.Defaults can change between releases.
User configIdentity, editor, pager, personal aliases.Hidden policy affects every repository.
Repository configTrunk, remotes, fixers, project aliases.Trust boundary for cloned repositories.
Workspace configSparse/build/review-specific behaviour.Only one workspace sees the setting.
Command lineTests, temporary overrides, automation.Harder to audit if embedded in aliases.

When debugging a surprising value, use jj config list, jj config get NAME, and jj config path. Record the invocation’s explicit config and environment in automation logs. Avoid aliases that silently add --ignore-immutable, select a remote, or permit an unsafe push.

GitHub and Gerrit conditional intent

Hosting-specific settings should be opt-in. A repository that has both remotes should not infer its publication target from whichever remote happens to be listed first. Use explicit --remote and --bookmark in scripts, and reserve defaults for a repository where the policy is unambiguous.

# GitHub publication
jj git push --remote github --bookmark pr/parser

# Gerrit review upload
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'stack(@)'

For 0.45.x, Gerrit-specific defaults such as gerrit.default-remote, gerrit.default-remote-branch, and gerrit.review-url can reduce repetition. In a shared config, document that those keys are not a guarantee that a GitHub push will use the same remote. Separate command aliases by transport and require a bookmark argument for destructive or public operations.

Aliases as an interface

An alias is a small program in configuration. Name it for its invariant, not its keystrokes. A good read alias prints stable IDs and uses a bounded revset. A risky alias should be explicit about its source, destination, remote, and safety behaviour. Version aliases that use a 0.45-only command or predicate, or gate them with a documented release check.

[aliases]
st = ["log", "-r", "stack(@)", "-T", "builtin_log_compact"]
pub = ["git", "push", "--remote", "github", "--bookmark"]
review = ["gerrit", "upload", "--remote", "gerrit", "--remote-branch", "main"]

Test aliases in a disposable repository. Configuration parsing errors, command aliases that recursively invoke themselves, and aliases whose revsets return multiple commits are all ordinary failure modes. A useful alias is shorter than the reasoning it makes visible; it should not hide the reasoning.

Configuration reference: the keys that shape daily behaviour

The schema is the authoritative machine-readable inventory, but a human reference must describe which decisions each family controls and what it does not control. The target 0.44 schema groups settings as follows; some keys are tables whose contents are repository-specific.

NamespaceImportant keysDecision controlledDoes not control
username, emailAuthor identity defaults.Gerrit Change-Id or cryptographic identity.
operationhostname, usernameOperation-log attribution.Commit author/committer.
uidefault-command, color, paginate, pager, editor, diff-editor, merge-editor, conflict-marker-stylePresentation and interactive tools.Graph semantics and remote ref movement.
fsmonitorbackend, watchmanFilesystem change detection performance.Which files are logically committed.
colorslabel-to-style mappingsTerminal style for semantic labels.Machine-readable output semantics.
diffcolor-words, git, statDiff presentation defaults.Patch ownership.
gitfetch, push, private-commits, sign-on-push, colocate, object-hashBackend/transport and publication defaults.Server branch protection and credentials.
remotesremote tablesRemote URL/name configuration.Which remote a human intended without an explicit flag.
gerritdefault-remote, default-remote-branch, review-urlGerrit upload defaults in 0.45.x.Gerrit server permissions and review identity.
mergehunk-level, same-changeMerge/conflict heuristics.Whether a conflict is conceptually acceptable.
revsetslog, fix, run, sign, short-prefixesDefault selections and display policies.What a command means when an explicit selector is supplied.
snapshotauto-track, auto-update-stale, max-new-file-sizeFilesystem snapshot behaviour.Files already in a commit’s tree.
working-copyeol-conversion, exec-bit-changeWorking-copy representation details.POSIX file permissions outside jj’s snapshot rules.
fixtoolsConfigured fixer programs and file patterns.Whether a fixer is semantically safe for every commit.
merge-toolsnamed tool tablesExternal conflict/diff merge commands.Correctness of the selected merge result.
templateslog, show, op, bookmark, tag, config, file templatesHuman output templates.Underlying graph/ref state.
aliases, revset-aliases, fileset-aliases, template-aliasesnamed commands/expressionsReusable interfaces.Version compatibility unless explicitly managed.

UI, editors, and pagers

ui.editor is used for descriptions and metadata editors; ui.diff-editor handles hunk-level editing; ui.merge-editor handles conflict resolution. They are different contracts. A program that can edit a unified diff is not automatically a three-way merge tool. Configure builtin tools first, then add external tools by name in merge-tools and test them with a disposable conflict.

[ui]
editor = ["nvim", "-f"]
diff-editor = ":builtin"
merge-editor = ":builtin"
pager = ["less", "-FRX"]
paginate = "auto"
color = "auto"
conflict-marker-style = "git"

The 0.45.x documented pager default changes to include K in the non-Windows less flags. If a printed book or CI log must not contain terminal controls, use --no-pager and --color never in the command rather than relying on user config.

Git policy and private commits

git.private-commits is a revset policy for commits that should not be exported/pushed by default. It is not encryption and does not prevent a user from explicitly selecting a private commit. A private commit can still be part of a local descendant chain and can still be rewritten. Use it to express publication policy, then verify the selected push set.

[git]
private-commits = 'description(regex:"(?i)wip|do not publish")'
sign-on-push = true
track-default-bookmark-on-clone = true

Do not combine broad private predicates with a script that later uses --all and assumes the result is public. Print the push preview. Signing on push can produce a new successor or alter exported metadata according to backend behaviour; test it against the server’s verification policy.

Revset defaults

The revsets namespace chooses default commit sets for commands such as log, arrange, fix, run, sign, and simplify-parents. Defaults should be readable and bounded. builtin_log() is usually a better base than copying a release-specific expansion. A default that uses a private alias or a 0.45-only function must be version-gated.

[revsets]
log = 'stack(@)'
fix = 'mutable() & ::@'
run = 'mutable() & ::@'
sign = 'mutable() & ::@'

Always remember that a default is used only when the user omits the relevant option. It does not override an explicit -r. For an operationally important command, include the explicit revset in the script even if the interactive default is convenient.

Fixers and merge tools

Fixer configuration is a table of named tools with command, root, patterns, environment, and working-copy behaviour. A fixer should be deterministic, bounded by a fileset, and tested on conflicted, generated, binary, and sparse trees. Formatters that rewrite every file can accidentally produce enormous successor chains or change line endings.

[fix.tools.rustfmt]
command = ["rustfmt", "--edition", "2024", "--emit", "files"]
patterns = ["glob:**/*.rs"]

[merge-tools.meld]
program = "meld"
edit-args = ["$left", "$right", "-o", "$output"]
merge-args = ["$base", "$left", "$right", "-o", "$output"]

Use the schema to confirm exact key names and shapes for the installed release. The book’s policy is to show the design and test contract, not pretend that every external program uses the same argument protocol.

Conditional configuration

The --when conditional configuration table can select repositories, hostnames, workspaces, commands, platforms, and environments. Use it to separate GitHub and Gerrit defaults only when the condition is more reliable than a human’s memory:

[[--when]]
repositories = ["~/src/github/**"]
[--when.git]
# repository-specific keys in the supported conditional shape

[[--when]]
repositories = ["~/src/gerrit/**"]
[--when.gerrit]
default-remote = "gerrit"
default-remote-branch = "main"

Check the exact TOML shape in local help/schema; conditional configuration has changed across versions and the illustrative table is not a substitute for that check. Explicit command flags remain the clearest safety boundary for publication.

Configuration debugging and migration

jj config path
jj config list --include-defaults
jj config get revsets.log
jj util config-schema > schema.json
jj config gc

When upgrading from 0.44 to 0.45.x, compare schema keys, default templates, default immutable heads, pager defaults, Gerrit keys, and revset functions. Do not run config gc as a migration tool without inspecting what it will remove. Keep a copy of the old config and apply changes in one namespace at a time.

Configuration laboratory: construct policy without losing the layers

Configuration is executable policy. A useful jj configuration says what an interactive command should do by default, what a script must state explicitly, and which repository boundaries must remain protected. It also has a trust boundary: user configuration is personal, repository configuration may be shared, workspace configuration is local, and command-line configuration is ephemeral. The same key can have a different operational consequence at each layer.

Find the effective value before changing it

jj config path
jj config list
jj config get user.name
jj config list --include-defaults
jj config list --include-overridden

Use config get when a script needs the effective scalar value without the formatting used by config list. Use config list when precedence and overridden values matter. The exact scope options belong to the installed command surface; in 0.44, the durable scope is chosen by the command's scope flags and an additional file is loaded with --config-file. In 0.45.x, the config commands add --file PATH targeting, which should be version-gated.

LayerBest usesReview question
Built-inFallback templates, revsets, pager, safety policy.Could a release change this default?
UserName/email, editor, pager, personal aliases.Would another checkout want this?
RepositoryProject aliases, tools, immutable policy, review conventions.Could a checked-out repository influence an unsafe tool?
WorkspaceLocal default command, sparse/build preferences.Does this affect only one working directory?
Command lineCI pinning, explicit remote/output, experiments.Will the value be visible in process arguments?

Do not store credentials or tokens in aliases or command-line values. A process listing, operation description, shell history, or repository config can expose them. Prefer Git's credential/SSH facilities and the host's agent. jj's role is to select the commit/ref operation and invoke the configured transport.

Progressive Linux layout

~/.config/jj/config.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/10-identity.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/20-ui.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/30-revsets.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/40-templates.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/50-remotes.toml
~/.config/jj/conf.d/60-tools.toml

Use jj config path to confirm what the build actually reads. XDG paths are a useful Linux convention, not a substitute for checking the installed binary and distribution patches. If the target release supports a configuration directory, keep fragments ordered and independently parseable. If it does not, concatenate or include them through the supported config mechanism rather than inventing a loader.

[user]
name = "A. Engineer"
email = "a@example.org"

[ui]
editor = ["nvim", "-f"]
pager = ["less", "-FRX"]
paginate = "auto"
color = "auto"
default-command = ["log", "-r", "stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()"]

Start with identity and output, then add selection aliases, then add transport policy. A broken template or alias in the user file can make every repository unusable; add one family at a time and run a disposable command after each change.

Revset and template defaults

[revsets]
log = 'stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()'
status = 'mutable() & ::@'
fix = 'mutable() & ::@'
run = 'mutable() & ::@'
sign = 'mutable() & ::@'

[revset-aliases]
'stack(tip)' = 'roots(trunk()..tip)::'
'unpublished(tip)' = 'tip ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
'reviewable(tip)' = 'heads(unpublished(tip) ~ empty() ~ conflicts())'

[template-aliases]
identity = 'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++ commit_id.short(10)'
state = 'separate(" ", if(conflict, "C", ""), if(empty, "E", ""), if(immutable, "I", ""))'

A default revset is invoked only when the command's explicit selector is absent. It is not a security boundary: a user can pass another revset. Keep defaults bounded and name aliases after the set they denote. A template alias changes rendering, not selection. If a default includes remote bookmarks, remember that a fetch changes the result without changing local mutable work.

Immutable heads: the safety setting that must actually work

In 0.44 the rewrite-protection policy is the revset alias immutable_heads(). The correct TOML location is [revset-aliases], with a key whose literal name includes parentheses:

[revset-aliases]
"immutable_heads()" = "main@origin | tags() | root()"

Verify both the value and an attempted protected rewrite in a disposable repository:

jj config get 'revset-aliases."immutable_heads()"'
jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj rebase -s @ -d main@origin

The alias controls the set jj checks; it is not a label and does not make every remote ref immutable. --ignore-immutable disables the check for one operation but does not change the query's value. A repository can therefore be configured with a public boundary that a normal command protects while an emergency command deliberately crosses it. Record why before using that override.

GitHub and Gerrit remote policy

[git]
private-commits = 'description(regex:"(?i)wip|do not publish")'

[gerrit]
# 0.45.x documented defaults; on 0.44 pass flags explicitly.
default-remote = "gerrit"
default-remote-branch = "main"
review-url = "https://gerrit.example.invalid/c/project/+/"

[command-aliases]
push-gh = ["git", "push", "--remote", "github", "--bookmark"]
upload-gerrit = ["gerrit", "upload", "--remote", "gerrit", "--remote-branch", "main"]

Use separate aliases for separate publication protocols. A default remote is a convenience, not a permission boundary. In the 0.44 baseline, a portable Gerrit script should pass --remote and --remote-branch explicitly. In 0.45.x the named defaults can reduce repetition, but explicit arguments remain clearer in a mixed-version fleet.

Conditional configuration is useful when a single clone talks to both hosts, but only if the installed release supports the mechanism being proposed. A conservative design keeps the host choice in the command alias and requires a bookmark argument. Do not infer “Gerrit repository” solely from the presence of a remote named origin, and do not let a GitHub default send a Gerrit review ref.

Editors, merge tools, fixers, and trust

ui.editor, ui.diff-editor, and ui.merge-editor are separate contracts. A diff editor selects changes between two trees; a merge editor resolves conflict sides; a normal editor writes a description or metadata. Configure a builtin tool first, then add an external executable only after testing it in an untrusted/reproducible environment.

[ui]
diff-editor = ":builtin"
merge-editor = ":builtin"

[merge-tools.meld]
program = "meld"
edit-args = ["$left", "$right", "-o", "$output"]
merge-args = ["$base", "$left", "$right", "-o", "$output"]

[fix.tools.rustfmt]
command = ["rustfmt", "--edition", "2024", "--emit", "files"]
patterns = ["glob:**/*.rs"]

External commands can read repository content, environment variables, credentials available to the process, and files outside the repository. Do not accept a repository's tool definition without review. A fixer also has graph scope: a broad default can rewrite many mutable commits. Bound both commits and files, capture operation output, and test no-op, changed, conflicted, generated, binary, and sparse cases.

Snapshot and workspace policy

Snapshot-related keys control when filesystem state is noticed, which files are tracked, and how large or ignored files are handled. Workspace settings control movement and defaults; sparse patterns control materialisation. These are not interchangeable. A snapshot setting can change the working-copy commit; a sparse setting can make a file absent without removing it from the commit tree; a movement setting can choose whether a navigation command edits a child or creates another working-copy commit.

jj config get snapshot.max-new-file-size
jj config get snapshot.auto-track
jj config get ui.movement.edit
jj sparse list
jj workspace list

Before changing snapshot policy in a repository with generated files, establish which files are currently tracked and which are ignored. Before changing workspace movement policy, make a two-workspace test and record each workspace's @. Defaults that feel convenient interact with editor and build processes; document the ownership boundary in the repository's developer notes.

Schema validation and migration

jj util config-schema is the machine-readable authority for types and available keys in the installed release. It does not replace a human reference: a schema can say that a value is an array without explaining why a list of command arguments is safer than a shell string. Use the schema to detect removed/added keys, then test the semantic contract.

jj util config-schema > schema.json
jj config list --include-defaults --include-overridden
diff -u schema-0.44.json schema-0.45.json

Migrate one family at a time. Preserve the old config, run the binary's config parser, inspect effective values, and run a fixture. In particular compare immutable heads, pager flags, remote/tag predicates, Gerrit defaults, aliases, and command defaults between 0.44 and 0.45.x. A successful parse is not proof that a key still has the intended meaning; an unknown key can be accepted as data and simply never be read.

Configuration review checklist

  1. Identify the layer and trust level.
  2. Read the effective value and overridden values.
  3. Check whether the key is a default, a language alias, or a command setting.
  4. Check whether the target release supports its spelling and type.
  5. Keep remote, bookmark, and force decisions explicit at publication boundaries.
  6. Test editors/tools in a disposable repository.
  7. Run a semantic fixture after changing immutable, snapshot, revset, or template policy.

Part XIV — Template language

Jujutsu templates are a small, typed, functional rendering language. They are not format strings with a bag of magic substitutions. A template is parsed, evaluated against a context object, and rendered as labelled output. In jj log the context is a Commit; in jj op log it is an Operation. A keyword such as description is shorthand for a zero-argument method on that context object.

Target surface. This chapter describes the template help captured from jj 0.44.0. The 0.45.1 template help was also captured and compared. Template details are release-sensitive: when building scripts, run jj help -k templates for the installed binary and treat a parse error as a compatibility signal. The examples below use only the 0.44.0 surface unless marked otherwise.

1. The evaluation model

There are three stages:

  1. Parsing: jj parses the expression and checks syntax and many type constraints before walking commits.
  2. Evaluation: the expression is evaluated once for each selected object. A method can return another typed object, a list, an optional value, or a renderable template.
  3. Rendering: the result is converted to terminal output. Colour labels and hyperlinks are metadata around output, not ordinary characters; stringify() deliberately removes those labels.

This explains why a template can fail before printing any commits, why a missing optional value can be tested but not blindly dereferenced, and why parsing JSON with a shell tool is safer when the template emits explicit separators and disables colour.

jj log -r '::@' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r '::@' --color=never -T 'commit_id ++ "\t" ++ change_id ++ "\t" ++ description.first_line().escape_json() ++ "\n"'

In the first command the log command supplies one Commit object per selected commit. In the second, full IDs and a JSON-safe description are emitted with tabs. The graph display is independent of -T; add -G when you explicitly want graph characters in a custom log.

2. Literals, shell quoting, and grammar

Use single- or double-quoted string literals. Double quotes support \\", \\\\, \t, \r, \n, \0, \e, and \xHH. Single-quoted literals have no escape syntax, which makes them convenient for a template containing double quotes and inconvenient for an embedded apostrophe. The shell adds another quoting layer: the outer shell quotes must preserve the inner template exactly.

# Shell single quotes; template double-quoted literals
jj log -T 'change_id.short(12) ++ "\t" ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# Shell double quotes; template single-quoted literals
jj log -T "change_id.short(12) ++ ' ' ++ description.first_line() ++ '\\n'"

Templates use method calls (x.f()), function calls (f(x)), unary operators, arithmetic and comparisons on integers, logical operators, and concatenation. Parenthesise mixed expressions even when the precedence is known; it makes a durable configuration reviewable.

BindingFormsMeaning and consequence
strongestx.f(), f(x)Method/function call. description.first_line() is evaluated before concatenation.
-x, !xInteger negation and Boolean negation.
p:xString-pattern or pattern-alias syntax where a pattern argument is required.
* / %Integer multiplication, division, remainder.
+ -Integer addition/subtraction; do not confuse with template concatenation.
>= > <= <Integer comparison.
== !=Equality for Boolean, byte string, integer, and string-compatible values.
&&Short-circuiting Boolean and.
||Short-circuiting Boolean or.
weakestx ++ yConcatenates template output. It is not integer addition.

For example, if(parents.len() > 1, "merge", "linear") compares an integer and chooses a string. The equivalent-looking parents.len() ++ 1 is not integer addition. Write (parents.len() + 1) for arithmetic, or stringify the result intentionally.

3. Values, conversions, and optional values

The type system matters at boundaries. A String, ByteString, List, and Option can be tested as Boolean. A non-empty string/list and a set option are true; an empty value or unset option is false. A Commit cannot itself be printed, but its methods can produce templates, strings, lists, or other objects.

Type familyCan becomeUse in practice
Boolean, IntegerTemplate/SerializeConditions, arithmetic, counts, JSON.
String, ByteStringTemplate, Stringify, SerializeDescriptions, paths, IDs, JSON-safe output.
List/AnyListBoolean and often TemplateMap/filter/join; empty lists can suppress separators.
Option<T>Boolean; methods of T when setTest optional signatures, remotes, keys, roots before use.
Commit, OperationSerialize but not directly TemplateCall methods; use json() where serialisation is intended.
jj log -r @ -T 'coalesce(description.first_line(), "(no description)") ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'if(signature, signature.status(), "unsigned") ++ "\n"'

try() catches runtime failures in its alternatives but not parse/type errors. coalesce() chooses the first non-empty rendered value, useful for a missing description or absent bookmark. Use if(option, option.method(), "fallback") when the branch itself should be explicit.

4. Global functions

The following functions are the compositional toolkit. A function's argument type is part of its contract; a value that merely prints as text may not be accepted where a parse-time string literal is required.

Function and signatureSemanticsApplication and edge case
fill(width, content)Wraps lines to a display width.Descriptions in show; control sequences require care.
indent(prefix, content)Prefixes non-empty lines.Indent a multi-line description without prefixing blank lines.
pad_start(width, content, [fill])Left-pads a one-line value.Align counts/IDs; newlines are invalid.
pad_end(width, content, [fill])Right-pads a one-line value.Align subjects; use one display-width fill character.
pad_centered(width, content, [fill])Pads both sides; odd remainder goes right.Terminal report headers.
truncate_start(width, content, [ellipsis])Removes leading characters to fit.Keep a path suffix; ellipsis appears only after truncation.
truncate_end(width, content, [ellipsis])Removes trailing characters to fit.Keep subject prefix; do not use on multi-line content.
hash(content: Stringify)Returns a hexadecimal digest.Fingerprint generated output; not a commit identity.
label(label, content)Applies a space-separated colour/style label.Mark conflicts or refs; custom labels need configured style.
hyperlink(url, text, [fallback])Uses OSC 8 when colour is enabled, otherwise fallback/text.Link commit IDs; terminals and PDFs may discard OSC 8.
raw_escape_sequence(content)Preserves escape sequences and strips labels.Terminal control only; visible output breaks wrapping.
replace(pattern, content, lambda)Replaces matches while preserving untouched formatting.Lambda receives RegexCaptures.
stringify(content)Converts to string and removes labels.Useful before hashing or embedding output.
json(value)Serialises a supported value as JSON.Object output; serialised fields are not an eternal API.
if(condition, then, [else])Conditionally evaluates a branch.Guard optional values and suppress empty punctuation.
try(expr, fallback...)Returns the first successful expression.Catches runtime errors, not static type errors.
coalesce(content...)Returns first non-empty rendered content.Defaults for descriptions and refs.
concat(content...)Equivalent to repeated ++.Readable aliases with many fragments.
join(separator, content...)Separates every argument.Use when empty arguments are meaningful.
separate(separator, content...)Separates non-empty arguments only.Optional bookmark/tag/signature fields.
surround(prefix, suffix, content)Wraps non-empty content.Render parentheses only when content exists.
config(name)Looks up config as an option.Extract with as_boolean(), as_integer(), as_string(), or as_string_list().
git_web_url([remote])Best-effort remote-to-HTTPS URL.Defaults to origin; empty on failure; SSH aliases unsupported.
jj log -T 'separate(" ",
  if(conflict, label("conflict", "CONFLICT"), ""),
  if(empty, label("empty", "EMPTY"), ""),
  bookmarks, tags, change_id.shortest(8)
) ++ "\n"'

separate is preferable to concatenating spaces inside each branch. The rendered value of bookmarks or tags can be empty, so output remains stable when a commit has no names.

5. Strings, bytes, and patterns

String is UTF-8 text; ByteString preserves arbitrary bytes and is used for some repository paths or raw content. Both provide len, contains, match, prefix/suffix tests and removal, trimming, substring, first-line, line splitting, splitting by a string pattern, replacement, upper/lower conversion. String indices are UTF-8 byte offsets, not Unicode scalar indexes. Do not use byte slicing as a human-character counter.

jj log -T 'description.first_line().substr(0, 48) ++ "\n"'
jj log -T 'description.lines().filter(|line| line.contains("TODO")).join("\n") ++ "\n"'
jj log -T 'description.replace("API", "Interface") ++ "\n"'

A bare string pattern literal is case-sensitive substring matching. Explicit pattern kinds make intent clearer. A pattern argument is normally written directly at the call site rather than stored as a dynamic value. String.escape_json() is useful for a field; json(value) is useful for a serialisable object.

6. Lists and lambdas

Lists are where templates become a language rather than a pretty-printer. The target surface provides len(), join(), filter(), map(), any(), all(), first(), last(), get(), reverse(), skip(), and take(). Lambda parameters are typed by the list being traversed.

jj log -r 'merges()' -T 'parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(", ") ++ "\n"'
jj log -T 'trailers.filter(|t| t.key() == "Change-Id").map(|t| t.value()).join(",") ++ "\n"'
jj log -T 'bookmarks.take(3).join(", ") ++ "\n"'

first(), last(), and get() fail on an empty/out-of-range list. Wrap them in try when emptiness is normal. map returns an AnyList; it can usually be joined, but is not a typed list with every original method.

7. Commit context and identity

In a commit template, all zero-argument methods of Commit are available as keywords. The following table is the core of a useful daily log.

Keyword/methodResultMeaning and trap
commit_id, change_idID objectsUse .short(), .shortest(), or full rendering. Rewrites change the former, normally not the latter.
description()StringUsually has a trailing newline when non-blank; use first_line() for one-line output.
trailers()List<Trailer>Preserves order and duplicate keys; do not assume one Change-Id.
parents()List<Commit>More than one parent means a merge; parent order can matter in display.
author(), committer()SignatureAuthor and committer can differ; each has name, email, timestamp.
signature()Option<CryptographicSignature>Presence is cheap; status/key/display can verify cryptography.
mine()BooleanMatches author's email against current user.
working_copies(), current_working_copy()List/ref and BooleanDistinguishes any workspace at this commit from this workspace's @.
bookmarks(), local_bookmarks(), remote_bookmarks()List<CommitRef>Use the specific method when tracked-remote presentation matters.
tags(), local_tags(), remote_tags()List<CommitRef>Tags have the analogous tracked-remote display rule.
divergent(), hidden(), immutable(), conflict(), empty(), root()BooleanState predicates. The template method is conflict(); the revset function is conflicts().
change_offset()Option<Integer>Useful for divergent evolution display; may be absent.
contained_in(revset)BooleanArgument must be a parse-time literal such as "mutable()".
diff([files]), files([files])TreeDiff/List<TreeEntry>TreeDiff itself is not printable; call summary(), stat(), or git().
conflicted_files()List<TreeEntry>Useful for conflict path/count reports.

CommitId and ChangeId both offer short([len]) and shortest([min_len]). The shortest form returns a ShortestIdPrefix with prefix() and rest(). Change IDs have normal_hex() for conventional hexadecimal rather than jj's canonical reversed alphabet.

jj log -T 'change_id.shortest(8).prefix() ++
  label("dim", change_id.shortest(8).rest()) ++ " " ++
  commit_id.short(10) ++ "\n"'

Short IDs are a display convenience, not an interchange format. A short prefix can become ambiguous after another object enters the repository.

8. References, tracking, and publication state

CommitRef provides name(), an optional remote(), presence/conflict tests, normal/added/removed targets, tracking tests and ahead/behind hints, and synced(). This lets a log distinguish a local publication bookmark from the remote state it tracks:

jj log -T 'bookmarks.map(|b|
  separate("",
    if(b.remote(), "@" ++ b.remote(), ""),
    b.name(),
    if(b.conflict(), "!", ""),
    if(b.tracked() && !b.synced(), "*", "")
  )
).join(" ") ++ "\n"'

The ! and * are presentation choices; they do not alter refs. A conflicted ref has multiple target sets, so normal_target() may be unset and added_targets()/removed_targets() are safer forensic fields.

9. Signatures, timestamps, paths, and diffs

A Signature has name(), email(), and timestamp(). Email values support local() and domain(). A timestamp supports ago(), format(strftime), utc(), local(), after(date), before(date), and since(timestamp). A timestamp range has start(), end(), and duration().

jj log -T 'author.name() ++ " <" ++ author.email() ++ "> " ++
  author.timestamp().local().format("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M %Z") ++ "\n"'
jj log -T 'committer.timestamp().ago() ++ " " ++
  author.timestamp().since(committer.timestamp()).duration() ++ "\n"'

For reproducible machine output prefer UTC and an explicit format. Signature verification is separate: testing signature() for presence is cheap, but status(), key(), and display() can invoke GPG or SSH tooling.

RepoPath offers absolute(), display(), and optional parent(). FsPath offers absolute() and relative(). Preserve repository-relative paths when output is consumed by another jj command; display paths are for humans and can depend on the current directory.

A TreeDiff can render summary(), a Git-style diff with git([context]), a colour-word diff with color_words([context]), or statistics with stat([width], [max_bar_width]). Its files() entries have path, status, status character, source, target, and display path. A DiffStats has per-file entries and total added/removed counts.

jj show -r @ -T 'description ++
  "\nFiles:\n" ++ diff().summary() ++
  "\nStat:\n" ++ diff().stat(72, 24) ++ "\n"'

A template is evaluated for each commit. A large inline diff is consequently expensive. Keep daily logs to summaries and reserve diff().git() for show or a tightly bounded revset.

10. Operation and workspace templates

In jj op log, the context is an Operation. Its methods are current_operation(), description(), id(), attributes(), time(), user(), snapshot(), workspace_name(), root(), and parents(). Operation IDs support short([len]). Compose scalar methods; the operation object cannot be printed directly.

jj op log -T 'id.short(12) ++ " " ++
  if(current_operation, "* ", "  ") ++
  if(snapshot, "snapshot ", "") ++
  workspace_name ++ " " ++ description ++ "\n"'

Keep operation ID, user, workspace, and description visible during recovery. An operation template describes local repository history; it does not synchronise operation IDs to another clone.

WorkspaceRef has name(), target(), and optional root(). The root can be absent for old records or stale/moved paths:

jj log -r 'working_copies()' -T
  'working_copies().map(|w| w.name() ++ ":" ++ w.root()).join(" ") ++ "\n"'

11. Colour labels, styles, and hyperlinks

Jj automatically attaches labels based on command, context, and method names. Discover exact generated labels with --color=debug rather than assuming a stable taxonomy. Use label("name", content) for an explicit semantic label, then configure its style in UI colour configuration.

jj log -T 'label("bookmarked", bookmarks) ++
  if(conflict, label("conflict", "✖"), "") ++ "\n"' --color=debug
