For scripts use --color=never. stringify() strips labels; raw_escape_sequence() bypasses sanitisation and should be limited to terminal control sequences. hyperlink() emits OSC 8 only when colour output is enabled. Keep a visible ID fallback:

jj log -T 'if(git_web_url(),
  hyperlink(git_web_url() ++ "/commit/" ++ commit_id, commit_id.short(10)),
  commit_id.short(10)
) ++ "\n"'

git_web_url() is best-effort and returns an empty string if conversion fails. It defaults to origin and does not resolve SSH host aliases. Terminals, pagers, PDF output, and machine readers may discard OSC 8 metadata.

12. JSON and machine-readable output

Templates can emit JSON, but JSON validity is the author's responsibility when fields are concatenated manually. Use json() for serialisable objects and escape_json() for scalar strings. Emit one record per commit with a newline delimiter and disable colour/pager:

jj log --color=never --no-pager -r '::@' -T
  '{ "commit_id": ' ++ json(commit_id) ++
  ', "change_id": ' ++ json(change_id) ++
  ', "description": ' ++ description.escape_json() ++
  ', "empty": ' ++ json(empty) ++
  ', "conflict": ' ++ json(conflict) ++
  ' }\n'

Use a real JSON parser to consume this output. The serialised representation of underlying jj objects can change across releases even when ordinary human fields remain available. Pin jj and test fixture output if it feeds a build or publication gate.

13. Template aliases

Aliases are configuration-defined expressions in [template-aliases]. They can define keywords, functions, and pattern aliases. Functions can be overloaded by parameter count, but an alias with a builtin name shadows the builtin; avoid such names in shared configuration.

[template-aliases]
short-id = 'change_id.short()'
'format-field(key, value)' = 'key ++ ": " ++ value ++ "\n"'
'json-line' = 'json(commit_id) ++ "\n"'
'pat:x' = 'x ++ "\n'

Alias names may be exposed to completion with documentation:

[template-aliases]
compact-id = { definition = 'change_id.shortest(10)', doc = 'Short unique change ID' }

Keep aliases small and composable. A repository's template policy is easier to review when ID, ref-state, subject, and JSON aliases each have one responsibility. If an alias embeds a revset through contained_in() or a value through config(), document the required scope and release assumptions beside it.

14. Building a daily advanced log

Build a template from stable fields rather than trying to clone every glyph of the builtin log. First create an unambiguous record:

jj log -G -r 'stack(@)' -T
  'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++
   commit_id.short(10) ++ " " ++
   description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

Then add graph-relevant state and publication names:

jj log -G -r 'stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()' -T
  'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++ commit_id.short(10) ++ " " ++
   if(conflict, label("conflict", "CONFLICT "), "") ++
   if(empty, label("empty", "EMPTY "), "") ++
   description.first_line() ++
   separate(" ", bookmarks, tags) ++ "\n"'

Finally add author/date only when the output is being used for review:

jj log -G -r 'stack(@) | bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()' -T
  'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++
   if(current_working_copy(), "@", " ") ++ " " ++
   commit_id.short(10) ++ " " ++
   author.timestamp().ago() ++ " " ++
   description.first_line() ++
   separate(" ", bookmarks, tags) ++ "\n"'

This is a display template, not a new definition of stack(). Keep the revset and template independently configurable so a log can change its search domain without changing its fields.

15. Debugging, failure modes, and performance

SymptomLikely causeRepair
Parse error before outputUnbalanced quote/parenthesis, unsupported method, or invalid operator.Reduce to a literal, add one method at a time, and run installed keyword help.
Type errorArithmetic used where concatenation was intended, or non-printable object emitted.Use + for integers, ++ for templates, and call a scalar method.
Inline runtime errorfirst()/get() on empty list or method on an unset option.Test with if or use try; do not hide parse errors with try.
Columns misalignNewlines, wide Unicode, colour control, or raw escape sequence in padding.Use one-line values with pad_*; keep raw escapes invisible.
Pipeline contains escape codesColour or OSC 8 enabled.Pass --color=never --no-pager and explicit delimiters.
Template is slowLarge diffs, signature verification, URL work, or broad revset.Narrow -r, render summaries, and avoid verification in routine logs.
Works on one machine onlyAlias/config absent or release surface differs.Version-control aliases and run fixture template tests after upgrades.

Debug in layers: jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id', then one method at a time. Test empty descriptions, merge commits, conflicts, unsigned commits, no bookmarks, divergent refs, non-UTF-8 paths, and a commit with multiple workspaces. A large inline diff, cryptographic verification, remote URL conversion, and a broad commit set can each make a template expensive.

16. Design rules

  1. Keep graph selection (-r) separate from rendering (-T).
  2. Use full IDs for interchange and short IDs for human scanning.
  3. Use first_line() for one-line fields; descriptions normally carry a trailing newline.
  4. Use separate and surround for optional punctuation.
  5. Guard optional values and empty lists before calling methods that require an element.
  6. Prefer summaries to full diffs in a multi-commit log.
  7. Disable colour and the pager in automation.
  8. Treat template aliases as code: review, test, document, and version them.
  9. Do not treat a template hash, short ID, or rendered label as repository identity.

Appendix E repeats the type/method inventory as a lookup reference. The official language source used here is the installed help capture in research/help-templates-0.44.0.md, corroborated by the official template documentation. The book paraphrases it and uses original examples.

Template laboratory: from a field to a review dashboard

Templates become manageable when developed as typed expressions. Begin with one value, add a separator, then introduce a conditional or list operation. Do not begin with a full replacement for the builtin log: a syntax error in a large template hides which value or method was wrong.

jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'if(conflict, "CONFLICT ", "") ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

Keywords in a commit template are typed properties. The form commit_id yields an ID object; commit_id.short() invokes its method and yields a shorter rendering. description is a description value with methods such as first_line(). A method call is not interchangeable with a global function call: signature() is not the right spelling in a commit context when the language exposes signature as a keyword. When in doubt, consult the type-specific help and test the smallest expression.

Formatting a record

jj log --no-graph -r 'stack(@)' -T '
commit_id.short() ++ "\t" ++
change_id.short() ++ "\t" ++
if(empty, "E", "") ++
if(conflict, "C", "") ++
if(mutable, "M", "I") ++ "\t" ++
author.email() ++ "\t" ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"
'

This is useful for a terminal report because the output has an explicit delimiter and a stable field order. It is not automatically safe as a TSV format: descriptions and emails can contain tabs or newlines, and labels can introduce escape sequences. For machine consumption, use JSON helpers or an unambiguous encoding policy and test descriptions containing control characters.

Conditional output and optional values

Use if for presence and state, not for string concatenation that can be achieved by a list separator. A conditional should make the empty case intentional:

if(bookmarks, " [" ++ bookmarks ++ "]", "")
if(remote_bookmarks, " <" ++ remote_bookmarks ++ ">", "")
if(conflict, "\n  conflict: " ++ conflict, "")

Some values are optional or collection-valued. Rendering an optional value directly may yield an empty string, while calling a method that requires a concrete object can fail. The try, coalesce, and collection functions are useful for controlling this boundary. Test a template on root, merge, conflicted, empty, signed, unsigned, bookmarked, unbookmarked, and working-copy commits; the ordinary linear non-empty case exercises almost none of the interesting type surface.

Lists, lambdas, and separators

Parents, bookmarks, remote bookmarks, and tags are collections. Use list methods and lambda expressions to transform elements, then use join, separate, or surround to render punctuation once:

parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(", ")
bookmarks.map(|b| b.name()).join(", ")
separate(" ", change_id.short(), author.email(), description.first_line())
surround(" [", "]", bookmarks)

The precise element methods depend on the object type. A list of commit objects is not a list of strings; call the ID or name method before joining. This is the same discipline as a typed programming language: if a method is missing, inspect the element type rather than adding arbitrary string conversion until the output happens to look right.

Commit, operation, and workspace contexts

Log templates run with a commit as the current object. Operation templates run with an operation object, whose fields include operation ID, description, timestamp, snapshot and workspace information as documented in the installed template help. Workspace-oriented commands may expose workspace records. A template written for one context cannot assume that commit keywords exist in another.

# Commit context
jj log -r 'all()' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# Operation context
jj op log -T 'id.short() ++ " " ++ description ++ "\n"'

# Workspace-aware commit output
jj log -r 'working_copies()' -T 'working_copies.map(|w| w.name()).join(",") ++ "\n"'

When designing a monitoring report, choose the context first. A repository health report may need two commands—one for operation history and one for commit state—because forcing both concepts into one template makes the output ambiguous.

Labels, colours, styles, and hyperlinks

Labels are semantic annotations that the UI can style. They are better than embedding raw terminal escape sequences because output policy can then decide whether colour is enabled. Functions such as label, raw_escape_sequence, and hyperlink should be used with awareness of output destinations. A terminal can interpret OSC hyperlinks; a PDF or log file may display the escape bytes or remove them inconsistently.

label("change_id", change_id.short())
label("conflict", if(conflict, "CONFLICT", ""))
hyperlink(git_web_url(), commit_id.short())

Keep machine output unlabelled and use --color never where the command supports it. Use a separate human template for colour and hyperlinks. Labels are also useful in the default log template because the graph renderer and pager can apply consistent styling to bookmarks, remote bookmarks, change IDs, and conflict indicators.

JSON and safe extraction

JSON is a boundary protocol, not a decoration. Quote and escape values with the template’s JSON function rather than hand-building a JSON string. Emit one complete object per record only if the consuming tool expects newline-delimited JSON; otherwise emit an array with deliberate separators. Test quotes, backslashes, Unicode, empty descriptions, and multiline descriptions.

jj log --no-graph -r 'stack(@)' -T '
"{\"commit\":\"" ++ json(commit_id) ++
"\",\"change\":\"" ++ json(change_id) ++
"\",\"subject\":\"" ++ json(description.first_line()) ++ "\"}\n"
'

The exact JSON helper’s return type and escaping behaviour are versioned; the checked-in validation template exercises it against the installed binaries. Do not parse the ordinary jj log graph output in scripts. The graph contains visual layout decisions and can change with labels, topology, and terminal width.

Aliases and composition

Template aliases let a repository define a named rendering function or reusable fragment. Use aliases to give stable names to concepts such as “review marker”, “compact identity”, or “publication refs”. Keep aliases small enough that an error points to one concept. A large alias that embeds a complete log, revset, and colour policy becomes difficult to override.

[template-aliases]
'book-id' = 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id.short()'
'review-mark' = 'if(conflict, "C", "") ++ if(empty, "E", "")'
'publication' = 'separate(" ", bookmarks, remote_bookmarks, tags)'

When an alias accepts parameters, document the parameter types and call syntax in the repository’s configuration comments. A parameterised alias is a small function, not a textual macro: quoting and type checking still apply. Test aliases with a direct -T invocation before making them the default template.

Reconstructing and replacing the default log

The default log is a composition of graph rendering, change identity, description, author/date metadata, ref labels, working-copy state, and conflict/empty markers. Reconstructing it is an educational exercise: first print the commit ID and description, then add change ID, author, refs, parents, state flags, and labels. Compare against builtin_log() or the documented builtin template in the target release. Do not copy an internal builtin into a long-lived config without recording the version it came from.

jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T '
change_id.short() ++ " " ++
commit_id.short() ++ " " ++
if(conflict, "[conflict] ", "") ++
if(empty, "[empty] ", "") ++
description.first_line() ++
if(bookmarks, " " ++ bookmarks, "") ++
"\n"
'

A compact daily template should privilege decisions: which logical change is this, what immutable object is checked out, is it conflicted or empty, and what publication names refer to it? A forensic template should add parents, timestamps, signatures, and remote refs. One template cannot optimise simultaneously for a one-line prompt, code review, and audit export.

Template debugging checklist

  1. Confirm the command’s template context: commit, operation, or workspace.
  2. Test the field without methods.
  3. Test the method on a commit known to provide the value.
  4. Test an empty, merge, conflict, root, and unsigned commit.
  5. Add one operator or function at a time.
  6. Run with --no-pager and colour disabled when comparing output.
  7. Use explicit separators and inspect trailing whitespace.
  8. Pin aliases and rerun validation after upgrading jj.

A failed template is often valuable evidence about the type model. Treat “unknown keyword”, “method not found”, and “expected one value” as distinct errors. The repair is usually to change context, call a method on the correct object, or map a collection—not to add more quoting blindly.

Template language laboratory: types, evaluation, and stable output

The template language deserves the same discipline as a small typed programming language. A keyword has a type, a method returns another type, operators have domains, and evaluation can fail at runtime for an empty option or list. The fact that a template is written inside a shell command adds another language boundary. Develop it incrementally and keep the output contract visible.

Contexts and values

In a commit template, zero-argument methods on the current Commit object are available as keywords. Thus commit_id is shorthand for self.commit_id(). In an operation template the current object is an Operation, so commit keywords such as author do not become available merely because the operation caused a commit. This explains many “unknown keyword” errors: the expression is valid in a different context.

ContextUseful valuesTypical consumer
Commitcommit_id, change_id, description, parents, signatures, refs, state predicates, diff/tree methods.jj log, jj show, jj diff.
Commit evolutionCurrent commit, predecessor list, operation that created it, inter-diff.jj evolog.
OperationOperation ID, description, timestamp, user, snapshot flag, workspace, parents.jj op log, jj op show.
Tree/diffPaths, status, conflict sides, line/file statistics, formatted patch.file, diff, annotation.

Start with a context probe:

jj log -r @ -T 'self ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id ++ "\n"'
jj op log -n 3 -T 'id ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

If a value is not printable directly, call the method that converts it to a display value. An ID object has short() and shortest(); a signature has name(), email(), and timestamp(); a list has map, filter, and join. A template that happens to print through implicit conversion is less explicit than one that states the desired representation.

Operators and evaluation order

Method and function calls bind most tightly. Negation and logical not apply to the next expression. Pattern syntax is used in places that expect a string pattern. Integer arithmetic uses *, /, %, +, and -; comparisons use integer or scalar values; && and || short-circuit; ++ concatenates template values. The difference between + and ++ is not stylistic: the former requires integers while the latter builds rendered output.

1 + 2 * 3
(1 + 2) * 3
if(conflict, "C", "")
commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line()
parents.len() == 2 && parents.first().empty()

Use parentheses when mixing arithmetic, Boolean tests, and concatenation. Short-circuiting is useful for guards, but it is not a substitute for understanding optional values. A method called on the right side of && is not evaluated when the left side is false; the same expression may fail when the guard is removed.

Strings, bytes, and escaping

The language distinguishes String, ByteString, and Template. A string is textual and has methods such as trim, first_line, lines, split, replace, upper, and lower. A byte string has byte-oriented length and slicing, which matters for commit descriptions or paths that are not guaranteed to be valid UTF-8. A template is rendered output and can carry labels. stringify() turns a stringifiable value into a string and strips colour labels; ++ preserves template composition.

description.first_line().trim()
description.lines().take(3).join(" | ")
description.replace(regex:"\\s+", " ")
description.substr(0, 40)
if(description.contains("WIP"), "[wip] ", "")

String literals recognise escaped quote, backslash, tab, carriage return, newline, NUL, escape, and hexadecimal byte forms. The shell parses its own quotes first. For a template containing double quotes, either use a single-quoted shell argument or escape both layers. A command that works interactively can fail in a shell script when the script changes quoting context.

# shell single quotes protect the template's double quotes
jj log --no-pager -T 'description.replace(regex:"\\s+", " ") ++ "\n"'
# a literal newline is a template escape, not a shell line break
jj log -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'

Lists, mapping, and empty collections

Collections are not strings. parents, bookmarks, remote_bookmarks, tags, files, and trailers contain typed elements. join can render elements, while map first transforms each element. filter retains elements satisfying a Boolean lambda; any and all reduce a list to a Boolean. first, last, and get require a valid element and can fail on an empty list or bad index.

parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(", ")
bookmarks.map(|b| b.name()).join(", ")
files.filter(|f| f.conflict()).map(|f| f.path().display()).join(", ")
if(parents.len() > 1, "merge", "linear")
if(bookmarks.any(|b| b.remote()), "remote-ref", "local-only")

To render optional punctuation, prefer separate or surround:

separate(" ", change_id.short(), author.email(), description.first_line())
surround(" [", "]", bookmarks)
surround(" <", ">", remote_bookmarks)
coalesce(description.first_line(), "(no description)")

These functions differ in a way that matters for empty values. join puts separators between all supplied content; separate omits empty content; surround emits both delimiters only for non-empty content; coalesce chooses the first non-empty value. Use them to prevent dangling brackets and spaces in root, empty, or unbookmarked commits.

Commit identity, refs, and review state

commit_id and change_id are different objects. A commit ID's short form is an exact-object display convenience; a change ID's short form represents the logical change. shortest(n) asks for a unique prefix with at least a requested length, while short(n) truncates to a requested display length. Use full IDs in machine output and shortest or short IDs only in human output.

commit_id
commit_id.short(12)
commit_id.shortest(10)
change_id
change_id.normal_hex()
change_id.shortest(10)

Reference collections contain CommitRef values. A ref exposes its name, optional remote, presence, conflict status, normal target, added/removed targets, tracking state, ahead/behind counts, and synced state. Rendering the ref object directly is not the same as rendering its name. The following separates local names, remote names, and tracking status:

bookmarks.map(|b| b.name()).join(", ")
remote_bookmarks.map(|b| b.remote() ++ "/" ++ b.name()).join(", ")
if(bookmarks.any(|b| b.synced()), "synced", "not-synced")
if(bookmarks.any(|b| b.conflict()), "REF-CONFLICT", "")

A conflicted bookmark can have more than one target. Do not call normal_target() without guarding or checking conflict(). A remote bookmark's presence is not proof that the server is current; it is the local observation produced by fetch/import. Templates can display that distinction, but they cannot fetch.

Signatures, timestamps, and diffs

A signature has a name, email, and timestamp. The email object can expose local and domain portions. Timestamps can be rendered as relative age, UTC, local time, or a format string, and can be compared with date-pattern methods. Use UTC for logs that cross machines and relative time for interactive scanning:

author.name() ++ " <" ++ author.email() ++ ">"
author.email().domain()
author.timestamp().utc().format("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ")
committer.timestamp().ago()

Diff values are structured as well as formatted. A tree diff can produce a summary, Git or colour-word output, statistics, or changed entries. A diff-stat entry carries path, status, status character, bytes, added/removed lines, and display path. Prefer structured statistics in dashboards and formatted patches only in human review output:

diff.stat().files().map(|f|
  f.status_char() ++ " " ++ f.path().display()).join("\n")
diff.stat().total_added() ++ " +" ++ diff.stat().total_removed()
diff.summary()

Diff methods may be relatively expensive, especially over a broad commit set. A daily prompt should not compute complete diffs for every visible commit. Build separate “scan”, “review”, and “forensic” templates and use the narrowest one for the task.

Conflicts, signatures, and operations

State methods such as conflict(), empty(), immutable(), hidden(), divergent(), mine(), and root() are Booleans. They are excellent for labels but should not be confused with a server's review state. A cryptographic signature is an option; inspect its status, key, and display string rather than merely printing whether an object exists.

separate(" ",
  if(conflict, "CONFLICT", ""),
  if(empty, "EMPTY", ""),
  if(immutable, "IMMUTABLE", ""),
  if(divergent, "DIVERGENT", ""),
  if(signature, "SIGNED", "UNSIGNED"))
if(signature, signature.status() ++ " " ++ signature.key(), "")

Operation templates have a different identity. An operation ID identifies a repository transformation in this clone. Its description, user, time, snapshot flag, workspace name, and parent operations answer questions that cannot be answered by a commit template:

jj op log -T '
id.short(12) ++ " " ++
if(snapshot, "snapshot ", "") ++
workspace_name ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"

Keep the plus signs out of the actual command when copying this example; they are line-continuation markers in the printed template source only. A production template should be written as one valid expression, for example:

jj op log -T 'id.short(12) ++ " " ++ if(snapshot, "snapshot ", "") ++ workspace_name ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

Formatting, labels, and terminal boundaries

fill wraps lines, indent prefixes non-empty lines, padding functions align single-line values, and truncation functions control width with an optional ellipsis. Padding counts display width rather than merely source bytes where the formatter can determine it; colour labels and raw escape sequences must not be treated as visible text by a custom terminal sequence.

pad_start(12, change_id.short())
pad_end(14, author.name())
truncate_end(60, description.first_line(), "…")
indent("  ", diff.summary())
fill(72, description)

Labels are semantic styling. label("conflict", "CONFLICT") allows the colour configuration to decide presentation, while raw escape sequences bypass sanitisation and can corrupt a file or PDF. Hyperlinks use OSC 8 when colour output is enabled, but a pager, terminal, log file, or PDF may not support them. Use a plain fallback for automation:

label("change_id", change_id.short())
hyperlink(git_web_url(), commit_id.short(), commit_id.short())

Do not put raw escape sequences in JSON or TSV output. Use --color=never and --no-pager for machine consumers. A template can be syntactically correct yet unsafe for a downstream parser because a label or hyperlink introduced control bytes.

JSON and aliases

json(value) serialises a serialisable value; String.escape_json() escapes a scalar string. Use one complete JSON object per output record and test quotes, backslashes, Unicode, empty values, and newlines. Do not parse ordinary graph output as JSON-like text.

jj log --no-graph --color=never -r 'stack(@)' -T '
"{\"commit\":" ++ json(commit_id) ++
",\"change\":" ++ json(change_id) ++
",\"subject\":" ++ json(description.first_line()) ++
"}\n"

The printed example is JSONL: each commit emits one object and one newline. If the consuming interface expects a JSON array, the template must deliberately emit brackets and separators; JSONL is usually simpler for streams. Pin the jj release when this is an API, because serialisation of object values is a versioned language surface.

Template aliases belong under [template-aliases]. Keep them small and typed by convention:

[template-aliases]
identity = 'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++ commit_id.short(10)'
state = 'separate(" ", if(conflict, "C", ""), if(empty, "E", ""), if(immutable, "I", ""))'
refs = 'separate(" ", bookmarks, remote_bookmarks, tags)'
'field(name, value)' = 'name ++ "=" ++ value ++ "\n"'

An alias name can shadow a builtin, so use a project prefix for shared configuration when necessary. A parameterised alias is not textual shell interpolation; its arguments are parsed as template expressions. Test it directly with -T before making it a default. The reference appendix lists the target-version methods; the laboratory approach explains how to combine them without losing type or output discipline.

Part XV — Exhaustive CLI reference for jj 0.44.0

The local binary reports 46 top-level commands and 120 public command paths including subcommands. This section is the reader-facing reference: it presents the important syntax, options, cardinality, state effects, and traps. The complete raw help capture in research/cli-help-0.44.0.txt and generated inventory in research/command-inventory.md remain reproducibility evidence, not a substitute for these entries.

Top-level commands

Command entries and traps

Command pathPurposeKey arguments/options and trap

absorbDistribute changes into mutable ancestors.Review the chosen stack and fileset; content may move farther than one parent.

workspace add/forget/list/rename/root/update-staleMultiple working directories.@ is workspace-local; commit graph is shared.

How to read the command entries

Each entry below is organised around semantics rather than a transcription of help output. “Snapshot” means the normal command boundary at which jj records the current filesystem in the workspace commit. “Rewrite” means new successor commits may be created and descendants may be rebased. “One commit” means a command requires a single resolved commit; a revset can still be syntactically valid and operationally invalid if it returns zero or several commits.

Short options are retained because they are useful at a terminal, but durable aliases and documentation should prefer the long option when it makes the dimension explicit. Global options apply to every entry: -R/--repository, --ignore-working-copy, --no-integrate-operation, --ignore-immutable, --at-operation/--at-op, --debug, --color, --quiet, --no-pager, repeatable --config and --config-file, and help/version options. Their interaction is explained after the command entries.

History, content, and rewrite commands

jj abandon

Purpose and model. Hides the selected commits from the ordinary visible graph and reconnects their children to the abandoned commits' parents. It is a graph operation, not deletion of immutable Git objects. The default commit is @; the argument is a revset and may select several commits.

Syntax/options. jj abandon [REVSETS...]; --retain-bookmarks keeps bookmarks by moving them to parent commits, and --restore-descendants preserves children’s content rather than preserving their patch relative to the changed parents. The latter can prevent a surprising descendant rewrite when the purpose is to discard an ancestor's content.

State effects. jj snapshots first, creates an operation, and may create a new empty working-copy commit if the current @ is abandoned. Descendants normally change because their parent edge and patch context change; conflicts can be produced. Bookmarks pointing at abandoned commits are normally deleted or handled according to the command's policy unless retained. Native Git refs are not the unit of selection, but exported bookmark movement can later affect Git.

jj log -r 'stack(@)'
jj abandon 'change_id(abc...)'
jj op log
jj evolog -r 'change_id(abc...)'

Trap and recovery. Do not use abandon as a private equivalent of “reset branch”: it changes visible graph topology and can rewrite descendants. Inspect the operation immediately; jj undo, jj op restore, or jj evolog can recover the intended change. Immutable commits require --ignore-immutable, which should be treated as an exceptional audit event.

jj absorb

Purpose and model. Distributes the current change's file-level modifications into suitable mutable ancestors. It is an inference-driven stack operation: jj compares the source patch with ancestor changes and moves a hunk to the nearest compatible destination, rather than blindly squashing all content into the parent.

Syntax/options. jj absorb [FILESETS...]; -f/--from REVSET selects the source (default @), -t/--into REVSETS bounds candidate destinations (default mutable(), only ancestors of the source), -i/--interactive chooses parts, and --tool NAME selects the diff editor and implies interactive mode.

State effects. The source and one or more mutable ancestors can be rewritten; descendants of every changed ancestor are rebased. The source may become empty or retain changes that could not be attributed. Change IDs normally follow their logical evolutions; commit IDs change for every rewritten node. A fileset restricts candidate content, not the commit domain.

jj absorb --from @ --into 'stack(@)'
jj absorb --interactive -- 'glob:src/**'
jj log -r 'stack(@) | conflicts()'

Trap. “Earliest compatible” is a semantic choice, not a promise that every hunk goes to the immediate parent. Review the diff and evolution log. Use jj squash when the intended destination is explicit, and jj undo if attribution was wrong.

jj arrange

Purpose and model. Opens the interactive graph arrangement interface for the selected commits. It is intended for visual reordering and abandoning of a graph region; it is not a text editor for commit messages and not a replacement for understanding the resulting parent edges.

Syntax/options. jj arrange [REVSETS...]; the default selection comes from revsets.arrange if no commit is supplied, and -r is the commit alias. There are no command-specific rewrite switches in 0.44. Global repository, immutability, colour, and operation-view options still apply.

State effects. The TUI can create several graph transformations in one operation. Mutable commits and descendants may receive new commit IDs; bookmarks can move or become conflicted according to the resulting graph. Snapshotting occurs at the normal command boundary. Review jj op show after leaving the interface because the visual action is otherwise hard to audit.

jj arrange -r 'stack(@)'
jj op show
jj log -r 'visible_heads() | bookmarks()'

Trap. Use a narrow revset. A broad arrangement selection can expose immutable boundaries or make the intended graph difficult to recognise. For repeatable automation use rebase, parallelize, new --insert-after, or explicit bookmark operations.

jj bisect and jj bisect run

Purpose and model. The parent command is a namespace for commit bisection and requires a subcommand in 0.44. Bisection searches a commit range using a user-defined test rather than moving a Git branch pointer. Candidate checkouts are temporary working copies; the result is a graph commit selected by bisection state and test outcomes.

Syntax/options. jj bisect run --range REVSET [COMMAND] [ARGS...]. The range is required; arguments beginning with a dash should follow --. The run subcommand accepts normal global repository/view options.

State effects. A zero/non-zero test status has the meaning defined by the local bisection help and script contract. Make the predicate deterministic and independent of the developer's primary filesystem. Do not assume a candidate checkout is the current @ or that a discovered commit should automatically become a bookmark.

jj bisect run --range 'good..bad' -- ./test-regression.sh
jj log -r 'bisect(@)'

Trap. A test that mutates files, depends on network state, or returns a status for an unrelated failure invalidates the search. Record the range and command; inspect the result with jj show and jj diff before applying a fix.

jj commit

Purpose and model. Describes the current working-copy change and creates a new empty child on top of it. In jj, the current filesystem is already associated with @; commit is a boundary in the local patch stack, not the act of transferring files into a hidden index.

Syntax/options. jj commit [FILESETS...]; a fileset commits only selected paths, -i/--interactive selects hunks, --tool NAME chooses the diff editor and implies interactive mode, -m/--message supplies a description, and --editor opens the editor even after a message was supplied. Global snapshot controls apply.

State effects. jj snapshots first, updates @ with selected content and metadata, then creates a new empty child working-copy commit. Unselected filesystem changes remain in the new child. The old commit's change identity is preserved by the description operation; the new child receives a new change ID. A bookmark at the old @ does not automatically follow.

jj commit -m 'API: validate quota'
jj commit --interactive -- 'glob:src/**'
jj describe -m 'API: split validation'
jj status

Trap. Do not use jj commit to save an arbitrary ancestor: use jj edit, jj describe, or jj squash according to intent. An accidental child is recoverable with jj undo.

jj describe

Purpose and model. Rewrites the description and selected metadata of one or more commits, defaulting to @. It preserves logical change identity unless metadata is explicitly changed by another command; the commit ID changes because the commit object changes.

Syntax/options. jj describe [REVSETS...]; -m/--message avoids the editor, --stdin reads the description, and --editor forces editing after either. Multiple commits receive the same supplied description. The -r alias selects the commit set.

State effects. This is a metadata rewrite, but descendants can still be rewritten because their parent commit IDs change. The filesystem normally remains the same tree. Bookmarks follow successor graph locations; Git export sees a new Git commit when the changed commit is exported.

jj describe -m 'API: introduce quota limits'
jj describe --stdin -r 'mutable()'
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T 'change_id ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

Trap. Supplying one message to a set is intentional but often wrong for a stack. Use one commit or a per-commit workflow. Use jj metaedit for author/timestamp/signature metadata.

jj diff

Purpose and model. Compares trees and renders a patch. Commit selection and path selection are independent: -r, --from/--to, and positional filesets describe separate domains.

Syntax/options. jj diff [FILESETS...]; -r/--revisions REVSETS shows the total change across a set, -f/--from REVSET and -t/--to REVSET compare endpoints, and no selector defaults to @. Important presentation options include --summary, --stat, --types, --name-only, --git, --color-words, -T/--template, context controls, and external diff controls.

Semantics. A multi-commit set must be connected: a gap such as A|C in a linear chain is not a patch range. A merge commit compares its tree with the automatic merge of its parents. A range expression such as B::D is not the same as selecting two unrelated trees.

jj diff -r @
jj diff --from 'main@origin' --to @ -- 'glob:src/**'
jj diff -r 'B::D' --stat
jj diff -r @ --name-only

Trap. A diff is not proof that a commit is single-parent; merges and conflicts have special tree semantics. For scripts prefer --name-only or controlled templates with colour disabled. Use jj interdiff for successive patch versions.

jj diffedit

Purpose and model. Touches up the content delta of an existing commit through a configured diff editor. It changes the patch while preserving the selected change's logical identity; unlike restore, it is expressed as editing a diff rather than copying an entire tree.

Syntax/options. jj diffedit [FILESETS...]; -r/--revision REVSET edits one commit (default @), --from/--to choose comparison endpoints, --tool NAME chooses the diff editor, and --restore-descendants controls descendant content. Positional filesets leave unmatched paths unchanged.

State effects. The target and descendants can receive successor commit IDs. The filesystem is updated to the resulting working-copy tree when the target is current; conflicts may be stored if the edited patch no longer applies cleanly.

jj diffedit -r A -- 'glob:src/**'
jj diffedit --tool meld -r @
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

Trap. --from/--to here define the diff shown to the editor, not rebase source/destination. Use jj describe for metadata and jj squash for patch movement.

jj duplicate

Purpose and model. Creates a new change whose content is equivalent to a selected commit, placed at chosen parents. It is a copy, not a successor: the new change has a new change ID even if its tree and description match.

Syntax/options. jj duplicate [REVSETS...]; source defaults to @ and accepts -r. Use repeated -o/--onto REVSETS (alias -d/--destination), -A/--insert-after, or -B/--insert-before. Repeated destinations can create a merge.

State effects. The original remains. The duplicate and descendants at the insertion site can be rewritten; move publication bookmarks explicitly. A workspace generally stays at the resulting current location according to normal movement policy.

jj duplicate -r A -o 'main@origin'
jj duplicate -r A -o X -o Y
jj log -r 'A | descendants(A) | mutable()'

Trap. Continue an existing logical patch with jj rebase or jj edit; publish an inverse with jj revert. Use duplicate when starting a new evolution is intentional.

jj edit

Purpose and model. Moves the current workspace's working-copy commit to exactly one existing commit. It is how an advanced user resumes or amends an earlier change; it does not create a bookmark or move another workspace's @.

Syntax/options. jj edit REVSET or -r REVSET; exactly one commit is required. --ignore-working-copy is usually inappropriate because edit is intended to update the filesystem. --at-op is for historical inspection, not continuation.

State effects. jj snapshots the old workspace first, then checks out the selected tree as the new @. The old working-copy commit remains unless abandoned. Filesystem edits made while another change was current become part of the pre-move snapshot.

jj edit 'change_id(abc...)'
jj edit 'bookmark(review)'
jj status
jj new

Trap. “Edit A” is not Git checkout of a branch: A remains mutable and unnamed. Use jj new A to leave A unchanged and create a fresh child.

jj evolog

Purpose and model. Shows the evolution of a logical change: successive commit versions and their predecessor relationships. It answers what happened to one change, not what every repository operation did.

Syntax/options. jj evolog [OPTIONS]; -r/--revisions REVSETS follows changes (default @), -n/--limit bounds entries, --reversed reverses order, -G/--no-graph flattens output, -T/--template renders an evolution entry, and -p/--patch shows an inter-diff.

State effects. Observational, but normal snapshot rules apply unless disabled. It does not restore a commit or move a bookmark. Evolution entries link to operations, allowing a rewrite to be correlated with jj op log.

jj evolog -r 'change_id(abc...)'
jj evolog -p -r @
jj evolog --reversed -n 10

Trap. Evolution is not a ref reflog and does not record every unrelated abandoned change. Use operation history for repository-wide recovery.

jj metaedit

Purpose and model. Rewrites commit metadata without changing file content. It is the precise tool for author, timestamp, description, change ID, and related metadata edits.

Syntax/options. The commit set defaults to @. Important 0.44 options include --update-change-id, -m/--message, --update-author-timestamp, --update-author, --author, --author-timestamp, and committer controls shown by local help. The -r alias selects commits.

State effects. The tree is unchanged, but metadata changes the commit ID. Descendants may be rewritten because their parent ID changes; bookmarks follow successors. --update-change-id intentionally breaks local evolution continuity and should not be used merely because a commit ID changed.

jj metaedit --author 'A. Engineer <a@example.test>' -r @
jj metaedit --update-author-timestamp
jj metaedit --update-change-id -r 'mutable()'

Trap. Author/committer identity, jj change ID, and Gerrit Change-Id are separate. Use jj describe for editor-driven descriptions and jj sign for cryptographic signing.

jj new

Purpose and model. Creates an empty working-copy change with supplied parents. One parent creates a child; multiple parents create a merge. It is the ordinary boundary between patch-stack changes.

Syntax/options. jj new [REVSETS...] defaults to @; -m/--message describes it, --no-edit leaves the workspace where it is, -A/--insert-after inserts after selected commits and rebases their children, and -B/--insert-before inserts before selected commits. -o/--onto is an alias for parent selection in 0.44.

State effects. The old @ becomes a parent and remains intact. Insert modes are graph surgery and can rewrite descendants. Bookmarks at the old commit do not automatically move to the new child.

jj new main@origin -m 'API: introduce quota'
jj new @- @--
jj new --no-edit A
jj new --insert-after A

Trap. Multiple commits are explicit merge parents, not alternatives. Use jj edit when continuing an existing change.

jj next

Purpose and model. Moves from the current working-copy commit toward a child, optionally jumping to the next conflicted descendant. It can create a new child or edit the target directly according to movement policy.

Syntax/options. jj next [OFFSET] defaults to one step. -e/--edit edits the target; -n/--no-edit creates a new working-copy commit on the target; --conflict searches for the next conflicted descendant. These override ui.movement.edit.

State effects. Normal snapshotting records current edits before movement. Forks may not have a unique next child. No bookmark is moved merely by navigation.

jj next
jj next --edit
jj next --conflict
jj next 2 --no-edit

Trap. “Next” is graph navigation, not next commit on a branch. Use an explicit revset with jj edit or jj new when precision matters.

jj parallelize

Purpose and model. Makes selected commits siblings by removing the edge that serialises them. It is useful when changes were accidentally stacked but should be independent.

Syntax/options. jj parallelize [REVSETS...]; -r is the commit alias and there are no additional 0.44 command-specific options.

State effects. New parent edges cause selected commits and descendants to receive successors. A child that depended on removed content may conflict or change patch. Bookmarks follow affected successors; remote publication does not happen automatically.

jj parallelize -r 'B|C'
jj log -r '::@'
jj log -r 'conflicts()'

Trap. Parallelisation is not reverting a dependency's files. Check every descendant; use jj rebase when an explicit destination describes the intention better.

jj prev

Purpose and model. Moves backward from the current working-copy commit. It can create a new child on the selected parent or edit the parent directly, mirroring next.

Syntax/options. jj prev [OFFSET] defaults to one parent. -e/--edit edits the parent; -n/--no-edit creates a new working-copy change; --conflict jumps to the previous conflicted ancestor.

State effects. Current edits are snapshotted first. A merge may have no unique parent, and an offset is graph navigation rather than a first-parent promise. Existing commits are not rewritten unless subsequently edited.

jj prev
jj prev --edit
jj prev --conflict
jj prev 2 --no-edit

Trap. Use jj edit for a non-linear review target. A new child protects the parent; edit mode makes later edits part of it.

jj rebase

Purpose and model. Rewrites parent edges for a selected set while preserving each selected commit's patch as far as possible. Source selection and destination placement are independent dimensions.

Syntax. jj rebase <destination option> [source option]. Source options are -r/--revisions REVSETS for explicit commits, -s/--source REVSET for a commit and descendants, and -b/--branch REVSET for the branch relative to the destination (default @). Exactly one destination mode is required: -o/--onto (aliases -d/--destination), -A/--insert-after (alias --after), or -B/--insert-before (alias --before).

ModeTransformationTypical intent
-r X -o YRewrite exactly X onto Y.Surgical move; useful with holes in a stack.
-s X -o YX and descendants onto Y.Move a whole stack.
-b X -o YBranch of X relative to Y onto Y.Update a current line without naming every node.
-A YInsert selected changes after Y and rebase Y's descendants.Insert between Y and its children.
-B YInsert before Y and rebase Y and descendants.Place a patch before a known commit.
repeat -oMultiple destination parents.Create an intentional merge.

State effects. Rebased commits get successor commit IDs; change IDs normally persist. Descendants automatically rebase. Conflicts are stored in the resulting commits. Bookmarks follow rewritten graph targets, but remote publication requires an explicit push.

jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..@)' -o main@origin
jj rebase -r 'A|C' -o B
jj rebase -s A -A X
jj rebase -r D -o K -o M

Trap. Several source roots can produce several direct children, and a destination must satisfy its own cardinality. Inspect log, evolution, and operation state after a non-trivial rebase. There is no Git-style continuation session: resolve the stored conflict or perform another rewrite.

jj restore

Purpose and model. Copies tree content or undoes the patch represented by a commit. It is a content operation, not a ref reset and not the same as revert. Ask whether the source tree, destination tree, or a commit's visible changes define the operation.

Syntax/options. jj restore [FILESETS...]; -f/--from REVSET is the source, -t/--into REVSET the destination, and -c/--changes-in REVSET undoes changes visible in a commit compared with the merge of its parents. Without options the default is effectively --changes-in @. -i/--interactive and --tool NAME select hunks; --restore-descendants preserves descendant content during rebasing.

State effects. The destination is rewritten with selected paths copied from the source or parent-derived inverse. Descendants may be rebased and conflicts may be stored. A fileset narrows paths, not commit selection. Restoring into @ changes the working-copy tree at the next snapshot.

jj restore --from main@origin --into @ -- 'glob:src/**'
jj restore --changes-in A
jj restore --from @- --into @ --interactive

Trap. Use revert for a review-visible inverse; use restore from a parent for local cleanup. Verify with jj diff before publishing.

jj revert

Purpose and model. Creates a new change that applies the reverse of selected commits. It preserves original history and expresses the reversal as a reviewable patch.

Syntax/options. jj revert --revision REVSETS --onto REVSETS, or use -A/--insert-after/-B/--insert-before. -r/--revision is required; -o/--onto (alias -d/--destination) supplies the destination. Multiple destinations can create a merge.

State effects. Original commits are not rewritten. A new change with a new change ID is created; conflicts can occur when the inverse no longer applies. Bookmarks move only if they point at the result or are advanced explicitly.

jj revert -r A -o @
jj revert --revision 'A::C' --onto main@origin
jj log -r '::@'

Trap. Revert is not abandon and does not erase a public patch. For unpublished cleanup, restore/squash may communicate intent better.

jj simplify-parents

Purpose and model. Removes redundant parent edges while preserving the selected commits' tree meaning. It is graph normalisation: it does not turn a merge into an arbitrary squash and should be used when parent reachability makes an edge unnecessary.

Syntax/options. jj simplify-parents; -s/--source REVSETS simplifies commits together with their descendant trees, while -r/--revision REVSETS limits the operation to specified commits. If neither is supplied, 0.44 uses revsets.simplify-parents or reachable(@, mutable()).

State effects. Parent edges and therefore commit IDs can change; descendants may be rewritten. Filesystem content should remain semantically equivalent, but conflicts and merges must be inspected. Bookmarks follow target successors.

jj simplify-parents -r @
jj simplify-parents --source 'mutable()'
jj log -r 'merges()'

Trap. Use rebase when you want a particular destination, and do not treat parent simplification as a content cleanup. Compare before/after graph and diff.

jj split

Purpose and model. Divides one commit into two changes. The first result contains selected content and the second contains the remainder; this is a graph replacement, not merely a file move. By default the target is @.

Syntax/options. jj split [FILESETS...]; -r/--revision REVSET chooses the commit, -i/--interactive and --tool NAME select hunks, -o/--onto/-d/--destination and -A/-B place the resulting changes, -m/--message supplies a description, --editor opens the editor, and -p/--parallel controls parallel handling in the installed surface. A fileset selects paths for the first side when non-interactive.

State effects. The original commit receives successors representing the split. Descendants are rebased onto the second resulting change and can conflict. The split changes have distinct change IDs; neither should be assumed to be the original change's sole continuation without checking evolution. Bookmarks at the original target follow the command's successor policy, but publication bookmarks may need to be moved.

jj split -r @ -- 'glob:tests/**'
jj split --interactive -r A
jj split -r @ -m 'API: implementation'
jj log -r '::@'

Trap. A fileset is a path selection, not a semantic hunk classifier. Check both diffs and descriptions. If the operation is wrong, undo before further edits; later, use evolution to identify the split successors.

jj squash

Purpose and model. Moves content from one or more source commits into a destination, normally the parent of the current change. It can fold all or selected paths and can perform experimental insertion-style rewrites in 0.44.

Syntax/options. jj squash [FILESETS...]; ordinary mode uses -r/--revision REVSET to squash a commit into its parent, or -f/--from REVSETS and -t/--into REVSET to name source and destination. -i/--interactive/--tool NAME select content. -m/--message sets the destination description, -u/--use-destination-message discards source descriptions, and --editor opens the editor. Experimental -o/--onto, -A, and -B insert the new commit.

State effects. Source and destination are rewritten; source may become empty and descendants receive new commit IDs. Change IDs of the surviving logical patches can persist, but moving content between changes changes which change owns the patch. Bookmarks at source/destination follow the resulting graph; remote bookmarks are not updated until push.

jj squash                         # current patch into parent
jj squash --from @ --into @- -- 'glob:src/**'
jj squash --use-destination-message
jj squash --interactive --tool meld

Trap. --revision mode is incompatible with experimental insertion options. Distinguish “copy these paths into a tree” (restore) from “move this patch ownership” (squash). Review descriptions and empty changes after a partial squash.

jj fix

Purpose and model. Runs configured fixer programs against files in selected commits. It is a history rewrite operation whose input is a commit domain and whose file scope is a fileset/configured fixer policy.

Syntax/options. jj fix [FILESETS...]; -s/--source REVSETS selects commits and descendants, defaulting to revsets.fix or reachable(@, mutable()). --include-unchanged-files fixes files even when not changed in the target; -a/--all-lines asks for all lines rather than modified lines when the fixer supports ranges.

State effects. Fixers can rewrite many commits and trigger descendant rebasing/conflicts. A failing fixer may leave a useful operation to inspect, but do not assume partial output is a valid stack. Fix configuration supplies commands, environment, file patterns, and range protocol; the working-copy commit is not special except as a selected commit.

jj fix -s 'stack(@)' -- 'glob:src/**'
jj fix --all-lines -s 'mutable()'
jj log -r 'mutable() & conflicts()'

Trap. Run first on a small revset and inspect the diff. A formatter that does not understand modified-line ranges can rewrite entire files. For reproducible automation, pin the fixer executable/version and use run --ignore-changes for checks that should not rewrite history.

jj resolve

Purpose and model. Resolves stored file conflicts with a merge tool. It changes the conflict value in a selected commit; it is not a Git index continuation phase.

Syntax/options. jj resolve [FILESETS...]; -r/--revision REVSET defaults to @, -l/--list lists paths instead of resolving, and --tool NAME selects a configured three-way merge tool. Built-in :ours and :theirs choose sides one and two for applicable conflicts.

State effects. The selected commit is rewritten with resolved file content; descendants that depend on its tree may be rebased and conflicts can disappear or move. The filesystem is materialised for the current workspace, but the stored conflict value is authoritative. A fileset limits conflicted paths.

jj resolve --list -r @
jj resolve --tool meld -r @ -- 'glob:src/**'
jj resolve --tool :ours
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

Trap. Multi-sided conflicts are not necessarily two-way, and marker files are a rendering. Inspect the resulting diff and conflict predicate. Resolving a child does not necessarily resolve an independent conflict in its descendant.

jj sign

Purpose and model. Adds or replaces a cryptographic signature on selected commits. Signing rewrites commits because the signature is metadata; it is separate from a Git hosting server's verification policy.

Syntax/options. jj sign selects commits with -r/--revision REVSETS, defaulting to revsets.sign, and accepts --key KEY. Commits are re-signed even if they appear already signed; hardware-key workflows should account for that.

State effects. Signed commits receive new commit IDs and may cause descendant successors. Change IDs normally persist. Bookmarks follow successors locally; pushing signed commits still requires the relevant bookmark/ref and server policy.

jj sign -r 'bookmark(release)'
jj sign --key KEYID -r @
jj log -r @ -T 'if(signature, signature.status(), "unsigned") ++ "\n"'

Trap. Presence does not prove a good signature. Template status/key/display can be expensive because verification may invoke GPG or SSH tooling.

jj unsign

Purpose and model. Removes cryptographic signatures from selected commits. It is metadata rewriting, not a content or ref operation.

Syntax/options. jj unsign accepts -r/--revision REVSETS; selection follows the command's default when omitted. Global immutability and repository-view options apply.

State effects. The affected commits and dependent descendants can receive new IDs while their trees remain unchanged. Change identities normally persist; bookmarks move with local successors and remotes remain unchanged until publication.

jj unsign -r 'mutable() & signed()'
jj log -r @ -T 'if(signature, "signed", "unsigned") ++ "\n"'

Trap. Do not unsign imported/public immutable history casually. Treat it like any other metadata rewrite and inspect the operation log.

Bookmarks, tags, configuration, and review transport

jj bookmark

Purpose and model. Names a commit for local navigation or publication and manages relationships with remote bookmarks. The parent command requires a subcommand; its aliases include b. A bookmark is a ref-like name over the commit DAG, not the unit of ordinary local work.

Subcommands. 0.44 provides advance, create, delete, forget, list, move, rename, set, track, and untrack. Each is described below. Names usually accept glob-like string patterns; commit options accept revsets with the cardinality required by the operation.

State effects. Bookmark operations change local ref state and create operations, but do not rewrite commit content. A later Git push exports the selected local bookmark; tracking is a relationship and does not make a bookmark follow @.

jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark create review -r @
jj bookmark set review -r @ --allow-backwards
jj bookmark track review --remote origin

Trap. Always qualify same-name remote refs in review scripts and specify the remote on publication. “Bookmark changed” and “commit changed” are different facts.

jj bookmark advance

Moves matching bookmarks forward to a target selected by -t/--to REVSET, defaulting to revsets.bookmark-advance-to. Names are patterns and can match several bookmarks. It is intended for advancing bookmarks along descendant history, not for arbitrary sideways replacement.

jj bookmark advance --to @ review
jj bookmark advance --to 'main@origin::@' 'pr/*'

It changes ref state only; selected commits are not rewritten. If the target or bookmark set is ambiguous, narrow the name/revset. Use bookmark set when an intentional backwards or sideways move is required and review its safety option.

jj bookmark create

Creates one or more local bookmarks. -r/--revision REVSET (alias --to) supplies the target and defaults to @. The target must resolve as required by the command; creating several names at one target is useful when a server policy needs aliases.

jj bookmark create github/pr-42 -r @
jj bookmark create base feature -r A

Creation does not move @, rewrite commits, or create tracking by itself. A later push may automatically establish tracking for an untracked bookmark. Avoid creating a publication name before the graph boundary is intentional.

jj bookmark delete

Deletes local bookmarks matching the supplied name patterns. It changes local ref state; it does not erase the target commits. A tracked deletion can be exported as a remote deletion by an appropriate push.

jj bookmark delete 'github/pr-*'
jj bookmark delete review

Use forget when the goal is to discard local knowledge rather than express a deletion to the remote. Check tracking and server state before deleting a publication bookmark.

jj bookmark forget

Forgets local bookmark and, optionally, remote-bookmark knowledge. --include-remotes also forgets corresponding remote bookmarks; a future fetch recreates still-existing remote refs. Forgetting is local bookkeeping and is not a remote deletion request.

jj bookmark forget review
jj bookmark forget --include-remotes 'old/*'

Use this after a merged or closed review when the remote state should remain untouched. If the server ref must be deleted, use bookmark delete and push the deletion.

jj bookmark list

Displays local and selected remote bookmark state. Names are optional patterns. -a/--all-remotes includes synchronized remote bookmarks; --remote REMOTE limits a remote; --tracked, --conflicted, --revisions REVSET, -T/--template, and output filters provide the important inspection dimensions in 0.44.

jj bookmark list
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked
jj bookmark list -T 'name ++ " " ++ if(synced, "synced", "changed") ++ "\n"'

Listing does not move refs but normally snapshots unless ignored. The default view can omit synchronized remote targets; use --all-remotes when auditing all server knowledge. A conflicted bookmark needs its target sets inspected before push.

jj bookmark move

Moves existing bookmarks to a target. Names are patterns, or -f/--from REVSETS selects bookmarks whose targets are in a commit set; -t/--to REVSET supplies the destination and defaults to @. It is a ref move, not a commit rewrite.

jj bookmark move review --to @
jj bookmark move --from 'heads(mutable())' --to @
jj bookmark move 'pr/*' --to 'main@origin::@'

Moving sideways or backwards may require the command's safety confirmation. Review local/remote divergence after a move; a tracked remote bookmark is not changed until push.

jj bookmark rename

Renames one local bookmark from OLD to NEW. --overwrite-existing permits replacing an existing name where the command's safety policy allows it. Target commit and content are unchanged; remote publication may need a new push/delete sequence.

jj bookmark rename review github/pr-42
jj bookmark rename --overwrite-existing old current

Renaming is not the same as creating a second name: tracking relationships and remote names need explicit review. Use list before and after.

jj bookmark set

Creates or updates bookmarks to a target. -r/--revision REVSET (alias --to) defaults to @; -B/--allow-backwards permits backwards or sideways movement that is otherwise protected.

jj bookmark set review -r @
jj bookmark set --allow-backwards review -r 'main@origin'

Set is convenient for a publication boundary but can hide whether a name was new or already existed. For an auditable existing-name move use bookmark move. Neither operation changes the commit DAG.

jj bookmark track

Creates a relationship between a local bookmark and a remote bookmark. Arguments can be bookmark patterns with --remote REMOTE or exact BOOKMARK@REMOTE symbols. Tracking does not move either target and does not make the local bookmark follow @.

jj bookmark track review --remote origin
jj bookmark track main@origin
jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked

Tracking supplies comparison and push safety information: ahead, behind, synced, and expected remote target. It is especially important when a repository has GitHub and Gerrit remotes with the same branch names.

jj bookmark untrack

Removes tracking relationships from local bookmark/remote bookmark pairs. It accepts the same pattern/remote forms as track, including BOOKMARK@REMOTE.

jj bookmark untrack review --remote origin
jj bookmark untrack main@github

Untracking is local metadata; it neither deletes the remote bookmark nor moves the local one. After untracking, a push may treat the name as a new publication, so inspect the push preview and specify the remote.

jj config

Purpose and model. Reads and writes layered TOML configuration. The parent namespace provides edit, gc, get, list, path, set, and unset. Scope is part of the command contract: user, repository, and workspace files are different state locations.

Safety. Config can invoke editors, external tools, aliases, fixers, merge tools, and transport behaviour. Treat repository/workspace config as input, not trusted code. Inspect effective values with config list before running a command from an unfamiliar checkout.

jj config list
jj config get ui.pager
jj config set --user user.name 'A. Engineer'
jj config path --repo

Configuration changes create operations only when they affect repository state according to the command; they do not rewrite commits by themselves. The full Linux design and grouped key reference appear in Part XIII and Appendix F.

jj config edit

Opens a selected configuration file in the configured editor. Exactly one of --user, --repo, or --workspace selects the scope in 0.44. The editor is an external side effect; --config-file can add input but is not the same as choosing the file to edit.

jj config edit --user
jj config edit --repo
jj config edit --workspace

Use this for multi-line templates, aliases, and tool definitions where shell/TOML quoting is risky. In 0.45.1, --file PATH is available for selecting a specific file; mark that as 0.45-only in shared instructions.

jj config gc

Garbage-collects obsolete configuration files or fragments according to the target release's config storage policy. It is maintenance, not a commit operation, and should be run only after understanding which generated/config files are no longer referenced.

jj config gc

Do not confuse config GC with repository object GC. Version-control important config and take a backup before maintenance on a non-standard layout.

jj config get

Reads one named configuration value. jj config get NAME reports the effective value after layers are applied; use scope options from local help when you need a particular layer. It is useful in shell guards because it tests the configuration jj itself will use.

jj config get revsets.log
jj config get git.push
jj config get gerrit.default-remote

Absent values and typed TOML values are different from empty strings. Use config list or config path to explain surprising results.

jj config list

Lists effective configuration, optionally restricted to a name/prefix. It is the best first diagnostic for precedence, aliases, templates, remotes, and tool definitions.

jj config list
jj config list ui
jj config list revset-aliases

Inspect before executing repo-provided aliases or external tools. Values can come from several files; use path/edit commands to locate and change the intended layer.

jj config path

Prints the path of a selected config scope. Exactly one scope flag is required in the 0.44 surface: --user, --repo, or --workspace.

jj config path --user
jj config path --repo
jj config path --workspace

Use it in diagnostics and backup scripts rather than assuming an XDG path. Repository and workspace config are intentionally separated from the repository in newer layouts to reduce accidental execution of untrusted checked-in configuration.

jj config set

Sets one TOML value in a selected scope. Syntax is jj config set --user|--repo|--workspace NAME VALUE. The value is a TOML expression; strings, arrays, booleans, integers, and multi-line strings therefore need both TOML and shell quoting.

jj config set --user ui.pager 'less -FRX'
jj config set --repo revsets.log 'stack(@) | bookmarks()'
jj config set --workspace ui.default-command '["log", "-r", "stack(@)"]'

Use config edit for complex values. In 0.45.1, --file PATH can target a specific config file. Do not assume a string without quotes has the desired TOML type.

jj config unset

Removes a named value from a selected user/repository/workspace layer. It exposes lower-precedence values again; it does not necessarily mean the effective value becomes absent.

jj config unset --user ui.pager
jj config unset --repo revsets.log

Check config get before and after. In 0.45.1, --file PATH can target a specific config file; mark scripts using it as version-specific.

jj gerrit and jj gerrit upload

Purpose and model. Provides Gerrit-aware upload on top of jj's mutable change graph. It is not a replacement for Gerrit's server identity: jj change IDs identify local evolution, while Gerrit uses a message Change-Id: I... footer or server/plugin policy to associate patch sets.

Syntax/options. The parent requires upload. In 0.44, jj gerrit upload accepts -r/--revision REVSET, -b/--remote-branch BRANCH, --remote REMOTE, -n/--dry-run, reviewer/CC/label/topic/hashtag/message options, edit/WIP/ready/private/remove-private, comment publication, notification, merged, validation, attention-set, deadline, custom and repeated -o/--option push options. Run local help for the exact spelling of server options.

State effects. Upload exports commits and review metadata; it can update remote-tracking state but does not make Gerrit a local bookmark manager. Rewriting an earlier jj change produces new commit IDs while a preserved Gerrit footer associates the new commit with its existing review. Descendants are automatically rebased locally before re-upload.

jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
jj gerrit upload --revision 'roots(main@origin..@)' --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
git push gerrit 'HEAD:refs/for/main%topic=quota'

Boundary. Local jj transport and footer handling are testable with fixtures; Gerrit server review, patch-set, topic, permission, and submit behaviour is server-dependent. Never claim that a jj change ID is a Gerrit Change-Id.

jj git

Purpose and model. Operates the Git backend, remotes, Git-compatible refs, and colocation. The parent is a namespace for clone, colocation, export, fetch, import, init, push, remote, and root.

Jj metadata and the Git object database are related but not identical. A commit can have a jj change ID and operation history that Git does not know. A bookmark can be exported as a Git ref; an operation ID and workspace pointer cannot.

jj git root
jj git remote list
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git import
jj git export

Native Git commands are useful in a colocated checkout, but after Git-side ref changes explicitly import them and avoid two writers changing the worktree concurrently. The detailed backend model appears in Part VII.

jj git clone

Clones a Git repository into a jj repository. Syntax is jj git clone SOURCE [DESTINATION]. In 0.44 the result is colocated by default unless git.colocate=false; --no-colocate selects the hidden backing layout and --colocate makes the default an explicit script precondition. Important options include --remote REMOTE_NAME, fetch branch/tag filters, and checkout/workspace options in the installed help.

jj git clone --colocate ssh://git@example.test/project.git project
jj git clone --remote upstream https://example.test/project.git project

A local source path is resolved to an absolute path. Colocation makes the directory usable by native Git and jj; non-colocation keeps jj metadata separate. The clone's operation DAG is new and independent of the source clone.

jj git init

Initialises a Git-backed jj repository in the destination, defaulting to the current directory. In 0.44, --colocate creates/uses a top-level .git and is the default unless git.colocate=false; --no-colocate explicitly puts the backing Git repository under the jj metadata layout. --git-repo PATH selects an existing backing Git repository and is mutually exclusive with --colocate, subject to the same-path special case described by local help. The destination is created if necessary.

jj git init --colocate
jj git init project                 # colocated in 0.44 by default
jj git init --no-colocate hidden-project
jj git init --colocate --git-repo ../existing.git

Choose colocation when GitHub/Gerrit tools and Git transport must share a worktree, but establish a single-writer rule for filesystem operations. Check jj root and jj git root after initialisation.

jj git fetch

Fetches Git objects and remote refs, then imports them as remote bookmarks/tags. --remote REMOTE selects a named remote; -b/--branch repeats branch patterns, -t/--tag repeats tag patterns, and the command has options for remotes, pruning, depth, and parallelism according to local help.

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git fetch --remote upstream --branch 'main'
jj git fetch --remote origin --tag 'v*'

Fetch does not rebase your mutable stack automatically. It moves remote bookmarks; rebase explicitly onto the imported commit. Branch filters use the fetch command's documented pattern rules, which are not necessarily identical to revset string patterns.

jj git import

Imports Git-side ref changes into jj's view without fetching from a network remote. It is the explicit bridge after native Git creates, moves, or deletes refs in a colocated repository.

git -C "$(jj root)" branch temp
jj git import
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

Import can alter remote bookmark/tag state and create an operation, but it does not turn a Git branch into a local jj working-copy commit or move @. Inspect the imported ref and rebase/publish deliberately.

jj git export

Exports jj bookmarks/tags and compatible commit information to Git refs. It is useful after local ref changes when a native Git consumer or hosting tool needs the Git view updated.

jj bookmark create review -r @
jj git export
git -C "$(jj root)" show-ref

Export does not publish to a remote; git push or jj git push is the network operation. Operation history, change IDs, conflict structure, and workspace pointers are not made into ordinary Git concepts.

jj git push

Purpose and model. Publishes Git refs selected from local bookmarks/tags. It is the boundary where unnamed local work must acquire a publication name unless a release-specific change push mode is used.

Syntax/options. --remote REMOTE selects a named remote; -b/--bookmark PATTERN repeats bookmark patterns, -t/--tag PATTERN repeats tags, --all pushes all bookmarks/tags including new ones, --tracked pushes tracked refs, and --deleted pushes deletions. Local help also defines --allow-new, --allow-empty-description, --change REVSET where supported, --dry-run, and force/remote options; verify exact 0.44 syntax before scripting.

Safety. An untracked selected bookmark can become tracked automatically. A non-fast-forward update is rejected unless the explicit safety/force policy permits it. Review local and remote targets, specify the remote, and use a dry run where available.

jj git push --remote origin --bookmark review
jj git push --remote github --bookmark 'gh/*' --dry-run
jj git push --remote gerrit --bookmark review

State effects. Push changes the remote server, not the commit DAG. It can update local remote-tracking state and operations. A later fetch observes server-side merge/rewrite. --no-integrate-operation only changes operation integration; it does not prevent the network push.

jj git remote

Manages named Git remotes. Subcommands are add, list, remove, rename, and set-url. Remote names qualify remote bookmarks and select push/fetch destinations; they do not identify a workspace.

jj git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote add gerrit ssh://gerrit.example/project
jj git remote list
jj git remote set-url github git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote rename origin upstream

Inspect full URLs before pushing. A two-remote repository should use explicit --remote in scripts and qualify main@github versus main@gerrit in revsets.

jj git remote add

Adds a named remote with a URL. It changes Git remote configuration; no commits are fetched until jj git fetch.

jj git remote add origin ssh://git@example.test/project
jj git git remote add github git@github.com:me/project.git

The second line is intentionally wrong: the command is jj git remote add, not jj git git remote add. Shell aliases should not obscure the remote name.

jj git remote list

Lists configured remote names and URLs. It is read-only repository configuration inspection and is the first command to run before a dual-host push.

jj git remote list

jj git remote remove

Removes a configured remote. Existing imported remote bookmarks can remain as local knowledge until forgotten or pruned according to the command's policy. Removing a remote does not delete the server repository.

jj git remote remove old-origin
jj bookmark forget --include-remotes 'main@old-origin'

jj git remote rename

Renames a configured remote and therefore changes the qualifier of its remote bookmarks. Review tracking relationships and scripts after renaming.

jj git remote rename origin github
jj log -r 'main@github'

jj git remote set-url

Changes the URL for an existing remote. It does not fetch or push and does not rewrite commits. Use it when moving from HTTPS to SSH or changing a fork URL, then verify with remote list.

jj git remote set-url origin git@github.com:me/project.git
jj git remote list

jj git colocation

Controls whether jj and Git share the repository/worktree layout. Subcommands are enable, disable, and status. Colocation is a backend layout property, not a branch or workspace mode.

jj git colocation status
jj git colocation enable
jj git colocation disable

Enable for Git-native tooling; disable only after checking scripts, Git roots, hooks, and worktree state. Existing Git objects/refs and jj metadata are not interchangeable merely because they reside nearby.

jj git colocation enable

Enables Git colocation for a compatible repository. Native Git commands can then use the same worktree, but an export/import boundary still exists.

jj git colocation enable
jj git colocation status

jj git colocation disable

Disables colocation. Git-native tools may no longer see the expected top-level Git repository, and scripts using git -C need revisiting. Do this only with a backup and an explicit transport plan.

jj git colocation disable
jj root
jj git root

jj git colocation status

Reports whether the current repository is colocated. It is a useful preflight for git, gh, hooks, and CI scripts.

jj git colocation status

jj git root

Prints the Git repository root used by the backend. It can differ in meaning from jj root, which prints the current workspace root. Use the result instead of assuming the current directory is a Git root.

git -C "$(jj git root)" show-ref
GIT_DIR="$(jj git root)/.git" git status

In a non-colocated repository, native Git use may be invalid or point at another repository. In scripts, first inspect colocation status.

jj tag

Manages tag names, which are release/reference names rather than moving review bookmarks. Subcommands are delete, list, set, track, and untrack.

Tags can be local or remote and may be tracked, but their expected immutability and publication policy differ from a review bookmark. Use tags for release identity and bookmarks for moving work.

jj tag delete

Deletes local tags matching names/patterns. It changes local tag state; a tracked deletion can be pushed according to Git/tag policy. It does not erase the tagged commit.

jj tag delete 'v0.44-*'

jj tag list
