Lists tags, optionally filtered by names, remote, tracking, or commit and rendered with a template according to local help. Use it to audit release names and remote tag state before a force-sensitive operation.

jj tag list
jj tag list --all-remotes

jj tag set

Creates or updates tags to a target commit. -r/--revision REVSET (alias --to) defaults to @; --allow-move permits moving an existing tag. Moving a release tag is a policy event, not ordinary patch editing.

jj tag set v1.2.0 -r @
jj tag set --allow-move v1.2.0 -r release-candidate

jj tag track

Associates local tag state with a remote tag. It changes tracking metadata, not the tag target or server.

jj tag track v1.2.0@origin

jj tag untrack

Removes a local/remote tag tracking relationship without deleting either tag. Review push behaviour after untracking because a later tag publication may be interpreted as new.

jj tag untrack v1.2.0@origin

Inspection, navigation, files, sparse state, and operations

jj log

Purpose and model. Displays a graph projection of a commit set. It does not show “branches” by default; it shows the selected visible commits, their topology, and a template rendering for each. The default revset and template are configuration, so a stable script supplies both.

Syntax/options. jj log [FILESETS...]; -r/--revision REVSETS selects commits, -n/--limit N limits after filtering and topological ordering, --reversed shows older first, -G/--no-graph produces a flat list, -T/--template renders commits, -p/--patch includes patches, and --count prints a count. A fileset restricts commits that modify those paths.

jj log -r 'stack(@)'
jj log -r 'mutable() & conflicts()' -p
jj log -G --color=never -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj log -n 20 --reversed -r 'main@origin..@'

State effects. Normal log snapshots the working copy first; use --ignore-working-copy for a prompt or monitoring read. It never moves bookmarks or rewrites commits. The graph can contain hidden/divergent information only when the selected revset includes it.

Trap. Do not parse graph glyphs or assume the default revset is a complete repository view. For automation use explicit revsets, templates, colour, and pager options. A fileset is applied to changed-path history, not merely to the displayed tree.

jj show

Shows metadata and the patch of selected commits compared with their parent(s), defaulting to @. --reversed changes ordering, -T/--template renders metadata, and diff formatting includes --summary, --stat, --types, --name-only, --git, and context/diff options from local help.

jj show -r A
jj show -r @ --stat
jj show -r @ -T 'change_id ++ "\n" ++ description'

For a merge, the displayed change is relative to the automatic merge of parents. Show is observational but can snapshot; it does not create a new commit. Use diff to compare arbitrary endpoints and evolog to compare versions of one logical change.

jj status

Reports high-level workspace status: current @, parent relationship, working-copy modifications, conflicts, and ignored/untracked information according to the workspace. Positional filesets restrict the status display.

jj status
jj status --ignore-working-copy
jj status -- 'glob:src/**'

By default status snapshots, so it can turn filesystem edits into a new working-copy commit. This is a major difference from a passive Git status check. Use the global ignore option when a prompt or monitor must not mutate repository state. Status does not move bookmarks or rewrite ancestors, except for the snapshot of @.

jj root

Prints the current workspace root and is a shortcut for jj workspace root. It is useful for scripts that need to locate the repository from a subdirectory.

cd src/lib
git -C "$(jj root)" status

It reports the workspace root, not necessarily the Git backend root; use jj git root for the latter. The command is observational but follows global snapshot policy unless ignored.

jj version

Prints jj version/build information. Use it as the first line of a bug report, validation log, and version-sensitive script.

jj version
jj --version

The book's normative baseline is 0.44.0; 0.45.1 is separately inventoried. Never infer feature availability from current online documentation alone.

jj help

Prints help for the root or a command path. jj help --help documents the help command itself; jj help command and jj help command subcommand select paths. Keyword search exposes language and conceptual material in the installed documentation.

jj help rebase
jj help bookmark track
jj help -k revsets
jj help -k templates

Help is the normative installed-CLI authority for syntax/defaults. It may link to a current documentation site; use the local version for behaviour. In a release audit capture root help recursively rather than relying on memory.

jj file

Provides file-oriented inspection and metadata commands: annotate, chmod, list, search, show, track, and untrack. These operate on filesets and often a single commit; they are not Git's index commands.

A fileset selects repository paths, while a commit option selects the tree/commit. File tracking changes whether ignored/large paths participate in snapshotting; it does not create a commit by itself until a snapshot records the filesystem.

jj file annotate

Shows line attribution for one path. Syntax is jj file annotate PATH; -r/--revision REVSET selects the starting commit and -T/--template renders AnnotationLine values, including commit, content, line numbers, original line numbers, and hunk boundaries.

jj file annotate src/quota.rs
jj file annotate -r main@origin -T 'line_number() ++ " " ++ commit().change_id() ++ " " ++ content()'

It is observational and can snapshot. The path is a single path argument, not a general fileset. Attribution follows jj's graph/evolution data and may differ from Git blame after rewrites.

jj file chmod

Changes executable metadata for filesets in a selected commit. Syntax is jj file chmod MODE FILESETS...; mode n/normal clears executable and x/executable sets it, while -r/--revision REVSET defaults to @.

jj file chmod x scripts/release.sh
jj file chmod n --revision A -- 'glob:bin/**'

This rewrites the target's tree metadata and can rebase descendants. It is not a filesystem chmod of every checkout; inspect the resulting tree and working copy. Use template TreeEntry.executable() to verify.

jj file list

Lists paths in the selected tree. Positional filesets filter output, -r/--revision REVSET selects the tree (default @), and -T/--template can render tree-entry metadata where supported.

jj file list --revision @
jj file list -r main@origin -- 'glob:src/**'
jj file list -- 'root-glob:docs/**/*.md'

Use it to validate a fileset before passing that fileset to restore, split, squash, or fix. It is observational but may snapshot.

jj file search

Searches file contents in a selected commit. Syntax requires --pattern PATTERN; -r/--revision REVSET defaults to @, positional filesets restrict file prefixes, and --name-only prints paths rather than matching lines. The pattern defaults to regex when no string-pattern kind is supplied.

jj file search --pattern 'quota' -- 'glob:src/**'
jj file search --pattern 'glob:*TODO*' --name-only
jj file search -r main@origin --pattern 'regex:^use ' -- 'glob:*.rs'

A glob pattern applies to the whole line, so glob:*foo* is different from glob:foo. This command searches a tree; it does not search the working filesystem independently.

jj file show

Prints file contents from a selected tree. Filesets are required; -r/--revision REVSET defaults to @, and -T/--template renders TreeEntry metadata.

jj file show -r main@origin -- src/quota.rs
jj file show -- 'glob:docs/**/*.md'

It is read-only repository inspection but normal snapshot policy still applies. A conflicted entry is not ordinary file content; inspect conflict metadata and resolve or select a side explicitly.

jj file track

Marks filesets for tracking even when ignored or too large only when requested. --include-ignored overrides ignore and maximum-file-size restrictions.

jj file track --include-ignored generated/schema.json
jj file track -- 'glob:vendor/**'

Tracking affects snapshot eligibility and can change a later working-copy commit; it does not itself publish or commit. Use sparingly because large/ignored content has performance and policy implications.

jj file untrack

Stops tracking selected paths. It affects future snapshots and working-copy materialisation, not historical trees already committed.

jj file untrack 'glob:generated/**'
jj status

Check status and sparse configuration afterward. Do not confuse untracking with deleting a file from a commit; use restore or filesystem deletion for content changes.

jj sparse

Manages which repository paths are materialised in the current workspace. Subcommands are edit, list, reset, and set. Sparse state changes the filesystem view, not the complete commit tree.

A sparse workspace can therefore run a build or review focused on one subtree while commits still contain paths outside the materialised set. A rewrite can update absent paths in the commit without writing them into the sparse filesystem.

jj sparse edit

Opens the editor for the current workspace's sparse patterns. The editor is external and operates on configuration-like path patterns.

jj sparse edit
jj sparse list

Use it for complex patterns; use sparse set for repeatable commands. Sparse editing can update the filesystem and therefore should not run concurrently with another process writing the workspace.

jj sparse list

Lists sparse patterns for the current workspace. It is the first diagnostic when files appear absent from a checkout.

jj sparse list

An absent file may be sparse rather than deleted. Compare jj file list against filesystem listing before restoring or committing.

jj sparse reset

Resets sparse patterns to the default/full materialisation policy. It changes workspace files, not commit trees.

jj sparse reset
jj sparse list

Use before a full test when a sparse workspace could hide inputs. It may be expensive in a large repository and can overwrite local filesystem materialisation; snapshot or inspect status first.

jj sparse set

Sets sparse patterns directly. The fileset expressions describe paths to materialise; all other paths remain absent from the filesystem view.

jj sparse set --clear --add src --add tests
jj sparse set --clear --add Cargo.toml --add Cargo.lock

Patterns are workspace state, not history filters. A command such as jj diff can still see complete commit trees; use filesets separately when the diff itself should be narrowed.

jj operation / jj op

Manages the operation DAG. Subcommands are abandon, diff, integrate, log, restore, revert, and show. Operation IDs identify repository transformations in this clone; they are not commit IDs and do not transfer through Git.

jj op log
jj op show
jj op diff --from OP1 --to OP2
jj undo

Use operation commands when repository state surprises you, especially after a rewrite, concurrent operation, or ref movement. The command family is explained in the recovery chapter and laboratory.

jj operation log alias: jj op log

Displays operation history as a graph. Options include commit/operation selection, -n/--limit, --reversed, -G/--no-graph, -T/--template, and patch/detail output according to local help.

jj op log
jj op log -n 20
jj op log -T 'id.short(12) ++ " " ++ description ++ "\n"'

Operation log is the first recovery command. Record the current head before attempting repair; a later operation can make an earlier state harder to recognise but does not necessarily remove it.

jj operation show alias: jj op show

Shows one operation's metadata and repository changes. Select an operation ID from jj op log; options include patch and graph formatting.

jj op show OP
jj op show -p OP

Use it to answer what a rewrite actually changed. It does not change state. For the state itself, use jj --at-op OP log; for a controlled restore, use jj op restore OP.

jj operation diff alias: jj op diff

Compares repository state between operations. --operation OP compares an operation with its parent; -f/--from and -t/--to compare endpoints; -G/--no-graph flattens changes and -p/--patch includes rebased patch detail.

jj op diff --operation OP
jj op diff --from OP1 --to OP2 --patch

It is especially useful when later operations have changed commit IDs: compare repository views, not only remembered IDs. It is observational and does not restore either state.

jj operation restore alias: jj op restore

Restores repository state to an operation. The operation must be selected explicitly; inspect it with jj --at-op OP log first.

--what can repeat and, in the experimental 0.44 surface, selects repo and/or remote-tracking. Omitting remote-tracking state can preserve the ability to push after undo; restoring it can make the local view match the old state exactly.

jj --at-op OP log -r 'all()'
jj op restore OP
jj op restore --what repo OP

Restore creates a new operation representing the restoration; it does not move a server and does not make the filesystem historical unless the workspace is updated. Treat it as a deliberate state change, then inspect the new operation.

jj operation revert alias: jj op revert

Creates a repository operation that reverses the effect of a selected operation while retaining later state as appropriate. It is not commit jj revert: the former operates on repository metadata/graph state, the latter creates an inverse commit.

jj op revert OP

Use operation diff first. Operation revert is a recovery tool for an operation-level mistake, not a way to undo a public remote push.

jj operation abandon alias: jj op abandon

Abandons an operation from the operation DAG. It is a history-maintenance action on local operation records, not the same as abandoning a commit. Do not use it until recovery is complete and retention policy is understood.

jj op abandon OP
jj op log

Once operation evidence is abandoned, recovery may become harder. Keep an external record of important operation IDs before pruning.

jj operation integrate alias: jj op integrate

Integrates an operation created with --no-integrate-operation into the current operation head. It is the explicit convergence point for concurrent/unintegrated local operations.

jj status --no-integrate-operation
# record the printed operation ID
jj op integrate OP

Integration can expose conflicts or divergent operation history. It does not suppress or reverse external side effects performed by the original command.

jj undo

Undoes the most recent integrated operation by creating a reverse repository operation. It is the fast recovery path for a mistake noticed immediately.

jj undo
jj op log
jj redo

Undo does not automatically undo an already-pushed remote change, and it may not be the desired repair when several operations have happened. Use operation restore/diff for a named historical state and evolution for one change's identity.

jj bisect run

Runs a test command over candidate commits in the required --range REVSET. Put command arguments after -- when they begin with a dash. The command's exit status is the test result; keep it deterministic and independent of the primary workspace.

jj bisect run --range 'good..bad' -- ./test-regression.sh

Candidate working copies are temporary and are not publication bookmarks. Inspect the discovered commit with jj show, then create a bookmark or new change only if that is the intended next operation.

jj interdiff

Compares the patch changes between two commit versions. Use --from REVSET and --to REVSET as required by the 0.44 syntax, plus positional filesets and diff-formatting options. It is the right comparison for successive versions of one logical change whose parent context changed.

jj interdiff --from A --to B
jj interdiff --from A --to B --stat -- 'glob:src/**'

It does not rewrite commits or move refs, but normal snapshot policy still applies. Use diff for endpoint tree comparison and evolog -p when the commits are known successors of one change.

jj run

Runs a command over selected commits. -r/--revision REVSETS selects the set, -j/--jobs N controls parallelism, --root changes the working directory, --clean discards reusable private workspaces, --passthrough connects output and requires one job, and --ignore-changes prevents rewriting commits.

jj run --ignore-changes --clean -r 'main@origin..@' -- ./check.sh
jj run -r 'stack(@)' -- ./format-and-test.sh

Without --ignore-changes, files written by the command can become commit rewrites and rebase descendants. The full execution model is in Part XVI.

jj gerrit upload

Uploads the selected commit stack to a Gerrit remote and branch. The principal selectors are -r/--revision REVSET, -b/--remote-branch BRANCH, --remote REMOTE, and -n/--dry-run. Reviewer, CC, label, topic, hashtag, WIP/ready/private, notification, validation, attention-set, deadline, custom, and repeated --option flags alter server-facing review metadata.

jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

Local jj change IDs do not replace Gerrit Change-Id footers. Server acceptance, patch-set association, and permissions remain Gerrit-dependent.

jj util backend name

Prints the active backend name for diagnostics. It does not report colocation, Git remote configuration, or the current workspace. Pair it with jj git colocation status and jj version in an environment report.

jj util backend name

jj redo

Reapplies the most recently undone operation when redo state is available. It is repository-operation recovery, not replay of a Git reflog entry.

jj undo
jj redo

Any unrelated mutating operation can affect the redo chain. Inspect operation log before assuming redo is available or safe.

Utilities and workspaces

jj util

Contains infrequently used administrative and generation commands: backend, completion, config-schema, exec, gc, install-man-pages, markdown-help, and snapshot. Treat this namespace as an implementation/tooling surface; commands can be version-sensitive and some can affect repository maintenance or external files.

jj util completion bash
jj util config-schema
jj util backend name

jj util backend and jj util backend name

Inspects backend information. The name subcommand prints the active backend name. These are diagnostics, not commands for changing commit content.

jj util backend name

Use this in bug reports and environment audits. A backend name does not describe colocation; check jj git colocation status separately.

jj util completion

Generates shell completion for the requested shell. It is output generation, not repository mutation.

jj util completion bash > ~/.local/share/bash-completion/completions/jj
jj util completion zsh > ~/.zfunc/_jj
jj util completion fish > ~/.config/fish/completions/jj.fish

Install the generated file according to the shell's search path, then start a new shell or source it. Regenerate after a jj upgrade because command and option surfaces change.

jj util config-schema

Prints the machine-readable configuration schema for the installed version. It is the validation authority for key types and structure, not a human explanation of how keys interact.

jj util config-schema > config-schema.json
jj util config-schema | jq '.properties | keys'

The book's Appendix F interprets significant keys for humans; keep this schema in the research artefacts when comparing 0.44 and 0.45. Do not treat every schema implementation detail as a stable public user-facing contract.

jj util exec

Executes a command in the repository/tooling context used by jj utilities. It is intended for internal or advanced integration use; ordinary shell commands should be run directly or through jj run when commits are the intended domain.

jj util exec -- command --arg

Read the installed help before scripting because argument forwarding and environment details are implementation-sensitive. It may have external side effects but does not by itself define a commit rewrite.

jj util gc

Runs repository object/storage garbage collection. It can remove unreachable data that would otherwise help recovery, so it is a maintenance boundary rather than a routine cleanup after every abandon.

jj op log
jj util gc

Run only after operation/recovery retention and remote publication state are understood. Garbage collection is distinct from commit abandonment, config GC, and Git remote pruning.

jj util install-man-pages

Installs generated manual pages for the current jj binary. It changes files outside the repository and should be run with an explicit destination/permission policy as shown by local help.

jj util install-man-pages --help

Regenerate after upgrading jj. Man pages are convenience documentation; local jj help remains the exact installed command authority.

jj util markdown-help

Generates Markdown help documentation from the installed command tree. This is useful for release audits and offline documentation, but generated output is not itself the authored book.

jj util markdown-help > jj-help.md

Keep it separate from the first-class HTML source. Compare generated help across binaries rather than silently mixing versions.

jj util snapshot

Forces or inspects a working-copy snapshot according to the installed utility help. It is useful in automation that deliberately owns snapshot timing, but it bypasses the convenient assumption that any ordinary jj command will establish the desired boundary.

jj util snapshot
jj status --ignore-working-copy

Coordinate with editors, build tools, and other jj processes. Snapshotting can create an operation and rewrite the working-copy commit; use the operation log when diagnosing unexpected changes.

jj workspace

Manages multiple working directories over one repository commit/operation store. Subcommands are add, forget, list, rename, root, and update-stale. Each workspace has a local @; a graph rewrite is shared.

jj workspace list
jj workspace add ../build -r @
jj workspace root
jj workspace update-stale

Use workspaces for build/review separation, not as lightweight bookmarks. Filesystem state and workspace metadata need a single-writer discipline.

jj workspace add

Creates a workspace directory. The destination is required. --name NAME chooses the workspace symbol; -r/--revision REVSETS supplies zero or more parents for its working-copy commit; -m/--message describes it; --sparse-patterns copy|full|empty controls sparse state and defaults to copy.

jj workspace add ../project-build --name build -r @ --sparse-patterns full
jj workspace add ../project-review --name review -r main@origin
jj workspace add ../project-ci -r @ --sparse-patterns empty

With no commits, the new working-copy commit shares the current workspace's parents; with explicit commits, it is like jj new r1 r2 .... The new workspace has a distinct @ and can be independently edited.

jj workspace forget

Forgets a workspace's metadata. It does not necessarily remove the directory or erase commits; inspect the filesystem and repository state separately before cleanup.

jj workspace list
jj workspace forget review

Do not forget a workspace whose unrecorded filesystem changes matter. A temporary bookmark or operation record can preserve graph recovery, but neither preserves uncommitted external files automatically.

jj workspace list

Lists workspaces and their working-copy commits. -T/--template renders WorkspaceRef fields such as name, target, and optional root.

jj workspace list
jj workspace list -T 'name ++ " " ++ target.commit_id() ++ "\n"'

Use it to find which workspace owns a commit before rewriting or updating stale state. A root path can be absent or stale.

jj workspace rename

Renames a workspace symbol. The directory path and commit graph are separate concerns; a renamed workspace changes symbols such as review@, not commits.

cd ../project-build
jj workspace rename ci
jj workspace list

Update scripts and templates that refer to the old workspace name. The operation remains in local operation history.

jj workspace root

Prints the root directory of the current workspace, equivalent to top-level jj root. It is useful from a nested subdirectory and differs from jj git root in a non-trivial backend layout.

jj workspace root
cd src && jj workspace root

jj workspace update-stale

Updates workspaces whose working-copy state is stale after shared graph changes. It is a repair/materialisation command, not a rebase selection command.

jj workspace update-stale
jj workspace list
jj -R ../project-build status

Inspect filesystem edits before updating. A stale workspace may contain useful unrecorded files, and update policy may materialise a new tree or conflict. Use a temporary copy/bookmark when the state is valuable.

Global option semantics

OptionOperational meaningUse/caution
-R/--repository PATHSelects a repository from any directory.Useful for workspaces/scripts; it does not select a Git remote.
--ignore-working-copyDoes not snapshot or update the filesystem.Reads a possibly stale commit; implied by --at-op.
--no-integrate-operationCreates an operation without integrating it into the current op head.Prints an ID for later integration; external side effects still occur.
--ignore-immutableDisables immutable rewrite protection.Use only with explicit audit/recovery intent.
--at-operation OPLoads a historical repository view.Read-only investigation; cannot snapshot today's filesystem into that view.
--color MODEControls colour/debug output.Use never in scripts; debug reveals labels.
--no-pagerDisables pager.Use for automation and captured fixture output.
--config NAME=VALUEApplies an inline TOML override.Repeatable; shell and TOML quoting both apply.
--config-file PATHLoads an additional config file.It is input, not necessarily the scope file selected by config edit.
--debugEnables diagnostic output/behaviour.Useful for bug reports, not stable machine output.

Global options are parsed before/alongside command options according to jj's CLI parser. Repeatable options such as --config accumulate. When combining a historical view, --ignore-working-copy is implied and filesystem state is deliberately not reconciled. When using --no-integrate-operation, the command's repository operation exists but another process may still advance the integrated operation head before you integrate it.

jj converge 0.45.x

Purpose and model. Groups visible commits by change ID in a selected search space and attempts to converge divergent evolutions. It is a newer command and is not available in the normative 0.44.0 CLI.

Syntax/options. Use jj converge -r REVSET; the default search space is controlled by the 0.45 revset configuration. --no-interactive suppresses prompts and asks the heuristic convergence to proceed without interactive decisions. Check the 0.45.1 local help for the complete current option surface.

State effects. Convergence rewrites commits and can rebase descendants, move local bookmarks that point at divergent successors, and create conflicts. It is not a remote merge and does not resolve Gerrit or GitHub review identity. Review jj op show and jj evolog after it; use jj undo if the selected convergence was not intended.

jj converge -r 'divergent()'
jj converge --no-interactive -r 'mutable()'
jj op show
jj evolog

Version note. In a shared 0.44/0.45 workflow, replace this command with explicit divergence inspection and manual graph surgery on 0.44. Never hide the version requirement in a generic alias.

Global options

The 0.44 global surface includes -R/--repository, --ignore-working-copy, --no-integrate-operation, --ignore-immutable, --at-operation/--at-op, --debug, --color, --quiet, --no-pager, repeatable --config, and repeatable --config-file, plus -h/--help and -V/--version. The raw help capture is the option index because defaults and accepted values are version-sensitive.

0.45.x comparison track

Verified against jj 0.45.1. The recursive help diff adds jj converge, which groups divergent visible commits by change ID and attempts an interactive or heuristic convergence; use jj undo or jj op show to review it. It adds --file PATH to jj config edit/set/unset, adds tracked_remote_tags() and untracked_remote_tags(), and includes untracked remote tags in the default immutable-head definition. The documented non-Windows pager default changes from less -FRX to less -FRXK. Gerrit configuration documents gerrit.default-remote, gerrit.default-remote-branch, and gerrit.review-url. These are 0.45.x notes, not 0.44 requirements.
Task0.44.00.45.x
Converge divergenceUse evolog, inspect divergent commits, manually edit/resolve, and recover with operation tools.jj converge -r REVSET; --no-interactive suppresses prompts.
Edit a particular config fileUse the scope flags; --config-file loads extra config for a command.jj config set --file PATH NAME VALUE and corresponding edit/unset.
Remote tag predicatesNo tracked/untracked remote tag functions in the captured help.Use tracked_remote_tags() and untracked_remote_tags().
Gerrit defaultsPass --remote and --remote-branch for portable scripts.The dedicated documented config keys provide defaults; explicit flags remain clearest.

Command contracts: how to predict side effects

The command reference is easiest to use when every invocation is classified along four axes: snapshot, selection, rewrite, and publication. Before running a command, ask four questions:

  1. Will jj read the filesystem and possibly create/update the workspace commit?
  2. What is the commit cardinality: zero-or-one default, exactly one, or arbitrary set?
  3. Does the command rewrite selected commits, their descendants, or only metadata?
  4. Can it move a bookmark, import/export a Git ref, or perform network I/O?

The answer predicts most side effects. For example, jj log -r 'all()' is a set-valued observation and normally does not rewrite commits. jj edit A is singleton-valued, snapshots before moving the workspace, and changes filesystem state. jj rebase -s 'B::D' -d main@origin is set-valued source selection, graph-rewriting, descendant-affecting, and non-networked. jj git push --bookmark review --remote origin is ref-publication and networked; it does not rewrite the local commit graph merely because the server accepts it.

DimensionInspection exampleMutation exampleQuestion to record in a script
Filesystem snapshotstatus, diffnew, describe, commitAre unsaved edits part of the intended operation?
Commit graphlog, showrebase, split, abandonWhich source set and destination set?
Content ownershipdiff, interdiffsquash, restore, absorbWhich tree contributes which paths?
Local refsbookmark listbookmark move/set/deleteWhich name should point where after the operation?
Remote stategit fetch, remote bookmarksgit push, Gerrit uploadWhich remote and publication ref are explicit?
Operation stateop log/show/diffundo, op restore/revertCan this be reversed and how will it be identified?

Rewriting commands: source, destination, and ownership

rebase, new, duplicate, parallelize, and simplify-parents change parent relationships. squash, absorb, restore, diffedit, and split change tree/patch ownership. describe, metaedit, and signing commands change metadata. The categories overlap in descendant effects, but keeping them separate prevents the wrong mental shortcut.

For a parent-edge operation, write the old and desired edge before selecting flags:

old:  A--B--C--D
want: A--C--B--D       (reorder)
want: A--B----D        (remove C contribution)
want: A--B--C
           \--D       (fork/parallelise)

Then choose the operation that expresses that edge change. Do not start with “which Git command resembles this?” Git’s interactive rebase todo is a user interface for several transformations; jj exposes those transformations separately and applies them to commit sets.

Rebase destination modes

In 0.44, -o/--onto and its destination spelling place source commits on a new parent. -A/--insert-after and -B/--insert-before express relative placement. The aliases in the local help are important because examples copied from different jj releases can use different long names. Use the long form in the book’s prose and include the short form only where it improves terminal ergonomics.

jj rebase --revisions 'C::D' --onto main@origin
jj rebase --source C --insert-after B
jj rebase --branch C --insert-before D
jj rebase -r 'C::D' -d main@origin

A source set and a destination set can each contain multiple commits. Multiple destinations can create a merge-shaped graph; multiple sources can move a disconnected set only when the command’s topology rules permit it. Inspect the result with jj log -r 'all()' and a parent template. The visual graph alone can hide that a node gained two parents.

jj log -r 'C::D' -T '
commit_id.short() ++ " parents=" ++
parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(",") ++ "\n"
'

Content commands: tree endpoints versus patch endpoints

restore copies tree content; squash moves a patch into another change; diffedit changes a selected patch through an editor. The commands may produce similar final files but differ in which logical change owns the content and which description/evolution receives the successor.

B tree: implementation
C tree: implementation + tests

restore from C into B:
  B′ tree = implementation + tests
  C may be unchanged

squash C into B:
  B′ owns implementation + tests
  C becomes empty/removed according to operation

diffedit C:
  C′ patch is edited; B is not destination

When a user says “bring these changes into B”, ask whether the source should remain, whether its logical review should survive, and whether the selected paths are a patch or a complete tree. Those answers determine the command.

Inspection and stable output

log is a graph-oriented view; show is a commit-oriented view; diff is a tree/patch view; status is a workspace/filesystem view. They answer different questions even when they display overlapping information.

NeedCommandWhy not the nearest alternative?
Find a topology or refjj log -r REVSETshow focuses on one commit and may hide the surrounding graph.
Inspect one commit’s metadata/treejj show REVlog may omit the full patch and file state.
Understand patch ownershipjj diff --from X --to Ystatus only sees workspace differences.
Understand disk versus @jj statuslog can snapshot or omit the filesystem dimension.
Understand a rewritejj op diff and jj evologCommit IDs alone do not explain successor relationships.

For machine output, use a template with explicit field delimiters and a controlled revset. Include full IDs when the output is evidence; use short IDs only for human reports. Never parse graph characters, colour labels, or the default description wrapping. The default template is a user interface and can change between releases.

Global options and their combinations

--ignore-working-copy is appropriate for a monitoring process that must not snapshot a half-written build tree. It can also make a human answer stale if unrecorded filesystem edits matter. --at-operation provides a historical repository view; combining it with the live filesystem is usually misleading because the files on disk belong to the current workspace, not the historical operation.

jj --ignore-working-copy log -r 'all()'
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'all()'
jj --at-operation OP --ignore-working-copy show @

--ignore-immutable changes the permitted rewrite boundary and should be visible in logs. --no-integrate-operation changes operation-head integration and should be used only by tooling that records the resulting operation. --repository changes the repository/workspace context; it does not turn a path into a revset. --config and --config-file alter interpretation and are therefore part of the command’s reproducibility record.

Command failure taxonomy

Most jj errors fall into a small set of categories:

  • Resolution: a symbol is unknown or a prefix is ambiguous. Inspect refs, IDs, and aliases.
  • Cardinality: a command expected one commit but got zero or several. Narrow the revset and print IDs.
  • Policy: a rewrite reaches an immutable commit or a push violates ref safety. Do not bypass until intent is established.
  • Tree/fileset: a path is absent, sparse, conflicted, or outside the selected tree. Inspect with file list and diff.
  • Editor/tool: an external program failed or returned an invalid result. Re-run with a builtin or explicit tool.
  • Transport: credentials, remote URLs, server permissions, or non-fast-forward state failed. Fetch and inspect before retrying.
  • Operation concurrency: an operation was created outside the integrated head or another writer changed state. Inspect operation history and integrate deliberately.

A failed command may have performed an automatic snapshot before reporting its main error. Check jj op log rather than assuming failure means no repository state changed. The operation record is the authoritative place to determine what was committed locally.

Version-aware CLI use

Scripts supporting both 0.44.0 and 0.45.x should probe the binary or branch on a documented version matrix. Do not test for a feature by running a destructive command and interpreting its error as a capability probe. The safe pattern is to compare jj version, inspect help in installation tests, and select a command alias or argument set accordingly.

case "$("$JJ" version | sed -n 's/^jj \\([0-9][^ ]*\\).*/\1/p')" in
  0.44.*) # no converge, no --file config target
           ;;
  0.45.*) # converge and config --file available
           ;;
  *)       printf 'unsupported jj version\n' >&2; exit 2 ;;
esac

For this edition, 0.44.0 is normative and 0.45.1 is the comparison binary. The 0.45 additions are intentionally marked at their use sites. Readers should rerun the inventory scripts after upgrading because command options, builtin templates, and schemas are not frozen interfaces.

Critical command dossiers

The per-command entries are deliberately uniform. The following dossiers go deeper for commands whose consequences routinely cross all four state dimensions: rewrite, content, refs, and publication. Use them when a command is important enough to deserve a pre-flight plan.

jj rebase: a pre-flight and post-flight protocol

  1. Snapshot or deliberately ignore the working copy. Do not accidentally include a half-edited file in the operation.
  2. Resolve each source revset and print full commit/change IDs. Confirm there are no unintended forks.
  3. Resolve each destination and inspect immutable heads. Decide whether a multiple destination means a merge.
  4. Create a temporary bookmark at the stack tip when recovery matters.
  5. Run the rebase with long option names and explicit source/destination.
  6. Inspect parent lists, conflicts, bookmarks, and descendant IDs.
  7. Run tests in the final workspace and only then move/push publication refs.
jj status
jj log -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::' --no-graph -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id ++ "\n"'
jj bookmark create safety/pre-rebase -r @
jj rebase --branch 'roots(main@origin..@)' --onto main@origin
jj log -r 'safety/pre-rebase | ::@' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

If the source is a fork, roots(main@origin..@) may select more than one root. That can be correct for a stack with a merge, or a sign that the query is too broad. The command will not infer the publication policy from your bookmark names.

jj split: commit boundary design

Split is a way to turn patch ownership into an explicit sequence. Let X be a mixed change. The selected files/hunks become X_1, and the remainder becomes X_2 or stays in the residual working-copy change according to the command’s mode. Descendants are rebased onto the new boundary.

before:
A--X(mixed implementation + docs)--Y(tests)--@

after:
A--X_1(implementation)--X_2(docs)--Y′(tests)--@′

The intended split must be stated in terms of review responsibility: which commit should be independently revertible, which files need to build together, and whether a descendant test should depend on both. A path-only split can produce a commit that does not compile; an interactive hunk split can create a logically incomplete first change. Use jj diff and run the project’s smallest valid test at each resulting node.

jj split -r X --interactive
jj split -r X -- 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj log -r 'X::@'
jj diff -r 'roots(X::@)::@'

The -p/--parallel option changes the resulting arrangement when the selected and remaining parts should be parallel rather than parent/child. Use it only when both parts have the same intended parent and the review system can represent the resulting publication layout.

jj squash: explicit patch transfer

Squash has a source change and a destination change. Its default source/destination are convenient for a current child, but advanced usage should name both. A fileset limits which patch paths are transferred, leaving a residual patch in the source.

A--B(API)--C(tests + fixture)--D(docs)

partial squash C into B, fileset tests/:
A--B′(API + tests)--C′(fixture)--D′(docs)

jj squash --from C --into B -- 'root-glob:tests/**'
jj diff -r B
jj diff -r C
jj log -r 'B::D'

Use --use-destination-message when the destination description should be retained by the result; otherwise determine how descriptions are combined in the installed release and edit them explicitly. Empty source changes are not automatically a problem: they may be useful review boundaries or may indicate that the selection was wrong. Inspect before abandoning.

jj restore: tree source and destination

Restore answers “which paths in which destination should become equal to the source tree?” This is distinct from moving a commit pointer. It can restore the current working copy from a parent, copy a file from another commit, or restore the full tree of a commit into another change.

jj restore --from B --into @ -- 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj diff -r @ -- 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj restore --from 'main@origin' --into @ -- 'root-glob:docs/**'

When restoring into an ancestor or a commit with descendants, the destination evolves and descendants may rebase. When restoring from a conflicted source, inspect the source’s conflict state and do not assume a concrete file value exists. The command’s --changes-in form is a different selection dimension: it asks which changes’ content to restore, rather than simply naming one source tree.

jj bookmark: ref state machine

Use create to introduce a local name, set to create or move it to a target, move to move an existing name, and advance to move it forward according to a source/destination policy. track establishes a local-to-remote relationship; untrack removes that relationship; forget removes local knowledge; delete expresses local deletion for publication.

jj bookmark create pr/cache -r C
jj bookmark track pr/cache --remote origin
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark move pr/cache --to D
jj bookmark set pr/cache -r D
jj bookmark forget pr/cache
jj bookmark delete pr/cache

Before deleting or forgetting, decide whether a remote ref should be deleted. Forgetting can be useful when stale remote knowledge should be re-fetched; it is not a server deletion request. Deleting a tracked local bookmark can generate a deletion to push, depending on the command and remote state. Read the status and push preview.

jj log -r 'bookmarks() | remote_bookmarks()' -T
  'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ bookmarks ++ " " ++ remote_bookmarks ++ "\n"'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache --dry-run

jj git push: publication transaction

Push is a transaction over refs, not over change IDs. It selects local bookmarks/tags, compares them with remote state, uploads reachable Git objects, and updates remote refs subject to safety and server policy. The local commit graph remains a graph of objects/evolutions; a successful push adds an external observation of one name at one tip.

jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache --dry-run
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache
jj git push --remote origin --tracked
jj git push --remote origin --deleted
jj git push --remote origin --tag v2.4.0

Never use --tracked or --all in a generic hook unless the publication set is an explicit policy. A local bookmark can be tracked but not intended for today’s push; a remote with multiple namespaces can contain stale names. For a force update, compare the remote target immediately before the command and understand whether the server’s lease/safety check is sufficient for your race model.

jj absorb: inference and review

Absorb is most useful after a stack has acquired small fixups in its tip. It maps changes to ancestors according to patch applicability and file ownership. Because inference can differ from a human’s intended review boundary, treat the result like a proposed refactoring:

jj bookmark create safety/pre-absorb -r @
jj diff -r @
jj absorb --interactive -- 'root-glob:src/**'
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj op show

Review descriptions, empty commits, moved hunks, and test behaviour. Use squash when the destination is known. Undo the whole absorb operation rather than manually reconstructing the old stack if the attribution is wrong.

jj resolve: selecting a semantic result

Resolve is the point where a conflict value becomes a concrete tree. List conflicts first. For each path, identify the intended invariant, inspect all sides, choose or merge content, save, and run a test that exercises the conflict boundary.

jj resolve --list -r @
jj resolve --tool=:ours -r @
jj resolve --tool=:theirs -r @
jj resolve --tool=:builtin -r @
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

The built-in tool or configured external tool may show base, left, right, and additional sides. Choosing ours/theirs is a policy decision and can silently discard a side. After resolution, descendants may still be conflicted; query the whole stack.

Command contracts: how to read an advanced jj command

A command reference is useful only when it describes the state transition, not just the spelling. For each command, ask four questions: what repository value is selected, what is rewritten or observed, which workspace is affected, and which refs or remote state remain unchanged. The same global option can alter several commands: normal snapshotting may turn an apparently read-only invocation into an operation, --ignore-working-copy reads a stale recorded tree, --at-operation selects a historical repository view, and --ignore-immutable changes only rewrite protection.

Contract fieldQuestion to answerEvidence to show
SelectionDoes the command accept one commit, a set, a range, or a fileset?Syntax and a cardinality/error example.
MutationDoes it create, rewrite, hide, or merely render?Before/after commit IDs and operation entry.
DescendantsWill parent identity changes rebase children?Graph showing successor IDs or conflicts.
WorkspaceDoes it move/edit @, materialise files, or leave the filesystem?Workspace-local log and filesystem check.
RefsDoes it move bookmarks/tags or only alter commits?Local/remote ref listing before and after.
NetworkDoes it contact Git/Gerrit or change only local observations?Remote bookmark/ref state and explicit remote option.
RecoveryWhat operation ID and evolution entry does it create?op show and evolog evidence.

Rewrite commands

jj describe changes the description of one mutable commit, usually @. It preserves the tree but changes commit metadata and therefore the commit ID; descendants can receive successors. jj metaedit performs metadata surgery such as author/timestamp and can intentionally update the change ID. jj edit moves one workspace pointer to one existing commit; it does not create a successor until a snapshot or rewrite occurs. jj diffedit edits a patch through a diff editor. These commands look similar from a Git perspective but have different state targets.

jj describe -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)' -m 'API: reject negative quota'
jj metaedit -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)' --update-author-timestamp
jj edit 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj diffedit -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)' -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src"'

jj squash moves the current or selected patch into a destination change. A partial fileset makes it a content-ownership operation; an unselected hunk stays at the source boundary. The destination's description and change identity are not the source's by default. Descendants of the destination can be rebased, and the source may become empty. If the source should remain as a separate logical change, use duplicate or restore instead of squash.

jj split turns one change into multiple changes. It is a boundary operation: the first resulting change receives selected content and the second receives the remainder according to the mode. Existing descendants are rebased over the new sequence. Treat the interactive editor as a content selector, then verify with two diffs and a parent-edge query. A split is not “make a branch”; it changes the patch stack's granularity.

jj absorb routes working-copy changes into suitable mutable ancestors. It is powerful precisely because it is a policy-driven multi-target rewrite. Before using it, inspect which ancestors are mutable, which files each owns, and whether the current change contains unrelated work. Use a narrow revset/fileset and an operation checkpoint. If a hunk matches several ancestors or no ancestor, the command's behaviour follows its configured applicability rules; do not promise ownership without inspecting the result.

jj duplicate creates a new logical change with copied content. It intentionally breaks evolution continuity. jj abandon removes a commit from ordinary visible work and reconnects or updates dependants according to graph rules. jj restore copies tree content or reverses a selected change. jj revert creates a new inverse change. The last two are both content operations but differ in whether the inverse is applied to an existing destination or represented as a new commit.

Rebase as a two-dimensional contract

jj rebase has a source dimension and a destination dimension. Source -r/--revisions X selects explicit commits; -s/--source X selects X and descendants; -b/--branch X selects the branch of X relative to the destination. Destination -o/--onto Y places the selected source on Y; repeated destinations create multiple parents where supported; -A/--insert-after Y inserts selected changes after Y; -B/--insert-before Y inserts them before Y. The names are not cosmetic: changing -r to -s can turn a one-commit repair into a whole-stack rewrite.

before: base--A--B--C
                  \
                   X--Y

-r B -o X:     base--A--B'--C'     and X--B' if destination is X
-s B -o X:     base--A--B'--C'     all descendants selected/rebased
-r B -A X:     base--A--X--B'--C'  insert the selected patch after X
-r B -B X:     base--A--B'--X'--Y' rebase X and descendants after B'

The diagrams show the shape, not a promise that every shorthand combination is accepted by every release. Check local help and inspect the selected set before execution. A source with holes may preserve an edge to an unselected parent, may be rejected, or may cause a split of the selected roots according to the rebase algorithm. This is why jj log -r SOURCE, jj log -G -r SOURCE, and a temporary bookmark are useful preconditions.

After a rebase, compare four things: each selected change ID, each new commit ID, each descendant's patch, and every affected bookmark. Then query conflicts and operation detail:

jj log -G -r 'SOURCE | descendants(SOURCE)'
jj evolog -r 'SOURCE'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & descendants(SOURCE)'
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj op show -p

Inspection and diff commands

jj log accepts a many-valued revset and renders a graph or a template. It normally snapshots first, so “inspection” is not synonymous with “no operation”. Use --ignore-working-copy for a stable recorded view and --no-pager --color=never for automation. jj show focuses on one commit's metadata, tree, and patch; it is a good cardinality check before editing. jj diff compares a commit's patch, endpoints, or selected set and accepts a fileset independently.

jj log -G -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj show -r 'exactly(change_id(API_CHANGE), 1)'
jj diff --from main@origin --to @ -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src"'
jj diff -r @ --summary --no-pager

jj interdiff compares two versions of a logical patch, not two arbitrary branch tips. Use it after a rewrite when the commit IDs differ but the review question is “what changed between patch set 1 and patch set 2”. jj evolog -p answers a related question by following a change's evolution and showing predecessor/inter-diff information.

Bookmarks and tags

bookmark create creates a local name at a commit; set creates or moves a name; move moves an existing name; advance moves it to a descendant under the command's safety rules; rename changes the local name; delete expresses local deletion suitable for publication; forget removes local knowledge without being the same as a remote delete; track/untrack manage a relationship with a remote bookmark; and list renders current state. None of these commands makes a bookmark follow @ automatically.

jj bookmark create pr/cache -r @
jj bookmark set pr/cache -r 'change_id(CACHE_CHANGE)'
jj bookmark move pr/cache --to @
jj bookmark track pr/cache --remote origin
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj bookmark forget pr/cache

Tags are release-oriented names with their own command family. A tag's presence in an immutable-head policy can protect an older commit even when no local bookmark reaches it. Fetch and tag tracking affect local observations; moving a tag is an intentional release/ref change. Display local and remote target states separately before any deletion or force publication.

Git transport commands

jj git clone creates a jj repository from a Git remote; git init initialises the backend and can colocate with an existing Git repository; git fetch imports remote objects and updates remote bookmarks; git push exports selected bookmarks/commits; git import notices Git-side ref changes; and git export writes jj refs/objects to the Git view where required. git root identifies the Git root and git remote manages the transport configuration. Colocation makes native Git visible but creates a second writer boundary.

jj git remote list
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git import
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/cache
GIT_DIR=$(jj git root) git show-ref

A fetch changes local remote observations, not the server and not the local mutable graph. A push is external side effect and may be rejected; inspect tracking and commit ancestry first. Explicit remote and bookmark selectors are safer than broad defaults, especially when GitHub and Gerrit coexist.

Operation, configuration, and workspace contracts

Operation commands act on the operation DAG. op log lists transformations; op show explains one; op diff compares views; op restore restores selected repository state; op revert reverses a selected operation; op abandon prunes an operation record; and op integrate joins an unintegrated operation. The op spelling is an alias, but the canonical family is operation in the 0.44 command tree.

config get/list/path are read operations with different output contracts; set/unset/edit mutate configuration; gc removes obsolete config entries according to its rules. A config command can target a scope or load an additional file. In 0.45.x, --file provides direct file targeting for relevant subcommands; do not use it in 0.44 scripts.

workspace add creates a second working directory over the shared store; list displays records; root reports the current directory's workspace root; rename changes the current workspace name and takes one new name in 0.44; update-stale repairs stale workspace state; and forget removes metadata without abandoning its commits. Treat workspace filesystem updates as a separate side effect from graph rewrites.

Content, conflict, and maintenance commands

file list/show/search/annotate inspect trees and content; file track/untrack change snapshot tracking; file chmod changes executable metadata. sparse set/list/edit/reset controls materialisation, with set accepting patterns and edit opening an editor without positional patterns. resolve operates on stored conflicts; fix applies configured fixers; sign/unsign rewrite cryptographic metadata; bisect and bisect run perform graph search using script exit status; and run executes a command over selected commits, potentially rewriting them unless changes are ignored.

Administrative commands have external or destructive boundaries. util gc can remove recovery data; util completion writes shell output; util install-man-pages writes files outside the repository; util config-schema produces a machine-readable schema; util snapshot controls snapshotting; and util exec forwards commands according to its own contract. Always read the installed help for a utility before placing it in automation.

A reader's command validation protocol

  1. Run the exact command with --help under the target jj binary.
  2. Identify revset and fileset positions and quote them as one shell argument.
  3. Build the smallest graph that exposes source, destination, descendants, refs, or conflicts.
  4. Record filesystem state and the working-copy commit before invocation.
  5. Run the command and capture the operation ID.
  6. Compare graph edges, commit IDs, change IDs, bookmarks, remote bookmarks, and conflicts.
  7. Inspect op show and evolog when the command rewrites.
  8. Repeat the experiment in 0.45.1 only when the version note claims a changed surface.

This protocol is deliberately more demanding than a successful exit status. It is the basis of the command inventory and the semantic validation scripts shipped with the project.

Part XVI — Advanced mechanics

Immutability and safety boundaries

Configure immutable heads to protect imported/public history. The check applies to rewrites; the revset/template notions of immutable state remain observable even with --ignore-immutable. Hidden commits and divergent change IDs are useful forensic states, not garbage to delete reflexively.

Evolution, concurrency, and operation merging

Run concurrent read commands with a clear snapshot policy. --no-integrate-operation lets automation create an operation without changing the integrated operation head; it does not suppress side effects such as a network push. Inspect and integrate deliberately.

jj fix and jj run

Fix tools are configured programs that transform files. jj fix can operate over a commit set and may rewrite a stack. In 0.43, jj run gained private working copies for each selected change, allowing checks or formatters to update changes and propagate conflicts. Treat command exit status and operation boundaries as part of your automation contract.

jj run -r 'stack(@)' -- cargo test
jj run -r 'mutable() & description(substring:"API")' -- cargo fix
jj fix -r 'mutable()'

Bisect, tags, signing, completion, and maintenance

jj bisect run automates commit bisection; tags provide stable release names; jj sign/unsign manage supported cryptographic metadata; jj util completion generates shell completion. Use jj util gc and backend maintenance only after understanding operation/recovery retention. In large repositories, narrow revsets, use immutable remote boundaries, avoid rendering enormous diffs, and use --ignore-working-copy for stable monitoring reads.

Machine-readable output

Templates plus --color never, explicit separators, and a controlled revset are the most portable interface. Do not parse the human graph glyphs. Pin aliases and templates in a versioned config, and run the inventory/fixture checks after upgrading.

Advanced mechanics in practice

Immutable heads are a policy boundary

Immutability is rewrite protection expressed as a revset. It is not the same thing as committed, pushed, on a branch, or old. A mutable commit can be published, and an immutable commit can be local. The useful policy question is which graph boundary must not be rewritten by an ordinary command.

jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'
jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@'
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..@)' -o main@origin

If a source set reaches an immutable commit, jj protects it unless --ignore-immutable is supplied. That flag is an explicit override, not a repair to the graph. Save the operation ID, explain which boundary is being crossed, and verify the result with operation and evolution logs.

StateOrdinary rewritePublication meaning
Mutable, unpublishedUsually permitted.No remote ref necessarily exists.
Mutable, publishedLocally permitted; push may need a safe non-fast-forward update.Server sees a Git ref, not jj mutability.
Immutable imported trunkRejected without explicit override.Remote bookmark is an observation and likely safety boundary.
Hidden predecessorNot in ordinary visible selection.May remain recovery evidence.

Hidden commits, divergent changes, and evolution

Abandoning a commit hides it from ordinary visible history but does not immediately erase its objects. Rewriting a change creates successors, and independent successors can be divergent. These states let jj preserve evidence instead of forcing every rewrite to destroy it.

jj log -r 'hidden()'
jj log -r 'divergent()'
jj evolog -r 'change_id(abc...)'
jj log -r 'all() & present(commit_id("prefix"))'

A hidden predecessor may be reachable from an operation view or evolution entry. A divergent change ID can have several visible versions. Use an explicit offset or full commit ID when a command requires one commit. Do not select an ambiguous change ID merely because a log can display the set.

Concurrency and operation integration

Jj operations support concurrent processes, but that is not permission for multiple processes to mutate one working directory without coordination. --no-integrate-operation creates an operation and prints its ID without advancing the integrated operation head.

jj status --no-integrate-operation
# record OP_PRINTED
jj --at-op OP_PRINTED log -r 'all()'
jj op integrate OP_PRINTED

The operation can still have external side effects: a Git push reaches a server, an editor writes files, and a fixer modifies a private working copy. Integrating later does not roll those effects back. A concurrent operation can advance the current head before integration; inspect jj op log and jj op diff first.

For concurrent read-only automation, use --ignore-working-copy with explicit templates. For concurrent filesystem writers, assign one process to one workspace. A build workspace and a development workspace can run simultaneously because their filesystems differ; two processes snapshotting the same workspace remain a race.

Fixers and modified-line protocols

A fixer is a configured program invoked over commits, often with a protocol describing changed lines. This can update a stack without rewriting unrelated historical lines, but only when the program supports the protocol correctly.

[fix.tools.rustfmt]
command = ["rustfmt", "--emit", "stdout"]
patterns = ["glob:*.rs"]

jj fix -s 'roots(main@origin..@)::' -- 'root-prefix-glob:"src/**"'

Validate the exact fixer keys and protocol against the target schema and help. Use --all-lines when the tool cannot safely act on modified ranges. Use --include-unchanged-files only when a repository-wide invariant is intentional: it can rewrite substantially more history.

Fix can affect many mutable commits and descendants. Run it on a small stack, inspect changed paths and descriptions, record the operation ID, and undo before another fix if the result is too broad.

jj run as a graph-aware test harness

jj run has two distinct modes. With --ignore-changes it tests commit states. Without it, files written by the command are recorded back into selected commits, so it is a transformation tool. Reused private working copies preserve build artefacts; --clean removes that state.

GoalControlsReason
Check commits--ignore-changes --clean --passthroughNo rewrite; clean state; interactive output.
Apply formatterNarrow -r, intentional rewrite, explicit tool.Scope and graph effects are visible.
Parallel tests-j N, captured output.Avoid interleaved logs.
Interactive tool--passthrough, one job.TTY-like output; stdin is not inherited.
jj run --ignore-changes --clean -r 'main@origin..@' -- ./ci/check.sh
jj run -j 4 --ignore-changes -r 'heads(mutable())' -- ./ci/check.sh
jj run -r 'stack(@)' -- ./format-current-tree.sh

The current directory is the invocation directory unless --root is supplied. Use -- before command arguments beginning with a dash. Capture exit status and inspect the operation when changes are permitted.

Bisect, signing, and release boundaries

Bisection assumes a monotonic predicate over the selected graph range. Merge-heavy graphs, non-deterministic tests, and environment-dependent failures violate that assumption. Define the range, make the script exit status unambiguous, and preserve the discovered commit before leaving the investigation.

jj bisect run --range 'good..bad' -- ./test-regression.sh
jj show -r 'bisect(@)'

jj sign and jj unsign rewrite metadata. Signing an ancestor changes descendant commit IDs even when tree content is unchanged. Presence of a signature is cheaper to query than status/key/display, which can invoke GPG or SSH verification.

jj sign -r 'heads(bookmarks())'
jj log -r 'signed()' -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ if(signature, signature.status(), "unknown") ++ "\n"'
jj unsign -r 'mutable() & signed()'

Tags name release points. Treat them as immutable policy even when --allow-move exists. Remote tag tracking is separate from bookmark tracking; 0.45.1 adds tracked/untracked remote-tag predicates, while 0.44 scripts must use the supported forms from local help.

jj tag set v2.4.0 -r @
jj tag list --all-remotes
jj git push --remote origin --tag 'v2.4.0'

Performance and machine-readable output

Cost is driven by selected graph size, tree/diff materialisation, external tools, signature verification, remote URL work, and rendering. Narrow the revset before optimising the template. A template that calls diff().git() for every commit can be much slower than one that renders IDs and subjects.

ConcernTechnique
Prompt latency--ignore-working-copy -r @, no patch, cached output.
Large log-n, heads(), explicit template, no full diff.
Slow signaturesQuery signed(); verify selected commits only.
Large fix/runNarrow commits/fileset; control jobs and clean/reuse.
Recovery retentionDo not GC until operation/evolution evidence is no longer needed.

For automation use explicit revsets, templates, --color=never, --no-pager, a pinned jj binary, and a fixture test. Human graph glyphs and default templates are presentation, not a stable API.

Fixers as controlled graph transformations

jj fix is not merely a formatter command. It can run configured tools over selected commits and turn their filesystem changes into successor commits. Its safe use therefore combines a revset, a fileset/pattern policy, a tool protocol, and a review of descendant effects.

selected stack:
A--B--C--@       each has Rust files

fix over 'mutable() & ::@':
A′--B′--C′--@′    if each fixer result is clean
       or
A′--B′[conflict]--C′[conflict]--@′

Fixers should be idempotent: running them twice should produce no new content after the first successful run. They should preserve files outside their patterns, return a meaningful exit status, and not depend on the user’s editor or network. A tool that rewrites generated files or line endings must be explicitly scoped. Run it on one commit in a fixture before applying it to mutable().

jj fix -r @
jj diff -r @
jj op show
jj fix -r 'mutable() & ::@' -- 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'

The exact fixer table belongs in configuration and the command’s accepted fileset form is version-sensitive. The invariant is independent: select commits first, select paths second, run a deterministic tool, then inspect the full evolved stack.

jj run: commit-scoped automation

jj run provides a controlled way to execute a shell command in private working copies for a commit set. This is useful for tests, formatters, and checks that need each commit’s tree rather than the current workspace. The private workspaces are an implementation mechanism; the important contract is whether filesystem changes are imported back as commit rewrites.

ModeUseGraph risk
--ignore-changesRun a read-only check or discard tool writes.Low graph mutation; tool can still affect external state.
defaultAllow command changes to update selected commits.Successors and descendant rebases are possible.
--cleanDo not reuse prior private workspaces.More predictable, slower, avoids stale generated files.
-j NRun independent commits concurrently.Output interleaves; tools must not share mutable external files.
--passthroughExpose one job’s live output.Incompatible with multi-job presentation.
--root PATHRun command at a chosen root.Relative paths and generated artefacts can differ.
jj run --ignore-changes --clean -j 4 -r 'main@origin..@' -- ./ci/check.sh
jj run --clean -r 'stack(@)' -- ./format.sh

Do not use an ordinary workspace path as a lock-free coordination directory for parallel jobs. A test that writes to /tmp/result, a shared compiler cache with unsafe locking, or a generated file outside the private root can make independent commits interfere. Capture each job’s status and map it back to the full commit ID. A successful command may still leave a failed graph rewrite if the tool’s exit status was misreported.

Bisect as a predicate search

Bisection is a search over a commit set given a monotonic predicate: there is a boundary where commits are good on one side and bad on the other. The graph need not be linear, but the selected range and the meaning of “between” must be clear. A test that is flaky or depends on an unrelated descendant invalidates the result.

jj bisect run --range 'good..bad' -- ./regression-test
# inspect the candidate
jj log -r 'bisect(@)'
jj show 'bisect(@)'

For a merge-heavy history, choose a first-parent or release corridor if the bug is associated with integration order. A query over all ancestors can find a commit that introduced the file change but not the first integration that made the system fail. Document the test environment, dependency versions, and whether the command mutates the candidate tree.

Signing and cryptographic evidence

Signing operates on a specific commit object. Rewriting description, metadata, parent, or tree produces a different object that needs independent verification/signing. A signature does not make a change immutable and does not make a bookmark safe to force-update. In a review workflow, sign immediately before publication or sign every successor as policy requires.

jj sign -r @ --key KEY
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ if(signature, signature.status(), "unsigned") ++ "\n"'
jj unsign -r @

Separate cryptographic evidence into three questions: does the object have a valid signature, does the key belong to the expected identity, and does the hosting server trust that identity? jj can answer the first and expose metadata for the second; GitHub/Gerrit policy answers the third. Hardware tokens and agents are external dependencies and are not exercised by this project’s non-interactive fixture.

Tags and release boundaries

Tags name release graph positions and are deliberately less fluid than review bookmarks. Treat a tag as an immutable publication boundary in policy even if the underlying ref can technically be moved. Fetch tags explicitly when a release query depends on them, and distinguish local tags, remote tags, tracked remote tags, and untracked remote tags in 0.45.x.

jj tag list
jj log -r 'tags()'
jj log -r 'remote_tags()'
# 0.45.x:
jj log -r 'tracked_remote_tags()'
jj log -r 'untracked_remote_tags()'

In 0.45.x the default immutable-head definition includes untracked remote tags; 0.44 lacks the new tag predicates in the captured help. This affects rewrite protection, not just display. A release script should use an explicit release tag and record the binary version rather than assuming that the default immutable set is identical across versions.

Large repositories and performance

Performance problems often come from asking for a broad graph, rendering a large patch, or repeatedly snapshotting a large working tree. Narrow the revset, use bounded limits, separate graph selection from patch display, and use --ignore-working-copy for repository-only monitoring. Do not solve a slow query by making its result semantically broader.

jj log -r 'heads(mutable() & ::@)' -n 30 --no-pager
jj log -r 'main@origin..@' --no-graph -T 'commit_id.short() ++ "\n"'
jj diff --stat --from main@origin --to @
jj status --ignore-working-copy

Filesystem monitoring, sparse working copies, and private run workspaces can improve latency, but they introduce state that must be inspected when results are surprising. Measure with representative repositories. A prompt that runs an all-history template on every shell command is a design problem even if jj itself is fast.

Debugging and bug reports

A reproducible jj bug report should include version, backend, repository layout (colocated or not), workspace name/root, operation ID, exact command with redacted paths, revset/fileset, configuration layers, and whether the filesystem contained unrecorded changes. Include jj op show and a minimal graph rendered with full IDs if the issue is a rewrite.

jj version
jj root
jj util backend name
jj git colocation status
jj workspace list
jj op log -n 10
jj config list --include-defaults
jj log -r 'all()' --no-graph -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id ++ "\n"'

Use --debug when requested by jj’s diagnostics, but redact credentials, remote URLs containing tokens, private descriptions, and filesystem paths. The operation log may contain descriptions and usernames; it is repository data, not automatically safe to attach publicly.

Secondary command dossiers

jj parallelize

Parallelize removes an ordering-only parent edge and gives selected commits a common parent. It is useful when a stack contains two changes that no longer depend on each other. It is not a visual rearrangement and not a promise that the resulting commits are independently buildable.

before: A--B--C
             \--D

parallelize C and D:
        +--C′
A--B----+
        \--D′

Inspect the selected commits and their parent relationships first. Descendants of C and D can be rewritten because their parent edges change. If a publication bookmark pointed at D, it still points at the D evolution, but its new ancestry may change what the host sees.

jj parallelize -r 'C|D'
jj log -r 'C|D|::C|::D' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(",") ++ "\n"'

jj simplify-parents

Simplify-parents removes redundant parent relationships while preserving the resulting tree where possible. It is most relevant to merge-shaped histories produced by repeated rebases, duplication, or multi-destination operations. Treat it as a graph normalisation operation, not as a general “make history pretty” command.

jj simplify-parents -r 'mutable() & ::@'
jj log -r 'merges(::@)'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@'

Its default selection is version/configuration-sensitive; 0.44 help describes source and commit selection and a configured default. A simplification can change commit IDs and descendant contexts even if the visible file tree remains equivalent. Save a safety bookmark and compare before/after parent lists.

jj converge — 0.45.x only

Converge groups divergent visible commits by change ID and attempts to produce one converged evolution. It is not available in the 0.44.0 baseline. Use it only after inspecting which evolutions exist, what each patch contains, and which description/metadata should survive.

# 0.45.x
jj log -r 'divergent()'
jj converge -r 'divergent()'
jj op show
jj evolog

The operation can require interactive decisions unless --no-interactive is supplied. Suppressing interaction does not make the heuristic semantically correct; it only changes how decisions are made. In a mixed-version team, use explicit 0.44 graph surgery and mark the difference in scripts.

jj next and jj prev

Navigation moves the workspace’s working-copy commit through nearby mutable commits. It changes filesystem state and can snapshot the current workspace. It does not create a bookmark and does not rewrite a commit merely because the workspace moved.

jj prev
jj next
jj next --edit
jj log -r 'stack(@)'

Use explicit jj edit REVSET when the destination must be one known logical change. Navigation is convenient for traversing a stack but can be ambiguous around forks and merges. After moving, inspect status because unrecorded filesystem changes are part of the snapshot boundary.

Reference dossier: jj tag

Tags name release or other stable graph positions. The subcommands delete, list, set, track, and untrack manipulate tag state and its remote observations. A tag is not a bookmark with a nicer name: it normally represents a release boundary and should be moved rarely.

jj tag set v2.4.0 -r @
jj tag list
jj tag track v2.4.0 --remote origin
jj tag untrack v2.4.0 --remote origin
jj tag delete v2.4.0

Tag deletion or movement is a ref publication decision. Inspect remote tags and immutable-head effects before rewriting a tagged ancestor. In 0.45.x, tracked/untracked remote tag revset predicates are available and untracked remote tags participate in the default immutable-head calculation; do not use those names in a 0.44 script.

jj file

The file namespace separates path-oriented inspection and filesystem tracking from commit graph operations. Its subcommands include annotate, chmod, list, search, show, track, and untrack.

jj file list -r @ 'root-glob:src/**'
jj file show -r @ 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj file annotate -r @ 'root-file:src/parser.rs'
jj file search -r '::@' 'root-glob:src/**' 'TODO'
jj file chmod +x 'root-file:scripts/check.sh'
jj file track 'root-glob:generated/**'
jj file untrack 'root-glob:tmp/**'

The exact position of commit, fileset, and search arguments is command-specific. file list answers what exists in a selected tree; file search answers where content matches; file annotate attributes lines to commits; file chmod changes executable metadata in the working-copy snapshot. A search result is not a patch selection and an annotation result is not a publication proof.

jj sparse

Sparse commands control materialisation in the current workspace. list displays patterns, set replaces them, edit changes them, and reset restores full materialisation or the configured default. Sparse state does not change the commit tree.

jj sparse list
jj sparse set 'root-glob:src/**'
jj sparse edit
jj sparse reset

Before a test or fixer, verify that all required inputs are materialised. A missing sparse path can look like a missing file in the commit, and a tool that recreates it can accidentally snapshot a new file. Keep sparse policies workspace-specific and record them in build logs.

Reference dossier: jj config

Configuration commands operate on layered TOML rather than the commit graph. get reads one value, list displays values, path identifies config locations, set writes a value, unset removes one, edit opens a file, and gc removes obsolete generated entries according to jj’s config policy.

jj config path
jj config get revsets.log
jj config list --include-defaults
jj config set --user user.email 'dev@example.org'
jj config unset --user ui.pager
jj config edit --repo
jj config gc
