Scope flags are part of the command contract. A user setting, repository setting, workspace setting, and command-line override can all have the same key but different effect. 0.45.x adds --file PATH targeting for config edit/set/unset; use scope flags and explicit files supported by 0.44 instead.

Reference dossier: jj git remote and colocation

The remote namespace manages URL/name records: add, list, remove, rename, and set-url. The colocation namespace manages whether the Git backend shares the working directory: enable, disable, and status.

jj git remote list
jj git remote add origin https://example.org/project.git
jj git remote set-url origin ssh://git@example.org/project
jj git remote rename origin upstream
jj git remote remove old
jj git colocation status
jj git colocation enable
jj git colocation disable

Renaming a remote changes how remote bookmark symbols are qualified and can invalidate scripts that refer to main@origin. Removing a remote does not erase commits already fetched. Disabling colocation changes native Git interoperability and should be coordinated with tools that expect .git in the project directory.

jj util administration

util markdown-help and util install-man-pages generate documentation; util completion generates shell completion; util config-schema emits the machine-readable schema; util snapshot exercises snapshotting; util exec runs an internal utility; util gc performs repository maintenance; and util backend name reports backend diagnostics.

Generation commands can write outside the repository if redirected by the shell; maintenance commands can make old objects or operation evidence harder to recover. Use them in a controlled environment and record the jj version. The inventory scripts in this project use help/schema capture rather than parsing generated prose.

Advanced mechanics laboratory: automation, signing, and maintenance

Running commands over commits

jj run provides temporary working environments for running a command against a commit set. It is useful for testing a stack, applying a deterministic fixer, or collecting a result at several graph points. It is not merely a loop around jj edit: the command can use private workspaces and, unless changes are ignored, files written by the process may become commit rewrites.

jj run --ignore-changes --clean -r 'main@origin..@' -- ./check.sh
jj run --ignore-changes -j 4 -r 'heads(mutable() & ::@)' -- ./test.sh
jj run --passthrough -r @ -- ./interactive-check.sh
Option/policyEffectUse when
--ignore-changesDo not record files written by the command as commit changes.Read-only tests or tools whose outputs are disposable.
--cleanDo not reuse existing private workspaces.Reproducible runs that must not inherit files.
-j NRun selected commits concurrently where supported.Independent deterministic tests; not a shared mutable worktree.
--passthroughConnect command output and constrain execution mode.Interactive diagnostics, not machine aggregation.
--rootChoose the command's working root.Scripts that must not depend on the caller's cwd.

Parallel execution requires test isolation. A test that writes to a shared cache, local database, or fixed temporary filename can make a graph query appear nondeterministic. Pin environment variables, use per-commit temporary directories, and record the selected commit in output. If the command is intended to rewrite commits, run one small fixture first and inspect its operation/evolution results.

Fixers are graph rewriters

jj fix applies configured fixer tools to selected commits and paths. A formatter is not automatically a safe no-op: line-ending changes, generated output, executable bits, and tool version changes alter commit trees and therefore commit IDs. A fixer that runs over an ancestor can trigger automatic descendant rebasing. Treat fixer configuration as both a tool policy and a graph-selection policy.

jj fix -r 'mutable() & ::@ & ~conflicts()' -- 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj log -r '::@' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id.short() ++ "\n"'
jj op show -p

Test a fixer in four states: unchanged input should produce no successor or a clearly explained no-op; changed input should have the expected paths; a conflict should be skipped or handled according to policy; and a sparse workspace should not silently imply that absent paths were checked. Keep generated files out of a source formatter unless the generator is part of the intended operation.

Bisect as graph search

Bisect narrows a commit set using a predicate supplied by the user or a script. The script's exit status is the test contract, so distinguish “known good”, “known bad”, and “infrastructure failure” in the script. A flaky or environment-dependent test can produce a plausible but wrong boundary. Keep the bisection range explicit and save the discovered commit before leaving the temporary checkout.

jj bisect --range 'main@origin..@'
jj bisect run --range 'main@origin..@' -- ./test-regression.sh
jj show REVISION

A bisect result is not a patch to publish. It is evidence about graph history. If it identifies an ancestor that has since been rewritten, use change/evolution information to locate the current successor and verify the test at that object.

Signing and verification

Signing rewrites commit metadata and can therefore create successor commit IDs. It does not make a change immutable, publish it, or establish a Gerrit review identity. Verification status is a property of a cryptographic signature and key environment; a template can display it, but a successful local signing command does not prove that a remote host will trust the key.

jj sign -r 'bookmarks() & mutable()'
jj log -r 'signed()'
jj show -r @
jj unsign -r 'change_id(CHANGE)'

Define whether signing applies before or after review publication. Signing an already pushed commit may require a force update because the exported commit object changes. In a stack, signing an ancestor changes descendant commit IDs as well. Test with a disposable key or a controlled agent, keep private material out of repository config, and record the host's verification policy separately.

Tags as release boundaries

A tag names a release point rather than serving as a daily work pointer. Treat tag movement as an exceptional publication change, even when the local command permits it. A release query should name the tag explicitly and record the target commit ID, version, and binary used to compute the set.

jj tag set v2.4.0 -r @
jj log -r 'v2.4.0:: & ::main@origin'
jj tag list
jj tag track v2.4.0 --remote origin

Remote tags are local observations after fetch. The 0.45.x track adds tracked/untracked remote-tag predicates and includes untracked remote tags in the documented default immutable-head expression; do not use those predicates in a 0.44 alias. A tag can be a policy boundary without being a bookmark and without making every descendant immutable.

Maintenance and debugging

Repository maintenance has a recoverability cost. Before jj util gc, inspect operation history, evolution records, local rescue bookmarks, and remote state. Config GC is a different operation: it removes obsolete configuration entries, not unreachable commit objects. Git maintenance and jj maintenance can operate on related object stores in a colocated repository; use the backend's documented tooling and avoid concurrent writers.

jj op log
jj evolog -r 'hidden()'
jj bookmark list --all-remotes
jj util gc
jj util backend name
jj version

For a bug report, capture jj version, backend/colocation status, effective relevant configuration, command line, operation IDs, and a minimal graph that reproduces the result. Do not include credentials or private repository content. A machine-readable template should disable pager and colour and use full IDs. A human log can use labels, relative times, and short IDs, but it is not stable interchange.

Completion and Linux integration

Generate completion from the same binary whose commands are being documented:

jj util completion bash > ~/.local/share/bash-completion/completions/jj
jj util completion zsh > ~/.zfunc/_jj
jj util completion fish > ~/.config/fish/completions/jj.fish

Static completion becomes stale after a release upgrade. Keep it under the shell's supported directory, start a new shell or source it as appropriate, and regenerate when the 0.45.x command tree differs. A prompt should use a bounded, no-pager query and should not run a broad diff on every redraw:

jj --ignore-working-copy --no-pager --color=never log -r @ -T \
  'change_id.shortest(10) ++ " " ++ if(conflict, "C", "") ++ " " ++ bookmarks'

Shell functions should preserve exit status. If a prompt suppresses errors, it should do so outside publication and recovery commands; never wrap a push, rewrite, or restore in a fallback that hides failure.

Part XVII — Complete real workflows

1. Branchless daily development

Fetch; create a child of the imported trunk; work in @; use jj log -r 'stack(@)'; make changes with describe/squash/split/absorb; create a publication bookmark only at the boundary; push; then forget it when its server purpose ends.

2. GitHub feature and stacked PRs

Use one bookmark per PR boundary, rebase the mutable stack onto main@origin, move bookmarks, push, and let GitHub merge. Treat server merge and local graph update as separate fetch steps.

3. Gerrit patch stack

Keep Gerrit Change-Id footers in descriptions, use jj change IDs for local evolution, edit an early review, let descendants rebase, then upload the series again. Fetch landed changes and rebase the remaining stack.

4. Rewriting an early patch after review

jj edit A
jj describe -m 'API: corrected quota model'
jj squash --from @ --into @- -- 'glob:src/**'
jj log -r '::@'
jj git push --bookmark review --remote origin

Review the operation log before and after. The review bookmark must be moved to the successor tip; the change ID is the local continuity signal.

5. Upstream synchronisation

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..@)' -o main@origin
jj log -r 'stack(@) & conflicts()'
jj resolve

Choose -b for a whole relative stack and -r for surgical selection. Do not substitute a Git branch mental model for the source/destination semantics.

6. Two remotes

Fetch GitHub and Gerrit independently, qualify symbols, name publication bookmarks explicitly, and make each push command specify its remote.

7. Moving unfinished work

Create a temporary bookmark, push to a private remote, fetch on the second clone, edit or rebase there, and delete the temporary bookmark after verifying the receiving clone.

8. Build/review workspaces

Add separate workspaces at the build and review commits, use sparse patterns where appropriate, and remember that a shared rewrite can make either workspace stale.

9. Recovery

Stop, record jj op log, inspect with --at-op, then undo or restore the operation. Do not attempt recovery by guessing commit IDs alone.

10. Git in a colocated repository

Use Git for inspection, hooks, and transport when useful; import ref changes before relying on them; never let independent worktree writers race.

11. Revset-driven daily work

alias stack='jj log -r "stack(@)"'
jj log -r 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
jj diff -r 'roots(stack(@))..@'
jj run -r 'stack(@) & conflicts()' -- test -f CONFLICTS

Revsets let policy be expressed once and reused across log, diff, run, rebase, and publication checks.

End-to-end workflow narratives

Branchless day: from fetch to publication

Start with an imported trunk and no local bookmark for the feature. This is not an unfinished branch; it is an intentional unnamed graph boundary.

main@origin -- T
                \
                 @_0  empty mutable working-copy commit
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin -m 'Cache: bound memory use'
# implement the change
jj status
jj diff
jj describe -m 'Cache: bound memory use'
jj new -m 'Cache: expose metrics'
# implement dependent change
jj log -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj log -r 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'

At this stage the graph is useful without a bookmark. The first change can be revised by selecting it through a change ID or description; the dependent change automatically follows. The publication boundary appears only when a server review is ready:

jj bookmark create gh/cache -r 'roots(main@origin..@)'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark gh/cache
gh pr create --head gh/cache --base main

If there are two changes intended as one PR, point the bookmark at the stack tip and use a server/UI description that explains the stack. If they are intended as two stacked PRs, create two bookmarks. The graph decides; the names are interfaces.

Review iteration and early rewrite

reviewed state:  main -- A -- B -- C
                              ▲    ▲
                            gh/base gh/tip

feedback on A:
main -- A′ -- B′ -- C′
         same logical A; all commit IDs after A may differ
jj edit A
# edit files
jj describe -m 'API: correct quota invariant'
jj log -r 'gh/base | gh/tip | ::@'
jj diff --from 'gh/base@origin' --to gh/base
jj bookmark set gh/base -r A
jj bookmark set gh/tip -r C
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark gh/base --bookmark gh/tip

Do not move only the tip if the base review has its own bookmark. Rewriting A can affect both server refs. If the push is rejected, fetch and compare expected remote targets before deciding whether the local rewrite should replace a remote update.

Upstream movement with a conflict branch

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@origin..@)' -o main@origin
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj resolve --list
jj resolve --tool meld
jj log -r '::@ & conflicts()'
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

Resolve before moving the publication bookmark. If a conflict is in an ancestor, inspect descendants even if only the ancestor's file was changed. A review bookmark that remains at a conflicted predecessor can make the push appear to succeed while publishing the wrong graph boundary.

Gerrit stack over time

day 1: main -- A(I-A) -- B(I-B) -- C(I-C)
day 2: main′-- A_2(I-A) -- B_2(I-B) -- C_2(I-C)
day 3: main″-- A(landed) -- B_3(I-B) -- C_3(I-C)
jj new main@gerrit -m 'API: introduce quota

Change-Id: Iaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa'
jj new -m 'API: wire quota

Change-Id: Ibbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbbb'
jj new -m 'API: expose metrics

Change-Id: Icccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccc'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
# later: edit the first change
jj edit 'subject(exact:"API: introduce quota")'
jj describe --editor
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main
# later: fetch landing
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj rebase -b 'roots(main@gerrit..@)' -o main@gerrit

At each review iteration, local jj change identity and Gerrit footer identity answer different questions. Record both in a review ledger if a team needs to audit patch sets. The local stack can be revised continuously; upload is a publication operation over the selected stack.

Two machines and a temporary publication

machine A: local unnamed @
              |
              \- temporary handoff bookmark → origin/handoff

machine B: fetch origin/handoff → new local @

# A
jj bookmark create handoff -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff

# B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new 'handoff@origin'
jj log -r '::@'
jj bookmark forget handoff@origin

The handoff transfers commits and a Git bookmark observation. It does not transfer A's operation DAG, workspace-local @, uncommitted filesystem edits, or editor state. If the work is unfinished, commit a deliberate WIP change rather than relying on files that another machine cannot see.

Multiple workspaces in one repository

shared DAG: root -- A -- B -- C
workspace dev:   dev@ = C (active edits)
workspace build: build@ = B (repeatable build)
workspace review: review@ = A (review baseline)
jj workspace add ../build --name build -r B --sparse-patterns full
jj workspace add ../review --name review -r A --sparse-patterns copy
jj workspace list
jj -R ../build run -- cargo test
jj -R ../review show -r @ --stat
jj workspace update-stale

The build command's private or workspace filesystem must not be confused with dev's current @. A rewrite of A changes the commit graph observed by all three workspaces. Update stale workspaces only after checking for unrecorded files and decide whether a stable build should instead use a temporary clone.

Native Git in a colocated checkout

jj bookmark create review -r @
jj git export
git -C "$(jj root)" show-ref
git -C "$(jj root)" log --oneline --all
git -C "$(jj root)" fetch origin
jj git import
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

Git is valuable for ecosystem tooling and transport, but it cannot show jj change IDs, operation history, or stored multi-sided conflict values. A Git-side ref change becomes visible to jj only at the import boundary. A Git-side worktree reset while jj is snapshotting can discard or misattribute filesystem changes; establish a single writer.

Revset-driven daily gate

[revset-aliases]
unpublished() = 'mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()'
review-candidates() = 'heads(unpublished() ~ empty() ~ conflicts())'

# human inspection
jj log -r 'review-candidates()'

# machine gate
jj log --no-pager --color=never --count -r 'review-candidates()'

# inspect each candidate
jj show -r 'review-candidates()' --stat

The gate is intentionally a policy expression rather than a command alias that hides selection. Teams may choose to allow empty or conflicted WIP reviews; the important property is that the policy is explicit, testable, and reviewed as code.

Case study: a branchless GitHub feature through review

Start with a fetched trunk and no local feature bookmark. The developer creates a child of the imported remote tip and works in the workspace:

main@origin--A--B--@
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin
jj describe -m "Parser: report malformed input"
# edit files and run tests
jj diff
jj status

The working-copy commit is already the reviewable logical change. If the tests reveal that documentation and implementation should be separate, split the change before publication:

jj split -r @ 'root-glob:src/**/*.rs'
jj log -r 'stack(@)'
main@origin--A--B--C(API)--D(docs)--@

Create a bookmark at the boundary GitHub should expose. If the two changes are one PR, use one bookmark at D. If they should be stacked PRs, create names at C and D and publish both explicitly. The bookmark is a ref for GitHub; it is not the local unit of editing.

jj bookmark create pr/parser-api -r C
jj bookmark create pr/parser-docs -r D
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser-api
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser-docs

Suppose review changes C. Edit C, amend its tree and description, and inspect the successors of D and @. Because C is an ancestor, the descendants receive updated commit IDs. Move only the publication bookmark that represents C and push it explicitly; the dependent PR bookmark must also be moved if its target changed.

jj edit C
# edit src/parser.rs
jj describe -m "Parser: report malformed input"
jj log -r 'C::@'
jj bookmark set pr/parser-api -r C
jj bookmark set pr/parser-docs -r D
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser-api
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/parser-docs

If GitHub rejects the update, fetch first and compare the remote bookmark. A rejection may indicate that another actor moved the ref, that the local tracking relationship is stale, or that server-side branch protection disallows the update. Do not respond with a blind force push. Determine whether the local graph is the intended successor of the remote commit and whether the GitHub branch policy allows replacement.

Case study: main moves under a stack

old: main@origin--A--B
                         \--C--D
new remote after fetch: main@origin--A--B--E--F

local stack before rebase:
                    C--D
                   /
main@origin(old)--B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj rebase -s 'roots(main@origin..D)' -d main@origin
jj log -r 'stack(D)'
jj diff --from old-main --to main@origin

Choose the source set carefully. A broad source selection can include an unrelated fork; a narrow selection can leave a dependent child behind. Record the old trunk commit before the fetch if you need a precise comparison. After the rebase, inspect changed commit IDs and run tests at the stack head. Update publication bookmarks only after the graph is correct, then push them explicitly.

Case study: Gerrit’s patch-set lifecycle

Gerrit recognises a patch set by its commit message’s Gerrit Change-Id footer, while jj recognises an evolving logical change by its own change ID. They can coexist in one description. A rewrite normally changes the Git commit ID, may preserve the jj change ID, and should preserve the Gerrit footer if the intent is a new patch set for the same Gerrit review.

origin/main--A--B_1--C_1--D_1
                  |    |
                  review  review
                  Change-Id Iabc  Change-Id Idef
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'B_1::D_1'

Use the command’s supported commit and metadata options to set reviewers, topic, work-in-progress/private state, and push options. The local upload transport and the server’s review interpretation are separate layers: validate the former with jj’s dry-run/verbose facilities and treat the latter as Gerrit behaviour that requires server documentation or a real Gerrit instance.

Now a reviewer requests a fix to B_1:

jj edit B_1
# edit and test
jj describe -m 'API: validate parser limits

Change-Id: Iabc...'
jj log -r 'B_1::D_1'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'B_1::D_1'

C_1 and D_1 are rebased descendants with new commit IDs. Their jj change IDs continue to identify their logical changes when the rewrite is an evolution; their Gerrit footers continue to identify their server reviews if descriptions were preserved. If the server reports that a Change-Id is missing or duplicated, inspect the actual commit descriptions rather than assuming jj metadata supplies it. jj change IDs do not generate Gerrit footers.

Case study: landed lower change, remaining upper stack

before server integration:
main@origin--A--B
                 \--C(review 1)--D(review 2)--E(review 3)

after review 1 lands:
main@origin--A--B--C′
local reviews:       D--E (still based on old C)

Fetch the new remote branch, identify whether the landed commit is equivalent to the local C by change ID, patch, or server metadata, then rebase the remaining stack onto the fetched trunk. Do not simply delete C and assume D/E will find the right parent: abandoning or rebasing the wrong node can produce duplicate patches or conflicts.

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@origin | C::E'
# choose the remaining stack explicitly
jj rebase -s 'roots(D::E)' -d main@origin
jj log -r 'stack(E)'

When a review is abandoned, a local change can be retained as a new change, moved to another publication bookmark, or abandoned after creating a safety bookmark. The correct action depends on whether the code is still wanted; server review state is not a command to delete local graph state.

Case study: two remotes and one local DAG

local graph:
upstream/main@origin--A--B--C--@--D

refs:
  origin/main       = B
  gerrit/main       = B
  github/pr-parser  = C

Qualify all remote symbols. Use origin/main or the exact jj remote-bookmark spelling shown by jj log, and pass --remote on push/upload. A local bookmark called main can obscure which publication channel is intended; avoid it unless the repository convention gives it one unambiguous meaning.

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@origin | main@gerrit | github/pr-parser'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark github/pr-parser
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'stack(@)'

One commit can be reachable from multiple publication systems, but server policy is not shared. A GitHub branch protection rule does not protect a Gerrit review ref, and a Gerrit upload option does not affect a GitHub push. Keep credentials, aliases, and remote names explicit.

Case study: moving unfinished work safely

Unfinished work has value but also a publication risk. Create a temporary bookmark with a name that says why it exists, push only to an access-controlled remote, and remove it after the receiving machine verifies the graph:

jj bookmark create transfer/2026-09-parser -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark transfer/2026-09-parser
# machine B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'transfer/2026-09-parser@origin'
jj new transfer/2026-09-parser@origin
# after verification on both machines
jj bookmark delete transfer/2026-09-parser
jj git push --remote origin --deleted

Do not treat a temporary bookmark as a private stash unless the remote and access controls actually make it private. If the graph is sensitive, transfer via an encrypted private remote or another controlled mechanism. The operation log itself is not the transport protocol between independent clones.

Case study: build, review, and development workspaces

Use one repository with three workspaces when the tasks need different filesystem states but share object storage:

jj workspace add --name build -r main@origin ../repo-build
jj workspace add --name review -r 'bookmark("pr/parser")' ../repo-review
jj workspace list

In the development workspace, rewrite the stack freely. In review, inspect the publication target and run review-specific tools. In build, run a clean build against the pinned commit. After a rewrite, check all three workspaces; a stale review or build workspace should be updated deliberately, not silently moved by a prompt hook.

Workflow casebook: maintain the graph across a working week

The following cases use the same operating rule: maintain a local graph that represents the work, and create or move a publication name only when an external system needs a ref. Each case records the graph, the publication boundary, and the recovery boundary. The commands are examples for 0.44 unless a 0.45.x label is present; hosting behaviour is marked separately.

Case 1: branchless daily development

Monday after fetch:
  main@origin--A--B(@)
                 B = API change, unnamed

Tuesday review preparation:
  main@origin--A--B--C(@)
                      C = test/docs tail

Thursday upstream moves:
  main@origin--A--B--C
                     \
  new main@origin-----B'--C'(@)  after rebase
  1. Fetch and inspect the imported trunk.
  2. Create a child and do not name it while the change is private.
  3. Use jj new for a new logical boundary and jj describe for the current change.
  4. Use jj log, jj diff, and a bounded revset to inspect the stack.
  5. Create a bookmark only when a review or handoff needs a Git ref.
  6. After upstream movement, rebase mutable roots, resolve stored conflicts, and republish explicit bookmarks.
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new main@origin -m 'API: enforce quota'
# edit and test
jj new -m 'API: add quota tests'
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj bookmark create review/quota -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark review/quota

The local history never required a branch to move the working copy. The bookmark was an interface introduced at publication time. If the review is abandoned, keep or forget the bookmark according to server state; abandoning a local commit is a separate decision.

Case 2: stacked GitHub review

main@origin--A--B--C--D(@)
               ▲     ▲
             pr/api  pr/metrics

Publish B and D as boundaries only after verifying that B is the intended base PR and D is the intended dependent PR. When review changes A, edit by change ID. Automatic descendant rebasing produces B', C', and D'. Compare each local bookmark with its remote counterpart, update all affected bookmarks, and push explicitly. When GitHub merges the base PR, fetch before rebasing the remaining stack. The server's PR base and merge strategy are not encoded by jj's operation log.

jj log -r 'pr/api | pr/metrics | pr/api@origin | pr/metrics@origin'
jj edit 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj describe -m 'API: correct invariant'
jj log -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj diff --from pr/api@origin --to pr/api
jj bookmark set pr/api -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj bookmark set pr/metrics -r 'change_id(METRICS_CHANGE)'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark pr/api --bookmark pr/metrics

Case 3: Gerrit patch stack over two review iterations

iteration 1: main@gerrit--B--C--D
             Iapi         Itest       Imetrics

iteration 2: main@gerrit--B'--C'--D'
             same footers, new commit IDs,
             new Gerrit patch sets when server accepts

Use one jj change per reviewable patch and preserve a Gerrit Change-Id footer in each description. Upload a selected stack to the review ref according to Gerrit policy. On an early review comment, edit the jj change by change ID; do not create a local fixup branch solely to obtain a new commit. Inspect descendants, resolve any stored conflict, and re-upload. jj can prove the footer and local IDs; only Gerrit can prove that the server associated the object with the intended review and patch set.

jj evolog -r 'change_id(API_CHANGE)'
jj log -r 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::' -T 'change_id ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main \
  --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main \
  --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

When B lands, fetch the new Gerrit main and rebase C/D, not the remote bookmark. If a submit strategy changes the effective ancestry, use the fetched remote bookmark as the new base after inspecting it. Abandoning a Gerrit review does not abandon the local change; clean the local graph only after preserving any needed footer and operation evidence.

Case 4: rewrite an early reviewed patch

before: main--A--B--C--D(@)
              A reviewed and published

after:  main--A'--B'--C'--D'(@)
         A/B/C/D change IDs follow successors;
         all affected commit IDs change

Make a safety record with op log and a temporary bookmark. Edit A by change ID, make the smallest semantic repair, run focused tests, and inspect the entire descendant stack. The fact that B' is clean does not prove its tests still test the same contract. Compare old and new patches with evolution/inter-diff output. Move every review bookmark and publish with an explicit safety decision.

jj bookmark create before-early-rewrite -r @
jj op log -n 3
jj edit 'change_id(A_CHANGE)'
# edit, test, and snapshot
jj evolog -r 'change_id(A_CHANGE)' -p
jj log -G -r 'roots(main@origin..@)::'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@'

Case 5: GitHub and Gerrit from one clone

                 +-- github/pr-cache
main@github --A-+
                 \-- gerrit review stack
main@gerrit ----A--B--C(@)

One local commit can be exported to both systems, but the systems may have different trunk objects, review identities, ref namespaces, permissions, and merge policies. Keep two publication stories. Fetch each remote explicitly; qualify revsets such as main@github and main@gerrit; create a GitHub bookmark only for the GitHub ref; and invoke Gerrit upload with its explicit remote/branch. Do not assume a Gerrit footer controls GitHub review or that a GitHub branch name supplies a Gerrit topic.

jj git fetch --remote github
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -G -r 'main@github | main@gerrit | ::@'
jj bookmark create github/cache -r @
jj git push --remote github --bookmark github/cache
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main \
  --revision 'roots(main@gerrit..@)::'

Case 6: move unfinished work between machines

Operation IDs and @ do not transfer through Git. Publish a temporary bookmark, fetch it on the second clone, and create a new local child there. The first machine can continue to observe the handoff ref but must not assume it follows the second machine's new working-copy commit. If both machines edit the same logical change, fetching can expose divergent successors. Compare evolog and select a policy before force-updating the handoff ref.

# A
jj bookmark set handoff/alice -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff/alice

# B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj new 'handoff/alice@origin' -m 'Continue cache work'
# after handoff:
jj bookmark set handoff/bob -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff/bob

Case 7: recovery after a wrong rewrite

Stop after noticing the mistake. Do not create several “repair” operations while guessing. Record the current operation, use op show and --at-op to identify the last good state, use undo only when it is the immediate last operation, and use named restore/revert when later operations must remain meaningful. If a remote push occurred, perform a separate server/ref repair and fetch afterward.

jj op log -n 20
jj op show OP -p
jj --at-operation OP log -G -r 'all()'
jj op diff --from GOOD --to CURRENT
jj op restore GOOD
jj op log -n 5
jj log -G -r 'all()'

After the repair, verify the commit graph, operation graph, bookmarks, remote bookmarks, working-copy files, and any published review identities. A command returning zero is not the recovery assertion; the assertion is that the repository is now in the intended state and that the choice is recorded.

Case 8: revset-driven automation

Use revsets to define a bounded domain and templates to emit stable records. Discovery should be read-only and cardinality-aware. Mutation should receive full IDs or explicit commit selectors, run with a known configuration, and leave an operation record. Never parse the graph glyphs of ordinary jj log in automation.

domain='mutable() & ::@ ~ ::remote_bookmarks() ~ conflicts()'
jj log --no-pager --color=never -r \"$domain\" \
  -T 'change_id ++ \"\\t\" ++ commit_id ++ \"\\t\" ++ description.first_line() ++ \"\\n\"'
jj diff --no-pager -r \"$domain\" --summary
# apply a selected, reviewed operation only after checking cardinality

Run this style of automation in CI with an explicit JJ_CONFIG or --config-file, a pinned binary, and no implicit remote. Make the script fail when the domain is empty or unexpectedly broad if either condition would be unsafe. Keep a fixture that exercises a fork, merge, conflict, remote bookmark, and duplicate subject so the query is tested against the cases that make shortcuts fail.

Part XVIII — Practice, reference, and fieldbooks

Part XVII closes the linear teaching route. Part XVIII is an intentional second mode of use: the advanced field manual applies the model to day-to-day decisions, the language and command sections are lookup references, the labbook and curriculum provide controlled practice, and the hosting/recovery sections supply incident and publication procedures. The headings below are grouped here so that the reader does not mistake a reference atlas for another competing introduction.

Read this part selectively. A first pass can stop after Part XVII. A reader investigating a difficult graph can enter the field manual; a reader writing automation can enter the language or command reference; a reader preparing a review can enter the hosting fieldbook; and a reader recovering from an error can enter the recovery recipes. The cross-links point back to the primary teaching chapters whenever a detail depends on an earlier invariant.

The advanced field manual

This interlude turns the book's model into repeatable engineering practice. The earlier chapters introduced the vocabulary; the sections below deliberately keep several facts in view at once. A jj operation can snapshot a filesystem, rewrite a set of commits, move local names, update a workspace, and leave an operation record. Looking at only one of those layers is how apparently impossible states are produced. The useful question is not “which command is the jj equivalent of my Git command?” but “which state transition do I want, and which of the other layers must remain invariant?”

Every investigation in this chapter uses the same notation. A capital letter is a symbolic commit label in a disposable fixture; cid(A) is its commit ID; chg(A) is its change ID; @ is the current workspace working-copy commit; and op_n is an operation-log node. A line such as bookmark review → C is a local name, while review@origin → C_0 is a remote bookmark observed after fetch. These are not interchangeable identifiers.

State accounting before action

Before a risky operation, write down a small state ledger. This is faster than recovering from a mistaken broad revset and it makes a code review of shell automation possible.

LayerQuestionEvidence commandWhat a mismatch means
FilesystemAre there edits not yet in the recorded working-copy tree?jj diff --summary, then jj statusThe next command may snapshot more than the operator thinks.
Working copyWhich workspace owns @ and which commit is it?jj log -r @, jj workspace listA second workspace may be the one that becomes stale.
GraphWhat are the exact parents, descendants, and heads?jj log -r 'all()' with a parent templateA visual linear stack may conceal a merge or fork.
IdentityIs this a commit ID, change ID, bookmark, tag, or operation ID?jj show, jj evolog, jj op logUsing a short ID from the wrong namespace can select nothing or the wrong object.
ImmutabilityWhich commits are protected by immutable_heads()?jj log -r 'immutable_heads()'A failed rewrite may be protection working correctly.
PublicationWhich local and remote names point into this graph?jj bookmark list --all-remotes, jj tag listA local rewrite may require an explicit force update or a new review ref.
OperationsWhat operation created the state, and is it integrated?jj op log, jj op showRecovery should start from the operation boundary, not guesswork.

Record the ledger before and after. In a real repository, use full IDs in the record but symbolic labels in discussion. A shell script should discover full IDs, validate its assumptions, perform one operation, and discover the resulting state again. It should not assume that a commit ID remains valid after a rewrite merely because the change ID is stable.

Read-only observation and snapshot policy

There are two different meanings of “read-only”. A command may not intentionally rewrite history, yet it may snapshot the working copy. For a human at a terminal that is normally a convenience: the graph catches up with the editor before the log is displayed. For a diagnostic script, it is an implicit write to repository state and can race another process editing the same workspace.

Use ordinary commands when the current filesystem is part of the question. Use --ignore-working-copy when the question is specifically about the last recorded commit, or when another process owns the workspace. Use --at-operation OP when the question is about a historical repository view; it implies that current filesystem state must not be inserted into that view.

# Human inspection: include edits in the snapshot boundary.
jj status
jj log -r 'stack(@)'

# Stable inspection of the recorded commit, without taking a snapshot.
jj --ignore-working-copy log -r @ -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# Historical inspection: OP is an operation ID from jj op log.
jj --at-operation OP log -r 'all()'

Do not infer that --ignore-working-copy makes a mutating command safe. It prevents snapshotting; it does not turn rebase, abandon, bookmark movement, or operation restoration into observation. The four questions remain separate: what state is loaded, what set is selected, what mutation is requested, and which workspace or ref is updated.

Graph invariants as tests

A robust workflow states invariants in terms of relationships rather than display order. For example, after rewriting an early patch in a three-change stack:

  • the edited change has the same change ID before and after;
  • its commit ID is different if its tree, parents, metadata, or conflict value changed;
  • the descendant changes still exist and are successors of their prior evolutions;
  • the local publication bookmark points to the intended new stack tip;
  • the remote bookmark has not moved until an explicit push;
  • the operation log contains one operation describing the rewrite and any automatic rebases.

These assertions survive changes to the default log template. Assertions based on “the third line begins with @” do not. In a fixture, print a tab-separated record with full commit ID, change ID, parent IDs, bookmark names, conflict state, and description. Compare the fields separately so an expected commit-ID change is not mistaken for a lost change.

Publication boundaries in practice

Local jj development can remain unnamed, but a Git server consumes refs. The publication boundary is therefore an interface, not a preference. The local graph may be:

main@origin -- A -- B -- C -- D -- @
                         ↑             ↑
                   review/base     review/tip (local only)

At publication, choose which node represents the server-facing boundary. If the review should contain C..D, create or move a bookmark to D and push that name. If it should contain the whole stack from A, the server's base and the pushed ref define that interpretation; a local bookmark is not a substitute for checking the server's base.

After a push, local and remote bookmarks are two observations. A tracking relationship lets jj report whether the local name is ahead, behind, synchronised, or conflicted with the corresponding remote name. It does not make a local bookmark follow the working-copy commit. Editing files after a push changes @, not a bookmark that happens to have pointed at the old @.

A safe publication protocol

  1. Fetch the target remote and inspect main@remote, the local bookmark, and the candidate stack.
  2. Choose a publication name that identifies the server-facing review, and set it to an exact commit only after inspecting that commit's parents.
  3. Run a dry-run or preview where the command provides one. Otherwise print the candidate ref and its remote counterpart with full IDs.
  4. Push one named bookmark to one explicitly named remote. Avoid an unqualified push in a repository with GitHub and Gerrit remotes.
  5. Fetch again if the server transforms or creates refs, then inspect the resulting remote bookmark.
  6. After review feedback, rewrite the local change, verify change and commit identity, and push the same publication name. The server-side interpretation of that update is host-specific.

The protocol is intentionally more explicit than “push @”. @ is a workspace symbol, and its value can change on every snapshot or jj new. A bookmark is a durable interface choice that can be reviewed, tracked, and published.

Same bookmark names on several remotes

Suppose a repository has github and gerrit remotes, each with a main bookmark. The unqualified symbol main denotes a local bookmark; main@github and main@gerrit denote different remote observations. They may have identical commit IDs today and diverge tomorrow. A local bookmark can track one corresponding remote relationship; it cannot mean “the main branch of the organisation” without naming which remote supplies that fact.

jj git fetch --remote github
jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@github | main@gerrit'
jj bookmark list --remote github
jj bookmark list --remote gerrit

# Publication remains explicit.
jj git push --remote github --bookmark github/pr-17
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'roots(gerrit-main..@)'

Do not use a config default to conceal this distinction in a shared script. A personal alias can choose a remote for interactive work, but the alias should print or encode the remote and should refuse to publish if the selected bookmark is absent, conflicted, or unexpectedly behind its tracking target.

Colocation and native Git boundaries

A colocated repository has a Git directory and jj metadata associated with the same working directory. This is valuable for an advanced Git user because Git-native tools can inspect exported commits and refs while jj provides mutable graph operations. It also means two tools can change related state. The safe model is “one filesystem owner at a time, explicit import after Git-side ref changes”.

ActionGit object/ref effectjj effectFollow-up
Edit a fileNone until Git writes a commitNext jj snapshot sees itDecide which command owns the next snapshot.
git commitCreates a Git commit and moves a Git refjj metadata is not automatically a matching logical changeUse the supported Git import path, then inspect the jj graph.
git update-refMoves a Git refjj's remote/local bookmark view may be staleRun jj git import and inspect remote bookmarks.
jj bookmark setMay export through backend policyMoves a jj bookmarkUse jj git export when the local Git view must be current.
jj rebaseCreates/exportable Git objects for successorsRewrites mutable jj commits and descendantsPush the intended bookmark, not an obsolete commit ID.
git reset --hardMoves a Git ref and overwrites filesCan leave workspace state stale or create surprising next snapshotsPrefer jj workspace commands; if used, stop other writers and reconcile.

Git object reachability and jj operation reachability are different graphs. Git garbage collection knows about Git refs and Git objects; it does not preserve an unexported jj operation history merely because that history is important. Conversely, jj operation recovery can restore a repository view whose commits have not yet been exported to Git. Do not run aggressive Git maintenance without knowing which objects and refs provide recovery.

Ref import/export experiment

In a disposable colocated fixture, create a bookmark in jj, export it, and compare the Git ref. Then move the Git ref directly and import:

jj bookmark create experiment -r @
jj git export
git show-ref --verify refs/heads/experiment

# This changes Git state without moving the jj bookmark first.
git update-ref refs/heads/experiment "$(jj log -r 'root()' -T commit_id)"
jj git import
jj bookmark list --all-remotes

The exact exported ref names and handling of imported Git refs are part of the Git backend contract, not an invitation to edit internal jj files. Always use supported import/export commands. The experiment establishes a habit: after a Git-side ref mutation, refresh jj's view before reasoning about a remote bookmark or immutability boundary.

Review identities: one local change, several server views

Review systems commonly associate successive Git commits with a review by a message footer, a ref, or server metadata. jj's change ID is a local identity for an evolving change. These identities can coexist without being equal. A rewrite may preserve the jj change ID while changing the commit ID; a Gerrit upload may preserve a Change-Id: footer while creating a new patch set; a GitHub pull request may continue to use the same branch ref while its tip commit changes.

jj change Q
  |- commit q_0, footer I123, pushed at patch set 1
  \- commit q_1, footer I123, pushed at patch set 2

server review identity             jj identity
review ref / Change-Id              change ID Q
particular object                   commit ID q_0 or q_1

When debugging a review update, record all three: old and new commit IDs, the jj change ID, and the host-side review identifier. If one is missing, the update may be correct but difficult to explain. A command that only prints a short commit ID is insufficient evidence for a review audit.

Rebase surgery: choosing the smallest transformation

Rebase is often described as moving commits, but in jj its source selection and destination selection are independent. Start by stating whether parent edges, tree deltas, or both should change.

IntentPrimary operationWhat remains invariantInspect afterwards
Move a stack to a new baserebase -s STACK -d BASESelected changes and order, subject to conflictsParents, successors, bookmark tip, conflicts
Move one independent changerebase -r CHANGE -d BASEUnselected graph edges as far as descendant rebasing permitsWhether descendants followed the rewritten parent
Put a change after anotherrebase -r CHANGE -A DESTChange patch, unless context creates a conflictExact parent list and merge shape
Make one change own selected filessquash, restore, or splitDepends on whether source remains and a new change is madeDiff of both source and destination
Apply a change elsewhereduplicateContent intent, not change identityNew change ID and parent edges
Remove a change from visible historyabandonUnselected commits and recovery recordsDescendants, operation log, bookmark targets

The source selection is not “the commit I clicked”. -r selects commits individually; -s selects a source commit and its descendants; -b selects a branch-like set according to the command's definition. A source set with a hole is not a contiguous stack: descendants that remain selected may be rebased around the hole, while the omitted commit stays in place. Use a parent template to verify the result.

jj log -r 'all()' -T '
commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++
" parents=[" ++ parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(",") ++ "] " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"

Ancestor rewrite with a publication bookmark

Consider a published two-change stack:

main@origin -- A -- B -- C
                    ↑       ↑
                 review/base review/tip

If B is edited, the new B′ has a new commit ID. Descendant C cannot retain its old parent edge, so jj creates a successor C′ rebased onto B′. A local bookmark pointing at C is moved to its successor as part of the rewrite. The remote bookmark remains at its previously exported commit until a push. The review server therefore sees a ref update containing new objects, while the local user experiences one logical edit to B and automatic maintenance of the stack.

If the rebase cannot apply cleanly, C′ can contain a conflict. The correct response is not to abandon B′ merely because the descendant is conflicted. Inspect the conflict value, materialise it if needed, resolve it in the descendant, and rerun tests at the stack tip. The operation log records the rewrite and resolution as separate boundaries, making “before the rebase” and “after resolution” recoverable states.

Three languages, three failure classes

jj's commit language, fileset language, and template language often appear in the same command, but they are evaluated in different domains:

LanguageValueTypical failureDiagnostic
RevsetSet of commitsA range includes a merge ancestor or has the wrong cardinalityjj log -r 'EXPR' -T commit_id
FilesetSet of repository pathsShell expands a glob or a relative path uses the wrong basejj file list 'EXPR'
TemplateTyped rendering valueMethod is called on the wrong type or an optional value is unsetjj log -T '...' in a fixture

Do not fix a revset problem by changing the template, or a fileset problem by changing the commit selector. First render the selected commits with one full ID per line. Then render selected paths against one known tree. Only then combine both in a mutating command.

Fileset command boundaries

A fileset can limit a display without limiting a graph operation, or it can define exactly which patch paths move. jj diff -r R -- root-glob:src/** restricts output. jj restore --from R --into S -- root-file:src/config.toml copies one path from one tree to another. jj squash --from R --into S -- root-glob:tests/** changes patch ownership for selected paths. The same syntax describes a path set, but the command supplies the semantics of applying that set.

When a command accepts both a commit option and fileset arguments, prove both independently. If R is a source commit and paths selects tests, first run jj diff -r R -- paths. Next check that resulting source/destination diffs differ only under paths. A successful command is not proof that the intended fileset was parsed; an empty selection can be a valid no-op.

Operation recovery as forensic work

The operation log should be treated as a forensic record, not a magical undo button. First identify the bad operation, then decide whether recovery is a new operation that reverses it or a restoration of the repository view as it existed then. These differ: restoring a historical operation can discard later integrated changes from the current view, while a revert-like operation creates a new state that preserves later history.

QuestionPreferred inspectionLikely recovery
Was the immediately previous operation wrong?jj op show, then compare current and parentjj undo
What did the repository look like before a named operation?jj op show OP, jj op diff OPRead with --at-operation; restore only after review.
Was useful work abandoned?jj evolog, hidden/explicit commit lookupDuplicate or restore useful evolution into current work.
Did a bookmark move incorrectly?jj bookmark list --all-remotes, operation diffSet/move the bookmark to the intended commit.
Did another process advance the operation head?jj op log and operation parentsInspect competing operation before undoing.

Do not run repository garbage collection while an abandoned commit or old operation is your only recovery path. Preserve the operation ID, full commit IDs, and any untracked files outside the workspace before a large repair. An operation record cannot restore an editor buffer or a file that was never snapshotted.

Recovery exercise: wrong ancestor

  1. Make a disposable three-change stack and record jj op log, the current tip, and every change ID.
  2. Edit the middle change when the intention was to edit the tip. Do not immediately continue working.
  3. Use jj op show and jj op diff to identify the operation that rewrote the ancestor.
  4. Use jj undo once. Confirm that the middle and tip commit IDs, bookmark target, and filesystem are back at the recorded state.
  5. Repeat the mistake, then perform one unrelated description edit before noticing. Compare undo with inspecting the historical operation and using an explicit restore/revert strategy.

The exercise teaches an important limitation: “undo the thing I meant” is not a semantic operation. undo operates on the latest integrated operation. Once other operations have been integrated, choose the historical operation deliberately and account for their changes.

Workspace ownership and staleness

A workspace gives another directory its own working-copy commit, not another copy of the commit store. Two workspaces can therefore have different @ values while sharing ancestors, bookmarks, and operation history. A graph rewrite seen by one workspace can make another workspace stale. The stale workspace's filesystem is still real; updating it is a reconciliation action, not a harmless refresh.

shared store
    A -- B -- C′
          \
workspace dev:   dev@ = C′
workspace build: build@ = C (stale working-copy successor)

Use a development workspace for editing and a build workspace for long-running tests when the build workspace can tolerate being updated. Before rewriting an ancestor, check jj workspace list and quiesce other writers. After the rewrite, inspect each workspace's filesystem edits before jj workspace update-stale. If build output is valuable, keep it outside the workspace or make the build reproducible; do not expect an update to preserve arbitrary generated files.

Moving unfinished work between machines

Clones do not share an operation log. They share commits and refs only when those objects are transferred through the Git backend. The portable unit is therefore an explicitly published bookmark, even if that bookmark exists for only a few minutes.

# Machine A
jj status
jj diff --stat
jj bookmark set handoff -r @
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff

# Machine B
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj log -r 'handoff@origin' -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
jj new handoff@origin
jj status

# Machine A, after confirmation
jj bookmark delete handoff
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark handoff

Do not promise that jj undo on machine B recovers an operation performed on machine A. It cannot. If the unfinished work was not exported, the second clone cannot discover it from Git. If a publication bookmark was moved twice, fetch and inspect both the current remote target and the originating machine's operation/evolution history before deciding which state is authoritative.

Language fieldbook: deriving selections and output

Revsets, filesets, and templates are all functional languages, but their values live in different universes. A revset value is a set of commit nodes. A fileset value is a set of repository-relative paths. A template expression evaluates against a typed object and emits formatted output. The three languages meet at command boundaries, not in one shared evaluator. That distinction explains why an expression can be syntactically valid in one position and meaningless in another.

Revset evaluation workbench

Use this graph for the evaluations in this section. The arrows point from parent to child. The merge has two parents; F is not a head of the set containing G because F is an ancestor of G.

                 E -----+
                /        \
root -- A -- B -- C ----- G -- H
                \        /
                 D -----+

bookmarks:
  main@origin → B
  topic       → H
  release     → G
ExpressionIntermediate reasoningResult
B::Start at B; follow every child path.{B,C,D,E,G,H}
::GStart at G; follow both parent paths, including their common ancestors.{root,A,B,C,D,E,G}
B..GAncestors of G minus ancestors of B.{C,D,E,G}
B::GDescendants of B intersect ancestors of G.{B,C,D,E,G}
heads(B::G)Remove every member that is an ancestor of another member.{G}
roots(B::G)Remove every member with a parent also in the set.{B}
(C|D)..Not ancestors of C and not ancestors of D; do not distribute .. over the union.{E,G,H} subject to the visible search domain.
C.. | D..Union two complements of ancestry sets.Usually larger than (C|D)..; calculate each side first.
topic ~ ::BStart at H and remove all ancestors of B.{H}\`

The exact set printer can be made independent of graph glyphs:

jj log --no-pager --color never -r 'B::G' \
  -T 'description.first_line() ++ "\t" ++ commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
jj log --no-pager --color never -r 'heads(B::G)' \
  -T 'description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

When checking a worked example, do not use only the visible order in jj log. A merge is normally shown with graph indentation, but the indentation is a rendering. The semantic test is the parent list. Render each selected commit's parents and verify the closure or boundary by hand.

Set algebra and complement

x | y is union, x & y is intersection, and x ~ y is relative difference. Prefix ~x is the complement of x in the revset search domain. The domain matters: hidden commits are generally absent unless explicitly introduced, and all() is not an unrestricted database query. If a hidden ID is mentioned, its ancestors become available to the expression; this can change what a later traversal includes.

Relative difference is the usual way to express “my set, except the published part”:

mutable() ~ ::main@origin
mutable() & ::@
(::@ ~ ::main@origin) & ~conflicts()

These are not interchangeable in every graph. The first removes every ancestor of remote main from the mutable set. The second first narrows to ancestors of the current workspace and then removes remote ancestry. The third additionally excludes conflicted nodes. State the intended universe before choosing the algebra.

A complement is best used inside a bounded expression. ~bookmarks() means every visible commit that is not named by a bookmark, not “the commits in my current stack that have no bookmark”. The latter is stack(@) ~ bookmarks() or another explicitly bounded set. This is one of the most common ways a correct operator produces an unsafe mutation.

Reachability and ranges

The double-colon operators are closures; the double-dot operators are ancestry differences. x::y is a bounded path-shaped intersection: descendants of x that are ancestors of y. x..y is ancestors of y excluding ancestors of x. The endpoints do not have to be related. In a fork, left..right can include the right branch and common descendants while excluding the common base; it is not a list of edges on one unique path.

QuestionDerivationWhy the obvious spelling can fail
What changed since base?base..tipIncludes all ancestors of tip not also ancestors of base, including side branches merged into tip.
What is on the ancestry path from base to tip?base::tip then remove the base if the patch should exclude itbase..tip has different endpoint and fork semantics.
What is the complete stack below the current change?stack(@) or an explicit configured alias::@ includes trunk and every ancestor, not only mutable stack nodes.
What is reachable from either publication ref?::(main@origin | release)::main@origin | release has the same result only if the grouping and parser precedence are understood.
What remains unpublished in a bounded topic?(topic:: & mutable()) ~ ::main@originmutable() ~ ::main@origin can include unrelated work.

For an expression with a range on the left, always parenthesise unions:

(A | B)..          # not ancestors of A and not ancestors of B
A.. | B..          # commits not ancestors of A OR not ancestors of B
(A | B).. = A.. & B..

This identity follows from De Morgan's law applied to ancestry complements. It is not a jj peculiarity; it becomes visible because .. is defined as a complement of reachability rather than as a path enumerator.

Symbol resolution in detail

Resolution is deliberately ordered. Tags take priority over bookmarks, which take priority over commit-ID or change-ID interpretation. This makes a short symbol convenient for humans but potentially unstable in automation. Use commit_id("..."), change_id("..."), bookmark("..."), or the appropriate explicit function when the namespace matters.

A full commit ID identifies one object. A unique prefix identifies one object until a later object makes the prefix ambiguous. A full change ID identifies the visible evolution of a logical change; a divergent change ID requires an offset or an explicit query over the divergent commits. A short change ID copied from a review message is therefore not a safe immutable handle. Record the full commit ID for an exact object and the full change ID for the evolving patch.

# Treat a string as a literal symbol when punctuation is meaningful.
jj log -r '"x-"'
jj log -r 'commit_id(exact:"a1b2...")'
jj log -r 'change_id(exact:"qwer...")'

# Qualify remote state.
jj log -r 'main@origin'
jj log -r 'main@github | main@gerrit'

Shell quoting is an additional parser. In jj log -r 'x-', the shell passes one string and jj interprets the suffix as the parents operator. In jj log -r '"x-"', jj receives a revset containing a quoted string symbol. A script should quote the complete expression, not merely the individual words that happen to be visible in an example.

Cardinality and command contracts

Revsets always produce sets, even when a command wants one commit. The command applies a cardinality contract after parsing. log, diff, and many reporting commands accept arbitrary sets. edit, describe, and other operations that need one current target generally reject zero or multiple results. Some commands accept several explicit commits but impose topology constraints. Read the command's help and test the exact invocation.

candidate='heads(mutable() & ::@)'
jj log -r "$candidate" -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
count=$(jj log -r "$candidate" -T 'commit_id ++ "\n"' | wc -l)
test "$count" -eq 1
jj edit "$candidate"

The shell guard is valuable because a query that was singleton yesterday may become multi-valued after a fork or a new workspace. If the operation is safe over several heads, use a set-valued command intentionally. Do not force a singleton with first() merely to make an error disappear; that converts an explicit ambiguity into an arbitrary policy.

Function families and their contracts

The function inventory is easiest to learn by the fact it computes. Topology functions transform or inspect graph reachability. State predicates select stored properties. Reference functions select names and remote relationships. Content predicates inspect trees or diffs. Metadata predicates match typed fields. The following tables state the contract, not just the spelling.

FunctionSignature/argumentsSemantics and edge caseOperational use
parentsparents(x[, depth])Parents at exactly the requested depth; merges return multiple parents; root becomes empty.Find the base of a stack without assuming one parent.
childrenchildren(x[, depth])Direct or depth-limited children; a merge's parents can have several children.Find immediate successors after a rewrite.
ancestorsancestors(x[, depth])Inclusive parent closure; depth zero is the starting set according to the release contract.Bound a review or build dependency.
descendantsdescendants(x[, depth])Inclusive child closure; branch and merge paths are all followed.Estimate rewrite blast radius.
first_parentfirst_parent(x[, depth])Follows first-parent edges rather than all ancestry; important for merge-shaped history.Produce a release-line view without selecting side branches.
connectedconnected(x)Connected graph component containing the set under the documented visibility domain.Check whether a proposed range spans unrelated roots.
reachablereachable(sources, domain)Walk from source commits but include only nodes satisfying the domain; a domain is not merely an output filter in every graph shape.Find commits reachable from refs while excluding immutable or private regions.
rootsroots(x)Members with no parent in x; a merge-boundary set can have more than one root.Find patch-stack bases.
headsheads(x)Members with no descendant in x; an ancestor of a selected merge is not a head.Find publication tips and test endpoints.
mergesmerges(x)Members with more than one parent.Audit merge commits before a linear-only operation.
Function familyMembersInterpretation
Stateempty(), conflicted(), mutable(), immutable(), divergent()Predicates over stored commit state; an empty commit can still be published, and a conflicted commit can have descendants.
Visibilityall(), none(), visible_heads(), immutable_heads(), builtin_immutable_heads()Define the query domain and rewrite boundary; custom immutable heads are configuration, not a new commit type.
Identitycommit_id(pattern), change_id(pattern), present(x), evolution(x)Resolve object or evolution identity explicitly; present avoids failing a query when an optional symbol is absent.
Referencesbookmarks, remote_bookmarks, tracked_remote_bookmarks, untracked_remote_bookmarks, tags, remote_tags, git_refsReference state is not the same as reachability from a branch-like name.
Workspace/operationworkspaces, working_copies, at_operationLocal workspace and historical-operation views are first-class query inputs.
Metadataauthor, committer, author_email, committer_email, author_date, committer_date, descriptionArguments are string/date patterns; there is no email: pattern kind.
Contentfile, files, diff_contains, diff_lines_added, diff_lines_removedTree and patch predicates can be expensive and should be bounded before mutation.

For an unfamiliar function, establish four facts before using it in a destructive command: does it accept a set or a single commit argument; does it include its argument or only its closure; does it search visible commits or explicitly named hidden commits; and does it inspect metadata at one commit or compare two trees? The name alone does not answer these questions.

Fileset language workbench

Create a tree with deliberately similar names:

src/main.rs
src/lib.rs
src/generated/schema.rs
tests/api.rs
docs/api.md
Cargo.lock
README.md
build/output.bin
notes/old api.txt

By default, an unqualified path is a cwd-relative prefix glob. From the repository root, src selects src recursively. From a subdirectory, that same spelling is interpreted relative to the current working directory. Root-qualified forms make scripts independent of the caller's location:

ExpressionMatchesRecommended use
srccwd-relative prefix below srcInteractive command from a known directory.
root:srcworkspace-relative prefixExplicit subtree in a command where prefix form is intended.
root-file:src/lib.rsone exact pathScripts that must not include neighbours.
root-glob:src/*.rsRust files directly below srcNon-recursive glob selection.
root-prefix-glob:src/*.rsmatching files and matching prefixesRecursive path-oriented glob policy.
root-glob-i:docs/*.MDcase-insensitive glob matchesRepositories with inconsistent case, if supported by the target pattern.

Operators have the same useful shape as revsets: function/pattern, complement, intersection or difference, then union. Thus 'src ~ root-glob:src/**/generated/**' selects the source subtree except generated files. Parenthesise a union before complementing it; '~a | b' is not the same as '~(a | b)'.

jj file list 'root:src ~ root-prefix-glob:src/**/generated/**'
jj diff -r @ -- 'root-file:README.md | root-glob:docs/**/*.md'
jj restore --from SOURCE --into @ -- 'root:src & ~root-glob:src/**/*.generated.rs'

Quoting has two layers. The shell expands unquoted *, ?, and brackets before jj can parse them. Then jj parses operators, function calls, and string literals. Quote the complete fileset. If a path contains whitespace or parentheses, quote at both layers as the target syntax requires:

jj diff -- 'root-file:notes/old api.txt'
jj diff -- '~"notes/old api.txt"'
jj diff -- 'root-file:"notes/old api.txt"'

Use the exact pattern spelling supported by the installed help for paths containing a colon or quote. When a command accepts multiple path arguments, do not assume they are unioned in exactly the same way as one fileset expression; test the command contract and prefer one explicit expression when the difference matters.

Template language workbench

Start with a scalar and keep the output contract visible:

# Typed object rendered through a method.
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id.short() ++ "\n"'

# A list mapped to strings and joined at the output boundary.
jj log -r @ -T 'parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(", ") ++ "\n"'

# A conditional over a Boolean.
jj log -r @ -T 'if(conflict(), "CONFLICT ", "") ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# Width and truncation for a human log.
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T 'pad_end(12, commit_id.short()) ++ " " ++ truncate_end(72, description.first_line(), "…") ++ "\n"'

Methods are resolved from the value's type. commit_id.short() is a method on a commit-ID value. description.first_line() is a method on a string-like value. parents.map operates on a list and invokes a lambda once per element. Calling a commit method on commit_id.short(), or a string method on the commit itself, is a type error, not a formatting preference.

Optional values require a policy. A signature may be absent, a remote ref may not be tracked, and a workspace root may be stale. Use a Boolean predicate, if, try, or coalesce as appropriate. A fallback should identify absence rather than quietly render a misleading zero value.

jj log -r 'all()' -T '
if(signature, signature.name() ++ " <" ++ signature.email() ++ ">", "(unsigned)") ++
" " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"

The output of json is a string, so escape and delimit it at the record boundary. A description can contain a newline, tab, or control character. In the 0.44 template language there is no general object-literal syntax; emit independently escaped JSON scalar fields or use an external filter to assemble records:

jj --no-pager --color never log -r 'all()' -T '
json(commit_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(change_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(description.first_line()) ++ "\n"

For display templates, labels are semantic style rather than content. A label can be mapped to a configured colour and can be stripped when colour is disabled. Machine output should avoid labels and use --color=never. Raw escape sequences are an escape hatch for terminal control; they should not be used in a file intended for a Kindle PDF or a parser.

Language debugging and regression

Keep a language fixture with one root, one merge, one empty change, one conflict, one bookmark, one remote bookmark, and at least two descriptions containing whitespace and control characters. For each expression, store the expected symbolic descriptions rather than unstable generated IDs. The validator can then compare the ordered set for a display command and the unordered set for an algebraic query.

# A small regression harness pattern.
expr='heads(mutable() & ::@)'
actual=$(jj log --no-pager --color never -r "$expr" -T 'description.first_line() ++ "\n"')
expected=$'tests\n'
test "$actual" = "$expected"

# A template regression is about fields, not graph glyphs.
record=$(jj log --no-pager --color never -r @ -T 'json(change_id.normal_hex())')
printf '%s\n' "$record" | jq -e 'length > 10'

When a release changes a default template, the language itself may be unchanged. When a method disappears or a type changes, that is a version difference. Keep 0.44 examples in the main flow, label 0.45.1-only methods, and do not “fix” a failed regression by accepting a different field without recording why.

Command dossiers for complex operations

The compact command entries answer “what is this called?” These dossiers answer “what does it do to the graph, and how do I prove that it did what I intended?” They focus on the operations for which a Git-expert's first intuition is most likely to be incomplete. Each dossier describes a precondition, a state transition, a verification method, and a failure/recovery path.

Dossier: jj rebase

rebase has two independent arguments: a source selection and a destination relation. In 0.44, the source may be expressed with -r/--revisions, -s/--source, or -b/--branch. The destinations are expressed with -d/--destination or its current short spelling, with -A/--insert-after, or with -B/--insert-before. The exact long-name aliases are release-sensitive; the installed help capture is the authority for scripts, while the semantic distinction is stable.

-r selects the named commits, not necessarily their complete descendant stack. -s selects a source and its descendants. -b selects a branch-shaped set, which is useful when a fork is the intended unit but dangerous when the graph contains merges or multiple visible heads. The destination can be a commit set. A destination set with several commits can create a merge-shaped result or be rejected by topology rules; never infer the parent list from the command's success alone.

before:
A -- B -- C -- D
     ↑         ↑
  source B   @/tip

jj rebase -s B -d X

after:
A       X
 \       \
  B′ -- C′ -- D′

In the diagram, the source selection includes the descendants even though only B was named. Every rewritten commit receives a successor and a new commit ID; the logical change ID normally remains associated with its evolution. A bookmark targeting the old tip follows the local successor. A remote bookmark does not move. If the operation produces conflicts, those conflicts are stored in the successor commit; there is no Git-style transaction that must be continued before the graph can be inspected.

Before rebasing, render the source set and destination parents. Afterward, render every affected commit's full ID, change ID, parents, conflict state, and bookmarks. If the destination is fetched trunk, make sure the operation did not accidentally include an unrelated branch. If the result is wrong, prefer jj undo immediately; after later operations, use op log and op diff to identify the exact historical boundary.

Dossier: jj squash

squash moves a patch from a source change into a destination change. Its important choices are source, destination, and fileset. With the default current working-copy form, the source is usually @ and the destination is its parent; explicit --from and --into make the ownership change visible in a script. A fileset restricts which paths move. This is not the same operation as restoring a complete tree because the patch's logical ownership changes.

before:
A -- B -- C
     B: parser
     C: tests + docs

jj squash --from C --into B -- root-glob:tests/**

after:
A -- B′ -- C′
     B′: parser + tests
     C′: docs

Both source and destination can receive successors, and descendants of the destination can be rebased. The source can become empty; an empty commit is still a valid graph node and may remain useful as a review boundary. Inspect descriptions as well as trees: a squash can combine content without producing the review message you wanted. Use jj show and two filtered diffs to verify patch ownership.

A common failure is selecting a fileset that matches the source tree but not the source patch. A complete tree path can be present in both parent and source and therefore contribute no patch. Another is using a relative path from the wrong cwd. Prove the fileset with file list and prove the patch with diff before squashing. If only one path should move, do not use an unqualified squash and then attempt to reconstruct the source with restore.

Dossier: jj split

split introduces a new graph boundary inside a change. The selected commit's tree delta is divided into two changes, usually by an interactive diff editor or a fileset. The first resulting change retains the original parent; the second is its child. If the original has descendants, they are rebased onto the new tip. The split is therefore both content surgery and topology surgery.

before:
A -- B -- C
     B: API + tests

jj split -r B -- root-glob:src/api/**

after:
A -- B′ -- B″ -- C′
     API    tests

Decide which side receives the description and which side should be empty or minimal before invoking the editor. A split is not automatically a clean “first selected paths, then everything else” review story; file renames, executable-bit changes, conflict values, and paths absent from the working copy can make the boundary surprising. After the operation, compare diff -r B′, diff -r B″, and the combined range against the pre-split diff.

If the wrong paths were assigned, undo while the operation is fresh. Otherwise, use squash or restore to repair ownership and metaedit to repair descriptions. If the original commit was immutable, the operation will be rejected unless the safety boundary is deliberately bypassed. A split of a published change creates new commit IDs and may require a different publication strategy; do not assume a hosting service can reinterpret the old single-review boundary automatically.

Dossier: jj absorb

absorb distributes changes from the working-copy patch into mutable ancestors that already touch the relevant lines. Its value is not merely saving an interactive rebase: it uses patch context to assign hunks to changes. Its danger is that the assignment is a semantic heuristic, not a human review of intent. Run it over a small stack first and inspect the resulting descriptions, diffs, and evolution entries.

jj diff --summary
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj absorb
jj log -r 'stack(@)' -T '
change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id.short() ++ " " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"
'

Absorb can rewrite several ancestors in one operation and automatically rebase descendants. A hunk that matches no ancestor may remain in the working copy. A hunk that matches more than one candidate can require an interactive decision or be left untouched, depending on the release and configuration.
