An empty working-copy change after absorb is not necessarily a failure; it means the selected patch was assigned elsewhere.

Use filesets or a narrower commit set where supported by the target command. Keep a pre-operation patch file if the change matters. The operation log gives repository recovery, but it does not replace a record of the intended hunk ownership. For a review stack, absorb is excellent for moving a forgotten test adjustment into its API change, but it should be followed by tests at the stack tip and a review of each patch boundary.

Dossier: jj restore

restore copies file content from one tree to another. Its source and destination are tree endpoints, not review identities. In the common form it restores the working copy from its parent, discarding the selected working-copy changes. With explicit --from and --into, it can copy selected paths between arbitrary commits. Because it operates on trees, it can be used to repair a patch boundary without asking whether the source change should continue to exist.

# Inspect before copying.
jj diff -r @ -- root-glob:src/**
jj restore --from @- --into @ -- root-file:src/generated.rs
jj diff -r @ -- root-file:src/generated.rs

A restore does not mean “reset this commit” in the Git sense. It creates the destination tree that results from the selected source content and leaves the rest of the destination unchanged. If the source and destination have conflicts, the resulting tree can contain a conflict value. If the path is absent at the source, the result may be a deletion. Verify both a positive selection and an excluded neighbour.

Restore is often the right operation when the source content should be copied but the source change should remain a separate review unit. Squash is better when the source patch should change ownership and the source should be emptied or transformed. A direct filesystem edit followed by snapshot can produce the same tree but loses the explicit source/destination record that makes intent easy to audit.

Dossier: jj duplicate and jj revert

duplicate creates a new change whose tree content is based on an existing commit, usually at a chosen destination. It preserves patch intent but deliberately creates a new jj change identity. This is appropriate when the same logical code needs to be applied to an independent stack or when a useful change must be recovered without continuing the old evolution. It is not a way to move the original change.

revert creates a new change containing the inverse of a selected change at a destination. It preserves the fact that the original occurred and is therefore useful for a public history where abandoning the original would obscure the event. Revert is not guaranteed to be conflict-free: if later changes changed the same lines, the inverse can produce a stored conflict. Resolve and test the new change as usual.

A -- B -- C             A -- B -- C -- R
     feature                 feature    inverse(B)

duplicate B onto C's line:
A -- B -- C
      \       \
       D       (new change, independent identity)

When choosing between them, ask whether the desired result is another copy of the patch or a compensating inverse. Ask also whether the new review should be associated with the old change identity. Duplicate says no; revert creates a new change that records an inverse operation. Both can be followed by rebase, squash, or publication.

Dossier: jj abandon

abandon removes selected commits from the visible graph and reconnects or rebases descendants according to the operation's topology rules. It does not mean that the underlying objects instantly cease to exist. The operation log and evolution data may retain the old state long enough for recovery, subject to retention and garbage collection. Bookmarks pointing at abandoned commits need explicit inspection; a local bookmark may move with a successor or become conflicted depending on the graph.

jj log -r 'all()'
jj abandon -r 'obsolete-change'
jj log -r 'all()'
jj op show
jj evolog -r 'obsolete-change'

Do not abandon an ancestor as a substitute for removing its patch without examining descendants. In a linear stack, descendants may be rebased onto the abandoned commit's parent and retain much of their content. In a merge, removing one parent-side commit can change the merge result and create conflicts. If the abandoned work is needed elsewhere, recover it first with an explicit evolution/commit query or use duplicate before abandoning.

Dossier: jj resolve

resolve operates on stored conflict values. A conflict is a value in a tree or commit, not merely a half-completed interactive command. The conflict may have multiple sides and may exist in a descendant even when the parent is clean. The command's fileset selection can restrict which conflicts are resolved; external merge tools and marker styles are configured separately.

jj log -r 'conflicts()' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj resolve --list
jj resolve --tool meld
jj status
jj log -r 'conflicts()'

Manual resolution is a snapshot boundary. Open the materialised file, combine the sides according to the domain invariant, remove or replace markers as required by the configured style, and run a jj command that snapshots the intended result. Then query conflicts() again and inspect the commit ID. A clean status for one workspace does not prove that a conflicted ancestor or sibling remains absent.

For a multi-sided conflict, do not collapse the sides by choosing “ours” or “theirs” without knowing which parents are present and which change introduced each side. Preserve a copy of the conflict materialisation if the resolution is review-critical. If the merge tool fails, the conflict value remains available; fix the tool configuration or resolve manually, then use the operation log if the materialisation itself was accidentally overwritten.

Dossier: jj op

The operation namespace exposes log, show, diff, restore, revert, abandon, and integrate. An operation records a repository view transition; it is not a commit and is local to the clone. Operation IDs and commit IDs have different formats and lifetimes. The operation graph can have multiple parents when operations were made concurrently and later integrated.

CommandQuestion answeredMutation
op logWhat operation views exist and which is current?Normally observation plus snapshot policy.
op show OPWhat did one operation record?Observation.
op diff OPHow did the repository view differ from its parent?Observation.
op restore OPMake the current repository view match a historical operation.Yes; can discard later integrated state from the current view.
op revert OPCreate a new operation reversing the selected operation's effects where possible.Yes; preserves a later operation boundary.
op abandon OPRemove an operation from the operation graph.Yes; affects recovery history, not directly a commit's tree.
op integrate OPIntegrate an operation created without automatic integration or from a concurrent head.Yes; inspect parents and conflicts.

For a mistake made two commands ago, first use op log and op diff. Do not assume undo is equivalent to restoring any operation selected by eye. Undo is a convenience for the most recent operation and can itself become the latest operation. Historical restoration is a repository-wide view change; use it only after recording current state and understanding what later work will be removed from the view.

Dossier: jj git push

Git push is publication of bookmarks, not publication of an arbitrary workspace symbol. The important options select the remote, bookmark, and whether to allow a backwards update or create a new bookmark according to the installed command contract. A push may export commits needed to make the bookmark reachable, but it does not make the remote a participant in local change evolution.

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked
jj log -r 'review' -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark review

Before a force-like update, compare the local tracking target with the fetched remote bookmark. A safe force publication is a policy procedure: identify the remote, identify the old remote target, identify the new local target, check that the old target is the one you intended to replace, and record the review system's rules. A syntactically valid push with --allow-new or backwards permission is not proof that the remote update is safe.

If the push is rejected, fetch again before changing local history. The remote may have moved, the bookmark may not be tracked, the server may reject non-fast-forward updates, or the selected commits may be private/unexportable. Treat each cause differently. Moving a local bookmark to match the remote is not the same as rebasing your work onto the remote.

Dossier: bookmark state

Bookmark commands are ref operations. create introduces a local name, set creates or replaces one, move moves an existing name, and advance moves names forward under its configured/default policy. track and untrack change the relationship to a remote bookmark. delete expresses local deletion that can be published; forget removes local knowledge and can optionally include remote observations.

Use list output in three passes: local names, all remote names, then tracked pairs. A local bookmark at C, a remote bookmark at C_0, and a tracking relationship between them are three facts. A bookmark conflict means the remote relationship has incompatible target information; it is not the same as a file conflict. Resolve ref state by inspecting the target set and fetching authoritative remote state, not by choosing a commit from the default log display.

Dossier: layered configuration

config get answers what effective value is selected for one key; config list gives the broader effective view; config path identifies a scope file; config set and unset modify one layer; edit is safer for complex TOML; and gc is configuration maintenance. The scope flags are not interchangeable. A repository policy placed in the user file affects every checkout; a workspace override may affect only one working directory; an inline --config applies to one invocation.

jj config path --user
jj config path --repo
jj config path --workspace
jj config list revset-aliases
jj config get 'revset-aliases."immutable_heads()"'

Validate a new key by observing behaviour, not by successful parsing. An unknown TOML key can be inert. Validate type as well: a scalar string, a list, and a command table can all be syntactically valid but have different command contracts. Keep secrets out of captured diagnostics and use a disposable repository when testing aliases that invoke external commands.

Dossier: workspaces

workspace add creates another working directory and its workspace-local @. list displays the shared view, root finds the current directory, rename changes the workspace symbol from inside that workspace, update-stale reconciles a workspace after shared graph changes, and forget removes workspace metadata. None of these commands makes a separate clone or copies the operation history.

Use explicit names for build, review, and development workspaces. Before a graph rewrite, inspect all workspaces. Afterward, update a stale workspace only after checking unrecorded files. A build workspace that points at an old successor can be useful for comparing binaries; updating it blindly can discard the exact state you intended to test. Workspace-local @ is not a publication ref and should not be used as a cross-machine handoff identifier.

Dossier: jj run and jj fix

run applies a shell command in a commit-oriented context; fix invokes configured fixer tools over selected commits and files. Both are graph automation, not just convenience wrappers around a terminal loop. The selection set, working directory, environment, exit status, and whether the command edits a snapshot are all part of the contract. Pin the command and configuration in CI.

domain='mutable() & ::@'
jj log -r "$domain" -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj run -r "$domain" -- cargo test
jj op show

Run a read-only or dry-run mode when available, but do not treat it as proof that a later mutable invocation will select the same set. The graph can change between commands. A fixer that formats a line in an ancestor causes descendant successors; a shell test that merely checks out a commit and exits does not necessarily create a jj rewrite. Inspect both the operation log and the filesystem after the command.

Dossier: jj bisect

Bisect is a graph search over a set of commits using a user-supplied predicate. It assumes that the predicate changes monotonically along the chosen history in the sense required by the command. A merge graph can violate that assumption: a bad side branch merged into a good mainline does not create a single total order. Choose a first-parent or explicitly linear domain when that is what the test represents.

jj bisect run --revision 'main@origin..@' -- cargo test -- package::regression
jj bisect log
jj bisect reset

Record the test command, environment, generated files, and return-code convention. A flaky test makes the result a statement about noise, not about a commit. After bisect, inspect the selected commit and compare its parents; a graph boundary discovered by the command is not automatically a bookmark or a new change. Reset or abandon bisect state according to the target command's documented lifecycle.

Dossier: tags and signing

Tags name release points and are normally more immutable in a release policy than working bookmarks. Tag tracking is distinct from bookmark tracking, and remote tags are distinct from local tags. A release tag should point to a fully tested publication commit, not to a mutable workspace merely because @ is convenient.

Signing rewrites commit metadata. A signed successor can have a new commit ID while retaining its change ID and local bookmark relationship. Verify the signature using the configured backend and record whether the verification invoked GPG, SSH, or another external program. Signing is not a server transport guarantee: a Git host may apply its own verification or require a particular key identity.

Revset reference atlas

This is a reader-facing reference, not a transcription of help output. Each entry states the input kind, the set returned, and the question it answers. The examples use the query-lab graph; replace the symbolic labels with real commits only after rendering the set. A function that accepts a commit expression does not necessarily require a single commit, and a function that returns a set does not make every consuming command set-valued.

Topology and closure

FunctionInputResultExample and use
parents(x)Revset, optional depthParents of members of x; unioned as a set.parents(@) finds the immediate base; use a parent template for a merge.
children(x)Revset, optional depthDirect or depth-limited children of x.children(B) distinguishes a fork from a linear successor.
ancestors(x)Revset, optional depthInclusive transitive parent closure.ancestors(@, 2) bounds a local context without including all trunk.
descendants(x)Revset, optional depthInclusive transitive child closure.descendants(B, 1) estimates immediate rewrite blast radius.
first_parent(x)Revset, optional depthFirst-parent walk, useful for merge-oriented views.Use on a release line when side-parent changes should not appear as a linear series.
roots(x)RevsetMembers with no parent also in x.roots(main@origin..@) identifies the base of each unpublished component.
heads(x)RevsetMembers with no descendant also in x.heads(B::G) is G in the worked merge, not its parent-side ancestor.
merges(x)RevsetMembers with more than one parent.merges(::@) audits whether a stack is actually linear.
connected(x)RevsetThe connected component or components containing the set under the documented graph domain.Use before a bulk rewrite to detect independent roots.
reachable(s, d)Source revset and domain revsetWalks from s while restricting the reachable result using d.reachable(bookmarks(), mutable()) asks which mutable nodes are reachable from named refs.

The closure functions are inclusive at depth one: ancestors(B, 0) and descendants(B, 0) are the useful boundary cases to test against the installed binary because an omitted depth and an explicit depth have different meaning. A merge makes set union visible: parents(G) contains both parent sides, and ancestors(G) contains their common ancestry only once. Do not interpret a returned set as an ordered path.

roots and heads are dual boundary operators within a selected set. They are not the repository root and the repository's all-time tips unless their input is the corresponding whole domain. On B::G, roots returns B and heads returns G. On ::G, roots returns the repository root and heads returns G. On a union containing unrelated stacks, both can return multiple commits.

State, visibility, and identity

FunctionContractEdge caseOperational pattern
all()All visible commits in the current repository view.Hidden commits are not silently included; explicitly naming one changes the available search space.Use for fixture inspection, not unbounded mutation.
none()Empty commit set.Useful in aliases and tests; commands may reject an empty selection even when parsing succeeds.Use as a safe default for an optional publication set.
visible_heads()Heads of the visible commit graph.Not the same as heads of an arbitrary selected set.Define the visible traversal boundary explicitly.
immutable_heads()Configured immutable-head boundary.The alias is configuration; an inert key with a similar name changes nothing.Inspect before --ignore-immutable or policy changes.
builtin_immutable_heads()Built-in immutable boundary before custom policy.Useful for comparing policy to release defaults.Audit a repository's safety policy after changing aliases.
mutable()Commits permitted to be rewritten under the current policy.A mutable commit may still have an immutable ancestor and a remote bookmark.Bound local surgery with mutable() & ::@.
immutable()Commits protected by the current policy.Protection is a command safety rule, not permanent object storage.Find the boundary before explaining a rejected rewrite.
conflicted()Commits containing conflict values.A clean working directory does not prove this set is empty elsewhere.Use after rebase, merge, squash, or restore.
empty()Commits whose tree delta is empty under the command's comparison semantics.An empty commit is still a graph node and can have descendants or a bookmark.Audit accidental empty review changes.
divergent()Commits belonging to divergent change evolutions.A divergent change ID may require an offset or a set query.Find local evolution forks before convergence or manual surgery.
present(x)Commits in x that are present/visible under the current view.Useful around optional names; it does not resurrect an arbitrary deleted object.Guard a publication query against a missing remote ref.
evolution(x)Evolution-related commits for the selected change or set.History includes predecessors/successors rather than a single current tip.Investigate what an ancestor rewrite replaced.
root()The repository root commit.Its parent set is empty; it is not a normal mutable working change.Use root():: for a complete ancestry domain.

Visibility and immutability are independent dimensions. A hidden commit can remain immutable, and a visible commit can be mutable. A remote bookmark can point at a mutable commit if local policy permits it; publication does not automatically make every local descendant immutable unless the configured boundary says so. Use separate predicates in a query when both dimensions matter.

Bookmarks, tags, remotes, and workspaces

FunctionArgumentsMeaningExample
bookmarks()Optional name patternCommits targeted by local bookmarks matching the pattern.bookmarks("pr/*") finds local PR boundaries.
remote_bookmarks()Optional name and remote patternsCommits targeted by remote-tracking bookmarks.remote_bookmarks("main", remote="origin") selects fetched trunk.
tracked_remote_bookmarks()Optional patternsRemote bookmarks participating in tracking relationships.Audit only refs that supply ahead/behind information.
untracked_remote_bookmarks()Optional patternsRemote bookmarks without local tracking.Find fetched refs that a push script must qualify explicitly.
tags()Optional name patternCommits targeted by local tags.tags("v*") finds release points.
remote_tags()Optional name and remote patternsFetched remote tag targets.Compare release tags across remotes.
git_refs()Optional name patternGit refs imported into the jj view.Audit refs created by native Git in a colocated repository.
workspaces()Optional workspace patternWorking-copy targets for matching workspaces.workspaces("build") locates the build workspace's @.
working_copies()Optional workspace patternCommits serving as working copies.Detect a commit held by multiple workspaces.
at_operation()Operation selector and optional revsetEvaluates a commit expression in a historical repository view.Compare at_operation(OP, @) with current @.

Reference functions return targets, not the names themselves. To render names, use a commit template's bookmark or tag methods. This separation is useful: bookmarks() can select a set of commits, while a template can then show all names attached to each selected commit. If two local names point to the same commit, the selection still contains one commit.

Remote arguments are patterns and can be qualified by labels according to the installed syntax. An unqualified remote_bookmarks("main") can select same-named refs from several remotes. In a publication safety check, pass the remote pattern or use an explicitly qualified symbol. In 0.45.1, remote tag predicates distinguish tracked and untracked relationships; those names are not presented as 0.44 syntax.

Metadata and content predicates

FunctionArgumentMatching ruleExample
description(pattern)String patternMatches commit descriptions using explicit pattern kinds.description(substring:"quota").
author(pattern)String patternMatches author identity according to the installed pattern semantics.author(glob:"A.*").
committer(pattern)String patternMatches committer identity.Use when import/rewrite actor differs from author.
author_email(pattern)String patternMatches the author email field.author_email(exact:"dev@example.org").
committer_email(pattern)String patternMatches the committer email field.Distinguish automation from the original author.
author_date(pattern)Date patternMatches author timestamps.author_date(after:"2026-01-01").
committer_date(pattern)Date patternMatches committer timestamps.Find changes rewritten this week.
commit_id(pattern)String patternResolves commit IDs without bookmark/change-ID precedence surprises.commit_id(exact:"full-id").
change_id(pattern)String patternResolves visible change IDs and their matching evolutions.Use a full ID or offset for divergence.
file(pattern)Fileset-like path patternSelects commits containing a matching file.file(root-file:"src/api.rs").
files(fileset)Fileset expressionSelects commits changing/containing paths under the documented predicate.Bound to a stack before use in automation.
diff_contains(pattern)String patternMatches changed content in a diff.Find commits whose patch contains a diagnostic string.
diff_lines_added(pattern)String patternMatches added lines.Audit introduction of an API or marker.
diff_lines_removed(pattern)String patternMatches removed lines.Find the change that removed a compatibility path.

Pattern arguments are not a single universal string syntax. String patterns support explicit kinds such as exact:, substring:, glob:, and regex:. Date patterns have their own forms and ranges. The invalid form author(email:"...") fails because email is not a pattern kind in 0.44; use author_email(exact:"...") or the documented author pattern semantics.

Content predicates can be expensive. A file predicate compares trees and a diff predicate examines patch content. First narrow the graph with mutable() & ::@, a publication range, or a date window. If the query is used in a bulk rewrite, render the result and record how many commits matched before invoking the mutation.

Patterns, dates, and aliases

A pattern is a typed value passed to a matcher. It is not the same thing as a shell glob and not the same thing as a fileset. The shell must pass the pattern intact; jj then parses the pattern prefix. These examples show the intent:

description(exact:"fix quota boundary")
description(substring:"quota")
description(glob:"fix * boundary")
description(regex:"quota[0-9]+")
author_email(exact:"dev@example.org")
author_date(after:"2026-01-01" & before:"2026-02-01")

Use exact patterns for identity and release automation. Use substring for interactive search. Use glob when a conventional naming family is deliberate, and regex only when the additional syntax is worth the portability and escaping cost. A pattern alias can hide that policy; give aliases descriptive names and document whether they match case, punctuation, and whitespace.

Revset aliases are parsed in the context in which they are used. A parameterless alias names a reusable set; a parameterised alias behaves like a function. Keep aliases small enough that jj log -r ALIAS remains explainable. Avoid aliases that silently choose the first result or that combine publication and mutability policy without a name that reveals both. Test recursive aliases and aliases that shadow builtin functions in a disposable configuration.

[revset-aliases]
"unpublished_stack()" = "stack(@) ~ ::remote_bookmarks()"
"owned_work()" = "author_email(exact:\"dev@example.org\") & mutable()"
"release_line()" = "first_parent(tags())"

The examples above show the conceptual shape; quote TOML strings and pattern arguments exactly as required by the installed parser. A parameterised alias's argument substitution is not shell interpolation. If an alias is used in a script, include one expansion test in the repository's validation suite.

Derivation exercises

Exercise: derive unpublished work

Start with a graph containing two local stacks and two remote bookmarks. The goal is “mutable commits in the current workspace's ancestry that are not reachable from any remote bookmark”. Begin with ::@. Intersect with mutable(). The publication exclusion is ~::remote_bookmarks(). The derived expression is mutable() & ::@ ~ ::remote_bookmarks(), with parentheses added around the positive domain if it is extended. Verify that a mutable commit on an unrelated branch is excluded, while a mutable unpublished ancestor remains. If the expression returns a merge, decide whether its heads or all patch members are wanted.

Exercise: heads of a non-linear query

Using the workbench graph, calculate B::G first. It contains B, both side paths, and G. Test each member for whether it is an ancestor of another member. B, C, D, and E are ancestors of G; G is not an ancestor of another member in the set. Therefore heads(B::G) is {G}. Now calculate heads((B::G) ~ merges()). The merge is removed from the domain, so the heads can change; the answer must be derived from the resulting set, not copied from the earlier result. This is why a heads query should be evaluated after every filter.

Exercise: a range across a fork

Let L and R be sibling tips from a common base. Calculate L..R as ::R ~ ::L. The result contains R's ancestry that is not also in L's ancestry; it does not represent a unique sequence of edges from L to R. Now add a merge descendant that contains both sides and recalculate. The merge can enter the set even though L is not an ancestor of R. A Git user accustomed to a branch range should write the intended review base and server ref explicitly rather than relying on the visual branch names.

Exercise: precedence proof

Compare A | B & C, (A | B) & C, and A | (B & C). The first and third are equivalent because intersection binds more tightly than union; the second is different. Then compare A ~ B & C with A ~ (B & C). Difference and intersection have the same precedence and associate left-to-right in the installed grammar, so the unparenthesised form is (A ~ B) & C. Do not rely on a language remembered from another tool.

Exercise: cardinality as a changing property

Define candidate = heads(mutable() & ::@). It is singleton-valued in a linear stack. Create a second child or a fork; the same expression becomes multi-valued. A command such as jj edit "$candidate" now fails rather than choosing arbitrarily. This is a useful failure. A script may assert one result, or it may use a set-valued command and handle several tips. The query has not become wrong merely because its cardinality changed; the consumer's precondition has changed.

Configuration and template reference in depth

Configuration is part of jj's execution model. It selects defaults, defines aliases, controls immutability, chooses external tools, and can change which commits an apparently unqualified command touches. A configuration file is therefore closer to a program's policy module than to a collection of cosmetic preferences. Keep the stable personal layer small, keep repository policy reviewable, and treat workspace overrides as local experiments.

The layer model

On Linux, jj loads built-in defaults and then the configured user, repository, workspace, and command-line inputs in the order documented by the target release. The exact paths are discovered with jj config path; do not hard-code an XDG path in an installer. --config NAME=VALUE is an invocation override. --config-file PATH adds a file as input; it is not the same as selecting the user/repository/workspace file that config edit modifies.

LayerTypical scopeGood contentsHazard
Built-inEvery invocationSafe defaults supplied by jjDefaults can change between releases.
UserOne Linux user across repositoriesIdentity, pager, editor, personal aliasesA remote or immutable policy can unexpectedly affect unrelated repositories.
RepositoryOne repositoryProject revsets, fileset aliases, tool policyOpening an unfamiliar repository can load policy you did not write.
WorkspaceOne working directoryTemporary log view, build/review defaultsAnother workspace in the same store does not necessarily share it.
Command lineOne processVersion gates, dry runs, explicit remote, diagnostic overrideQuoting and TOML typing errors are easy to hide in shell history.

For a scalar key, a higher layer normally replaces a lower value. For tables and aliases, merging and replacement rules make it unsafe to assume that “unset the top-level table” removes every child. Diagnose with config list, then inspect the scope files. Use a temporary alternative config file when testing a policy; never edit the production user config first.

A progressive Linux configuration

# ~/.config/jj/config.toml, discovered with jj config path --user
[user]
name = "A. Engineer"
email = "a.engineer@example.org"

[ui]
pager = "less -FRX"
paginate = "auto"
color = "auto"
default-command = ["log", "-r", "stack(@)"]

[revset-aliases]
"mine()" = "author_email(exact:\"a.engineer@example.org\")"
"my_work()" = "mine() & mutable()"
"published()" = "::remote_bookmarks()"
"unpublished()" = "my_work() ~ published()"

[fileset-aliases]
"LOCKFILES" = "root-glob:**/Cargo.lock | root-glob:**/package-lock.json"

[template-aliases]
"brief_commit(commit)" = "commit.change_id().short() ++ \" \" ++ commit.description().first_line()"

Start with identity and output. Add revset aliases only after testing them against a non-linear fixture. Add fileset aliases when the same path policy appears in several commands. Add template aliases last, because a typo in a type-sensitive method can make all log output fail. A repository-specific config may redefine the log revset, but a publication alias should name its remote explicitly or accept the remote as a visible argument.

Splitting files under a conf.d directory is convenient only when the installed release supports the layout and load order. Verify it with config list and a harmless sentinel value in a disposable configuration. The maintainable principle is independent of the directory name: keep UI, aliases, templates, Git transport, and Gerrit policy reviewable as separate units and document their precedence.

UI, snapshot, and editor policy

FamilyKey examplesDesign choiceInteraction
Identityuser.name, user.emailDefault author/committer identityExplicit metadata options can override it; operation username/hostname is a separate record.
Outputui.color, ui.paginate, ui.pager, ui.graph, timestampsInteractive readability versus stable scriptsCommand-line --color=never and --no-pager should win in automation.
Editingui.editor, ui.diff-editor, ui.merge-editorHow descriptions, patches, and conflicts are editedExternal tools have their own merge-tools.* configuration and exit-status contract.
Conflict renderingui.conflict-marker-styleHow stored conflicts are materialisedChanging style changes the working-copy representation, not the underlying conflict value.
Snapshotsnapshot.auto-track, size/EOL/exec-bit policyWhich filesystem paths enter the next treeIgnored/untracked files can make a command appear to have done nothing.
Movementworking-copy movement/update policyHow commands choose or update @Workspace-local state and stale-workspace repair must be considered.

The pager is a particularly common source of false failures. A program that pipes jj log to a parser may receive a pager process or terminal control sequences unless it explicitly disables both. Conversely, forcing --no-pager in every interactive alias can make a large history unusable. Keep human and machine aliases distinct.

Snapshot settings are policy, not a substitute for .gitignore-style project discipline. Test a maximum new-file size with a disposable large file, test executable-bit changes on a filesystem that supports them, and test a repository from a subdirectory. Record whether the policy prevents tracking, warns, or refuses an operation. A configuration that “looks right” in TOML can still be ineffective if the command's default snapshot path never considers the file.

Revsets, aliases, and rewrite safety

Defaults such as the log, status, fix, sign, run, and bookmark-advance revsets are executable policy. A default is evaluated in a graph that changes over time, so a command that was narrow when configured can become broad after a merge or workspace handoff. Before changing a default, run the old and new expressions side by side on the query fixture.

old=$(jj config get revsets.log)
jj log -r "$old" -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r 'stack(@) | main@origin' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

The immutable boundary is a special case. In 0.44, the configured function is revset-aliases."immutable_heads()", whose definition can refer to tags, remote bookmarks, and the root. A key called revsets.immutable_heads is merely unknown data and does not redefine the function. Demonstrate a safety setting by querying the function and attempting a protected rewrite in a disposable repository.

[revset-aliases]
"immutable_heads()" = "builtin_immutable_heads() | main@origin | tags()"

Keep parameterised aliases small. An alias that accepts a pattern and inserts it into a regex or fileset can be useful, but it also creates a second quoting boundary and can be a source of injection-like mistakes in shell automation. Prefer fixed aliases for publication and require an explicit commit argument for destructive operations.

GitHub and Gerrit configuration

Git transport configuration should identify credentials and remote URLs, not silently encode a publication decision that differs by repository. Use jj git remote list to inspect names and URLs, and use explicit --remote in pushes. A repository can have an origin GitHub remote and a gerrit remote without either being “the” remote in the abstract.

[git]
# Keep publication remote explicit in shared scripts.
private-commits = "mutable() ~ ::remote_bookmarks()"

[gerrit]
# Use only keys supported by the installed release; on 0.44 pass
# --remote and --remote-branch to gerrit upload.
default-remote = "gerrit"
default-remote-branch = "main"

The Gerrit defaults shown are a 0.45.x comparison surface where documented; 0.44 instructions should pass the remote and branch explicitly. Configuration can select a default, but it does not convert jj change IDs into Gerrit Change-Ids. Preserve the server footer in commit descriptions and inspect both identities before upload.

For conditional configuration, match repository identity or path only when the condition is visible and testable. A safe pattern is to keep the user layer neutral, place GitHub/Gerrit aliases in repository-level policy, and make each alias print a dry-run or require the remote in its invocation. Never make a broad command alias called push choose a server based solely on the current directory without showing the choice.

Template reference: types and composition

Templates have values, methods, functions, and a rendering boundary. In a commit template, zero-argument commit methods are available as keywords; in an operation template, operation methods are available. Explicit method calls are clearer when a template is copied between commands.

ValueImportant methods/fieldsUseFailure mode
Commitcommit_id(), change_id(), description(), parents(), author(), committer(), state predicatesLog/show recordsPrinting the object directly or calling a method with the wrong return type.
CommitId/ChangeIdshort(), shortest(), normal_hex()Human and exact identitiesConfusing a shortest prefix with a stable full identifier.
Signaturename(), email(), timestamp()Author/committer displayAssuming author and committer are one object.
Timestampago(), format(), utc(), local()Review age and reproducible reportsLocal timezone or clock changes output.
CommitRefname(), remote(), target/tracking predicatesBookmark/tag statusConflicted refs have more than one target state.
Listmap, filter, join, first, lastParents, refs, pathsEmpty list and wrong element type.
Optionconditional/unwrap/fallback functionsSignatures and optional refsCalling contained methods before testing presence.
Templatefill, indent, padding, truncation, labelsHuman layoutWidths and escape sequences differ from visible byte length.

Arithmetic operators operate on integers; comparisons operate on compatible scalar values; boolean operators short-circuit; and ++ concatenates template values. Parenthesise mixed arithmetic and concatenation. A style label is metadata attached to rendered content and may disappear when colour is disabled. This is why styles should not be parsed as data.

Building a compact daily log

# 1. Identity and subject.
jj log -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

# 2. Add the object ID and graph state.
jj log -T 'change_id.short() ++ "/" ++ commit_id.short() ++ " " ++
if(conflict(), "[conflict] ", "") ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"

# 3. Add refs and a bounded parent list.
jj log -T 'change_id.short() ++ "/" ++ commit_id.short() ++ " " ++
bookmarks.map(|b| b.name()).join(",") ++ " [" ++
parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(",") ++ "] " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"

The source examples use line-leading operators only inside a code block; indent them in shell files if a patch or code-generation tool treats a leading plus specially. A daily template should remain cheap: avoid full diff rendering, signature verification, and expensive file predicates for every commit. Create a second “forensic” template for those fields.

# A forensic record, with colour and pager disabled.
jj --no-pager --color never log -r 'all()' -T '
json(commit_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(change_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(description.first_line()) ++ "\n"
'

Test each expression against root, merge, conflict, empty, and missing-ref cases. A template that works at @ can fail on root because a parent list is empty, or on an unsigned commit because an optional signature is absent. Optional values are not exceptional data; they are part of the normal graph.

Template debugging and portability

Debug a template by reducing it to self, one keyword, one method, and one combinator. Keep a “type probe” template for a fixture rather than trying to infer types from an error message after a complex expression has failed. Use explicit full IDs when output will be used to select a commit again; use short IDs only for human scanning.

When moving a template between 0.44 and 0.45.1, compare the local help captures for method names and argument order. A template alias can hide a version difference, so include the alias definition in a release note. If a field is not available in both releases, either omit it from the shared template or supply two clearly named aliases. Never label a 0.45-only method as if it were an installed 0.44 builtin.

Command reference atlas: the remaining surface

The 0.44 command inventory contains more than the history-rewriting core. These commands are smaller individually but matter in a Linux field workflow because they form the inspection, transport, and maintenance boundary. The canonical names below are the names used by the installed help; short aliases such as op for operation are shown only as conveniences.

Inspection and navigation

CommandArguments/options that matterSemantic contractTypical trap
jj statusGlobal repository, snapshot, pager and colour controlsReports working-copy state and normally snapshots first.Treating “clean” as proof that every graph commit is conflict-free.
jj log-r/--revisions, -T/--template, limit/reverse/graph/output controlsRenders a commit set; the default revset and template are configuration.Parsing graph glyphs or assuming the displayed order is a topological proof.
jj showCommit selector, template, patch/output optionsShows commit metadata and tree difference for selected commits.Confusing the displayed patch with the complete tree or with a fileset-limited diff.
jj diffCommit endpoints, fileset arguments, format/stat/template controlsCompares trees or working-copy state and limits paths through a fileset.Using an unquoted glob and letting the shell choose the paths.
jj interdiffTwo commit/change endpoints and output/fileset controlsCompares the patches represented by two commits, useful for review iterations.Using it as a tree diff when the two endpoints have different bases.
jj rootRepository selectionPrints the workspace/repository root used by jj.Confusing it with jj git root, which answers a backend Git-directory question.
jj nextMovement options and optional commit selectionMoves the workspace through graph children according to the command's selection policy.Assuming it moves a bookmark or chooses the only visual child in a fork.
jj prevMovement options and optional commit selectionMoves the workspace through graph parents.Assuming a merge has one parent or that movement rewrites history.
jj editExactly one commit in normal use; movement/allow optionsMoves the current workspace's working-copy pointer to an existing commit and materialises its tree.Expecting another workspace's @ or a bookmark to move.

For stable evidence, separate the selected set from rendering. A log command may show an abbreviated ID, labels, or a graph; a diagnostic template can render the full commit ID and parent IDs. A diff command may select a set of commits but render them in a chosen order. An inspection command can still snapshot unless the global working-copy option is used.

File subcommands

CommandPurposeSelectionState effect
jj file annotateBlame-like line attribution for a commit/treeCommit plus fileset/path argumentsInspection; normally no graph rewrite, but snapshot policy still applies.
jj file chmodChange executable-bit stateFileset plus executable/non-executable optionChanges working-copy tree on the next snapshot.
jj file listList files in a treeCommit and filesetInspection; ideal for testing fileset parsing.
jj file searchSearch text in a tree/workspacePattern and optional commit/filesetInspection; output can contain arbitrary user text.
jj file showPrint one file from a commitCommit and path/fileset contractInspection; binary and conflict values need explicit handling.
jj file trackMark paths for snapshot trackingFilesetChanges tracking state, which affects a future snapshot.
jj file untrackStop tracking pathsFilesetChanges tracking state; it is not deletion of the file from a commit.

File commands are where the distinction between a path and a fileset has practical consequences. file show generally wants a path whose content can be printed; file list can display a set; file track changes what the next snapshot will consider. A command can successfully parse a path and still find no matching file. Test a positive and a negative case in the fileset fixture.

Git backend and remote subcommands

CommandPurposeNetwork?Important state distinction
jj git initInitialise a jj repository with a Git backendNoCreates/associates Git storage; it is not a remote clone.
jj git cloneClone a Git repository into a jj working copyYesCreates a new clone with an independent operation history.
jj git fetchFetch Git refs and update remote-bookmark observationsYesRemote names are observations until local tracking/publication policy uses them.
jj git pushExport and push selected bookmark refsYesPublishes refs; it does not push the operation log or workspace pointer.
jj git importImport Git ref changes into jjNoReconciles native Git-side ref changes with jj's view.
jj git exportExport jj ref/commit state to Git storageNoMakes local jj state available to Git-native tools.
jj git rootPrint Git backend root/directoryNoNot necessarily the same path as the workspace root.
jj git colocation statusReport whether Git and jj are colocatedNoAnswers backend layout, not whether refs are synchronised.
jj git colocation enableEnable colocated Git stateNoAdds interoperability surface; establish one-writer discipline.
jj git colocation disableDisable colocationNoChanges backend arrangement; check native Git tooling before use.

Remote management has its own namespace: remote add records a name and URL, remote list displays them, remote remove removes a configured remote, remote rename changes the local name, and remote set-url changes its URL. None of those operations fetches or pushes by itself. A remote URL can be valid while its credentials or server permissions are not.

jj git remote add github git@github.com:org/project.git
jj git remote add gerrit ssh://review.example.org/project
jj git remote list
jj git fetch --remote github
jj git fetch --remote gerrit

After a native Git fetch in a colocated repository, use the supported jj import operation before querying remote bookmarks. After a jj bookmark update, export when a Git-native tool must inspect the new ref. Concurrently changing the worktree with Git and jj is unsafe even if the two commands target different refs; snapshot ownership is a filesystem concern.

Tag commands

jj tag set creates or moves a local tag at a selected commit; tag list inspects tags; tag delete changes local tag state; tag track and tag untrack manage relationships to remote tags. Tag names are publication labels, not mutable stack organisation. A release tag should be placed only after the exact commit and parents have been tested, and the release policy should make the tag part of the immutable-head boundary.

jj tag set v2.4.0 -r release-candidate
jj tag list
jj tag track v2.4.0 --remote origin
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark release-candidate

The transport command for tags and its supported options should be checked in the target help; a local tag operation alone does not publish it. 0.45.1 adds tracked/untracked remote tag revset predicates to the comparison surface. Do not write those predicates in a 0.44 shared alias.

Utilities and maintenance

CommandUseWhy it deserves a contract
jj versionPrint binary versionUse in every reproducibility report and version-gated script.
jj helpDiscover command tree and topicsHelp is the installed release's normative syntax surface.
jj util completionGenerate shell completionCompletion can expose aliases and descriptions but is not a substitute for help.
jj util config-schemaEmit machine-readable config schemaSchema validates types and keys but not human policy interactions.
jj util backend nameIdentify the backendUseful when a repository's storage assumptions are unclear.
jj util gcGarbage-collect jj storageCan reduce recovery options; run only after op/evolution retention review.
jj util snapshotExplicit snapshot utilityChanges the normal command-boundary assumption; coordinate workspace ownership.
jj util execUtility-level command executionArgument forwarding and environment are release-sensitive; do not use as an undocumented shell wrapper.
jj util markdown-helpGenerate installed help materialUseful for research; generated help is not the authored book.
jj util install-man-pagesInstall local man pagesWrites outside the repository and should be treated as an installation action.

Maintenance commands are deliberately absent from the everyday alias layer. A daily alias may call status, log, or a bounded diff; it should not call storage GC, config GC, or an operation-abandoning command as a side effect. Keep the research captures and the PDF build independent of repository maintenance.

Global options as an explicit contract

-R/--repository selects a repository. It does not select a Git remote, workspace, or operation. --ignore-working-copy prevents snapshot/update of the filesystem. --at-operation loads a historical repository view and therefore implies that current filesystem edits must not be inserted into it. --ignore-immutable bypasses rewrite protection and should be rare in a script. --no-integrate-operation changes operation integration and creates a concurrency/recovery responsibility.

--color, --no-pager, and --quiet affect output. Repeatable --config and --config-file affect input. -h/--help and -V/--version are discovery/version controls. The fact that a flag appears globally does not make its semantic effect identical in every subcommand; the command-specific help remains authoritative.

Advanced mechanics labbook

The unusual features of jj become useful only when they are treated as mechanics rather than slogans. This labbook gives experiments for the features that most often alter an expert user's operating assumptions: immutable heads, hidden commits, divergent changes, concurrent operations, sparse working copies, fixers, signing, and machine-readable output.

Immutable heads: a policy boundary, not a property of a commit

The immutable set is computed from a revset alias. A commit is not born immutable because it has a particular author, description, or age. It is protected because it is reachable from the configured immutable heads under jj's rewrite rules. This makes the policy inspectable and changeable, but it also means that a configuration change can materially alter which commands are permitted.

[revset-aliases]
"immutable_heads()" = "builtin_immutable_heads() | main@origin | tags()"

In the target 0.44 configuration syntax, query the resulting set with jj log -r 'immutable_heads()', then compare it with builtin_immutable_heads(). Create a disposable child below a protected commit and try an edit or rebase. The expected failure is evidence that the alias is active. Change an unrelated key named revsets.immutable_heads and repeat: the function result should not change. This negative experiment is important because unknown TOML keys are not necessarily rejected.

Do not make every remote bookmark immutable by reflex. A repository that routinely rewrites review bookmarks may need a boundary at fetched trunk and release tags while leaving local topic commits mutable. Conversely, a release repository may intentionally include all remote tags. In 0.45.1, the default treatment of tracked/untracked remote tags differs; the book's comparison note names the newer predicates rather than silently altering the 0.44 policy.

Hidden commits, evolution, and divergence

When a mutable commit is rewritten, its predecessor may become hidden from ordinary visible queries. The predecessor is not the same thing as a deleted Git commit: it remains addressable through its full commit ID, an evolution query, or a historical operation view while retention permits. The successor is the current visible form of the logical change. A divergent change occurs when two visible successors exist for one change identity, commonly because two independent operations rewrote the same predecessor and were later brought together.

          q_0 (predecessor, hidden)
         /  \
    q_1 (visible)  q_2 (visible)
      same jj change ID
      divergent evolution

Use jj evolog to see the evolution rather than relying on jj log -r 'all()'. A change ID alone can be ambiguous in the divergent state; use a change offset or an explicit set of successors. The commit IDs distinguish the two concrete trees and parent relationships. If one successor is the intended one, inspect its descendants and bookmarks before abandoning the other. In 0.44 there is no converge command; use explicit graph surgery and resolution. In 0.45.1 the comparison command can assist, but it remains a rewrite whose result must be reviewed.

Evolution is a local explanatory history, not a publication protocol. A remote server sees exported Git objects and refs, not the full jj predecessor/successor relation. Preserve a mapping in review notes when a server-side review is being updated. The mapping is also useful when a test report or CI system still mentions the old commit ID.

Concurrent operations and integration

Jj operations are serialised through an operation head, but concurrent processes can create operation nodes from a common parent. One process may use --no-integrate-operation or otherwise finish while another process has advanced the integrated head. The operation graph then records more than one line of activity. This is not the same as two commit branches: the competing nodes describe repository-view transitions, not necessarily different commit parents.

          op-A: rebase stack
         /
op_0 ----+
         \
          op-B: update bookmark

integrate or inspect both before choosing a current view

When diagnosing concurrency, identify the common operation parent, inspect each operation's diff, and determine whether their changes touch disjoint repository state. An operation that changed a bookmark and an operation that rewrote a commit may be integrable, but this is a property of their repository transformations. Do not infer it from process start order. The operation integration command and its conflict behaviour are documented by the installed help; test them in the recovery fixture.

A single-writer rule is still the simplest policy for a workspace filesystem. Operation integration does not make simultaneous editors safe. Two processes snapshotting the same files can race before the operation graph has a chance to record the difference. Separate workspaces for build/test processes reduce that risk; separate clones eliminate shared filesystem ownership but also create independent operation histories.

Sparse working copies

Sparse patterns define which files are materialised in a workspace. They do not change the commit tree, the revset result, or the publication ref. A file absent from the sparse working copy can still exist in the commit and can still be selected by a root-qualified fileset. This is the correct mental model for a large monorepo: sparse state is a materialisation choice, not a history filter.

# Inspect the current pattern set.
jj sparse list

# Replace it with an explicit root-relative selection.
jj sparse set --clear --add src --add tests

# Restore the full materialisation when needed.
jj sparse reset

sparse edit opens the sparse-pattern configuration editor; it does not accept a fileset argument in 0.44. Use sparse set for command-line patterns and sparse reset for the full default state. A successful sparse command should be verified by checking both sparse list and actual filesystem presence. The validator's fixture deliberately includes a selected file and an excluded file so a no-op or inverted pattern cannot pass on exit status alone.

Fileset matching and sparse materialisation interact in a predictable but easy-to-miss way. jj file list -r @ -- root-file:docs/api.md can report a path that is not materialised. A subsequent external tool may fail because the file is absent locally, while jj can still reason about it in the stored tree. Use a full workspace for a tool that needs the entire tree or materialise the required sparse paths first.

Fixers as controlled rewrites

A fixer is an external transformation associated with a fileset and a commit selection. The tool should be deterministic, preserve the intended file encoding, and return a meaningful exit status. The fix command can rewrite selected commits, which means an innocent-looking formatter run may create successors throughout a stack. Treat fixer configuration as executable code: review the command, arguments, environment, and selected paths.

[fix.tools.rustfmt]
command = ["rustfmt", "--edition", "2021"]
patterns = ["root-glob:**/*.rs"]

[revsets]
fix = "mutable() & ::@"

The exact schema fields and command capabilities must be taken from the target version's config schema. The example illustrates the design, not an assertion that every tool accepts every field. First run the fixer on one disposable commit and one fileset. Record the patch before and after, inspect its change ID and descendants, then expand the commit set. If the tool changes generated files, keep generated-file policy separate from source formatting policy.

Fixers and absorb solve different problems. A fixer computes a transformation from an external program; absorb assigns existing working-copy hunks to ancestor changes. Running a formatter and then absorb may produce a clean stack, but it can also distribute formatting changes across reviews in a way the reviewer did not expect. Choose one ownership policy and inspect the resulting patch boundaries.

Running commands over commits

jj run is a graph-oriented facility for invoking a command over selected commits. The crucial question is whether the command is a predicate, an inspection, or a mutation. A test command should leave no meaningful filesystem mutation; a generator or fixer can intentionally alter the commit context. The commit domain must be printed before running, and the command's exit status should be associated with the selected commit in an artefact.

domain='main@origin..@ & mutable()'
jj log -r "$domain" -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'

# The exact run options are target-version specific; inspect jj run --help.
jj run -r "$domain" -- ./ci/check-one-commit
jj op log

Do not build a shell loop around jj edit unless you explicitly need one. Such a loop changes workspace state, can snapshot files at each iteration, and is easy to interrupt in a state that the operation graph does not describe as one conceptual test. If the command is intended to run once per commit, use jj's commit-oriented interface and capture its documented environment. If it is intended to test the current stack as a whole, run it once at a deliberate workspace commit instead.

Signing lifecycle

Signing belongs to the commit metadata layer. It is not a property of the change ID, and it is not equivalent to a remote host's verified badge. A signing operation can rewrite a commit and its descendants. If a local publication bookmark points at the affected tip, the bookmark can move locally while the remote ref stays at the old object until push.

jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@' -T '
commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ " " ++
if(signature, "signed", "unsigned") ++ "\n"
'
jj sign -r 'mutable() & ::@'
jj log -r 'mutable() & ::@' -T '
commit_id.normal_hex() ++ " " ++ change_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"
'

Configure a signing backend and key only after testing verification outside jj. GPG and SSH agents, key trust, hardware tokens, and server acceptance are external dependencies. Do not include secret material in configuration snapshots or research output. When signing a published stack, record the old commit IDs and verify that the review system accepts the new exported objects before moving a release tag.

Lab: machine-readable output

Human output is a user interface. Machine output should define a schema. Use full IDs, JSON or a delimiter that cannot occur unescaped, explicit colour/pager settings, and a version field when output crosses process boundaries. A template that prints a short ID and description is suitable for a human log but not for a database key.

jj --no-pager --color never log -r 'all()' -T '
json("jj-0.44") ++ "\t" ++
json(commit_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(change_id.normal_hex()) ++ "\t" ++
json(description.first_line()) ++ "\n"
'

Descriptions may contain newlines, tabs, quotes, and Unicode. JSON encoding handles these at the record boundary; a hand-rolled tab format must escape or reject them. Parent order is meaningful for a merge, so preserve the list rather than converting it to an unordered set. Remote and bookmark names can also contain characters that make naive whitespace parsing unsafe.

For a script that performs mutation, use a two-phase protocol. Phase one renders and validates the candidate set and all preconditions. Phase two repeats the selection in the actual command and records the operation ID and resulting state. The graph can change between phases, so a long-running automation process should either hold an appropriate repository lock through the command or fail if its preconditions no longer match.

Shell completion and debugging

Generate completion from the installed binary with jj util completion and install it according to the shell's conventions. Bash, Zsh, and Fish have different completion file locations and evaluation rules; test the generated file in a clean shell. Completion describes command and alias syntax, but it is not a compatibility promise across releases. Regenerate it when upgrading 0.44 to 0.45.1.

For a bug report, include jj version, the command, the exact revset/fileset/template, whether the repository is colocated, the relevant operation ID, and a reduced graph. Use --debug when requested by the installed help or issue instructions, but do not treat debug output as a stable API. Redact remote URLs, credentials, private descriptions, and signing identifiers before sharing.

Practice curriculum: twenty graph-first labs

These labs are designed for an experienced Git user who wants to internalise jj's semantics. Each lab starts with a disposable repository and ends with an assertion. Run them with the target binary and a clean environment; use the symbolic descriptions in the expected state rather than hard-coded generated IDs. The point is to observe the graph and operation transitions, not to memorise a transcript whose IDs will differ on every run.

Lab 1: snapshot boundary

Initialise a repository, create a file, and inspect jj status. Then edit the file without running jj and execute jj log -r @. The ordinary command snapshots the edit before rendering the log. Repeat with jj --ignore-working-copy log -r @ before and after a deliberate snapshot. The recorded tree differs from the filesystem in the first observation and agrees after the snapshot. The experiment separates a filesystem edit from a commit-tree edit and proves why a read command can create an operation.

printf 'one\n' > value.txt
jj status
printf 'two\n' > value.txt
jj --ignore-working-copy diff
jj diff
jj --ignore-working-copy log -r @ -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'

Do not use git status as the only comparison in a colocated repository: Git's index/worktree view and jj's working-copy commit are different observations. Record the operation log before and after each command. A snapshot-only operation is still relevant when diagnosing a race.

Lab 2: identity through description changes

Create and describe a change, record its commit and change IDs, then run jj describe again. The commit ID changes because metadata is part of the commit identity. The change ID normally remains the same because the operation is an evolution of one logical change. A child of the commit receives a successor because its parent object changed. Render all three values before and after, and use evolog to connect them.

Repeat with a metadata change on an ancestor that has a local publication bookmark. Verify that the local bookmark follows the successor while the remote bookmark remains unchanged. This is the minimal experiment that prevents a Git expert from treating commit IDs as long-lived patch identifiers in a mutable repository.

Lab 3: edit versus a bookmark

Create a bookmark at a child and then run jj edit on its parent. The bookmark remains at the child; only the current workspace's @ moves. Edit the parent, snapshot, and inspect the child successor. The bookmark's target may follow the rewritten local evolution, but its name and the workspace pointer remain separate state. Add a second workspace and repeat the edit there to see that each workspace has a local @.

The assertion is not “the prompt changed”. It is: the workspace symbol resolves to the expected commit, the bookmark resolves to its expected target, and the graph has the expected parent edge. Use a template rather than relying on the current default display.

Lab 4: rebase source shapes

Build a forked graph and apply the same destination with -r, -s, and -b in separate disposable copies. With -r, only the selected commits are explicitly sourced; with -s, the source's descendants are included; with -b, the branch-shaped selection follows the command's topology definition. Compare the parent arrays after each operation.

Then introduce a merge and repeat. The visual graph can make the three results look similar while their selected sets differ. A proper lab report contains the source expression, the expanded selected IDs, destination IDs, and resulting parent edges. This is the evidence needed to choose a rebase mode for a real stack.

Lab 5: insertion after and before

Start with A-B-C and a separate D. Use the insert-after mode to place D after B and the insert-before mode to place D before C. The final graph can be linear in both cases, but the operation has different destination semantics when the named commit has multiple children or when the destination is a merge. Add a second child to B and verify whether the command rewrites one path or creates a graph with multiple parents/children according to the documented selection.

Use a parent template in the assertion. “It appears between B and C” is not enough if D gained a second parent or if C was rebased to a new successor.

Lab 6: partial squash

Create one change touching src/api.rs, tests/api.rs, and docs/api.md. Squash only the tests into the parent. Use a path listing before and after, then compare the patch of the source and destination. The source tree may still include unchanged paths; what matters is the patch relative to its parent. Repeat using an unqualified src path from a subdirectory and then using root:src. The differing result demonstrates why portable scripts should use root-qualified paths.

Lab 7: split with a descendant

Split a mixed change that has a child. Record the original child commit ID and parent. After the split, locate the child's successor through its change ID and inspect the new two-edge path. Confirm that the first and second split changes contain disjoint patches whose combination equals the original patch. Then modify the description of the first part and show that the second part's commit ID can change again because its parent changed.

This lab makes two consequences visible: a split is not only a file-selection operation, and descendant rebasing is not an optional follow-up step. The child is maintained as a graph successor even when the user never selected it directly.

Lab 8: absorb ownership

Make an API change and a later test change, then edit one line in the API and another line in the test while at the tip. Run absorb and inspect which hunks moved into which ancestors. Use a hunk that is deliberately ambiguous to see how the tool handles uncertainty under the target configuration. The expected assertion is about patch ownership, not merely that the working-copy diff became empty.

Keep the original diff in a file before absorb and compare the union of resulting diffs with it. If the union is not equivalent, investigate line-range handling, ignored files, or a tool/configuration problem before accepting the operation.

Lab 9: duplicate versus revert

Create a feature change and a later change that modifies the same area. Duplicate the feature onto a separate base, then in another copy revert it at the later base. The duplicate carries the positive patch and gets a new change identity. The revert carries the inverse and gets a new change identity. If later context conflicts, the revert can contain a stored conflict; resolve it without deleting the fact that the original feature occurred.

Use this lab when a reviewer says “bring that patch over” or “undo that patch”. Those sentences do not specify whether the desired operation is copy or inverse.

Lab 10: abandon and recovery

Make a stack with a bookmark at its tip and abandon its middle change. Inspect the resulting descendants, bookmark state, evolution log, and operation log. Do not run storage GC. Recover the old change by its explicit identity, then either duplicate it onto a new base or restore the prior repository view in a copy of the fixture.

Repeat after making an unrelated description edit. Compare immediate undo with historical operation inspection. The lab's conclusion is that recovery is easier when the operator records operation IDs and uses an explicit recovery policy before performing further work.

Lab 11: non-distributive ranges

Use a merge graph and compare (C|D).. with C.. | D... Render both sets as symbolic descriptions. Then compute C.. & D.. and demonstrate the equality with (C|D)... Add a hidden predecessor explicitly and repeat; observe how the search domain changes. This lab should be a permanent regression test because range intuition is one of the most persistent sources of Git-to-jj mistakes.

Lab 12: symbol collision

Create a bookmark whose name is also a unique prefix of a commit ID, then resolve the bare symbol, commit_id(), and bookmark() forms. Add a tag with the same name and observe the documented priority. Repeat with a non-unique prefix and with a divergent change ID. The expected results belong in the query-lab documentation so later aliases do not accidentally depend on resolution priority.

Lab 13: fileset and shell parsing

Create files whose names contain spaces, parentheses, wildcard characters, and a colon. Run a simple path without inner syntax, then a complement and a function expression. Capture the shell's arguments with a harmless wrapper if necessary. The wrapper shows whether the shell expanded a glob before jj received it. Quote the complete expression and use explicit root-file: or root-glob: forms in the corrected command.

Repeat from a nested directory. A fileset can be syntactically correct yet select the wrong directory because its base coordinate changed. Root-qualified expressions are the portable answer.

Lab 14: one fileset, four consumers

Define source_paths = root:src ~ root-prefix-glob:src/**/generated/**. Use it with file list, diff, restore, and squash in copies of the same fixture. The set of matched paths should be stable, but the command effect differs: list displays, diff filters output, restore copies tree content, and squash changes patch ownership. Record the source/destination commits for the two mutating commands.

Lab 15: template optional values

Render author and committer signatures for a commit with distinct identities. Render a root with no parents, an unsigned commit with no signature, and a commit with no bookmark. Build a template that handles all three without error using conditionals and fallbacks. Then deliberately remove the guard and keep the failing command in the lab notes as a type/error example.

The assertion is that the template has a defined output contract for absence. A blank field is not the same as the literal string (none); choose deliberately.

Lab 16: JSON output

Make a description containing quotes, a newline, a tab, and Unicode. Render a JSON object with full commit/change IDs and parent arrays, then parse it with a JSON parser. Compare with a hand-written tab-separated template. The JSON record survives the description content; the hand-written format needs escaping rules. This is the minimum experiment before using jj output as CI input.

Lab 17: conflict propagation

Create two branches that edit the same line, merge or rebase them, and inspect the resulting conflict value. Add a descendant without resolving the parent. Query conflicts() over the stack and materialise the conflict in the workspace. Resolve only the intended file, snapshot, and query again. Inspect the descendant successor and run the test at the tip.

Repeat with a multi-sided conflict and write down each parent. The purpose is to prevent “ours/theirs” from becoming an unexplained policy when the graph has more than two meaningful sides.

Lab 18: operation forensics

Perform a description edit, a bookmark move, an ancestor rewrite, and a snapshot. Use op log to identify them and op diff to distinguish their repository-view effects. Read the repository at an earlier operation with --at-operation. Verify that this historical read does not import current filesystem edits. Then use op restore only in a disposable copy and compare the current operation graph before and after.

Lab 19: workspace staleness

Add a build workspace at a stack tip. Rewrite the ancestor from the development workspace and list workspaces. Inspect the build workspace's filesystem before updating it. If it contains no valuable edits, update-stale and assert that its @ is current. If it contains an unrecorded build configuration, preserve that file first and repeat. This demonstrates why stale repair is a stateful operation rather than a screen refresh.

Lab 20: cross-clone handoff

Create a bare remote and two clones. Make an unfinished change in clone A, publish a temporary bookmark, fetch in clone B, and create a new working-copy child there. Check that the commit is available in B but the operation IDs from A are not. Rewrite in B and push a deliberately different bookmark. Fetch in A and inspect both remote observations. Clean the temporary name only after confirming which machine owns the work.

This is the final lab because it combines the book's layers: a bookmark is the transport interface, the commit graph is portable through Git, the working-copy symbol is local, and the operation graph is clone-local.

Hosting protocol fieldbook

Jj does not know whether a Git ref will be shown as a GitHub branch, a pull request head, a Gerrit change, or an internal deployment ref. It exports Git-compatible objects and names; the host interprets the ref and commit message according to its own protocol. A disciplined workflow therefore has a local half and a server half. The local half can be validated with a bare Git remote. The server half must be labelled as documented or server-dependent.

GitHub pull requests as a ref state machine

PhaseLocal jj stateGit ref stateGitHub state
UnpublishedMutable unnamed stack below fetched trunkNo feature refNo PR or an old PR unrelated to current stack
Boundary chosenBookmark at intended review tipLocal branch/ref candidateNot yet changed
First pushBookmark tracked or explicitly related to remoteRemote feature ref createdPR can be opened against selected base
Review iterationOne or more changes rewritten; successors and IDs changeSame ref updated to a new tipNew commit set/iteration displayed by the host
Stack updateEarly change rewritten; descendants automatically rebasedSeveral publication refs may moveDependent PR bases and diffs may need UI updates
MergeFetch host result; rebase/clean remaining local workTrunk ref advances; feature ref may remainHost applies chosen merge strategy
CleanupForget/delete local publication names intentionallyDelete feature ref only when intendedPR remains an audit/UI object even if its head ref is deleted

GitHub's pull request is not a jj object. The local bookmark is the bridge because the host requires a Git ref. A PR can remain open while the local bookmark is deleted if the remote ref remains; conversely, deleting a local bookmark cannot close a PR. Decide explicitly whether cleanup is local, remote, or UI-level.

For a single-change PR, the local stack may be main@origin-A-B-@ with a bookmark at @. For a stack, create a bookmark at each review boundary and ensure each dependent ref has the intended base. When an early change is amended, the descendant ref must move as well. Push order matters for a human review experience, even if the Git transport can send all reachable objects in one negotiation.

Rejected updates and remote movement

A rejected push is an information event. Fetch the remote and render the local and remote targets. The remote may have moved because another machine pushed, because a reviewer or automation updated the branch, because the bookmark was renamed, or because the server refuses the selected update. Do not immediately rebase onto whatever appears first in the default log.

jj git fetch --remote origin
jj bookmark list --remote origin --tracked
jj log -r 'review | review@origin' -T '
commit_id.normal_hex() ++ " " ++ change_id.normal_hex() ++ " " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"

If the remote target contains work that must be preserved, rebase or merge onto it according to the review policy. If the remote target is an obsolete version of the same review and the server permits an intentional force update, document the old target and use the command's explicit backwards-update option. Tracking helps compare the states; it does not make the decision for you.

Fieldbook: fork/upstream topology

Keep the fork remote and upstream remote distinct. Fetch upstream trunk under its own remote name, publish PR refs to the fork, and avoid aliases that treat origin/main as universally authoritative. The graph can contain both:

main@upstream -- A -- B
                  \
                   C -- D
                        ↑
                 pr/42@fork

For a contributor, main@upstream is the update base while pr/42@fork is the publication observation. A local bookmark named pr/42 can track the fork ref; it should not be confused with a local main that tracks upstream. If the fork ref is deleted after merge, forget or delete only the local state whose removal is intended.

Gerrit changes as commit-message identity

Gerrit commonly associates patch sets by a Change-Id: I... footer in the commit message. That footer is not jj's change ID. The local jj change ID may stay constant across a rewrite; the Gerrit footer may stay constant so the server associates a new commit with the same review; and the commit ID changes every time the relevant object content, metadata, parent, or footer changes.

Local eventjj change IDCommit IDGerrit interpretation
Initial description and uploadQq_0Change I123, patch set 1
