Edit subject/body but preserve footerQq_1Usually another patch set of I123, subject changed
Rewrite parent through descendant rebaseQq_2New patch set if uploaded with same footer
Remove or replace footerQq_3May create a new change or be rejected by server policy
Duplicate the changeD, not Qd_0Footer determines whether it is associated with I123 or another review
Abandon locallyQ may remain in evolutionOld objects may remain locallyServer review is unaffected unless a protocol command changes it

Never use a jj change ID where a Gerrit footer is required. Conversely, do not replace a jj change ID with a Gerrit footer in local revsets. Keep both in the commit description/log record and explain which identity a review comment refers to.

Three-change upload sequence

main@origin -- T -- Q -- R -- S
                       Iq   Ir   Is
                       ↑         ↑
                    review Q   review S
  1. Fetch the target branch and create the stack in local jj without publication bookmarks.
  2. Verify each description has the intended Gerrit footer and that the changes are in dependency order.
  3. Render the stack with full commit IDs and jj change IDs; record this as upload iteration zero.
  4. Run jj gerrit upload --dry-run with explicit remote, branch, and commit selection where supported.
  5. Upload the stack and record the server URLs/identities returned by the Gerrit instance.

The upload command's review options can set reviewers, CCs, labels, topics, hashtags, WIP/ready state, privacy, notifications, and other server push options. Their spelling and server acceptance are version/instance-sensitive. In a shared script, pass the remote and branch explicitly and keep server options in a reviewed configuration file. Test the local selection and footer before relying on the server response.

Early review feedback

Suppose the reviewer comments on Q. Run jj edit Q, make the smallest change that addresses the comment, and snapshot. The local graph becomes Q′, R′, S′. The Q change ID normally remains Q; all three commit IDs can change because R and S inherit a new parent. The Gerrit footer should remain Iq. Before upload, compare the interdiff of Q and Q′, and separately compare R/R′ and S/S′ to identify changes caused only by rebasing.

jj evolog -r 'Q'
jj log -r 'Q::S' -T '
change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id.short() ++ " " ++
parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(",") ++ " " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"
'
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'Q::S'

Upload the intended commits with the same footer. The server-side patch-set association and displayed diff are documented by Gerrit and can vary with submit rules. If a footer was accidentally changed, stop and decide whether to restore it or intentionally create a new review; do not guess from a short commit ID.

Partial landing and trunk movement

If Q lands but R and S remain under review, the remote main ref may point to a server-created commit rather than q′. Fetch it. The remaining local stack is no longer necessarily based on the object that was uploaded for Q. Rebase R/S onto the fetched trunk and inspect their patch ranges against the new base. Preserve their Gerrit footers and upload new patch sets.

jj git fetch --remote gerrit
jj log -r 'main@gerrit'
jj rebase -s 'roots(main@gerrit..S)::' -d main@gerrit
jj log -r 'main@gerrit..@' -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj gerrit upload --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'main@gerrit..@'

The exact source expression must be adjusted for the actual graph. The point is to avoid keeping an obsolete local Q as the base merely because it shares a description with the landed review. If a server performs a squash or merge, the fetched ref is the authority for the new base.

Topics, reviewers, and push options

Topics and reviewers are Gerrit metadata, not jj bookmarks. A topic can group several Gerrit changes while the local graph supplies their dependency edges. Reviewers and labels can be supplied at upload time or through server UI/API. Push options can request draft/WIP, ready, private, notifications, validation, attention-set changes, or topic updates where the server allows them. Treat these options as side effects and record them in the upload command log.

Do not make a local bookmark called topic carry the meaning of a Gerrit topic. A bookmark names a commit; a Gerrit topic groups server-side changes. They can share a string for human convenience, but they are different namespaces and can drift.

Conflict, abandonment, and replacement

If the stack conflicts while rebasing onto new Gerrit trunk, resolve the stored conflict locally, test, and upload. If a review is abandoned, decide whether the local change is also abandoned, duplicated for another target, or retained as a historical patch. If a replacement review is created, use a new Gerrit footer when the server expects a distinct change and a new publication boundary. A jj operation restore can recover local graph state; it cannot resurrect a review the server has permanently deleted.

One repository, two publication protocols

A single local DAG can feed GitHub and Gerrit independently:

                 +- github/pr-42 --→ GitHub PR
main@upstream ---+
                 \- gerrit stack ---→ refs/for/main

The graph is shared; the names, remotes, review identities, and server policies are not. Put host-specific publication names in separate namespaces, such as github/pr-42 and gerrit/topic-42. Fetch both remotes but qualify their trunk symbols. After rewriting a shared ancestor, inspect both publication boundaries before pushing either. It may be correct to update one review and leave the other at the previous object.

Keep a local publication ledger with columns for local bookmark, remote, remote bookmark, host review ID, jj change IDs, commit IDs, base ref, and last fetch. This is especially valuable when the same code is under review on two hosts with different policies. The ledger is not jj state; it is a human review aid that explains why a local graph has multiple outward interfaces.

Standalone references: keys and command paths

These tables are intended to be used without opening a raw help capture. They describe the significant target-version keys and the canonical command paths that an engineer is likely to use. The machine-generated schema and inventory remain the release audit trail; this section supplies the human interpretation that a schema cannot provide.

snapshot.max-new-file-sizeSize/integerLarge-file snapshot policyCan make a file remain untracked despite appearing in the filesystem.
experimental-advance-branches.enabled-branchesList/patternsExperimental automatic branch advancementDo not confuse with tracking or working-copy movement.
signing.backendEnumCryptographic signing backendRequires external key infrastructure and verification policy.
signing.keyString/key identifierSigning key selectionKeep identifiers/private paths out of shared reports.
signing.behaviorEnum/policyWhen signing is required/attemptedCan affect push and rewrite workflows.
signing.backends.gpg.programPathGPG executableValidate with a test key; never assume an agent is available.
signing.backends.ssh.programPathSSH signing executableAllowed-signers/revocation configuration controls verification.
fix.toolsTableFixer tool definitionsExternal commands can rewrite a broad commit/fileset domain.
split.legacy-bookmark-behaviorBoolean/policyCompatibility behaviour for split/bookmarksVersion-specific migration setting; test before enabling.
hintsBoolean/tableHelpful command hintsPresentation, not a semantic assertion.
templates.*Template stringsDefault output templates for log/show/operation/file/ref commandsHuman defaults can change; scripts should pass -T.
working-copy.eol-conversionEnum/policyWorking-copy line-ending conversionCan change the snapshot representation and commit ID.
working-copy.exec-bit-changePolicyExecutable-bit handlingFilesystem support and Git backend semantics both matter.
--when.*Conditional tablesRepository/host/workspace/command/platform/environment conditionsTest effective config in every intended context.

The reference intentionally groups some table-valued keys. Their child schema is a user-defined namespace: for example, merge-tools.NAME, fix.tools.NAME, and template-aliases.NAME. The correct way to document a new child is to state the executable or expression, its inputs, its output, and whether it can rewrite state. A name alone is not a safe configuration.

This table uses canonical operation names even where daily use favours the op alias. The canonical path is what a release inventory must cover. The per-command entries earlier explain syntax and examples; this atlas supplies the cross-command effect classification for selection, graph, refs, workspace, and backend state.

Book-wide analysis: one change through its entire life

The following narrative deliberately follows one logical change rather than one command. It is a useful model for review notes, incident reports, and CI tooling because it keeps the change ID, commit IDs, graph edges, workspace, operation log, and host ref visible at each stage.

Stage 0: imported base

root -- A -- B
             ↑
         main@origin
workspace dev: @ = B + empty working-copy commit

After fetch, main@origin is an observation of a remote bookmark. The local repository may have an ordinary local main as well, but that is a separate ref. The working-copy commit is a child of the selected base even if it is empty. It has a commit ID and change ID, and its workspace association is local metadata. A Git user may describe this as detached HEAD plus an index; the jj description is more exact because the working-copy commit is part of the commit DAG and is updated through snapshot operations.

At this stage, the safe audit is: render @, @-, main@origin, full IDs, and parents; run jj status; and record jj op log. Do not create a bookmark just to make the working copy feel named. The absence of a local publication name is intentional.

Stage 1: first edit and description

Edit src/quota.rs. The filesystem changes immediately; the commit tree does not change until a snapshot boundary. Running jj status ordinarily snapshots the edit and updates the working-copy commit. The new working-copy commit has a new commit ID because its tree changed, while its change ID identifies the same evolving working-copy change. Use jj describe -m to add a subject. That metadata change can create another successor, so a script should not cache the commit ID across both operations.

jj status
jj describe -m 'add quota API'
jj log -r @ -T '
change_id.normal_hex() ++ "\t" ++ commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\t" ++
parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().normal_hex()).join(",") ++ "\n"

The working-copy commit is not a staging area. There is no need to ask which hunks are “staged” before creating a new change. If the edit belongs in a separate logical change, use jj new before editing or use split/diffedit after the fact. The choice is about graph and patch ownership, not the durability of the current files.

Stage 2: dependent change

Run jj new and add tests. The old API change remains a parent, and the new working-copy commit is an empty or partially edited child while you work. Once the tests are snapshotted, the graph is:

root -- A -- B -- Q -- R
             main@origin  API  tests
                              @ = R

Q and R are separate change identities. The fact that R is a child of Q is the dependency relationship. If a test change also needs a documentation file, that is patch ownership within R; it does not require a second bookmark. The query Q::R returns both commits, while Q..R excludes Q and expresses the patch range above Q. Using the wrong one in a review tool can change the displayed diff even though both look like “the changes between Q and R”.

Stage 3: publication

For GitHub, create github/quota at R. For Gerrit, the upload command can select the range without a local bookmark, although a local name can help human review. The publication choice is not a graph operation: Q and R keep their IDs. The push may export Git objects and create a remote bookmark, after which the local tracking relationship records the remote target.

jj bookmark create github/quota -r @
jj git push --remote github --bookmark github/quota
jj bookmark list --remote github --tracked
jj gerrit upload --dry-run --remote gerrit --remote-branch main -r 'Q::R'

The GitHub ref and the Gerrit upload are different interfaces to the same local graph. A GitHub PR normally names one head ref and one base ref. Gerrit commonly receives each commit in a selected range through a review ref and associates it using message metadata. The local fact that Q is the parent of R is shared; the host-side display and review identities are not.

Stage 4: review feedback on the ancestor

The reviewer finds an API problem in Q. Use jj edit Q, modify the file, and snapshot. Jj rewrites Q to Q′ and automatically rebases R to R′. The graph now has successors:

old: root -- A -- B -- Q -- R
new: root -- A -- B -- Q′ -- R′
                         ↑       ↑
                   same change  same change
                   IDs normally IDs normally

Q′ and R′ have new commit IDs. Q and R retain their logical change IDs in the ordinary evolution. The local GitHub bookmark follows the local successor of R. The remote GitHub bookmark remains at R until pushed. The Gerrit footer in Q′ should remain the same if the intended result is a new patch set of the same server review. This is the exact point at which a Git user who thinks only in branch tips may miss the fact that the dependent review also changed.

Run an interdiff or a pair of diffs for Q/Q′ and R/R′. The first shows the review response; the second mostly shows the parent-context update but can contain conflict-resolution or test changes. Record both so reviewers can distinguish intended feedback from automatic descendant consequences.

Stage 5: trunk movement

While the review is open, upstream adds C and the remote main moves. Fetch it. Do not move the local publication bookmark to C; it is a review boundary, not trunk. Rebase the local stack onto the fetched trunk and inspect the new parent edges. If the server has merged Q in the meantime, fetch its actual landed result instead of assuming C is the new parent.

jj git fetch --remote github
jj log -r 'main@github'
jj rebase -s 'roots(main@github..@)::' -d main@github
jj log -r 'main@github..@' -T '
commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ " " ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"

A merge from upstream can cause conflicts in Q′ or R′. The conflict is part of the new commit value. Continue to inspect, materialise, resolve, snapshot, and test; there is no separate rebase continuation token to preserve. If a stack contains multiple publication boundaries, move and push them in a way that matches the host review dependency order.

Stage 6: closure and cleanup

When GitHub merges the PR, fetch main and compare the fetched commit with the reviewed tip. The server may have used squash, merge, or rebase; the new remote main is the authority for the next local base. The feature bookmark can be forgotten locally after its remote ref is no longer needed. If deleting the remote ref is desired, express and push that deletion separately. Closing a GitHub PR, deleting its ref, forgetting a local bookmark, and abandoning a jj change are four different actions.

When Gerrit submits Q but R remains, fetch Gerrit main, rebase R onto the landed server result, preserve R's Change-Id footer, and upload a new patch set. The local jj evolution of R is still useful, but it cannot predict the exact commit that Gerrit created when Q landed. This is why host integration always begins with fetch.

What to record in a lifecycle review note

FieldExampleReason
Target versionsjj 0.44.0, Git 2.53.0Syntax and default policies are versioned.
Base refmain@github at full IDExplains the patch range and host comparison.
jj change IDsQ and R full IDsTracks logical evolution through rewrites.
Commit IDsq_0/r_0 then q_1/r_1Identifies concrete uploaded objects.
Publication refsgithub/quota, remote targetExplains what a push changed.
Server identityGerrit Change-Id: I... or PR numberMaps local change to host review.
Conflict stateCommit, path, resolution operationDistinguishes a stored conflict from a clean rewrite.
Operation IDsrewrite, resolve, push-related operationsProvides local recovery and evidence.

A review note like “rebased branch and force-pushed” is too lossy for mutable history. The fuller record states which logical changes survived, which concrete objects were replaced, which server ref moved, and which host identity remained stable.

Edge-case catalogue

Empty commits

An empty commit has no tree delta relative to its parents, but it can carry a description, a bookmark, a signature, a workspace target, and descendants. It may be an intentional review boundary or an accidental result of a squash. Query empty() within the domain you care about. Do not remove every empty node from a stack without checking whether its message records a meaningful change or whether a publication workflow uses it as a boundary.

Merge commits

A merge has multiple parents, and many operations treat its parent list as a set rather than a single base. parents(G) returns both sides. roots, heads, ranges, first-parent traversal, and descendant selection all produce different results around a merge. Before a linear-only command, query merges(domain). If it is non-empty, state whether the operation should preserve, flatten, or deliberately introduce the merge.

Hidden commits

Hidden does not mean unreachable from every mechanism. An explicit full commit ID can identify a hidden predecessor; an ordinary visible query may omit it. If a hidden ID is introduced into a traversal, its ancestors can enter the search space. This is useful for recovery and surprising in a broad query. When comparing two results, state whether the search was current-visible or explicitly seeded with a hidden commit.

Divergent changes

A divergent change has multiple visible successors for one logical change ID. A command requiring one commit may reject the bare change ID. Use offsets or an explicit query that chooses by topology, bookmark, description, or operation provenance. Do not pick the first displayed successor solely because it appears first; the display order is not an authorship or recency guarantee unless the template says so.

Bookmark conflicts

A bookmark conflict is ref state with competing target information, usually after incompatible local/remote updates. It is not a file conflict and is not resolved by editing a conflict marker. Fetch authoritative remote state, inspect target sets, decide whether to retain, move, or delete the name, and publish only after the result is explicit. Use bookmark list with remote and tracking filters rather than a commit log alone.

Private commits

Private is a publication policy defined by a revset, not a filesystem attribute or a second commit type. A private commit can be mutable, conflicted, bookmarked, or visible locally. Ordinary push excludes it according to configuration; an explicit allow option changes that safety policy for one invocation. Always render the private set and the candidate push domain before overriding it.

Working-copy edge cases

The working-copy commit can be empty, conflicted, bookmarked, immutable after a push, stale in another workspace, or based on multiple parents. A command that creates a new child can leave the old working copy as a useful change. A command that edits an ancestor can cause the current working copy to become a successor or a conflict. Query @, @-, working_copies(), and conflicts() when the prompt alone is ambiguous.

Shell edge cases

There are at least three parsers in a complicated invocation: the shell, jj's revset/fileset/template parser, and any external editor/tool. Quote the whole expression, use -- when the command's help documents it, and avoid command substitutions that turn an empty result into a missing argument. A path containing whitespace is not evidence that a fileset is wrong; an unquoted glob being expanded before jj sees it is.

Automation edge cases

Automation must decide whether it owns snapshotting, whether it can tolerate multiple commits, whether external tools may write files, and whether network publication is allowed. Use explicit binaries, config, commit sets, filesets, templates, remotes, and colour/pager modes. Capture operation IDs and exit statuses. Fail closed on unexpected cardinality, a conflicted publication target, an untracked remote, or a changed remote target.

Mechanics textbook: why the graph changes

The commands in jj are easier to predict if the underlying transformations are understood. This chapter describes the conceptual algorithms without pretending to expose an implementation API. The commit store, working-copy state, reference state, and operation store are separate pieces of repository state. A command can update more than one, but the updates have different invariants.

Rewrite as a successor function

Let a commit be a tuple containing parent IDs, tree ID, author/committer metadata, description, and conflict values. Its commit ID is a function of that tuple. A rewrite changes at least one component. The new tuple receives a new commit ID. Jj also records that the new tuple is a successor of the old logical change. Descendants whose parent tuple contains the old commit ID cannot retain their old commit object unchanged, so jj derives successors for them too.

old B = (parent A, tree tb, metadata mb) → cid(B)
new B = (parent A, tree tb′, metadata mb) → cid(B′)
old C = (parent B, tree tc, metadata mc) → cid(C)
new C = (parent B′, rebased tree, metadata mc) → cid(C′)

The logical change ID is attached to the evolution rather than recomputed as the commit ID. This is why a commit message can refer to a change after several rewrites, while a CI system that stores only the commit ID must update its record. If two processes derive B′ and B″ independently from B, both successors can remain visible and the change becomes divergent.

The successor relationship is not a promise that the patch is textually identical. A descendant rebase may resolve cleanly, may alter context, may introduce a conflict value, or may require a new merge shape. The invariant is graph evolution: the descendant is the successor of the old descendant under the changed parent context. Review the diff to determine whether the logical patch survived as intended.

Automatic descendant rebasing

Automatic descendant rebasing is the consequence of maintaining parent identity, not an optional convenience. If an ancestor changes, leaving descendants pointing at the old object would produce a stale branch of the graph. Jj instead rewrites affected descendants so their parent edges point at the new successors. This can be surprising when editing a small early patch changes many commit IDs, but it is what keeps a stack coherent.

Ancestor operationDescendant conditionTypical result
Description/meta editDescendant directly or transitively depends on changed commitNew descendant commit IDs with equivalent or rebased trees.
Patch editDescendant's patch applies to new ancestorClean successor with changed parent and often changed tree ID.
Patch editDescendant's patch cannot applySuccessor contains a stored conflict.
Ancestor abandonDescendant depends on abandoned nodeReparenting/rebase according to topology; possible conflict.
Ancestor splitDescendant points at old changeSuccessor points at the new split tip.
Ancestor duplicated elsewhereOriginal descendant does not depend on duplicateOriginal stack unchanged; duplicate has independent identity.

Bookmarks are updated as references to local successors where the operation can determine the corresponding evolution. A remote bookmark is not silently changed because it represents a fetched external state. A workspace working-copy pointer can also be updated or become stale depending on whether its commit was rewritten and whether its filesystem has unrecorded content.

To reason about a large rewrite, compute the descendant closure before the command:

jj log -r 'descendants(TARGET)' -T '
commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\t" ++ change_id.normal_hex() ++ "\t" ++
description.first_line() ++ "\n"

Then run the operation and compute it again. The set of exact commit IDs will differ; the set of intended logical changes and their parent relationships is the useful comparison. If the new closure includes unrelated work, the source set or destination was too broad.

Trees, patches, and context

A commit stores a tree, while a review usually discusses its patch relative to a parent. The same tree can represent different patches under different parents. Rebase preserves a change's intended tree transformation as far as possible, but its patch can change when context changes. Restore copies tree content; squash changes which commit's parent-to-tree difference owns content; diffedit edits a patch; and split creates new boundaries through one tree delta.

parent P:  config = old, code = old
change Q:  config = new, code = new

restore Q → R:
  R receives selected tree values
  Q remains a separate change

squash Q into P:
  P′ owns the patch
  Q may become empty

split Q:
  P -- Q_1 -- Q_2
  Q_1 and Q_2 compose to Q's original tree delta

When a path is selected, ask whether its value is present in the source tree, whether it differs from the source parent, and whether the destination has a conflicting value. A fileset that selects an unchanged path can be a valid empty patch selection. A path missing from a sparse workspace can still be present in the stored tree and must be reasoned about with a commit/fileset command rather than a filesystem listing.

Patch context is also why automatic descendant rebase can produce a conflict without losing the descendant's logical change. The source side, destination side, and common ancestor are retained in the conflict value. The resolution must choose a tree that satisfies the domain invariant; “take ours” is only correct when the parent orientation and desired semantics make it correct.

Conflict values as algebra

A conflicted tree entry records sides and a base relationship. Materialising the working copy displays a representation of that value. Resolving it replaces the conflict value with a normal tree value. The commit can therefore move through these states without an interrupted transaction:

clean Q
  | rebase/merge
  ▼
conflicted Q′ -- edit/resolve/snapshot -->  clean Q″
  |                                      |
  \-- descendant R′ may also be conflicted +

Conflict propagation follows graph operations. A descendant created on top of a conflicted parent can remain meaningful, but a subsequent rewrite may materialise or propagate the conflict. Query conflicts() over the full review stack, not only the current workspace. If a merge editor resolves a path in one workspace, other workspaces may need stale update/materialisation before they see the resolved tree.

Conflict marker style controls presentation. The “diff” style can show base and sides differently from “git” style, and experimental styles can change between releases. Do not store marker text as the conflict identity. The stable evidence is the conflicted predicate and the resulting tree after resolution.

The operation DAG model

The commit DAG answers “what commits exist in this repository view?” The operation DAG answers “how did this repository view change?” One operation can update refs without changing commits. Another can rewrite commits and update workspace state. A third can recover a historical view. The commit graph and operation graph therefore have different nodes, edges, and recovery semantics.

commit DAG:       A -- B -- C′
                         \
                          D

operation DAG:       op_0 -- op_1(rewrite B)
                              |- op_2(snapshot)
                              \- op_3(move bookmark)

Operation descriptions are useful but should not be treated as a complete semantic diff. Use op show and op diff to inspect the repository-view changes. A snapshot operation may rewrite the working-copy commit; a bookmark operation may change only a ref; a push has external side effects that the local operation record cannot roll back at the server.

An operation created without integration can be inspected at its operation ID. Integrating it is a deliberate change to the current operation head. Concurrent operation nodes can be compared and possibly integrated. If two operations rewrite the same logical change differently, the operation graph may be easy to merge while the commit evolution becomes divergent. These are separate conflicts.

Recovery granularity

Recovery toolGranularityUse whenLimit
undoLatest integrated operationThe last operation was clearly wrong.Later operations change what “latest” means.
redoPreviously undone operationThe undo itself was a mistake and the operation remains redoable.Not a general commit reapplication mechanism.
op restoreRepository viewYou want the whole repository state at a known operation.Can remove later integrated state from the current view.
op revertRepository-view inverseYou want a new operation that reverses a selected operation.Not every external side effect is reversible.
evologLogical change evolutionYou need an old or divergent form of a change.Does not restore unrecorded files or remote server state.
Git remoteExported commits/refsYou need to move work to another clone.Does not transfer operation history or filesystem buffers.

Recovery should preserve evidence. Before a destructive-looking command, create a full-ID record and note the current operation. After a mistake, stop making changes until the wrong operation is identified. If a server push already happened, treat the server state as a separate incident; local undo cannot erase a remote review or ref update.

Git backend object boundary

The Git backend gives jj a way to exchange commit trees and refs with Git. It does not turn all jj metadata into Git metadata. Commit IDs can be represented as Git objects; bookmarks can be exported as refs; operation IDs, workspace symbols, change evolution, and conflict representation are jj concerns. A Git-native tool sees the exported object graph and refs, not the complete local jj state.

Colocation is therefore a coordination contract. After git update-ref, import. After jj changes a bookmark, export before a Git-native inspection. After a Git commit, import through supported mechanisms and inspect how it entered the jj graph. Do not edit .jj internal files or assume the Git index is a jj staging area.

Object reachability also affects recovery. An unexported jj commit may not be available in another clone. An abandoned local commit may be retained by jj operation/evolution storage but not by a Git remote. Git GC and jj GC have distinct roots. Preserve a temporary bookmark or an exported ref before maintenance if the object matters.

Synthesis: proof-driven jj practice

The most useful way to close the gap between knowing individual commands and operating a difficult repository is to treat every non-trivial jj action as a small proof. The proof is not formal verification in the theorem-prover sense; it is a disciplined record of the state that makes the intended transformation unambiguous. This matters more in jj than in a branch-centred workflow because a single command can rewrite an ancestor, rebase several descendants, move local bookmarks, update a workspace, and create a new operation while leaving remote observations untouched.

Begin by naming the question in graph terms. “Put the tests with the API change” is underspecified. It might mean move the test patch into the API change, copy the test tree into the API change, split one mixed change, or create a new dependent change. Those are different tree and identity transformations. A useful precondition record therefore contains the selected commit set, the destination commit set, the selected fileset, and the intended patch owner. It also records whether the operation is allowed to cross an immutable head and whether a publication bookmark currently names any affected commit.

Proof itemQuestionTypical observation
DomainWhich commits can the expression see?jj log -r 'visible() & ::@'
SelectionWhich exact commits will the command consume?jj log --no-graph -r 'roots(stack(@))'
Tree deltaWhich paths and hunks are actually changing?jj diff -r R -- fileset
DestinationWhich parent edge or tree endpoint will change?jj log -r 'parents(R) | destination'
IdentityWhich commit IDs and change IDs may evolve?jj log -T 'commit_id ++ " " ++ change_id'
PublicationWhich local and remote names are involved?jj bookmark list --all-remotes
RecoveryWhich operation and temporary ref preserve the before-state?jj op log plus a temporary bookmark

After the command, repeat the observations rather than trusting the success message. For a rewrite, compare parent edges and the patch at every affected change. For a bookmark operation, compare the local target with the imported remote target. For a workspace operation, inspect both the workspace symbol and the filesystem. For a push, record the remote response separately: the local operation log can show what jj requested, but it cannot undo a server-side ref update or remove a review patch set.

proof loop:
  state description
       |
       v
  source/destination/fileset query
       |
       v
  operation O_n
       |
       +-- commit DAG and evolution
       +-- workspace materialisation
       +-- bookmarks and remote observations
       +-- operation DAG
       |
       v
  postcondition checks

This loop also explains why a command that merely “works” can still be wrong. A broad -r expression may be accepted and produce a coherent graph, but if it includes a sibling stack the operation has violated the intended precondition. A fileset may parse and select existing paths while selecting no changed hunks. A bookmark may be moved locally while the user believes a server ref has moved. A template may render a plausible empty field for an absent optional value. Proof-driven practice makes these errors visible at the boundary where they are cheapest to correct.

Rewrite proof: patch ownership, topology, and identity

Consider a stack with a public base and two private changes:

main@origin
    |
    A  public API change
    |
    B  tests and compatibility
    |
    C(@) documentation and examples

Suppose the intent is to move only the compatibility tests from B into A while keeping the documentation as a separate change. The operation is not “amend A”. First determine whether the tests are complete files, hunks within a file, or a patch that is already shared with C. A path-level squash can move a complete path; a diff editor is needed for hunk-level ownership. The precondition is that A and B are mutable, that the selected paths are present in B's patch, and that C is a descendant whose patch can be reapplied over the new B.

jj log -r 'A|B|C' -T 'change_id.short() ++ " " ++ commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj diff -r B -- root-glob:tests/**
jj diff -r A -- root-glob:tests/**
jj op log --limit 3
jj squash --from B --into A -- root-glob:tests/**

The postcondition is not simply that one file appears in A. A's successor must contain the intended test patch, B's successor must retain the unselected compatibility patch, and C's successor must have the same documentation patch relative to its new parent. If the test patch touched a file that A already changed, compare the combined tree rather than comparing file presence. A path can exist in both commits while contributing no delta. If C now conflicts, the conflict is evidence that its old patch depended on context changed by the ownership transfer; it is not evidence that squash failed to perform the transfer.

Identity gives a second independent postcondition. The rewritten A and B normally retain their respective jj change IDs while receiving new commit IDs. C also receives a new commit ID because its parent identity changed, even if its logical patch is unchanged. A bookmark at C may follow the local successor, while C@origin remains the fetched remote observation. A Gerrit footer in the commit message is a third identity channel and must be inspected independently. This is why a review note should record all three values when a published stack is rewritten.

The same proof pattern applies to split and rebase. For split, prove that the two new changes partition the original patch and that the descendant is rebased onto the second result. For rebase, prove the source set before the destination: -r, -s, and -b describe different source policies. Then prove every new parent edge. A visual log is useful, but a template printing full IDs and parents is stronger evidence when a graph has several forks or merges.

Publication proof: local graph, review ref, and remote observation

Publication is best modelled as a state machine with three layers. The local graph contains changes and their current commit successors. The local bookmark is a name chosen for a publication boundary. The remote bookmark is the last imported observation of a server ref. A tracking relationship connects the local and remote names but does not cause either to follow @. A GitHub pull request adds a server-side base/head view; Gerrit adds a review identity usually carried by a commit-message footer. None of those server objects is the jj change ID.

local graph:       A -- B -- C(@)
                                   |
local ref:                         review -> C
                                   |
tracking pair:                     review <-> review@origin -> B
                                   |
server request:                    update review@origin from B to C

In this state, the local bookmark is ahead of its imported remote target. The safe publication procedure is to fetch first if the observation may be old, verify the remote name and URL, compare B with the currently fetched target, and then push the explicit bookmark. The command is a request to update a Git ref; it is not a request to publish “the current change” in the abstract. If another contributor has moved the server ref, the expected old target no longer matches and the operator must decide whether to rebase, merge, or deliberately force-update under the hosting policy.

jj git remote list
jj git fetch --remote origin
jj bookmark list review --all-remotes
jj log -r 'review | review@origin' -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ " " ++ change_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
jj diff --from review@origin --to review
jj git push --dry-run --remote origin --bookmark review
jj git push --remote origin --bookmark review

A rejected push has a small diagnostic decision tree. If the bookmark is absent remotely, determine whether creation is allowed and whether the chosen name is correct. If the remote target has advanced, fetch and inspect the new ancestor relation. If the local target is behind, a normal fast-forward push may be inappropriate; moving the local bookmark is a ref decision, not a rebase. If the server rejects the update because the branch is protected, the local graph is still valid but the publication protocol requires a pull request, a Gerrit review ref, or an authorised force update. If the commit cannot be exported, inspect private-commit policy and backend state before trying another ref.

GitHub and Gerrit can share one local graph without sharing publication semantics. A GitHub bookmark might name the head of a PR stack, while Gerrit upload selects a range and sends commits to refs/for/main. The same local commit can therefore be viewed as a GitHub branch tip and as one patch set in a Gerrit series. Keep remote names explicit, and use distinct local prefixes when that reduces operator ambiguity. Do not make a local bookmark track two server concepts merely because their visible names happen to be equal.

Cleanup is another state transition, not an aesthetic operation. After a GitHub PR merges, fetch and determine whether the merged commit is reachable from the imported base. Only then forget or delete the local publication bookmark according to whether the server ref should remain. After a Gerrit change lands, fetch the target branch and check whether the local change is now an ancestor of it; a server-side rebase or squash can mean that commit IDs differ while the patch is integrated. Preserve a temporary ref until that comparison is complete.

Conflict and recovery proof: preserve both graphs

A conflict drill should distinguish three events: conflict creation, conflict materialisation, and conflict resolution. Rebase or merge can create a conflicted successor in the commit DAG. A command can materialise that value as marker files in one workspace. Editing and snapshotting creates a successor with a resolved tree. These are not stages of an interrupted Git transaction; they are ordinary repository states connected by operations. A descendant may be created while an ancestor is conflicted, and a later rewrite may propagate or replace the conflict.

before:       A -- B -- C(@)
                       B changes shared API
rewrite B onto X:
              X -- B'! -- C'!
                   conflict value propagates
resolve B':   X -- B'' -- C''(@)
                   clean tree; new successors

When the conflict appears, record the current operation and identify the conflicted commits with a revset rather than assuming that the current workspace is the only location:

jj op log --limit 8
jj log -r 'conflicts() & ::@' -T 'change_id ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj log -r 'conflicts() & descendants(@-)' -G
jj status

Materialise only after deciding which workspace owns the resolution. If a build or review workspace is also present, record its workspace-local @ before updating it. Inspect the conflict sides and the parent list; “ours” and “theirs” are poor descriptions in a multi-parent graph. The domain invariant—such as preserving both API behaviours or retaining generated output policy—should decide the merged file. After editing, run a command that snapshots the result, then query conflicts() again and compare the tree and patch of every descendant.

Recovery has the same evidence requirement. jj undo is appropriate when the latest integrated operation is clearly the mistake. It is not a general reflog selector. If later operations occurred, inspect jj op log, use jj op show and jj op diff to identify the historical view, and decide between restoring that view, reverting its effects with a new operation, or recovering only a logical change from evolog. If work must move to another clone, create a temporary bookmark and push it; operation IDs and unrecorded filesystem contents do not travel through Git.

IncidentFirst safe actionEvidence before repair
Wrong ancestor editedStop ordinary commands; inspect the latest operation.Old/new commit IDs, change IDs, descendants, bookmark targets.
Useful change abandonedQuery evolution and operation history before creating replacements.Change ID, predecessor/successor chain, whether a remote copy exists.
Bookmark moved too farCompare local ref with fetched remote ref.Old target, new target, tracking state, server response if pushed.
Several mistakes accumulatedMake a recovery note and stop mutating the graph.Operation IDs and diffs for each candidate boundary.
Other machine needs unfinished workPublish an explicit temporary bookmark.Full commit ID, change ID, remote URL, and intended cleanup owner.

Do not use recovery to erase evidence prematurely. An operation restore changes the current repository view and can make later local work disappear from ordinary reachability. A remote push is outside the operation DAG. A workspace's unrecorded files are outside both graphs. The correct repair may therefore be a new graph operation, a filesystem backup, a server-side review action, or a combination of them.

Language and automation proof: revsets, filesets, templates

Revsets, filesets, and templates are three different languages that often appear in one command line. A revset selects commits; a fileset selects paths relative to the command's tree/filesystem context; a template renders typed values. Shell quoting happens before any of the three languages sees the expression. A reliable automation contract documents all four boundaries.

revset='heads(mutable() & ::@)'
fileset='glob:src/**/*.rs & ~glob:src/generated/**'
template='commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\t" ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'
jj --no-pager --color never log -r "$revset" -T "$template"
jj diff -r @ -- "$fileset"

The two commands above do not necessarily consume the same kind of value. log -r accepts a commit set and can print many commits. diff -r accepts a commit expression whose tree comparison has its own endpoint semantics, then consumes a fileset after the -- boundary. A command such as edit generally requires one commit; a query that returns two heads should fail rather than silently choose one. Cardinality is therefore part of the script's precondition. Use an explicit assertion or a command designed for sets when the result can branch.

For revsets, calculate closures before reductions. For example, given two independent stacks:

root -- A -- B
               \
                C(@)
root -- X -- Y
               \
                Z(@2)

heads(mutable()) may return both C and Z. heads(mutable() & ::@) restricts the domain to the current workspace's ancestry before reducing it. heads(mutable()) & ::@ reduces globally and intersects afterward; these operations can differ when a global head is an ancestor of a current-workspace head or when a filter removes the descendant that made a node non-maximal. Parenthesise the policy and inspect the result in a template before handing it to a rewrite command.

For filesets, evaluate the path set against the actual tree. A glob that matches a generated file is not automatically the same as a glob that matches a changed generated file. A relative literal is interpreted from the relevant working-directory or repository-root coordinate, depending on the command and prefix. Quote metacharacters so that Bash does not expand them first. If a fileset is intended to move hunks, prove it with a diff editor or a before/after patch comparison; file list proves names, not patch ownership.

For templates, keep typed values typed until the output boundary. A commit ID's short() method is not interchangeable with a string method; an absent signature is not an empty signature string; a list must be mapped or joined before concatenation. Machine output should disable colour and use an unambiguous record separator. JSON escaping belongs at the field boundary, and a template regression should test control characters, absent optionals, merge parents, and conflicts rather than only checking that the command exits successfully.

Versioned automation should pin the target jj binary and test the effective syntax. A 0.45.x-only function, option, or command belongs behind a version gate rather than an optimistic fallback. Keep a small fixture with a merge, a fork, a conflicted commit, a bookmark, a remote bookmark, a divergent change, and descriptions containing whitespace. Store expected symbolic results and assert full IDs only where the fixture is deterministic. This turns the book's examples into executable contracts and makes release upgrades a deliberate review rather than a silent change of query meaning.

Cross-version maintenance and release discipline

A two-release book needs two kinds of precision. Stable semantics can be explained once when both versions implement them; spelling and defaults must be tied to the release that supplied the help text. The installed 0.44.0 binary is normative for the main examples. The separately installed 0.45.1 binary is a comparison track. Current-main documentation is useful for finding direction, but it is not evidence that a 0.44 command accepts a newer option.

When an upgrade changes the command surface, update the inventory first. Then classify the change: new command, renamed option, changed default, changed template method, changed revset function, altered configuration key, or changed server integration. Add a small executable experiment for semantic changes. A help diff alone can show that a spelling changed but cannot prove whether an operation now selects descendants, changes a default remote, or treats a missing optional value differently.

Upgrade questionEvidenceBook action
Does the command exist?Versioned recursive help inventory.Add or remove a command entry and command-tree row.
Did the argument meaning change?Help plus a disposable graph fixture.Update the semantic dossier and before/after output.
Did a query function change?Revset help/source plus evaluated fixture sets.Update signatures, edge cases, and version callout.
Did a config key change?Schema, effective config query, and behaviour test.Update tutorial, reference table, and migration note.
Did rendering change?Template help and output regression.Update templates without treating formatting as identity.
Did hosting behaviour change?Authoritative GitHub/Gerrit documentation or server test.Mark local mechanics and server-dependent behaviour separately.

Keep the source ledger beside these experiments. A short citation attached to a normative claim is enough for ordinary syntax, while a version-sensitive claim should identify the local help capture, official documentation branch, or source/test that supports it. This preserves licensing and provenance discipline without turning the reader-facing chapters into raw documentation dumps. The goal is a book whose explanations remain useful when command spelling changes, because the graph and state invariants are taught independently of the release-specific interface.

Workspace and concurrency proof: local materialisations are not branches

Workspaces complicate the word “current” in a productive way. The commit DAG and operation DAG are shared by the repository, but each workspace has its own working-copy commit and filesystem. A development workspace can be editing C while a review workspace is inspecting B and a build workspace is materialising a clean candidate. These are not three branches, and moving one workspace's @ does not move the others. The correct unit of coordination is a workspace symbol plus its working-copy commit, not a branch name inferred from the directory.

shared DAG:       A -- B -- C -- D
                       |         |
development:          dev@      |
review:                review@ --+
build:                 build@ at B

each @ is workspace-local; A, B, C, D are shared commits

Before a rewrite, list workspaces and record their working-copy commits. If a workspace has unrecorded filesystem edits, updating it after an ancestor rewrite can either snapshot those edits into a successor or require explicit stale-state repair. A build workspace should normally be treated as disposable: reproduce its commit from a full commit ID or bookmark, run the build, and avoid using its @ as a publication handle. A review workspace may intentionally remain at an old commit while the development workspace evolves; that can be valuable evidence during a code-review comparison.

jj workspace list
jj log -r 'working_copies()' -T 'change_id ++ " " ++ commit_id ++ "\n"'
jj -R ../project-review log -r @
jj -R ../project-build status
jj workspace update-stale

Concurrency adds a second distinction. Two processes can begin with the same repository operation head and create operations that have the same logical parent but different effects. Operation integration combines repository views according to jj's operation model; it does not guarantee that two independent rewrites of one change become one unambiguous patch. The commit evolution may still be divergent. Inspect operation parents and change evolution separately, and do not call a clean operation integration proof that the resulting review stack is semantically correct.

When an automated job runs against a shared repository, choose one of three contracts. It can use --ignore-working-copy and inspect a stable commit without owning snapshotting. It can own the workspace and allow normal snapshot boundaries, provided no editor or other jj process writes there. Or it can use an independent clone and exchange commits through an explicit remote bookmark. The third contract is usually easiest for CI because it separates filesystem ownership, operation history, and publication. A shell loop that happens to pass in a quiet checkout is not evidence that two writers are safe together.

Reference-reading protocol: from a question to a safe command

The command reference is most useful when read as a contract rather than a catalogue of flags. Start with the object being changed: a commit tree, a patch boundary, a bookmark, a remote observation, a workspace, a configuration layer, or an operation view. Then identify the selector language and its cardinality. Finally identify what jj will snapshot or rewrite at the command boundary. This order prevents a familiar option such as -r from hiding the fact that the selected values are commits while a nearby path expression is a fileset.

QuestionReference section to consultMinimum proof
Which changes should move?Rebase/squash/split/absorb dossier and revset reference.Print source roots/heads and affected descendants.
Which content should move?Fileset reference and tree-endpoint command contract.Show the selected patch, not only matching filenames.
Which review should update?Bookmark, GitHub, or Gerrit fieldbook.Print local target, fetched remote target, and review identity.
Why did a commit disappear?Evolution and operation reference.Compare operation views and the change's predecessor/successor chain.
Why did output change?Template and configuration references.Disable colour/pager and test the typed field or effective key.
Can another machine continue?Git backend, workspaces, and recovery recipes.Export a reachable commit/ref; do not rely on an operation ID or dirty files.

This protocol also supplies a stopping rule. If the command's source set is not printed, the remote destination is not explicit, the fileset has not been evaluated, or the current operation cannot be identified, the next action should be inspection rather than mutation. That is not excessive caution: jj makes broad rewrites cheap and recoverable, but external publication and unrecorded filesystem state remain expensive to reconstruct. A clear precondition is often faster than diagnosing a valid command applied to the wrong graph.

For readers migrating a detached-HEAD Git practice, the resulting routine is intentionally light. Keep local work unnamed while it is private. Use change IDs to follow mutable evolution. Use bookmarks only at publication boundaries. Use revsets to describe the graph region and filesets to describe the tree region. Use templates to make the evidence legible. Use the operation log before recovery, and use Git remotes when work must cross a clone or machine. The point is not to reproduce Git's branch choreography with new verbs; it is to make the graph, its identities, and its interfaces explicit enough that the choreography is unnecessary.

Template debugging as typed observation

Templates deserve the same proof discipline as graph operations because a misleading display can cause a correct command to be applied to the wrong commit. Build a template in layers: first print one stable scalar, then add identity, then add topology, then add optional state, and only then add labels or formatting. Run with --no-pager --color never while debugging. This separates a template-language error from a pager, terminal-colour, or shell-quoting problem.

jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id.normal_hex() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'commit_id.short() ++ " " ++ change_id.short() ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'parents.map(|p| p.commit_id().short()).join(", ") ++ "\n"'
jj log -r @ -T 'if(conflict(), "CONFLICT ", "clean ") ++ description.first_line() ++ "\n"'

If the second command fails, the problem is identity or method syntax; if the third fails, inspect the parent value's type and the lambda's receiver; if the fourth renders an unexpected state, query conflicts() independently. A template should not be used to infer a Boolean property from a pretty symbol when the corresponding predicate can be queried directly. Conversely, a human display can deliberately combine several fields, provided its labels make absence, divergence, and remote state visible.

Templates also define an interface for scripts. Stable machine output should use full hexadecimal IDs, explicit separators, disabled colour, and a documented treatment of empty descriptions and absent signatures. Do not parse graph indentation or the default log template. If a release changes the default display, that is a presentation change; if a method changes its type or disappears, it is a compatibility change that belongs in the version matrix. Keep one regression fixture for both cases so the book's examples remain executable observations rather than attractive but unverifiable output.

One final distinction is useful when auditing a result: an observation can be locally correct and still operationally incomplete. A template can show that @ has a particular commit ID without showing that a second workspace has another working-copy commit. A revset can show all visible mutable heads without showing hidden predecessors that explain divergence. A bookmark listing can show a local target without proving that the target was exported to Git. A clean conflict query can describe the current commit while a stale workspace still contains marker files from its previous materialisation. Compose observations across state components whenever the question crosses a boundary.

That compositional habit is the book's durable reference algorithm: identify the state component, choose the language that selects or renders it, make the version and scope explicit, perform the smallest operation, and inspect every coupled component that the operation could have changed. It is equally applicable to a one-line daily edit and to a Gerrit stack with several descendants, multiple review identities, a moving trunk, and a conflict. The command syntax is release-specific; the accounting discipline is the jj skill.

For publication notes, write the before and after in the same vocabulary as the repository: “local bookmark review moved from C to C′; fetched review@origin remained at C; change ID qqq continued; commit ID changed; descendants R and S were rebased; operation O_n recorded the rewrite.” This sentence is more useful than “the branch was force-pushed” because it states which facts are local, which are remote, which identities evolved, and which consequences require testing. It also gives a future maintainer enough information to reproduce the decision without reconstructing it from a terminal scrollback buffer.

Use the same record for negative results. “No bookmark moved” is different from “the remote bookmark was not fetched”; “no conflict is materialised here” is different from “no commit is conflicted”; “the command selected one commit” is different from “the selected commit is the intended change”. jj rewards this precision because its separate state machines let work continue in more situations, but that flexibility means an operator must state which absence was actually observed.

In practice, these records become lightweight review artefacts: a shell transcript with full IDs, a saved revset, a temporary bookmark name, and the operation ID. They are especially valuable around force publication, immutable-head overrides, Gerrit patch-set replacement, and cross-machine handoff. Delete the temporary bookmark only after the receiving ref or fetched trunk proves that the intended state is reachable elsewhere.

That final reachability check closes the loop: the work is no longer dependent on one workspace, one operation head, or one operator's memory. It is represented by a reachable commit and, where publication is intended, by an explicitly named ref whose remote observation has been fetched and recorded.

If publication was not intended, the same proof ends at a local bookmark or a documented reachable commit. This is the practical meaning of branchless work: no name is required for every intermediate state, but every state worth preserving must have a deliberate reachability story.

The discipline is deliberately symmetric: before an operation, prove selection; after it, prove reachability. Between those points, jj may change many commit IDs, but the operator can still account for every intended change, descendant, workspace, and publication boundary.

That is the shortest reliable description of advanced jj work: selection is a query, rewriting is a graph transformation, publication is an explicit ref transaction, and recovery is evidence-led state selection.

Keep that sentence beside the terminal until it becomes instinctive.

It is a compact operational model for every command and every difficult history.

It keeps local graph work, filesystem materialisation, publication, and recovery visible as separate but connected responsibilities.

That separation is the practical safeguard against confusing a valid local rewrite with a completed publication.

Every boundary should be named and independently inspected.

That is deliberate.

Appendix directory and quick lookups

Use this directory to choose the right appendix. The short entries below are deliberately labelled as directory cards where a fuller reference appears later in the same book. The links are the authoritative route: they prevent a reader from mistaking a one-page orientation card for the complete fileset, template, configuration, or hosting reference.

Appendix A — Complete command tree

    remote [add list remove rename set-url] root]

Role: quick lookup. This is generated from 0.44.0 help. The 120 command paths are expanded in the reader-facing entries in Part XV; 0.45.1 additions are marked in the comparison note there. Use the tree to find a family, then use the command entry for arguments, options, cardinality, mutation, and examples.

Appendix B — Option index

FlagWhere/meaning
-R, --repositoryOperate on a repository path.
--ignore-working-copyDo not snapshot/update filesystem.
--no-integrate-operationCreate but do not integrate operation.
--ignore-immutableBypass rewrite protection.
--at-operationLoad historical repository view.

Do not transfer a short flag between commands by analogy. The expanded index below records ownership and interactions for the options most likely to affect graph scope or publication. The generated raw help snapshot remains an audit artefact for every spelling and default, while the command entries in Part XV explain the complete command-specific contracts.

B.1 Options by contract

OptionOwnerArgumentEffect and interaction
-R, --repositoryGlobalPATHSelects the repository. It does not select a workspace; the repository still resolves the active workspace from the current directory unless another workspace option applies.
--ignore-working-copyGlobalnoneReads the recorded repository view without snapshotting the filesystem. Pair with historical operation queries and machine output when a stable view matters.
--at-operation, --at-opGlobalOPERATIONViews an earlier operation. It implies a historical repository view; do not treat today’s filesystem as if it were part of that view.
--no-integrate-operationGlobalnoneLeaves a newly created operation unintegrated for explicit concurrency handling. It is an operation-DAG control, not a commit-DAG selection.
--ignore-immutableGlobalnoneBypasses the immutable rewrite guard. It does not make a commit mutable in policy or make a remote update safe.
--colorGlobalauto/always/neverControls terminal colour. Use never for parsers, fixtures, and evidence captures; labels and hyperlinks are presentation, not data.
--no-pagerGlobalnonePrevents a pager process. It should be paired with explicit colour suppression in automation.
--configGlobalKEY=VALUEApplies an ephemeral configuration override. It is useful for testing an alias or safety policy without mutating files.
--config-fileGlobalPATHAdds a configuration file. Layer order matters; inspect config list before relying on a repository policy.
-r, --revisionsMany commandsREVSETSelects commits. Cardinality is command-specific: log accepts a set, while edit normally requires one commit.
-s, --sourcerebaseREVSETRebases a source set and its descendants as defined by the command. It is not a synonym for “the current branch”.
-b, --branchrebaseREVSETSelects a branch-shaped source domain. Inspect the resulting set before using it on a forked graph.
-d, --destinationrebaseREVSETMoves selected source roots onto destinations. Multiple destinations can produce merges or duplicated placement; use a bounded source set.
-A, --insert-afterrebaseREVSETInserts source commits after a destination. Parent-edge changes can rebase descendants even when patch contents are unchanged.
-B, --insert-beforerebaseREVSETInserts source commits before a destination. It has different graph semantics from --insert-after; do not infer one from the other.
--skip-emptiedrebasenoneControls handling of commits whose patches become empty during the rewrite. Inspect successors and bookmark targets afterwards.
--intosquashREVSETChooses the destination change. It is distinct from the source commit selected by -r and from a fileset restricting moved paths.
--from, --tosquash, diff, file commandsREVSETNames tree/patch endpoints where supported. Read the command entry: the same words do not imply the same direction in every consumer.
--keep-emptiedsquashnonePreserves a source commit after its selected patch is moved. It changes graph shape, not merely display.
--insert-before, --insert-aftersplitREVSETPlaces the split result relative to a chosen commit when the command supports explicit placement. Check the resulting parent edges.
--message, -mdescribe, split, commit, newTEXTSets a description. A description rewrite changes the commit ID and can rebase descendants; it does not inherently change the jj change ID.
--bookmark, -bgit push, bookmark commandsNAMENames a publication ref. In git push, specify the remote separately when multiple remotes exist.
--remoteGit/bookmark/Gerrit commandsNAMESelects a remote. Tracking relationships do not replace explicit remote selection for a mixed GitHub/Gerrit repository.
--trackedgit push, bookmark commandsnoneSelects tracking state. It does not mean “all local bookmarks” and does not move a bookmark to @.
--allGit transport commandsnoneBroadens publication or ref selection according to the command. Treat it as a review-required option.
--deletedgit pushnonePublishes deletion of eligible remote refs. Verify the local/remote bookmark relationship before using it.
--allow-privategit pushnoneAllows private commits into the push selection where supported. Privacy policy and server access remain separate concerns.
--allow-conflictsgit pushnoneAllows conflicted commits to be pushed where supported. It does not resolve the conflict or guarantee host acceptance.
--named NAME=REVISIONgit pushassignmentCreates an explicit publication mapping for a push. Use it when a temporary publication name is safer than moving a durable local bookmark.
-c, --changegit pushCHANGEPublishes by change identity where supported. Verify the selected evolution and resulting remote ref instead of assuming commit-ID selection.
--dry-runGit/Gerrit push commandsnoneShows a prospective publication. It is a precondition check, not a lock against another process or contributor changing the remote.
--revisiongerrit uploadREVSETRestricts Gerrit upload selection. Make the base and range visible before upload; server patch-set association is a separate identity layer.
--remote-branchgerrit uploadNAMENames the Gerrit destination branch. Pass it explicitly on 0.44 in repositories with multiple publication protocols.
--reviewer, --ccgerrit uploadADDRESSAdds review metadata to the upload request. It affects the server request, not the local commit graph.
--topic, --hashtaggerrit uploadTEXTSupplies Gerrit review grouping metadata. Server plugins and policy determine its later interpretation.
--wip, --ready, --privategerrit uploadnoneControls review state in the upload request. Keep these options explicit in publication aliases.
--operationop commandsOPERATIONSelects a repository operation for inspection or recovery. It is not a commit and cannot be substituted with a commit ID.
--toolresolve, diff/merge commandsNAMESelects an external or builtin tool. Validate executable paths and tool exit status before automation.
--workspaceworkspace commands where supportedNAMENames a workspace in commands that accept it. The active @ still belongs to one workspace at a time.
--clear, --addsparse setPATTERNReplaces or extends sparse patterns. sparse edit opens the configuration editor and does not consume a fileset argument in 0.44.

Three rules prevent most option errors. First, classify the option as global view control, commit selection, fileset selection, graph transformation, publication, or output. Secondly, inspect whether its argument is a single commit, a set, a fileset, a name, or an external program. Finally, make the postcondition explicit: selected IDs, successor relation, bookmark target, remote ref, filesystem paths, or operation ID. A syntactically valid option with the wrong domain is still a semantic failure.

Appendix C — Revset quick reference

::x ancestors; x:: descendants; x..y ancestors of y excluding ancestors of x; x::y descendants of x that are ancestors of y; x- parents; x+ children; & intersection; | union; ~ difference/complement depending on position; root(), none(), all(), heads(), roots(), visible_heads(), and typed predicates. Patterns use explicit kinds such as exact:, substring:, glob:, and regex:. Parameterised aliases are TOML functions.

Appendix D — Fileset reference (directory card)

Directory only — continue to the complete reference. Use quoted literal paths and explicit glob: patterns; combine sets with the installed fileset operators/functions. The complete grammar, scope prefixes, quoting rules, and consumer matrix are in Appendix D.1.

Appendix E — Template reference (directory card)

Directory only — continue to the complete reference. Templates evaluate typed commit/operation objects. The complete type/method and formatting maps are in Appendix E.1 and Appendix E.2.

Appendix F — Configuration reference (directory card)

Directory only — continue to the complete reference. The human configuration reference is in Appendix F.1 and Appendix F.2. The schema captures machine-detectable release changes; it is not a substitute for the explanations and interaction rules in those sections.

Appendix G — Git-to-jj lookup

Git intentionJj conceptNon-equivalence

git resetedit, restore, abandon, op restoreSeparate pointer, tree, commit, and repository-state intentions.
git checkout Redit RWorkspace @ changes; other workspaces remain.
git checkout -bbookmark createPublication name, not required for local work.

Reference appendices: dense lookup material

The earlier appendices are quick navigation aids. The following reference pages are intentionally denser: they are designed to be useful at the terminal after the teaching chapters have established the mental model.
